AGENCY 

In the discussion to follow, "A" represents the agent or servant, “P” the principal or master, and "T" the third party involved.

Who May Be a Principal 

Any person legally qualified to contract may be a principal. An infant therefore can appoint an agent, but this appointment like any contract made by an infant, is voidable at the option of the infant. 

Casey v. Kastel, 237 N. Y. 305.

An insane person may appoint an agent, but such agent's acts may be voided by the insane principal. Where the parties can be restored to status quo, knowledge of the insanity by T is immaterial. If there can be no restoration to status quo the insane principal is bound if T did not have reasonable ground to believe that P was insane. It is to be noted, however, that where the agent is appointed after P has been judicially declared incompetent, the appointment is void and A's acts under the power do not bind P. 

McCarthy v. Bowling, 182 A. D. 18, 169 N. Y. S. 463.

Neither spouse is the agent of the other merely because of the marital relationship. Nor is there any legal restraint on the right of a married woman to appoint an agent. 

Her husband may be appointed as such agent. 

A non-resident alien enemy cannot appoint an agent in this country. 

Who May Be An Agent 

Anyone can be appointed an agent and his acts within the scope of the authority bind P. Thus, if P appoints A, an infant, to buy six tons of steel for him and A contracts with T for them, P is bound to T and the contract cannot be avoided on the ground of the agent's infancy.

The Contract of Agency 

The relationship between P and A is contractual and the elements required for the formation of a contract are necessary for the creation of an agency. The result of this agency is that A may, by his acts, attach legal rights and liabilities to P provided those acts are within the scope of the agency, as hereinafter set forth. 

There are certain acts which cannot be effected through an agent, i.e., no relationship of principal and agent is deemed to exist between the performer and the person in whose behalf the act has been done. (1) One cannot contract as agent for the commission of a crime, for the contract being illegal, cannot exist. 1

(2) One cannot delegate to an agent the performance of an act for a principal which is of a personal nature, as, hiring A to paint a picture, when P was engaged to do it; or getting A to sing at a concert when P was engaged to sing. (3) One cannot designate another as his agent for the performance of an act which P according to statute is to do personally. Thus when P holds a public office and has duties which by law must be performed personally P cannot delegate these duties to A. Nor may 

A do P's voting for him at a state election. 

How the Agent Is Appointed 

An agency can be created in five ways: (1) Orally. (2) In writing, under seal and not under seal. (3) By implication. (4) By estop pel. (5) By necessity. 

(1) and (2).—An agent can always be appointed orally unless the instrument which the agent is to execute must, under the orders given the agent, be under seal, or unless the statute requires that particular contract of agency to be in writing. 

Royal Indemnity Co. v. Dansiger, 101 Misc. 505, 167 N. Y. S. 379; 

Wood v. Wise, 153 A. D. 223, 137 N. Y. S. 1017.

The agency for the conveyance of an interest in realty, or the execution of a lease for a period over a year, on behalf of a principal can only be created by an instrument in writing. This requirement is statutory. But even in this case where P orally asks A to sign for him in his presence, P is bound despite the fact that A under an oral appointment signed a conveyance or a paper under seal. 

Real Property Law, § 242. 

Other contracts to be made with T by A for P which are required by the Statute of Frauds to be in writing though not necessarily under seal may properly be made by A though his appointment was. orally created.

One partner may delegate authority to the other orally to act on behalf of the partnership in any situation, regardless of any of the above rules. (3) By implication.-An example of such agency would be a case where A, the captain of P's ship, faced with an emergency borrows money for the ship's needs. The master is liable for the debt incurred. The captain is impliedly authorized so to act. 

McCready v. Thorn, 51 N. Y. 454. (4) Estoppel.-If there is a representation by P either by word or act that A is his agent whereas he really is not, and T acts on this misrepresentation, P is estopped from denying the agency. Thus where a mortgagor pays the interest to a lawyer who properly holds and exhibits the bond and mortgage, it would be a good payment as

AGENCY 

3 against the mortgagee, since leaving the bond and mortgage in the lawyer's possession gave the apparent authority to collect. 

Crane v. Gruenewald, 120 N. Y. 274.

If a person authorizes another to assume the apparent right of disposing of property in the ordinary course of trade, it may be presumed that the apparent authority is the real authority. Where 

P sends his commodity to a place to be exhibited as for sale in a place where such articles are sold, it may be presumed that the commodity was sent there for the purpose of sale. It should be noted, however, that mere possession in and of itself is not such apparent authority as to permit the passing of a valid title. 

Smith v. Clews, 105 N. Y. 283.

There is a presumption that brokers in possession of negotiable instruments, such as bonds, have the right to sell them. 

Kittredge v. Grannis, 236 N. Y. 375. (5) By necessity.—Suppose a horse is shipped by T to P, and P delays in calling for it at the station. The railroad station-master, in feeding the animal until P calls for it, is P's agent by necessity. 

P is responsible for his necessary purchases. 

The Relationship Between Principal and Agent 

Since relationship between principal and agent is contractual, a breach of the contract of agency, although it terminates the agency gives the plaintiff a cause of action for breach of contract. 

An agent is under an obligation to aid his employer in the work for which he is engaged. He must do nothing to make profit for himself at the expense of his principal, without the fullest knowledge and consent of the principal. Thus where P's salesman secretly procures a lease of P's premises to take effect upon the termination of P's lease, the salesman can be forced in equity to assign his new lease to P. It was his duty to aid his employer, P, in his endeavor to continue to carry on his business. 

Kinney v. Glenny, 231 A. D. 311; 247 N. Y. S. 119.

On the same principle an agent may not embark on an adventure on his own account in a business which is similar to that for which he has been engaged to act as agent and make a profit himself, which profit might reasonably have been made by the employer had A not embarked on this adventure. He is a trustee for P of any profits thus made. 

Beatty v. Guggenheim Exploration Co., 225 N. Y. 380.

If an agent is hired to buy stock for his principal, he cannot secretly sell to the latter his own stock, even though the sale may be a bargain for the principal. An agent cannot buy from himself. The principal can have the transaction set aside. 

Taussig v. Hart, 58 N. Y. 425.

The penal law forbids a purchasing agent to get any commission from one who sells to P, or the seller to give the purchasing agent any commission for purchasing from him on behalf of P. 

Penal Law, § 439. 

When an agent is authorized to sell an article for a specified sum and he sells such article for a larger amount, the larger amount belongs to the principal. 

Greenslete v. Ferguson, 191 A. D. 745, 182 N. Y. S. 198.

When the agent is engaged in a scheme to defraud his principal, his knowledge of certain facts is not notice to the principal so as to bind the principal with that knowledge. In one case T applied to P for a loan. P sent A to appraise T's equity in realty which was to be the security for the loan. A learnt of an unrecorded lien but in collusion with T, he kept the knowledge from P. The loan was made and when P tried to foreclose, the owner of the prior lien defended claiming that although his lien was unrecorded it was known to P because A, his agent, knew of it. This was held to be no defense. 

When an agent attempts to defraud his own principal, his knowledge is not that of the principal. 

Henry v. Allen, 151 N. Y. 1; 

Granite v. Hutchins, 225 A. D. 412, 233 N. Y. S. 404.

An agent cannot represent both parties to any transaction, unless both parties agree that A is to be the agent of both parties to the transaction. Of course, if his own interest is opposed to that of his P, he cannot serve as agent. 

Jarvis v. Schaefer, 105 N. Y. 289.

The Relationship Between Agent and Third Party 

Where an agency is disclosed and the contract relates to the matter of the agency and is within the authority conferred, the agent will not be personally bound unless there be clear and explicit evidence of an intention to substitute or to superadd the personal liability of the agent to that of his principal. 

Voss v. Lowry, 225 A. D. 507, affd. 252 N. Y. 587; 

Lewitus v. Brown, 228 A. D. 146, 239 N. Y. S. 261.

Where a person commits a tort, he is personally liable for the tort, whether or not he is an agent and whether or not the agency is disclosed.

If the third party has advanced money to the supposedly authorized agent to be given to the principal, the injured party in an action for moneys had and received cannot recover from the agent moneys paid to the agent and later delivered to the principal, for the agent has never received such moneys. They would be recoverable from

AGENCY 

5 the principal only, upon a rescission of the contract made with the principal. 

Harriss v. Tams, 258 N. Y. 229.

An agent impliedly warrants his authority to make the representations through which he induces another to make a contract with the principal. For the breach of that implied warranty, the agent is liable to the injured party for all damages which flow naturally from reliance upon the agent's assertion of authority. Even though the agent's liability on his implied warranty attaches the instant the contract is made, his liability is a continuing one, and no action will lie until a breach thereof occurs, i.e., when there is a breach of the contract made with the third person. 

Moore v. Maddock, 251 N. Y. 420.

Scope of Authorities in Various Agencies 

Any person dealing with an agent is bound at his peril to ascertain the limits of the agent's authority beyond which the principal will not be held liable. But where the principal clothes the agent with apparent authority to do a certain act, he will be held responsible to the extent of such appearance. This is not extended beyond the strict letter of the apparent power for which the principal himself is responsible, and will not include additional acts or representations made by the agent in excess thereof. 

In re Welton's Estate, 143 Misc. 830, 257 N. Y. S. 590. (1) Warranties.—If the business in which A is engaged for P is one in which it is customary to give a warranty, it is implied that a warranty may be given by A. This is so, even if P expressly orders 

A not to warrant, provided this order is unknown to T. In what business a warranty usually is given is a question of fact. 

If A sells by sample it is implied that he can warrant goods to be like the sample. 

Sharlette v. Lake Placid Co., 194 A. D. 844, 185 N. Y. S. 543. 

First Natl. Bank v. Farson, 226 N. Y. 218. (2) Credit.-A selling agent has no power to sell on credit unless it is the known usage of the trade to do so; nor can he pledge and so bind P; nor can he receive payment, unless in possession of the goods to be delivered, in which case power to receive payment is implied.

Iroquois Mfg. Co. v. Davis, 122 Misc. 273, 203 N. Y. S. 607. 

If P sends A to sell an article to T for a specific price, the sale to be on credit and not cash, should A take cash and abscond, P has no rights against T. Where private instructions are given, the instructions are not limitations on T, if T does not know of them.ų, oer 

A has no implied power to buy on credit unless credit is indisafebor

pensable to carrying out his duties; nor may he execute negotiable paper in payment for the goods. 

The agent must buy in the manner authorized. If authorized to buy commodities for cash he cannot buy them for part cash and part as exchange for other goods. Should the principal fail to ratify this latter transaction, the agent cannot recover for any money advanced by him, as he is deemed to have bought the commodities for himself. (3) Contracts.—Where A has been engaged to carry out the provisions of a contract, he has no implied authority to waive any of the contract provisions. (4) Real Estate Agents.-A real estate broker is a mere negotiator. He brings the parties together. Having done this his task is completed. If such broker in his zeal to carry out his own purposes makes false representations as to the condition of the property, the principal is not bound. 

Friedman v. N. Y. Telephone Co., 256 N. Y. 392.

No obligation can be imposed upon the principal by the act of an agent outside of his real or apparent authority; but a contract made on behalf of the principal may be rescinded by the other party if tainted by fraud in its inception, though the principal was himself innocent of any fraud. That is not because through retention of moneys received upon a contract made by an authorized agent an innocent principal ratifies the unauthorized acts of the agent in procuring the contract, but because a principal who asserts the validity of a contract made by the agent holds the contract subject to any invalidity or taint in its inception. 

Harriss v. Tams, 258 N. Y. 229.

In one case a landowner had signed a deed which was blank as to the name of the grantee. He temporarily intrusted it to a friend that he might exhibit it to a third person, a prospective buyer. The friend filled in his own name as grantee, and mortgaged the land to a bank which loaned him money. It was held that the owner was not estopped by his carelessness from asserting the invalidity of the instrument. Consciousness of peril to strangers, in the careless execution of instruments of the negotiable type, would induce the reasonably prudent man to exercise attentive care that the peril might be avoided. In the case of instruments of the non-negotiable type, the risk involved is less easily perceived; correspondingly the duty of care less frequently arises. 

Hulbert v. Walker, 258 N. Y. 8, 19.

A general power given to an agent to manage property does not by itself include authority to bind the principal to negotiate commercial paper.

AGENCY 

7 One holding an unindorsed commercial paper has no implied authority to collect on it. 

Gussenhoven v. Turner, 260 N. Y. S. 514.

Liability of a Master for the Torts of His Servant 

A master is responsible for the acts of the servant within the general scope of his employment, while engaged in his master's business, and done with a view to the furtherance of that business. Thus, in an action for false imprisonment, where it appeared that the plaintiff was arrested by servants of a department maintained by the defendant company for the purpose of investigating criminal complaints, straightening out matters and doing such acts as might lead to the recovery of articles stolen from customers, it was held that whether the servants were acting within the scope of their authority was a question for the jury. 

Mott v. Consumers' Ice Co., 73 N. Y. 543; 

Curran v. Buckpitt, 225 A. D. 380, 233 N. Y. S. 249; 

McLoughlin v. N. Y. Edison Co., 252 N. Y. 202.

A principal is liable for the fraud and deceit of his agent, which was committed for the principal in the course of his employment, and within the scope of his authority, even though the principal did not authorize the fraud.

In a recent case, A was P's agent to sell a lot. As an inducement to T to buy, A contracted to finance the building of a bungalow on the lot. A never intended to carry out his contract. It was held that A's act was within the scope of his agency, whether or not P knew A's false representation of an existing fact-his state of mind. 

Moore v. Abbey, 213 A. D. 787, 210 N. Y. S. 766; 

Bennett v. Judson, 21 N. Y. 238; 

Haydock v. Stow, 40 N. Y. 363, 368.

The liability of the principal for the acts of his agent or servant has been carried over to situations where A does damage because of an incidental negligent act done while in the very act of carrying out the business of the agency. In one case, A was employed at 

P's garage to sell gasoline. While the gasoline was flowing from the pump to a customer's car, A flipped his cigarette over the car. 

Sparks ignited the flowing gasoline, and the explosion which followed injured the plaintiff. P was held liable, as it was A's negligent act while in the very act of doing his proper work. 

Wood v. Saunders, 228 A. D. 69, 238 N. Y. S. 571.

An auctioneer, for the purposes of sale, represents the owner and his acts are those of the owner. If the auctioneer secretly employs puffers for the purpose of enhancing the price, the buyer can recover any sum paid by him for the property bought at the auction. 

Cerreta v. Costello, 212 A. D. 687, 209 N. Y. S. 257.

But where a master is without authority to do an act, such act wilfully done by the agent will not be considered within the scope of his employment, and the master will not be held liable unless he actually authorized or ratified the act. Therefore, if A, while collecting rents for P, assaulted T, a tenant, for the latter's refusal to pay the rent, P cannot be held liable for the assault because P himself would have no authority to do what A did, and he cannot delegate such power to another. Similarly, P would not be held liable if A, who ran the elevator for P's apartment house, wilfully assaulted a tenant riding in the elevator. This case is to be distinguished from the cases where an employee has legal authority to exercise reasonable force to perform his duties, such as a railroad conductor in removing a disorderly person from his train. 

Zucker v. Lannin, 217 A. D. 487, 217 N. Y. S. 65; 

Trebitsch v. Goelet, 252 N. Y. 554.

The fact that A, in the exercise of his duties, did not use the best or shortest method in the execution of his duty is immaterial. 

So if a driver A sent to a railroad depot in the southern part of the town drives the wagon south, but takes the longest route through the park, so as to see his friend on the way, his master as well as he, A, would be liable to T if T were hurt by A's negligent driving on the way. But the distinction between a "roundabout" way and a "different" way should be noticed. If A had gone in an entirely different direction, say he took a northerly instead of a southerly route, and while off his route injured someone, the employer would not be liable. It is for the jury to determine when A started to be outside the scope of his employment. 

Baum v. Schweitzer, 124 Misc. 516, 208 N. Y. S. 549; 

Bloodgood v. Whitney, 235 N. Y. 110.

A was A very interesting situation was raised in a recent case. employed by P to drive his truck. While so engaged, A negligently ran over his wife standing in the highway. The wife sued P for injuries sustained. Of course she could not recover against A, her husband, in tort, as one spouse cannot be sued in tort by the other spouse. Could she recover against the master? The Court held that she could recover, as the act of the servant became the act of the master. The liability of the servant did not matter. It was the act which attached itself to the master. 

Schubert v. Schubert Wagon Co., 249 N. Y. 253; 

Allen v. Allen, 246 N. Y. 571.

Effect of Statute Governing Operation of Motor Vehicles 

By statute many of the common-law principles of the law of agency have been changed in cases involving motor cars. Under the present law if an owner of a motor vehicle expressly or impliedly permits X to use his automobile, the owner is liable for any damage

AGENCY 

caused by the negligent operation of the automobile while X is legally using the automobile, even though the use be only in the business of X. The burden of proving the permission to use the motor vehicle is upon the plaintiff. 

Atwater v. Lober, 133 Misc. 652, 233 N. Y. S. 309;

Vehicle and Traffic Law, § 59. 

The injured party may sue the operator alone or the owner alone, as it is a joint and several liability. The death of either the owner or the operator does not abate the action against the other. 

Hatch v. Lovejoy, 142 Misc. 137; 254 N. Y. S. 35.

The negligence of the driver is not imputable to the owner so that the latter may sue the former for damages resulting from the driver's negligence.

Webb v. Elmira Water Co., 258 N. Y. S. 892. 

What is "use with permission of the owner" under Section 59 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law has been the subject of a number of decisions. If the owner specifically prohibits the use of the car by the borrower in a certain territory, a collision in the proscribed territory would not subject the owner to liability as the borrower would then be using the car without permission. 

Chaika v. Vandenberg, 252 N. Y. 101.

An owner who leaves an automobile with an independent contractor to be repaired is liable for the independent contractor's negligence while giving the automobile the customary and necessary road test. 

Zuckerman v. Parton, 260 N. Y. 446.

It has further been decided that where the person using the motor vehicle was in possession of it because he was the servant of the owner, the common-law principles of master and servant apply rather than the above-mentioned statute. Thus, if a chauffeur of an automobile owner injures X, while on a frolic of his own, the owner would not be liable to X for injuries sustained because of the chauffeur's negligence. 

Psota v. Long Island R. Co., 246 N. Y. 388.

The scope of Section 59 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law is broad enough to hold the owner liable to one who is an invitee with the consent of the owner, of the person to whom the automobile is loaned. The court has not passed upon the question whether the liability of the owner extends to an invitee of the borrower when the owner did not consent to the presence of the invitee in the car. 

Cohen v. Neustadter, 247 N. Y. 207.

When the lender of the car is engaged in the business of renting cars to others, he is not liable for the negligence of the borrower if the lender carries insurance against liability arising out of negligence in the operation of said motor vehicle in limits not less than $5,000

for any one person injured or killed, and $10,000 for more than one injured or killed in any one accident, and for property damage of not less than $1,000. This policy inures to the benefit of the borrower operating with the permission of the renter in the same manner and under the same conditions and to the same extent as the renter. 

Vehicle and Traffic Law, § 59. 

It thus appears that an owner in loaning his car may reasonably restrict the uses to which it may be put; consequently its employment for a proscribed purpose cannot be a permitted use. On the other hand, if the limiting instructions relate to the manner of operation, such as the speeding or careless pilotage of the car, though the instructions be disobeyed, nevertheless the use is with "permission" of the owner. It thus follows that if the owner permits the borrower to have a guest in the car and the borrower permits the guest to drive the car with him in it, the owner would be subject to liability for damage caused by the guest's negligence. This would be true even if the owner had clearly instructed the borrower that only he was to drive. The thing forbidden related to the operation of the car, not to the use which might be made of it. 

If the borrower permits the guest to use and entirely control the automobile borrowed without the presence of the guest in the car and without the consent of the owner, the owner would not be liable for any damage caused by such guest. 

Owen v. Grunts, 216 A. D. 19, 214 N. Y. S. 543; 

Arcara v. Moresse, 258 N. Y. 211.

If the motor vehicle was sold to A under a contract of conditional sale, so that title was in the vendor, A is the sole "owner" for the purpose of this rule, despite the terms of the contract between the vendor under the conditional sale, declaring title to be in the vendor. 

Nor is a chattel mortgagee of a motor vehicle, out of possession, the "owner" for the purposes of this rule.

Vehicle and Traffic Law, § 59. 

Liability of an Independent Contractor 

If X hires P to do certain work, a question frequently arises whether P is X's agent or is an independent contractor, so as to determine X's liability in case someone is injured by P or his help during the work. This is really best determined by the circumstances. P is an independent contractor if he (1) used his own tools, (2) directed the work, (3) paid the help, (4) had the sole right to hire and discharge the help, (5) had contracted with X for a lump sum or its equivalent, so that P was put in a position of profiting or losing. If some of these situations exist and some do not, it would seem that it is then a question of weight. 

Beach v. Velay, 238 N. Y. 100; 

Dutcher v. Victoria Paper Mills, 219 A. D. 541, 220 N. Y. S. 625.

AGENCY 

11 In a recent case, X directed when and where the work was to be done; P and his men actually did the work, P controlling the operation and the manner of accomplishment of the work. P was held to be the master, and not X. 

Bartolomeo v. Bennet Contr. Co., 245 N. Y. 66.

The general rule is that the owner of property is not liable for the negligent act of an independent contractor with whom he has an agreement for the performance of work. There is no duty resting upon an owner or a general contractor to inspect the machinery and tools furnished by a subcontractor, which that contractor exclusively possesses and controls; nor of any duty after an inspection disclosing defects, to make repair of a defective appliance. 

Iacono v. Frank & Frank, 259 N. Y. 377. 

There are some exceptions to this rule. (1) If the work which the contractor is hired to do constitutes a public nuisance, the one who hired the independent contractor is also liable for the contractor's negligence. (2) If the work which the contractor is hired to do is of such nature that the injury necessarily results from the very doing of the work, and not from the method of its performance, the owner is liable. (3) If the owner interferes with the work of the independent contractor and as a result X is injured, the owner is liable. (4) If the independent contractor is hired to do unlawful work, the owner is liable. (5) If the employer is bound to repair by statute, his obligation cannot be avoided by engaging others to do the work. 

Paltrey v. Eagen, 200 N. Y. 83. 

Berg v. Parsons, 156 N. Y. 109;

Kowalsky v. Conseco Co., 260 N. Y. S. 688. 

Thus a lessor who undertook to repair a roof was held liable for damage to a lessee's goods by rain coming through a portion of the roof left unprotected by an independent contractor whom the lessor hired. The lessor was under an obligation to keep the roof of the leased building in good repair. 

Komar v. Wynn, 125 Misc. 176, 210 N. Y. S. 422. 

Fellow-Servant Rule

In cases where the Workmen's Compensation Law is not applicable, a servant is precluded from recovery against the master for the negligent act of a co-employee wherever the functions which he and the negligent co-employee were discharging, although not identical or even similar in character, were yet such that the two servants were contributing directly to the common object of their

common employer in that enterprise for which their services were engaged. 

Fay v. DeCamp, 257 N. Y. 407.

If one servant was using his own property (e.g. an automobile) in connection with his work solely for his own convenience, and this servant was not being specially paid by the master for the use of this property, he can recover from the master for the damage to this property caused by the negligence of a fellow servant in the course of the latter's employment. 

Setzkorn v. City of Buffalo, 219 N. Y. 351.

Where the Workmen's Compensation Law does apply a servant must seek his relief under this law when he is injured in the course of his employment. If a fellow-servant causes the injury and this fellow-servant is sued, he cannot set up the defense that the employer carried insurance under the Workmen's Compensation Law and that the plaintiff must sue thereunder. 

Shelter v. Grobsmith, 143 Misc. 380, 257 N. Y. S. 353.

Delegation of Authority by the Agent to Another 

An agent may appoint another to act for him and the sub-agent's act will bind P, if (1) The act is purely ministerial or mechanical, as where A, employed to get people insured, employs clerks the collect the premiums, or deliver the policies, or (2) If the nature of the work is such as to require additional help. Where A is employed to buy land-in such case he may employ an attorney to close the title. If A is to manage a large business he may employ help. It would be impossible for him to do it all alone. When a depositor deposits a check which he holds, with his bank, which check is drawn on a foreign bank, it is known that the first bank will require the services of a foreign bank to complete the transaction. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 350d; 

Commercial Bank v. Norton, 1 Hill 501. 

If a servant, A, without authority, express or implied, engages another to do his work, the master is not liable for damage caused by the person so engaged if the latter was competent. If, however, the servant engages one who is not competent, or if the servant directly supervises or controls the acts of the person he engages, the employer is liable for any damage caused T by this subservant. The basis of the master's liability is the negligence of the servant A, in the execution of his selection, and this negligence is fused with his agency and is attributed to the master. Thus, if P engages Al to drive a team of horses, and Al, without authority, permits A2, who

AGENCY 

13 is totally incompetent and inexperienced, to drive the team, the master is liable to T for damage caused by the negligence of A2 to T. 

Grant v. Knepper, 245 N. Y. 158.

In a recent case it was held that the master was liable for the negligence of his servant in causing injury to one who had been invited by the chauffeur to ride with him, because the invitation had been extended to this plaintiff for his ability to point out to the chauffeur where certain furniture was to be delivered. The chauffeur was acting in his master's interest, and the court held that he had implied authority to get aid in the accomplishment of his duty from one not a servant. A case almost identical with the Gross case was decided the other way in Anderson v. Eastern Brewing Co. (71 Misc. 519), that case stating that the plaintiff was a fellow-servant and even though injured through the servant's negligence he could not recover against the master. The trend seems to be in favor of the Gross case. 

Gross v. Mertz, 216 A. D. 726, 214 N. Y. S. 198.

Where the servant has found it necessary to obtain additional help because the master has not supplied him with sufficient help, the negligence of the master in failing to furnish the help so necessary will permit recovery by the third person so asked to help for injuries sustained and he will not be considered a fellow-servant. 

Bloss v. Pure Oil Co., 133 Misc. 486, 232 N. Y. S. 232.

In cases where A may properly appoint subagents, A is not responsible for their acts unless there has been fraud or gross negligence in choosing them.

The burden is on the person dealing with the agent to show that the agent has the authority, real or apparent, which he assumes to exercise. If an agent in T's presence telephones to his P before assuming a certain authority, and then states that he has such authority, evidence of that is not enough to prove the actual fact of such authority, in the absence of evidence that the telephone conversation was really held with the principal. 

Dudley v. Perkins, 235 N. Y. 448. Ratification

This applies to the subsequent adoption of acts of A on behalf of a disclosed principal which were really unauthorized. Once P ratifies, he is bound. After the ratification and notice to T, T cannot withdraw even with A's consent, for it now has become P's contract. 

It must be emphasized that the act which is later ratified must have originally been done in behalf of P, a disclosed principal who was in existence and named as the principal when the contract between A and T was made or sufficiently specified so as to be capable of later identification. A could, under this, insure a house for "the benefit of those concerned" and anyone concerned could ratify.

Though ratification must be by a positive act, there can be ratification by silence in cases where A exceeded his authority. In such case P is bound to disavow A's act within a reasonable time after knowledge of the facts comes to him. Where A had no authority at all to begin with, it depends on the particular circumstances of the case whether mere silence will operate as presumptive proof of ratification. If the circumstances are such that the silence of P would induce T to rely upon an agency and to act upon such reliance to his detriment, then such silence or inaction may be considered as a ratification. 

Ketcham v. Morsland, 18 Misc. 450, 42 N. Y. S. 7.

The ratification must be made in the same manner that the authority of agency would have had to be given originally If the authority should have been in writing, the ratification must be in writing.

To constitute a valid ratification, P must know all the facts of importance when he ratifies. A deliberate ratification without investigation, is a valid ratification. Yet when A was originally authorized to do a certain act, and he goes beyond his instructions, there is at once a presumption that P has ratified, which presumption can be overcome by evidence to the contrary. 

Merritt v. Bissell, 136 N. Y. 521.

The ratification must be of the whole and not of a part. 

Hyatt v. Clark, 118 N. Y. 563.

Once there is ratification, the agent is no longer responsible to the other contracting party. It becomes an ordinary case of an agent having contracted for a disclosed principal.

Ratification dates back to the creation of the contract between the unauthorized agent and the third party. Where, however, the subject-matter of the contract is destroyed before the ratification, the ratification is invalid. In a leading case, A, an insurance broker, placed insurance with T Insurance Company for P, without authority. 

The insured building burned and then P ratified A's act. 

It was held that the ratification was invalid.

An executor's power relates back to the decedent's death. He may ratify an act done between the time of the decedent's death and the time of his appointment.

Ratification can be applied to cases where A committed a tort, in which case a sharing of the fruits of the tort would be considered ratification. In such an instance both A and P would become liable. 

But there can be no ratification of an illegal contract. So, a contract made by A for P with an enemy alien non-resident cannot be ratified. But note that a forged instrument can be ratified. The

AGENCY 

15 crime of forgery, however, is not excused thereby. By the ratification the signature becomes valid only for purposes of suit in civil cases.

Though an insane person can ratify, he cannot do so while he is non compos mentis. 

Undisclosed Principal

Where A deals in his own name, and later T learns that A was really P's agent, T can elect to sue either A or P and get judgment against one or the other in the alternative. And any set-off that T might have against A may be asserted against P, when he learns of his identity. 

Foreign Trade Banking Corp. v. Gersetta Corp, 237 N. Y. 265.

If T can recover against either A or P, a satisfaction of a judgment against A or P bars any suit against the other. But if T enters judgment against A when he is aware of P's identity, it is an election, even though there is no satisfaction of the judgment. 

If he didn't know of P's identity, it is no election, if merely brought to judgment against A. 

Lasher v. McAdam, 125 Misc. 685, 211 N. Y. S. 395; 

Georgi v. Texas Co., 225 N. Y. 410.

Where A discloses the name of his principal, if he signs the written contract in his own name merely, which contract does not upon its face show that he was acting as the agent of another, he will be personally bound thereby. 

Meyer v. Redmond, 205 N. Y. 478.

Even if A had signed, “A, president of Z Corporation," A would be liable, as the signature would be A's, with the rest of it only descriptive of A.

Werner v. Emerson Hotel, 192 N. Y. S. 273. 

If, however, the contract on its face indicates even to a slight degree, that A, though he signed the contract in his own name, was acting for another, the question of whether the contract was executed by him as agent or as principal is one of fact for the jury to determine. This, of course, permits parol evidence to discharge the agent. 

Golden v. Shaw, 204 A. D. 404; 198 N. Y. S. 155.

In connection with undisclosed principals, the following may be noted. A signs his name to a note. Only A can be sued on the note. Any undisclosed principal cannot be sued on the note, but only on the debt which the note represents, for the rule is that on a negotiable instrument you can sue only those whose names appear on it.

The rule that only those named in a negotiable instrument can sue or be sued on it, is equally true of any sealed instrument.

The presence of a seal prevents suit against a principal not named in the instrument even if the principal accepts the benefits of the instrument. Where an agreement under seal read "Agreement made May 8, 1926 between J. J. Dowd and Gerfish Realty Co.

Inc. etc." and was signed by J. J. Dowd and Max Fishelman, the corporation was allowed to sue upon the instrument upon proof that Max Fishman was its agent. The principal was named in the instrument clearly enough to show that it was a party thereto. 

Gerfish Realty Co., Inc. v. Dowd, 248 N. Y. 539.

If the agent signs a contract with T, as agent of P, he cannot sue T in his own name when nothing on the face of the agreement shows that he is a party to, or a beneficiary of, the contract. 

If A contracts as agent for P, without the authority of P, A cannot be held as a principal and thus held to the contract. A may only be held liable on his breach of warranty of authority. 

It follows from this that if A contracts as agent for the P corporation, to purchase realty from T, the fact being that the P corporation is not yet organized but is going to be, specific performance cannot be compelled against A should this P corporation not be in existence when the day for the passing of title arrives. A's liability rests only on the warranty of authority. 

Weiss v. Baum, 218 A. D. 83, 217 N. Y. S. 820.

Termination of Agency 

An agency may be terminated at the will of P or A at any time, despite the existence of a contract which may exist between P and A. The principal is liable to the agent for damages if the revocation of the agent's authority involves a breach of contract between P and A. And A is liable to P if the former breaches his contract of agency.

If P has revoked the agency, which was not an agency for one particular act, but rather a continuing agency, and T, who knew of the agency but not of the revocation, continued to deal with A, P would be bound. It was up to P to give actual notice of the revocation to those who had dealt with A previously. As to all other people who had merely heard of the agency, but had not dealt with A, constructive notice (as a publication in the papers) would be sufficient. 

McNeilly v. Continental Life Ins. Co., 66 N. Y. 23.

There are two exceptions to the rule that the agency can be terminated at the will of either: (1) An agency coupled with an interest in the subject-matter of the agency.-P places collateral with a bank (A) for a loan. 

P fails to pay on the due date and the bank, which is now an agent

AGENCY 

17 in the case, attempts to sell. P cannot revoke the bank's agency, for it amounts to a lien in the bank's favor and cannot be wiped away by revocation. The lien constitutes an interest. 

In this connection it should be noted that if P owes A $500 and says to A, "Sell my race-horse and take your debt out of the sale," this authority is revocable, even if P had said that it was to be irrevocable. The mere fact that he was to take his debt out of it does not make it an agency coupled with an interest. The "interest did not grow out of that agency" as it did in the first case. 

Haiss v. Becker, 198 A. D. 123, 189 N. Y. S. 791. (2) An agency coupled with an obligation.—A spends some of his own money while acting for P. After A has spent the money, the agency becomes irrevocable; A can go through with the deal so as to protect his expenditures and P cannot revoke the agency. 

This rule does not apply in the case of attorney and client. An attorney may be discharged by his client at any time, with or without cause. The attorney cannot recover for breach of contract, but is limited to a claim upon quantum meruit. The incurring of expense by the attorney prior to the discharge does not keep the relationship alive after the discharge. The expenses are to be added to the amount earned upon quantum meruit. 

In re Driscoll, 131 Misc. 613, 228 N. Y. S. 335.

Death of P revokes any and every agency (except one coupled with an interest or an obligation), and even though neither A nor T knew of his death. This rule applies no matter what hardships it may work on T. 

Glennan v. Rochester Trust Co., 209 N. Y. 12.

Insanity of P terminates the agency if T knew of the insanity, or there was a formal adjudication of insanity even without T's knowing it. The insanity of A likewise terminates the agency. But if 

T cannot in these instances be put in status quo and was ignorant of the insanity (there having been no adjudication), P is bound. 

The adjudication of bankruptcy of A or P terminates the agency over matters affected by the bankruptcy.

Bailment and Sale

BAILMENTS 

In order that a bailment exist, there must be (1) actual or constructive delivery by the bailor, (2) of personalty, (3) which is accepted by the bailee, actually or constructively. 

Where a patient leaves his overcoat in the reception room of a dentist, there being no closet in which to deposit wraps, the dentist is the bailee of this overcoat. 

Webster v. Lane, 125 Misc. 868, 212 N. Y. S. 298.

Where A delivers goods to B, who is to remodel, reshape or do other work upon these goods and return them in the altered form to A, we have a bailment. The fact that it will be necessary for B to add some of his own material to A's goods does not alter the situation. The title to the materials used by the bailee and added to the article worked on, passes to the bailor by accession. 

If A delivers goods to B and B is to return other goods in exchange, it is a sale.

Title to goods which are the subject of a bailment remains in the bailor, whereas in a sale, title is in the vendee, regardless of possession.

Should the sheriff levy on bailed goods while in the bailee's possession under an execution issued upon a judgment against the bailee, the sheriff is liable because title was in the bailor and not in the bailee. Where bailed goods are destroyed through no one's fault, the bailor stands the loss.

Where goods are delivered to A for safekeeping, A being given an option to buy, it is a bailment until A actually buys. 

Bailment for Sole Benefit of Bailor

In such bailment the bailee is liable only for gross negligence. 

A gratuitous agreement to undertake a trust in the future, gratuitously, is not obligatory. But if the trust is entered upon, the bailee is responsible on his undertaking, even though he be a gratuitous bailee. 

Siegel v. Spear & Co., 234 N. Y. 479.

Bailment for Sole Benefit or Profit of Bailee 

In a bailment of this kind, the bailee is liable for even slight negligence on his own part. If, however, the property is stolen, damaged or destroyed through an independent agency, the bailee is 18

BAILMENTS 

19 not liable if he has exercised ordinary care in respect to the property entrusted to him for safekeeping. 

Hobbie v. Ryan, 130 Misc. 221, 223 N. Y. S. 654.

If the bailee was to use the bailed article in a certain manner, or for a certain purpose, he is liable in conversion for any slight departure. Thus if A hires a car to go to Plattsburg and goes instead to Rouse's Point past Plattsburg and the car is damaged, A is liable though it was not the fault of A.

The fact that it was agreed that the bailee was not to be liable in case of theft does not mean that he is not liable if he is negligent. 

To be relieved of liability in this case it is necessary to stipulate that he is not to be liable even if negligent. 

Balice v. Erie R. R. Co., 208 A. D. 427, 203 N. Y. S. 636. 

Bailments for Mutual Benefit of Bailor and Bailee (1) Pledges. In a pledge, title remains in the pledgor. The pledgee obtains only possession with a right to sell the property at a public sale in case of the pledgor's default and after demand. A sale by the pledgee before the pledgor's default is absolutely ineffective as against the pledgor.

There must be actual or constructive delivery of the property to constitute a pledge. If the pledgee parts with possession, his lien is destroyed. If, however, possession is restored to the pledgor for some special purpose and then to be returned, the lien remains a lien against all except bona fide possessors. 

McCoy v. American Express Co., 253 N. Y. 477.

A wrongful use of the pledged property by the pledgee does not terminate the pledge. The pledgee remains liable for any resulting damage. If he is unable to restore the pledged article upon the payment of the debt, he is liable in conversion for the actual loss suffered.

The pledgee owes a direct duty to the pledgor to be reasonably careful in his custody of the pledge so that no harm should come to it through his act. 

Little v. Acceptance Corp., 215 A. D. 427, 213 N. Y. S. 606.

A pledge cannot be sold by a pawnbroker who is the pledgee unless written or printed notice of intention to sell with a statement of the article or articles to be sold has been first mailed addressed to the pledgor at the address given at the time of pledging at least ten days prior to the date of sale. Notice of every such sale must be published for at least six days previous thereto, in at least two of the daily newspapers printed in the city where the business shall be carried on, and also in two daily newspapers of the city where the sale is to take place and to be designated by the mayor, and such

notice shall specify the time and place at which such sale is to take place and the name of the auctioneers by whom the same is to be conducted together with a statement of the class of pledges to be sold and the inclusive dates and numbers of the pawn tickets of the pledges to be sold. If the pledge, at such sale, shall be purchased by the pawnbroker, the pledgor shall be entitled to redeem same within ten days thereafter by tendering to the pawnbroker the amount of the loan with the interest due thereon. 

General Business Law, § 49. 

(2) Hire of Chattels.-The care to be used by this bailee is the same as that of a pledgee.

The bailor in this kind of bailment like one who makes a sale, impliedly warrants the article to be fit for the purpose for which it was hired, and he is liable for damages which may result from its unsuitability. One who lets a horse and carriage for a particular journey impliedly warrants that they are suitable for the purposes of the trip. A dealer who supplies sacks for bagging peas, in order that they may be unloaded from a ship, is liable for damages if the sacks, while being hoisted, break because of their insufficiency, and a bystander is injured. 

In re People, 250 N. Y. 410.

One who installs a loaned tank for the purpose of storing gasoline impliedly warrants that it is reasonably fit and suitable for such purpose. 

Standard Oil v. Boyle, 231 A. D. 101; 246 N. Y. S. 142.

Misuse or wrongful use by the bailee constitutes conversion. 

There need be no demand and refusal in order to recover. The measure of damages is the value of the property at the time of the conversion. 

Particular Bailments¹ (a) Storekeepers.—In a leading case, the plaintiff entered the defendant's store to buy a coat. The plaintiff took off her coat, and there being no other place to put it, laid it on a counter. No one said anything. After trying on one of the defendant's coats, the plaintiff went to get her own, but it could not be found. The defendant was held liable on the ground that there was an invitation by implication extended by the defendant to lay aside the coat the customer was wearing while trying on a new one, and the store was a custodian for profit of the coat so laid aside. 

Bunnel v. Stern, 122 N. Y. 539.

Note that the liability for the loss is limited to such articles as are necessarily laid aside for the business in hand. In one case, the 

1For a discussion of the liability of a bailor of a motor vehicle, see "Agency," p. 8 et seq.

BAILMENTS 

21 plaintiff, in order to try on a new hat in the defendant's store, laid the hat she was wearing on the counter and placed her purse under the hat. The defendant was held not liable for the purse stolen. 

Powers v. O'Neill, 89 Hun 129, 34 N. Y. S. 1007.

This limitation was carried still further in the case where a customer went into a booth to try on a new pair of trousers. He left his old pair with some money in it in the booth while the tailor took measurements. The money was stolen from the trousers. The court held there was no bailment of the money, as the shopkeeper's liability is limited only to such articles as are necessarily laid aside. 

Barnes v. Stern Bros., 89 Misc. 385, 151 N. Y. S. 887.

Even the liability as to articles necessarily laid aside exists only for the time necessary for the completition of the business at hand. 

McAllister v. Simon, 27 Misc. 214, 57 N. Y. S. 733. (b) Restaurateurs.-A restaurant owner is not liable for the loss of a guest's overcoat when the coat is hung on a hook in close proximity to the guest's seat and which the guest can take with him at any time.

The fact that the waiter helps him remove the coat or hangs it up for him does not alter the situation. 

A restaurateur is not liable beyond $75, for property deposited in a checkroom, for which no fee is charged, unless the excess value of the article is stated in the receipt. If the value is so stated the proprietor is not liable beyond $100, unless the property was lost through the negligence of the restaurateur or stolen by him. 

Honig v. Riley, 244 N. Y. 105. (c) Theatre Owners.-The occupants of a box in a theatre cannot recover for the loss of clothing hung upon hooks attached to the wall of the box. The hooks are for the convenience of the occupants. 

Burden of Proof of Bailee's Liability 

A plaintiff bailor may make a prima facie case of conversion against the bailee by showing a delivery to the bailee and a failure to redeliver upon due demand. The burden of going forward then shifts to the bailee. The bailee may make out a prima facie defense by showing that the goods were destroyed or were stolen through no fault of his own. It then becomes the plaintiff's duty to again go forward and show that the loss was caused by the defendant's negligence or affirmative act. The concession on the part of the bailor that the article was stolen from the defendant bailee does not destroy the prima facie case nor relieve the bailee from proof that the theft was not through the fault of the bailee. 

Fed. Ins. Co. v. Lindsley, 132 Misc. 54, 228 N. Y. S. 614;

Vereinigte Alum-Werke v. Atl. Tidewater Term., 241 N. Y. S. 597. 

The ultimate burden of proof is upon the plaintiff bailor.

A bailee may show the bailed article was stolen from him through no fault of his own, under a plea of general denial. Innkeepers

An inn or hotel is a place where the public will be admitted to receive food or lodging for compensation. Boarding houses, lodging houses and apartment houses are not inns, because they do not fit under every part of the above definition. So-called boarding houses in summer resorts which furnish lodging, food and entertainment are inns or hotels.

When a steamship provides staterooms in which the passenger is expected to deposit the baggage needed for the voyage and to retire, the responsibility of the carrier with respect to such baggage is that of an innkeeper. 

Holmes v. North German Lloyd S. S. Co., 184 N. Y. 280; 

Dixon v. Robbins, 246 N. Y. 169; 

Friedman v. Shindler, 224 A. D. 232, affd. 250 N. Y. 574.

An innkeeper is an insurer of the safety of the property of his guest, brought infra hospitium. He is liable for its loss, whether by burglary, theft, fire or negligence, unless it arises from the neglect or misconduct of the guest, the act of God, or the public enemies. 

The innkeeper's liability at common law did not originally extend to cover property not within the walls of the inn or the buildings used in connection therewith. Whether that is the law in New York today is an open question, though the Court of Appeals has hinted that this common law rule is to be preferred. Where an innkeeper maintains a system for the transportation of baggage of guests from the station to the hotel, for which a charge is made, the innkeeper would be liable for the negligent transportation on the theory of negligence. The court refused to determine that the defendant was or was not liable for the breach of a duty owed by an innkeeper to his guest. 

Davidson v. Madison Corp., 257 N. Y. 120.

Under the statute, if (1) the proprietor of a hotel provides a safe in the hotel for the safekeeping of the valuables of guests and (2) notifies the guest by a notice posted in a conspicuous manner in the hotel of the limitation of liability, and that there is such safe in the hotel, and (3) the guest neglects to deliver such valuables for deposit to the person in charge of the office, the proprietor is not liable for loss of such valuables by theft or otherwise, unless due to the fault of the innkeeper personally. If the guest does deliver such valuables to the person in charge, the innkeeper may refuse to accept and be liable for over $500. If the innkeeper does accept more than $500, the liability of the innkeeper in the event of loss,

BAILMENTS 

23 not due to his negligence, would not exceed $500 unless there were a special agreement in writing to the contrary. 

Morris v. Manischewitz, 129 Misc. 329, 221 N. Y. S. 363;

General Business Law, §§ 200, 201. 

The liability of the innkeeper is the same under the statute, even though it be an employee of the innkeeper who stole the valuables. 

The statute however, does not control if the theft or similar wrongdoing has been committed by the defendant himself. The common law liability applies. 

Millheiser v. Beau-Site Co., 251 N. Y. 290; 

Glinsky v. Dunham, 230 A. D. 470; 245 N. Y. S. 359.

It should be noted that the duties of an innkeeper are nondelegable. If in this performance he selects third parties, the latter are not deemed independent contractors, but are agents of the innkeeper. 

Davidson v. Madison Corp., 257 N. Y. 120.

If the guest leaves his property other than jewels or money or securities in his room, the limit of the innkeeper's liability is $500. 

An innkeeper is not, and was not under the common law, an insurer of the safety of the person of the guest. He must exercise only reasonable care for his safety and comfort. There exists an implied warranty that the premises are, for the purpose of personal use by the guest, as safe as reasonable care and skill on the part of any one can make them. 

Friedman v. Shindler, 224 A. D. 232, affd. 250 N. Y. 574.

Those who are guests so as to be able to hold the innkeeper to his liability as such are those who come to use the inn as such, and to pay for the accommodation. One attending a function at a hotel or one who merely leaves his baggage at the hotel and does not stay there is not a guest. If, however, a person intending to be a guest sends his baggage ahead and later becomes a guest, the responsibility relates back to the time of the delivery of the baggage. Liens

One who alters or repairs, or enhances the value of, or shapes personalty at the owner's request, has an artisan's lien while the goods are in his possession. Title does not rest with the artisan. 

Lien Law, § 180. 

A lien is limited to the article upon which work was done. If A delivers a suit to be repaired and lends the tailor his scissors for the work, the tailor has no lien on the scissors for the price, as he did not alter, repair or enhance its value. He merely used them. The lien is limited to the article on which he worked.

Moreover, the lien is a specific lien, i.e., the lien is good only as to that article. If he returns the suit to A and A does not pay, the lien is lost. If A brings another suit the next day, this second suit cannot be held until the price on the first is paid. 

In one case the defendant wrapped newspapers for the plaintiff, a publisher, and addressed them and mailed them to the plaintiff's customers. It was held that the defendant did not have a lien upon the newspapers, as no artisan's lien was shown. 

N. Y. Dem. Pub. Corp. v. Laing, 132 Misc. 57, 229 N. Y. S. 45.

If the artisan has made any contract inconsistent with the ∙lien, the lien is waived. Thus, if the property was to be delivered before payment was to be made, the bailee has no lien. 

A lienor who stores, maintains, keeps or repairs a motor vehicle or while in his possession supplies fuel thereto at the request of the legal possessor, obtains a lien superior to the rights of a chattel mortgagee, despite the fact that title is in the mortgagee. 

Courtlandt v. Yellow Cab Co., 217 A. D. 4, 215 N. Y. S. 789;

Lien Law, § 184. 

The right to retain the property under the lien is exercisable only as to that possessed. The possession may be actual or constructive. 

The owner of the article who obtains possession from the lienor by force, fraud or trick does not thereby destroy the lien. 

Johanns v. Ficke, 224 N. Y. 513, 520.

If the owner obtains lawful possession and during such lawful possession sells the article to a bona fide purchaser, the lien is destroyed. Thus, if the bona fide purchaser should take the article, e.g., an automobile, to the lienor for repairs, the lienor cannot retain the car because of a lien he may have had for labor when the car was owned by the seller. 

Kamaran v. Sidney Garage, 137 Misc. 744; 244 N. Y. S. 337.

An innkeeper's lien extends to any personalty the guest brings in, even though it does not belong to the guest. It might even have been stolen-provided the innkeeper does not know that the goods when brought in are not those of the guest.

A lienor may pledge the article upon which the lien exists, but the owner can get back the article by payment or tender of the amount due from him, despite the pledge.

A bank has no lien upon the account of a depositor who may be indebted to the bank. Thus, if a surety for the depositor paid the latter's debt to the bank after default, the surety would not be subrogated to the deposit. The bank could continue to pay the depositor out of his account with it.

BAILMENTS 

25 Where a plaintiff pledged stock with the defendant as collateral security for a loan, a tender of the amount of the debt discharged the lien. The defendant has no further claim. 

Hearn v. Leary, 125 Misc. 446, 211 N. Y. S. 668.

Every mortgage or conveyance intended to operate as a mortgage of chattels, which is not accompanied by immediate delivery and followed by an actual and continual change of possession of the chattel, is absolutely void as against the mortgagee, and as against subsequent purchasers and mortgagees in good faith, unless the mortgage or a true copy is filed in the proper county office. 

Lien Law, § 230. 

Gifts Inter Vivos 

There are three requisites of a valid gift of personalty: (1) An intent to make an immediate gift; (2) An unconditional and complete delivery of the subject thereof, either actual or constructive, operating to divest the donor of possession and dominion over the thing given; (3) An acceptance by the donee.

A mere promise to give an article of personalty, no matter how definite, is insufficient to constitute a legal gift inter vivos. A gift inter vivos cannot be made to take effect in possession in futuro for this amounts only to a promise to make a gift. 

Gannon v. McGuire, 160 N. Y. 476. 

In re Peno's Estate, 128 Misc. 718, 221 N. Y. S. 205, 218.

The law does not presume in favor of gifts, but requires proof. 

Nor is there a presumption of delivery. Delivery may be proved by the former owner's admissions that the property belongs to another. 

In re Brown's Ex'r, 130 Misc. 865, affd. 252 N. Y. 366.

To prove intent, evidence of prior declarations of the donor, showing intent to give, is admissible as corroborative evidence of a gift. 

Subsequent declarations tending to show a gift are admssible as they really are declarations against interest. 

In re Swade, 65 A. D. 592, 72 N. Y. S. 1030; 

Van Cleef v. Maxfield, 196 A. D. 734, 188 N. Y. S. 322.

In one case a testator placed certain bonds in an envelope across the face of which he wrote, "property of Miss Lizzie Beck." The envelope was placed in his safe-deposit box to which he alone had access. It was held that this did not constitute a gift to Miss Beck. 

The element of delivery was missing. 

Beck v. Staudt, 133 N. Y. S. 529, affd. 208 N. Y. 566.

Of course, a writing may constitute admissible evidence tending to show a transfer because the writing is against the interest of the writer, but the point is that the writing standing alone and the deposit standing alone do not constitute a valid gift in themselves, without the delivery. 

In re Smith's Will, 132 Misc. 421, 230 N. Y. S. 434.

It is not less a gift because it is accompanied by a condition that it is subject to defeasance by the death of the donee before the death of the donor. 

People's Trust Co. v. Dickson, 126 Misc. 580, 214 N. Y. S. 73. 

Where M, engaged to marry F, gives the latter jewelry, he cannot get back the jewelry upon the breaking of the engagement unless his complaint and proof show that there was a definite understanding that the presentation was conditional on the later marriage. 

Rosenberg v. Lewis, 210 A. D. 690, 206 N. Y. S. 353. 

There are the following ways of turning what would be a bailment into a gift: (1) G gives a book to T and says, "It is a present for you." The delivery plus the words of gift made a good gift. The delivery, even if made to a trustee, would be a good delivery to constitute a gift to the cestui. So if a father assigns a stock certificate in the company's books to his son and delivers the certificate to the mother as trustee, it is a good gift to the son. (2) There can be delivery followed by a later intent to make a gift. For example, L lends B a book. While the book is in the latter's possession, G tells him he may keep it. This is a valid gift. (3) Suppose G has a box of diamonds and gives T the key, saying, "Keep the key; you can have the diamonds." As soon as T takes possession of the diamonds, there is a gift, for the giving of the symbol alone is not sufficient. Possession must be taken in order that there be a gift.

If the donor gives the donee the key of a vault or the combination of a safe or the key of a trunk, with words of gift of the contents, we have a good gift, since the article is incapable of manual delivery. 

Bank Accounts as Subjects of Gifts 

It follows from what has heretofore been said, that if A transfers his savings-bank book to B, signifying his intention to present his account there to B, a valid gift inter vivos is established. 

Gilkenson v. Third Ave. Railway, 47 A. D. 472, 63 N. Y. S. 792; 

In re Keating's Estate, 214 A. D. 802, 210 N. Y. S. 588; 

In re Richardson's Estate, 134 Misc. 174, 235 N. Y. S. 747.

The delivery of the bank book of a check account does not create a gift of the amount in the account. The distinction is this: A savings-bank book is an evidence of debt and the bank is not obliged to pay the depositor except upon the production of the bankbook, but in the case of a commercial account, it is not necessary to present the bank book in order to withdraw money. In the latter case the depositor controls the fund though the book may have been given away. The book is not evidence of a debt but only a memorandum. 

Brophy v. Haeberle, 220 A. D. 511, 221 N. Y. S. 698.

BAILMENTS 

27 Since a check is merely an order on a bank to pay money and is not a transfer or delivery of that fund, such check is not a gift. 

In re Gibbons Estate, 139 Misc. 658; 249 N. Y. S. 753.

Let us consider some variations of this situation. A deposits his own money in a bank, in X's name, without any qualification. Title does not pass to X unless there is a trust created in his favor or a valid gift to him. A trust is not implied from these facts. Nor is there a gift, because there was no delivery of the thing given or of a symbol which would represent possession. 

Beaver v. Beaver, 117 N. Y. 421.

A deposits his own money in his own name as trustee for X. A trust is thus established, but it is revocable. If A dies before X dies, without having disaffirmed, X becomes entitled to the balance on hand at A's death. 

Matter of Totten, 179 N. Y. 112.

Joint Bank Accounts 

If a husband and wife each contribute to a joint investment or to the purchase of a security, and the title is taken in their joint names to be held by them, no presumption arises that either intended to make a gift of his or her share to the survivor. They hold as tenants in common. But where a husband buys personally with his own funds, and takes title in the joint name of himself and his wife, there is a presumption, in the absence of other evidence, of an intent to confer upon his wife the right of survivorship. 

In re Blumenthal's Estate, 236 N. Y. 448, 452.

A bank account in the name of husband and wife, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, creates a survivorship in the wife whether or not there has been a delivery of the bank book. The intention is presumed to benefit the wife to the extent of conferring the rights of survivorship upon her and to leave him with the control and right of disposition thereof during his life. 

Brumer v. Brumer, 223 A. D. 186, 228 N. Y. S. 63.

If a bank account is opened in the name of "A or B, payable to either or survivor," a presumption at once arises that the interest of the depositors is that of joint tenants. This presumption is not conclusive and may be overcome by proof that the depositor when making the deposit had no intention to create a joint tenancy. 

Upon the death of one of the depositors, this presumption becomes conclusive in favor of the survivor in respect of any moneys then left in the account. It continues to be a mere presumption in respect of any moneys previously withdrawn. The withdrawal did not destroy the joint tenancy or the title of the survivor, if a joint tenancy had been created. It did, however, open the door to competent evi-

dence, if any was available, that the tenancy created at the opening of the account was in truth something different from the tenancy defined by the presumption. 

Marrow v. Moskowitz, 255 N. Y. 219; 

In re Porianda's Estate, 256 N. Y. 423.

Nothing in the Banking Law prevents one joint owner from destroying the joint ownership in the entire deposit to the extent of his withdrawals of no more than his equal share for his own use, although, if the entire account had been withdrawn, the result might have been otherwise. 

In re Suter's Estate, 258 N. Y. 104.

Gifts Causa Mortis 

The following elements are all essential in order to constitute an effective gift causa mortis: (1) The gift must be of personalty; (2) There must be such a delivery as vests in the donee control and dominion over the property; (3) An intention that it take effect on death; and (4) The donor must die of the ailment from which he suffers at the time he delivers the article. But note that if the donor thinks he is suffering from one disease but is actually suffering from another, and he dies of the latter, the gift would be good, for he did die of the disease from which he was suffering. 

In re Duffy, 127 A. D. 74, 111 N. Y. S. 77; 

In re Siefracke's Estate, 132 Misc. 407, 229 N. Y. S. 247; 

Beaver v. Beaver, 117 N. Y. 421, 428.

The gift takes effect on the donor's death and is revocable up to that time.

The burden is on the donee to establish, by a fair preponderance of credible testimony, that a gift was intended. Finders 

In re Buoninfante's Estate, 125 Misc. 907, 212 N. Y. S. 265.

Though generally a bailment depends upon a contractual relation, there are situations where the element of lawful possession, however created, plus the duty to account for an article as the property of another, creates a bailment. For that reason, finders are bailees. 

Armstrong v. Sisti, 242 N. Y. 440.

When one abandons an article which is found by another, the finder obtains title and the former owner cannot reclaim it. If an owner loses an article, the finder's rights are good against everyone but the owner. As against him, the finder is a bailee. 

If a person loses a package on a street car or train, and a passenger turns it over to the company, should the owner never call for it, it

BAILMENTS 

29 belongs to the car company, because when it was lost in the car the company became first finder. 

Foulke v. N. Y. Cons. R. R. Co., 228 N. Y. 269. 

To take another type of case: Suppose Jones finds Smith's horse on his farm and he feeds it until Smith calls for it. Smith must pay 

Jones for feeding the horse. But if Jones had found Smith's wagon on the road, with a broken wheel, and had fixed it, Smith need not pay Jones for his mending.

What is a Negotiable Instrument?

The most frequent forms of negotiable instruments come as (1) promissory notes, and (2) bills of exchange (drafts).

A promissory note is an unconditional promise to pay a definite sum of money at a definite time, to the order of some person or to bearer. A bill of exchange is a direction by one person to another person to pay to the order of a third person a definite sum of money at a definite time. $600.00

Example of Promissory Note 

New York, N. Y., August 27, 1930. 

Ninety days after date I promise to pay to John Smith.... the sum of Six Hundred and 00/100.. with interest at 6% per annum .or order

Dollars at Corn Exchange Bank of New York 

Value received.

RICHARD ROE. 

Example of Bill of Exchange 

$600.00 

New York, N. Y., August 27, 1930. ...Dollars 

At twenty days sight pay to John Smith or order the sum of Six Hundred and 00/100... and charge my account. 

To John Doe, 2 Lafayette St., N. Y. C.

RICHARD ROE. 

A trade acceptance is a draft or bill of exchange drawn by the seller on the purchaser of goods sold and accepted by such purchaser. 

Dubler v. Toscana, 142 Misc. 369; 254 N. Y. S. 464.

A study of these instruments reveals that there are two parties on the face of a promissory note, a maker (Richard Roe) and a 30

payee (John Smith). The parties on the face of the draft are drawer (Richard Roe), a payee and a drawee (John Doe). These characters together with the indorsers and holder are all that are necessary to be considered. Our study is the rights and liabilities of each of these.

To be negotiable the instrument must contain each of the following six characteristics: (1) The promise to pay or the order to pay must not be conditional. If the instrument calls for payment to be made if a certain ship arrive, or provided the maker is able to pay, or when A shall marry, it is not a negotiable instrument and the later happening of the event does not cure the defect. Each of these is a condition which might never occur, reckoning as of the date of the execution of the instrument. A statement that the sum is to be paid out of a special or particular fund would render the instrument non-negotiable. An order in a draft to pay "out of avails or sale of merchandise," or "out of a certain payment when made," or "out of my profits on the building job," creates conditions, for there is an order in terms conditional upon the existence of money in the bank. But if an order was to pay "out of account No. 1 first," it is not conditional but an order to pay and to charge the general account of the drawer if the sum stated is more than the designated fund consists of, and the instrument would be negotiable. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 22; 

Fulton v. Varney, 117 A. D. 572, 102 N. Y. S. 608. (2) What is to be paid must be money. It may be foreign coin or domestic coin so long as it is money. A promise in an instrument to pay "in a good wagon worth $500," or even in bonds, renders the instrument non-negotiable. (3) The money to be paid must be a definite sum. The fact that interest or attorney's fees are to be added to a principal sum does not make the payable sum indefinite. (4) The money must be payable either on demand (this is called a "sight instrument") or at a fixed or definite time, or after a certain occurrence. A promise to pay "ninety days after my reaching the age of 21," makes the instrument non-negotiable, for the time is neither fixed nor definite, nor certain. But an instrument "payable sixty days after my death" is negotiable, for death is certain to occur.

A demand or sight instrument is payable at once when issued, and parol evidence is inadmissible to show that a date was set for payment. (5) The instrument must be payable to the payee's order or to bearer.

(6) When the instrument is addressed to a drawee, he must be named or otherwise indicated with reasonable certainty. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 20, 23; 

Hegeman v. Moon, 131 N. Y. 462.

The negotiability of an instrument is not affected if (1) It fails to have date, seal, or to mention consideration or place where drawn or where payable; or if or (2) It authorizes the sale of any collateral; or if (3) It authorizes a confession of judgment if not paid on time; (4) If the holder is given the right of choosing between having something done instead of getting his money. But, of course, if it only contains a promise to do something other than pay money, or to do something and pay money, it is not negotiable. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 24, 25. 

Acceptance of Bills of Exchange 

Since a bill of exchange is merely an order on the drawee, and not an assignment, the drawee is not liable until he accepts, i.e., writes "Accepted" or a similar word, and date and signature, across the bill. When the drawee does accept he becomes primarily liable. 

The acceptance may be written on a separate paper, but in such a case the acceptor is bound only to those bona fide holders who acted on the faith of the acceptance. The acceptance can be executed before or after the bill is signed or completed, or even after the bill of exchange is due.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 222, 226. 

Acceptances are either general and qualified. The general acceptance assents without any qualification to the order of the drawer, but a qualified acceptance varies the effect of the bill as drawn. 

The qualifications may be (1) As to amount, i.e., a partial acceptance, as when the drawee accepts as to $50, whereas the draft was for $100. (2) As to time, e.g., the draft is payable in ten days, and it is accepted as payable in twenty days. (3) As to place. It should be noted that if the acceptor indicates a place of payment, as "payable at the Corn Exchange," it is not a qualified acceptance. But if it is restricted to that place only, as "payable only at the Corn Exchange,” it is qualified. (4) If not all the drawees accept but only some of them. (5) If the acceptor imposes a new condition, as "payable when drawer sends me more money."

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 227, 228, 229.

The effect of a qualified acceptance is to bind the acceptor only according to the tenor of his acceptance. But the holder need not receive the qualified acceptance. If he does, he is bound by it; but all prior parties, including the drawer, who do not agree, are discharged from further liability. They are deemed to have assented, unless they signify dissent within a reasonable time after notice of the qualified acceptance.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 230. 

No acceptance is valid or complete until notification or delivery of the acceptance. But the drawee has 24 hours in which to accept or refuse. If he finally does accept, the acceptance dates as of the presentation for acceptance. Should the drawee refuse to return. the bill after 24 hours, and the holder demand it, it is deemed accepted as of the time of presentation.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 224, 225. 

The holder may wait until maturity and then present his bill to the drawee for payment, without bothering to have it accepted. If a bill is due for payment October 10, 1925, it need not be presented until then. But it may be presented for acceptance at any time, and if acceptance is refused, the bill is considered dishonored at once, even though it may have a year to run. There need be no further presentation for payment any more.

However, there are three cases where the holder must present the bill for acceptance within a reasonable time or else negotiate it to some one else within a reasonable time, who in turn must so present it for acceptance or negotiate it. These cases are: (1) If the bill orders payment within a definite number of days after sight, e.g.: "Please pay 30 days after sight." In such a case the time of asking for the acceptance fixes "the sight" and that fixes the date of maturity. The holder must in such a case present for acceptance, otherwise there will not be a date of maturity. (2) Where the bill says that it must be presented for acceptance. (3) Where the bill is payable at a place other than the drawee's home or place of business.

If there are joint drawees (there cannot be two or more in the alternative), presentment must be made to all, unless one has authority to accept for the others. If a drawee is dead presentment should be made to his personal representative. If bankrupt, presentment for acceptance can be made to the bankrupt or to his trustee. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 242. 

Bearer Paper 

An instrument is payable to bearer— (1) When it is expressed to be "payable to bearer."

(2) "Payable to payee or bearer." But not if it said "to the order of the bearer, Ralph Jones," for then the word bearer is merely descriptive of a definite person. (3) Payable to a non-existing person and the maker of the promissory note or acceptor of the bill of exchange, i.e., those primarily liable, know that the person named is fictitious.
