(4) When the last indorsement is in blank. (5) When it is payable not to a specific person, as "to the estate of John Smith."

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 28. 

The Holder in Due Course 

A holder in due course is one who (1) in good faith, (2) pays value for an instrument, (3) without knowledge of any defenses which there may exist in favor of those apparently liable upon the instrument, (4) before maturity of the instrument. Value may even be an antecedent debt. 

Frank v. Von Bayer, 236 N. Y. 473. 

Every holder is deemed prima facie a holder in due course; but when it is shown that the title of any person who has negotiated the instrument was defective, the burden is on the holder to prove that he or some person under whom he claims acquired the title as a holder in due course.

Negotiable Instruments Law §§ 98, 91. 

Where there is fraud, the presumption is that he who is guilty will part with the note for the purpose of enabling some third party to recover upon it, and such presumption operates against the holder and it devolves upon him to show that he is a holder in due course. 

Even proof of value will not avail unless coupled with proof that the value was given without notice of the fraud. 

New York Bankers v. Duncan, 257 N. Y. 160;

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 98. 

If a person takes the note or check of a corporation in payment of a personal obligation of its officer, he is not a holder in due course, for this fact is sufficient to put him on inquiry. If, however, a reasonable inquiry on the part of the taker of the note would have resulted only in dispelling the idea of fraud on the part of the officer, the taker is a holder in due course. 

Bressman v. Mosson, 127 Misc. 282, 215 N. Y. S. 766.

A New York bank is liable for the amount of checks of a corporation, indorsed by the president thereof and deposited in such bank for his personal use unless such act is authorized or the corporation is negligent or has otherwise deprived itself of its right to recover against the bank. The bank is not liable to the corporation for mis-

appropration of that fund by the officer if it has on file a proper resolution of the corporation, authorizing that officer to endorse checks for the corporation. 

Weissman v. Banque de Bruxelles, 254 N. Y. 488;

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 95. 

The purchase of an instrument a short time before its maturity at a large discount requires the submission of the question of good faith to the jury. 

Becker v. Hart, 135 A. D. 785; 120 N. Y. S. 270.

When a note is marked, "This note is secured by a chattel mortgage," such verbiage is sufficient to put a holder on notice of a defense to the payment thereof. Where a note contains the verbiage, "This note is subject to the terms of a conditional sales agreement executed by the maker on this date," the promise of payment is not absolute, the note is not negotiable and the holder is put on notice. 

Old Colony Trust Co. v. Stumpel, 247 N. Y. 538; 

Securities Investment v. Maxwell, 131 Misc. 160, 226 N. Y. S. 273.

Real Defences Against a Holder in Due Course 

There are three defenses, called real defenses, which the maker, acceptor or drawer can use to defeat a recovery upon the instrument, even by a holder in due course: (1) Where he (the maker, acceptor or drawer) had no contractual capacity, as where he was an infant or adjudged lunatic at the time he bound himself under the instrument.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 41. 

(2) Where he did not consent, as for example where the defendant's signature was forged and the instrument was passed on to an innocent holder for value. The latter cannot then recover from the one whose signature was forged. But note that fraud in the inducement would not be a case of lack of consent.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 42. 

(3) When the instrument is void by statute. So negotiable instruments given for gambling debts, or delivered under a usurious rate of interest, are not good in the hands of a holder in due course. 

Suppose M makes a note to P's order for $1,000, for P's accommodation, and P takes it to B and obtains $700 for it. B cannot recover from M, even though he thought it was not an accommodation. It was a usurious negotiation. Where the note is not unsurious in its inception, but later becomes so, usury is no defense. The legal inception makes impossible the existence of usury. 

In one case M gave P a check in payment for goods. P, losing at a card game, indorsed that check to X, the winner. X collected as that check had a valid inception. 

Bernstein v. Fuerth, 132 Misc. 343, 229 N. Y. S. 791.

Where P loaned money to D and received notes bearing 6% interest therefor, and in addition an undertaking that D was to repay the sum advanced, plus the interest, in any event, plus an additional sum if D made a certain profit in the venture in which he was to use the money, the transaction and the notes were usurious. It does not matter that the additional rights are contingent. It is a right, and this right plus the legal rate of interest constitute a usurious interest. 

Webster v. Roe, 212 A. D. 756, affd. 241 N. Y. 570.

A bank which discounts notes for full value and without notice of any usury may recover upon such notes, although usurious in their inception.

Penal Law, §§ 991, 992; Banking Law, § 114; 

Schlesinger v. Lehmaier, 191 N. Y. 69; 

Weaver Hardware Co. v. Solomovitz, 235 N. Y. 321.

If the consideration alone is merely illegal (as payment by note for helping in a crime or illegal transaction), the note in the hands of a holder in due course is good. 

Howard Mfg. Co. v. Cohen, 207 A. D. 588, 202 N. Y. S. 449.

If the holder obtained the instrument through the indorsement of a foreign corporation, which could not collect on the instrument because of a failure to file a certificate of doing business with the Secretary of State, he cannot be defeated by such defense if he would otherwise be a holder in due course. 

Allison Hill Trust Co. v. Sarandrea, 136 Misc. 262.

Defenses Available Only Against Others Than Holders in Due 

Course Such defenses are called personal defenses and are unavailable against holders in due course. (1) Fraud in the inducement. Where the defendant was induced to sign a note for the price of worthless stock, fraudulently represented by the payee to be valuable, and the payee sold to a holder in due course, the defendant is liable to the latter but not to the payee. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 94. 

(2) Failure to deliver a complete instrument. If a complete negotiable instrument is stolen and passed without any forged indorsement to a holder in due course, the latter gets a good title. The title is so good that even if the holder in due course negotiated the instrument to one who knew of the theft, the recipient would have good title, for he was not an immediate party. 

But if an incomplete instrument is obtained otherwise than by delivery, e.g., by theft, and filled in without authority, there exists a defense even against a holder in due course.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 34.

The rule illustrated in the "thief" case applies if the maker gives the payee a note complete on its face to hold for a while and to return it later. This is not delivery. Should the payee pass it on to a holder in due course, the latter can collect, because failure to deliver a complete instrument is no defense against a holder in due course. 

It is that only between the immediate parties. (3) Want or failure of consideration. Every negotiable instrument is deemed prima facie to have been issued for valuable consideration. Lack of it must be proved by the defendant. 

Merchants Bank v. Home Deposit Co., 128 Misc. 446. 

Credit given on an antecedent debt is sufficient consideration. 

Placing the proceeds of a discounted note to the credit of an indorser at a bank is not such payment therefor as to render the bank a holder in due course. It is only when the indorser has drawn against that credit that the bank becomes such holder. 

Albany Bank v. People's Co-op. Ice Co., 92 A. D. 47; 

Citizens State Bank v. Cowles, 180 N. Y. 346.

Partial failure of consideration is a defense pro tanto. But where the contract is entire and it is impossible to determine the price of each article, partial failure of consideration is no defense pro tanto. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 54; 

Kelso v. Ellis, 224 N. Y. 528. (4) Payment is no defense against a holder in due course, but it is as between the immediate parties. If the holder is paid, he cannot collect again. But if when paid he is not required by the maker to give up the note and he passes it on to a holder in due course, the latter can collect. We are not considering the maker's rights against the person who fraudulently passed the note. (5) When the negotiable instrument is delivered to the payee with the understanding that it is not to be used unless a certain thing is first done by the payee, and that thing is not done, the payee's title is defective. If the instrument is transferred in violation of the agreement, the burden is upon the holder to show that he acquired title as a holder in due course. 

Silverman v. Loan Assn., 125 Misc. 843, 211 N. Y. S. 491.

To constitute notice of an infirmity in the instrument, or a defect in the title of the person negotiating, the person to whom the instrument is negotiated must have actual knowledge of the infirmity or defect or such knowledge that his taking the instrument amounts to bad faith. Mere negligence or mere evidence creating suspicion is not enough.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 85; 

Dutch v. McMahon, 219 A. D. 203, 219 N. Y. S. 321.

Where the transferee receives notice of any infirmity in the instrument or defect in the title of the person negotiating the same before

he has paid the full amount agreed to be paid therefor, he will be deemed a holder in due course only to the extent of the amount theretofore paid by him.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 93; 

Freeport Bank v. Viemeister, 227 A. D. 457, 238 N. Y. S. 169. 

Note this case: A holder in due course passes the instrument to

X, who knows that the note had been procured by fraud. Yet X, if he has not himself been a party to the fraud, can recover against the maker. He got his title from a holder in due course, and he therefore succeeds to the rights of a holder in due course. If X was a party to the fraud or had been the payee with knowledge of the fraud at the time of the transfer, he cannot recover as his previous knowledge of defects in title is revived. 

Gruntal v. Natl. Surety, 254 N. Y. 468.

Under the same reasoning, a payee of a note unenforceable because of lack of consideration does not acquire the rights of a holder in due course by a repurchase of the note from a holder in due course before maturity. Blanks 

Crawford v. Debevoise, 241 N. Y. S. 65. 

Whoever signs and puts in circulation a blank negotiable instrument impliedly confers authority upon a subsequent holder to fill in the blanks at will, and may not urge as against innocent third persons, that the words thereafter inserted were contrary to the authorization actually given. 

Hulburt v. Walker, 258 N. Y. 8.

If X had seen the instrument before it was complete, and then saw P fill in the blank, X would not be a holder in due course. 

Although a holder of an incomplete instrument has prima facie authority to fill in blank spaces, it does not aid X, who saw the blanks. 

He takes the note subject to all the equities. If, however, X had then passed the complete instrument to Y for value, Y would be a holder in due course, if acting in good faith.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 33. 

Note that there was delivery of an incomplete instrument. But if there has been no delivery of an incomplete instrument, but X had obtained the signature by stealing it, it could not have been valid in anyone's hands.

If a negotiable instrument, such as a check, complete on its face but exhibiting blank spaces wherein additional figures or names may readily be inserted, is signed in that condition by a drawer, and the instrument is altered by the introduction of other figures or words, the drawer may be liable to an innocent drawee who has paid the check, because his negligence has facilitated the forgery. 

Hulburt v. Walker, 258 N. Y. 8; 

Natl. Exchange Bank v. Lester, 194 N. Y. 461.

Negotiation and Indorsements

If a bill or note is payable to bearer, it is negotiated by delivery. 

If it is payable to order, it is negotiated by the indorsement of the holder plus delivery.

The indorsement must be written on the instrument or on a paper attached to it, and must order the payment of the whole sum to the transferee. An indorsement of a part to another is not a valid negotiation. But "to pay X and Y jointly" is not a partial indorsement and there can be a valid negotiation or delivery. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 60, 61, 62. 

There are five kinds of indorsements: (1) Blank.—The payee indorses in blank by merely writing his name, usually on the back of the order paper. The order instrument is now bearer paper, and the indorsee or holder can negotiate by mere delivery. An instrument is considered payable to bearer if the last indorsement is one in blank.

Blank Indorsement

JOHN SMITH 

(2) Special or Full Indorsement.-Where the holder does not want the paper to be bearer paper, but prefers order paper, because of its greater safety in handling, he can write above the blank indorsement, "Pay to Robert Jones or order." This keeps it order paper.

So an indorsement "to the order" of someone is the second kind-a special or full indorsement. 

Another form of this special or full indorsement may be "Pay to Robert Jones." In such a case there cannot even be a further transfer of the instrument without the indorsement of Robert Jones. much less negotiation. 

Special Pay to ROBERT JONES JOHN SMITH 

Pay to ROBERT Jones or order 

JOHN SMITH 

or Full Indorsements

If an instrument on its face is payable to bearer, and is then indorsed specially, it remains a bearer instrument. But the liability of the special indorser exists only in favor of such later holders as are the indorsees of the one to whom he indorsed specially. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 70. 

(3) Qualified.-The holder writes on the back, "Pay to Robert

Jones or order without recourse", and signs his name. The paper remains negotiable, but the holder who so indorses incurs no liability as an indorser.

An indorsement "I hereby assign all my right and interest in this note to Richard Fay in full" was held a qualified indorsement. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 68; 

Fay v. Witte, 260 N. Y. S. 683. Qualified Indorsement

Pay to ROBERT JONES 

or order without recourse

JOHN SMITH 

It seems plain that either a blank or special indorsement may be made qualified. (4) Conditional.-Such an indorsement transfers title to the indorsee subject to the fulfillment of a stated condition. The paper remains negotiable. If the contingency does not occur, the indorsee is entitled to no money. Conditional Indorsement 

Pay to the order of

ROBERT JONES 

upon completion of the Main St. Library JOHN SMITH 

If the condition had been on the face of the instrument it would have made it non-negotiable.

The maker may disregard the condition and pay to the indorsee or his transferee, whether the condition has been fulfilled or not.

But if the indorsee or transferee is paid, he holds the proceeds subject to the rights of the conditional indorser. (5) Restrictive.-"Pay to Robert Jones only," "Pay to Robert 

Jones for my use," "Pay to Robert Jones for Arthur Rose's use," "Pay to Robert Jones for collection," "John Smith (holder), for deposit." This kind of an indorsement renders the paper nonnegotiable, and the indorsee usually has title in trust for the use of someone else.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 66. 

The indorsee of an instrument for collection may sue the maker thereon in his own name, and this even though the indorsement is restrictive. 

Carson v. Federal Reserve, 254 N. Y. 218, 237; 

Marine Trust Co. v. Lauria, 213 A. D. 64; affd. 244 N. Y. 577;

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 67. 

Restrictive Indorsement 

Pay to

ROBERT JONES 

for Arthur Rose's use

JOHN SMITH 

An indorsement by the holder "for deposit" or "pay any bank or banker", or equivalent, is a restrictive indorsement and indicates that the indorsee bank is only an agent for collection. The indorser, however, does guarantee to subagent holders the genuineness of prior indorsements and authority of prior indorsers to indorse. 

Very often holders indorse instruments in blank when depositing them for collection. The bank may change the indorsement to a restrictive indorsement by writing over the signature of the indorser such words as "for deposit" or "for collection", to negative the presumption that the bank of deposit is the owner. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 350c. 

Suppose a holder, H, strikes out an indorsement of an indorser. 

The one whose name is stricken out and all those who indorsed after that name up to H are released.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 78. 

Suppose the holder, Smith, transfers an order note to Robert

Jones without an indorsement. Jones thus gets the note subject to all the equities. Then Jones learns that Smith had obtained the

note by fraud. This is a defense as between the immediate parties, but not against a holder in due course. So Jones demands Smith's indorsement. Smith indorses. Jones does not become a holder in due course, because the indorsement takes effect as of the date it was actually made, and when the indorsement was made Jones knew of the fraud and so was not a taker in good faith. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 79. 

Negotiation is deemed prima facie to have been effected before maturity and at the place where the instrument is dated. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 75, 76. 

Liability of Maker and Acceptor 

The maker and acceptor are both primarily liable. At maturity the holder need not make any formal demand. The maker or acceptor must be ready to pay at the place of payment. If payment is not made at maturity, suit may be started at once. 

A failure to present the note does not bar the holder's right to recover against the maker, though the note be past due. If the maker was able and willing to pay at the place of payment at maturity, and had the funds there available for that purpose, the law considers that a tender has been made. This tender is to stop the running of interest; it is not payment. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 130; 

Shapiro v. Weber, 220 A. D. 667, 222 N. Y. S. 421.

The reception of interest in advance upon a note is prima facie evidence of a binding contract to forbear and delay the time of payment, and no suit can be maintained against the maker during the period for which the interest has been paid, unless the right to sue is reserved by the agreement of the parties. The payment of interest in advance is not of itself a contract to delay, but is evidence of such contract and while this evidence may be rebutted, yet in the absence of any rebutting evidence, it becomes conclusive. 

Reardon v. Wood, 140 Misc. 889; 252 N. Y. S. 5.

The holder of a note though holding collateral to secure the note, may sue the maker on default without first tendering the collateral. 

The maker is entitled to the collateral upon payment of the amount due. The proper procedure for the maker is, when sued, to set up a counterclaim, and in it demand the return of the collateral upon payment of the amount due. 

Recourse Holding Co. v. Nitke, 136 Misc. 139; 239 N. Y. S. 26; 

Natl. Bank of Rochester v. Erion-Haines R. Co., 213 A. D. 54.

The acceptor warrants the genuineness of the drawer's signature and his authority to draw, and that the drawer has sufficient funds with him. He cannot assert the forgery of the drawer's signature as 1

a defense. If he accepted the forged instrument he is liable to a holder in due course. But any alteration of the amount is a defense for the acceptor does not warrant the genuineness of the amount. 

Nor does he warrant the genuineness of a subsequent indorsement. 

He also engages that he will pay the instrument according to its terms. He also admits the payee's capacity to indorse. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 110, 112. 

Though the indorser is an infant, he can pass good title and one who holds through an infant may recover from the maker though the infant is not liable.

A corporate maker cannot set up a defense of usury. If an individual is a co-maker with a corporation, the individual may set up a defense of usury. If, however, this individual co-maker was an accommodation party and not a victim of the usury, he cannot set up the defense of usury. 

Rockmore v. Epstein, 127 Misc. 526, 217 N. Y. S. 76. 

A note which reads: "We promise to pay, etc.," is presumed to be a joint obligation only, which is rebuttable. On the death of one of two joint makers, the survivor remains liable in law. Equity might permit recourse to be had against the estate of the deceased maker, if the survivor were insolvent. 

First Natl. Bk. v. Knickerbocker, 126 Misc. 467; 214 N. Y. S. 465.

If an agent signs a note as an agent of a disclosed principal, he is liable on the note itself, if he actually had no authority to so sign as agent. 

New Georgia Bank v. Lippman, 249 N. Y. 307.

Liability of Drawer and Indorser-Notice of Dishonor 

The drawer and indorsers are secondarily liable. Indorsers are liable prima facie in the order in which they indorsed, but evidence is admissible to show that as among themselves they agreed otherwise. 

Such agreement is a distinct contract and proof thereof does not violate the parol evidence rule. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §118; 

Faigelman's Estate, in re, 142 Misc. 167; 254 N. Y. S. 161.

One who negotiates by delivery or by a qualified indorsement warrants the genuineness of the instrument, his title thereto, capacity of prior parties to contract and that he has no knowledge of any facts which would impair the validity of the instrument. Where the negotiation is by delivery only, the warranty extends in favor of no holder other than the immediate transferee.

It thus follows that if A negotiates a usurious instrument to B by delivery or by a qualified indorsement, he would not be liable if he did not know of the defect. His warranty extended only to his knowledge.

One who indorses generally warrants the genuineness of the instrument, his title, capacity of prior parties to contract, that the instrument is valid at the time of his indorsement and that on due presentment it shall be paid or accepted as the case may be, and if dishonored and the necessary proceedings on dishonor duly taken, that he will pay.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 115, 116. 

Note that there must be presentment by the holder or his agent in order to charge those secondarily liable, even though the indorsement be made after maturity. If the note is a demand note, it must be presented within a reasonable time. Whether or not there was a reasonable presentment of the demand note depends upon the particular facts. In some cases a few months may not be a reasonable time-in other cases five years may be a reasonable time, e.g., the holder delayed presenting the note in accordance with the request of all the parties liable thereon. 

Kllemann v. Collins, 223 A. D. 161, 227 N. Y. S. 589; 

Moore v. Alexander, 63 A. D. 100, 71 N. Y. S. 420.

To constitute due presentment to the maker or acceptor so as to hold the indorsers, the instrument must be ready to be delivered. 

A demand over the telephone is not a sufficient presentment. 

Gilpin v. Savage, 201 N. Y. 167.

If no place of payment is specified in the instrument, the presentment must be made at the maker's residence or place of business, no matter how inconvenient it may be to do so. If payment is to be made at a bank, presentment must be made during banking hours. 

The maker has till closing time to pay before there can be dishonor. 

If payment is to be at a business place, then presentment must be made during business hours.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 132, 133, 135. 

After the instrument is dishonored, notice must be given to those secondarily liable. If it is a foreign bill of exchange (a bill which is either drawn or payable in another state) it must be protested formally. The protest is the making of a public record, usually by a notary, of the facts showing that the instrument has been dishonored. 

Notes never need a formal protest. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 160, 189. 

Suppose X is the holder; A, B, C and D are indorsers. Upon dishonor X gives notice of it only to D, the last indorser. This makes 

D responsible. If D wants to protect himself, he should notify the prior indorsers. If he serves notice only on two, B and C, they are each responsible to those indorsers that came after them. But they can get nothing from A, as he was not notified. The notice to A

may be given by anyone in the name of the one entitled to give a notice.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 161, 162 

The notice of dishonor, if the parties live in the same city, must be given before the close of business on the day following dishonor, or if it is given at a residence, before the usual hours of rest, the next day following dishonor. If sent by mail, it must be deposited so as to arrive during these hours the next day. But should the instrument be in the hands of the holder's agent, the holder is given an additional day in which to give notice of dishonor. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 174, 165. 

If the parties live in different places, then it must be deposited in the post office in time to go by mail the following day, or if there be none, then the next mail after that. The sender is not responsible for miscarriage in the mails.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 175, 176. 

An oral notice of dishonor is sufficient. All that is necessary is that it sufficiently describe the instrument and state that it has been dishonored.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 167. 

If an address has been added to the signature, the notice of dishonor is to be sent to that address. If not, then it is to be sent to the post office nearest his place of residence. The notice in the latter case may be sent to his place of business.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 179. 

Where a party receives notice of dishonor he has the same time for giving notice to prior parties, after he gets his notice, as the holder had after dishonor.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 178. 

If those primarily liable were partners when they accepted or made the note and no place of payment was specified, presentment may be made to any one of them, even though they are no longer partners. The same is true of notice to partners who are indorsers. 

If they are joint debtors and not partners, then presentment must be made to all. The same applies to notice of dishonor. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 137, 170; 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 138, 171. 

If the drawers or indorsers know that there will not be any acceptance or payment, presentment need not be made to charge them. 

This would occur when (1) the drawee is a fictitious person or (2) when presentment was waived by the indorser. But if the maker is insolvent, it is not an excuse for failing to present the instrument. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 129, 142.

Notice of dishonor to the drawer may be dispensed with when (1) the drawer and drawee are the same person; or (2) the drawee had no contractual capacity; or (3) the drawer has a right to expect dishonor, as when the drawer knows that he has no money with the drawee, unless the circumstances are such as to lead one to reasonably expect that there will be funds to meet the bill of exchange when it is presented. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 184; 

Masukiewicz v. Hanover Nat'l Bank, 240 N. Y. 317.

Notice of dishonor to the indorsers may be dispensed with when (1) the indorser was an accommodated party; or (2) the indorser was the one to whom the instrument was to be presented for payment; or (3) the drawee was a fictitious person or (4) the drawee had no contractual capacity, and this was known to the indorser when he indorsed; or (5) such notice cannot be given after the exercise of reasonable diligence.

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 186, 183. 

Notice of dishonor may be waived, either before or after dishonor, by making payment or by promising to pay, despite a failure to receive due notice. Consideration is not needed to support a waiver. 

Commonwealth Fuel Co. v. Powpit Co., 212 A. D. 553; 

Kraut v. Nordlinger, 215 N. Y. S. 496, affd. 243 N. Y. 649.

Successive accommodation indorsers are not co-sureties so as to be entitled to contribution among themselves, unless they agree to be bound jointly and not severally. 

Suppose there has been a failure to give notice in proper time. 

The indorser promises to pay just the same. A clear, unequivocal promise waives the indorser's right to refuse to pay because of failure to receive notice. A request by the indorser that the holder extend time of payment is a waiver.

Even though the indorser may be able to successfully resist any action on the note because of failure to give him notice, or because the action is barred by the Statute of Limitations, property pledged by the indorser to insure payment is not therefore released. 

Reynolds v. Doyle, 125 Misc. 778, 211 N. Y. S. 509. 

Discharge of a Negotiable Instrument 

There are three ways of discharging a negotiable instrument: (1) A negotiable instrument is discharged if it is paid off to the real holder by the one primarily liable, or cancelled by the holder,

or if the principal debtor becomes its owner, at or after maturity. 

If the principal debtor pays for and receives it before maturity and resells it to a holder in due course, the latter can recover against the maker, acceptor, drawer and indorsers. Until maturity the bill is alive.

Negotiable_Instruments Law, § 200; 

Persky v. Bank of America, 235 A. D. 146; 256 N. Y. S. 572.

A payment to the holder of a promissory note by an indorser, not as agent for the maker, but simply in discharge of his own obligation as an indorser, where the note was executed by the maker for value does not inure to the benefit of the latter. In an action upon the note, the maker is liable for the whole amount. To the extent of the money paid, the indorser becomes equitably entitled to be substituted to the rights and remedies of the holder, and becomes, pro tanto, the beneficial owner of the debt; so that the maker's obligation to pay the note in full, as first due to the holder solely in his own right, becomes after the first payment by the indorser still wholly due to the holder, but partly in his own right and partly as trustee for the indorser. 

Seventh Natl. Bank v. Cromwell, 131 Misc. 276, 226 N. Y. S. 721. (2) Alterations.-When there is an alteration the instrument and all the parties to it at the time of the alteration are discharged. It differs in nature from a cancellation only in that a cancellation must have been effected intentionally by the holder. To get the effect of an alteration so as to discharge the instrument, the alteration may have been made even by a stranger, and that even against the holder's will. But should the instrument be sold to a holder in due course, who knows nothing of the alteration, he is protected only as to the terms of the instrument before the alteration. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, §§ 204, 205. 

The alteration, to be effective, must be a material one. This includes a change in the date; sum payable either for principal or interest; time of payment; place of payment; number or relation of parties; or the currency in which the instrument is payable. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 206. 

At common law a material alteration of a negotiable instrument avoided the instrument even in the hands of a holder in due course. (3) An absolute renunciation by the holder of his rights, made after maturity, discharges the instrument and all the parties thereto. 

The holder in due course is not affected.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 203. 

When the instrument is discharged all parties, primary and secondary, are no longer liable. But it is possible that those secondarily

liable may be discharged, even though the instrument itself may not be. This we have already seen in the case where the holder scratched out the signature of an indorser. This discharged the one whose name was scratched out and those who indorsed after that one. In fact, a discharge of a prior party, in any manner, or a tender by him, discharges the subsequent indorsers. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 201. 

If B, C, D and E are indorsers and E has indorsed it back to B, B cannot hold C, D, or E, the subsequent indorsers, if there is default and due notice. Having been a prior indorser, he is liable to them. 

To allow him to sue on his later indorsement would only create a circuity of actions. 

But suppose the holder releases the principal debtor. Or suppose the holder binds himself in a binding contract to extend the time of payment. This releases all those secondarily liable, unless the holder has expressly reserved his rights against them.

Now, suppose there are five indorsers, A, B, C, D and E. The first, A, is liable to the other four. The second only to the last three, and so on down the line. It is clear that since payment by an indorser does not discharge the instrument, but only allows him to get reimbursement, a payment by an indorser in no way helps those who indorsed before him. So if D paid, A, B and C are not helped. They are liable. And if the holder sues B, B must pay the holder, even though the holder has been paid by D. The holder must, however, hold the proceeds he receives from B in trust for D, for D could have collected from B. B, of course, can collect from A, and so on up the line.

Checks 

A check is in reality only a negotiable bill of exchange which is drawn on a bank and payable on demand; its purpose is usually to pay money. The purpose of a bill is to collect money. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 321. 

A check is not a transfer or delivery of that fund until presented at the bank and paid, because until payment the fund still remains subject to the control and order of the testator. 

In re Gibbons Estate, 139 Misc. 658; 249 N. Y. S. 753.

Though checks cannot be accepted, something similar is effected by certification By a certification the drawee bank represents that the signature of the drawer is genuine; that it has funds of the drawer in its possession sufficient to meet the check, and it engages not to permit a withdrawal from the funds to the prejudice of any bona fide holder of the check. If it does not certify, it is in no way responsible to the holder for such refusal. By certification the bank

does not guarantee the body of the check. The certification must be in writing and signed by the drawee. 

On certification the drawer and those indorsers prior to the certification are discharged. But note that if the drawer himself had his bank certify the check, he remains liable as well as the bank. 

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 324. 

The refusal on the part of the drawee bank to certify the check at the request of the holder is not tantamount to a dishonor, which would render presentment for payment unnecessary. The bank owes no duty to certify any checks; and it is no part of the contract between the maker of the check and the payee. A bank is obligated to honor and pay checks when properly presented if the drawer has sufficient funds in the bank's possession. When a holder of a check requests certification instead of payment, he enters into a new contract with the bank, and one which was not within the contemplation of the drawer upon such certification. 

Wachtel v. Rosen, 249 N. Y. 386.

If a check was in fact certified by mistake and if plaintiff's position was not in fact changed on the faith of the certification, the bank has an equitable defense. 

Metropolitan Life Ins. Co. v. Bank of U. S., 259 N. Y. 365.

Nor does the negligence of the bank, in certifying a check at a time when the depositor did not have on deposit a sufficient sum of money for payment, prevent the bank from recovering from the maker any overdraft, for as between the bank and depositor an overdraft is a loan for which the depositor is liable to the bank. 

T. G. & T. Co. v. Emadee, 240 N. Y. S. 36.

Where a holder in due course presents a certified check to the bank on which the depositor's signature has been forged, and the bank pays, the bank cannot get back its money from the holder in due course, and it must make good to the depositor.

A drawer of a check is different from a drawer of a bill of exchange. The former is regarded as the principal debtor. 

A drawer undertakes that there shall be paid by the drawee, to the holder of the check, the sum of money mentioned, at the place named, when the check is presented. Ordinarily where a check is delivered at the same place where the bank is located, it must, in the absence of special circumstances, be presented during the business hours of the next regular business day after it is received. If the holder fails to present it within that time, the loss resulting from neglect to so present the check falls upon the holder. Thus if while the holder holds the check the drawee becomes insolvent, or the money deposited with the drawee to meet the check depreciates, the drawer is discharged. One who becomes a holder of a check, which

has been negotiated for an unreasonable length of time after its issue, is not deemed a holder is due course.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 322; 

Weinberg v. Goldstein, 226 A. D. 479, 235 N. Y. S. 529.

It has been held that a deposit by the payee in his own bank for collection on the day following receipt of the instrument and presentation by the collecting bank on the day thereafter, is a sufficiently prompt demand. 

Jaysee Holding v. Block, 245 N. Y. S. 35.

A drawer is excused if he has closed his account with the drawee long after the issuance of the check but prior to presentment for payment.

What is a reasonable time depends on usage. The next day is usually the customary time for presentation. It is worthy of note that, in cases of bills, further negotiation extends the time of presentment. But such is not the case in the case of checks. 

In the absence of some agreement between the drawee and the payee, by which the payee may be induced to delay presentation, an interval of nine months is an unreasonable delay as a matter of law. 

Ferrari v. First Natl. Bank, 246 N. Y. 382.

Suppose a depositor dies. The bank does not know of the death, and it pays out on the check. The bank is not liable. But if it knew of the death, it is liable if the obligation represented by the check was not owing. 

Suppose a check is raised, or the maker's signature forged, and the bank, not noticing it, pays out the amount according to the tenor of the check. The depositor does not notice the fact that too much of his money has been paid out, until more than one year has passed, and then tells the bank. The bank is not liable; its liability lasted to the end of the year after the return of the vouchers, and when a year went by and the depositor failed to inform the bank, its liability ceased. This one-year rule applies only to cases where the maker's signature has been forged or where the check has been raised. It does not apply to a case where the indorser's signature has been forged. 

Kleinman v. Chase Natl. Bank, 124 Misc. 173, 207 N. Y. S. 191;

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 326. 

But it should be noted that, even if a year had not gone by, and the same kind of a forgery had been successful several times, and nothing was said because the depositor had been negligent in not learning of the forgery, the depositor could get back from the bank only the loss caused by the first forgery.

It has been held that a depositor, upon the return of his vouchers from the bank, is bound to do at least three things in order not to be considered negligent:

(1) He must compare the vouchers returned by the bank with the check stubs of his book. (2) He must compare the balance in the bank's statement with the balance in his stub book. (3) He must compare the returned vouchers with the list of checks entered in the bank's statement. 

Stumpp v. Bank of New York, 212 A. D. 608, 209 N. Y. S. 396.

This duty calls for no more than the exercise of ordinary care. 

The depositor may intrust the examination of the account and vouchers to a competent employee and thus discharge his duty to the bank. 

Takenka v. Bankers Trust Co., 132 Misc. 322, 229 N. Y. S. 459.

The depositor is under no duty, upon the return of the vouchers, to examine the indorsements in order to discover whether the signatures indorsed were genuine. 

Natl. Surety Co. v. Bank of Manhattan, 252 N. Y. 247.

If the negligence of the depositor enables one to easily alter the check and so causes the bank to pay on a forged order, the depositor is estopped from disputing the bank's authority to pay. If the bank and the depositor were both negligent, the bank must make good to the depositor any payment on the forged order. 

Gutfreund v. East River Natl. Bank, 251 N. Y. 58.

Where the maker gave a check to the payee's agent, who forged the payee's indorsement, collected from the bank and absconded with the proceeds, the bank must make good to the maker. A bank must, at its own peril, determine whether the indorsement of the payee is genuine. The maker's rights are unaffected by the fact that the check was certified. 

Greenwalk v. State Bank, 125 Misc. 260, 210 N. Y. S. 378;

Markovich v. Amer. Exch.-Irving Trust Co., 132 Misc. 128. 

A wilful dishonor of a depositor's check by his bank makes the bank liable for the consequences. If the dishonor is a result of an innocent mistake, the bank is liable only for nominal damages. The payee of the check, however, cannot maintain an action against the bank, even though the maker has on deposit sufficient funds to pay it, because the payee is merely a stranger to the bank. 

Katz v. Pacific Bank, 212 A. D. 601, 209 N. Y. S. 497; 

Gruber v. Bank of America, 127 Misc. 132, 215 N. Y. S. 222.

If a payee violates an agreement not to use a check until a certain date, the maker may rescind the transaction and stop payment on the check. 

Stadtmauer v. Lukin, 126 Misc. 318, 213 N. Y. S. 33.

When Negotiable Documents Are Considered Payment 

If a debtor gives his creditor a check, the debt is not extinguished until the drawee pays, unless there is a special agreement or cir-

cumstances which imply that the giving of the check was complete payment. Even though the creditor give a paid receipt for the debt when he receives the check, this in itself does not evidence an intent on the part of the creditor to take the check in absolute payment. 

And this is true even though the check be certified. Should the drawee bank refuse to pay, the creditor may elect to bring an action on the check or on the original debt. Where the check is transferred or indorsed over to a third party, there is an election by the creditor to release the debtor on the original lebt, and his liability exists only upon the check. Should the original payee creditor bring an action upon the debt while the check is in the hands of a third party, the defense of payment would be good. 

Bernstein v. Fuerth, 132 Misc. 343, 229 N. Y. S. 791.

If an uncertified check is given to the creditor, and the latter presents it to the bank for certification, the debt is discharged upon certification. A certification by the maker before delivery to the creditor is only an assurance that the check is genuine. A certification by the payee, however, substitutes the drawee for the debtor and leaves the funds in the bank for the accommodation of the payee creditor. 

Krikorian v. Fermanian, 119 Misc. 535, 196 N. Y. S. 629.

Where the debtor gives the creditor an uncertified check, and he has funds in the bank at the time the check is given, if the creditor is guilty of laches in the presentation of the check, and in the meantime the drawee bank becomes insolvent, the debt is extinguished. 

Williams v. Brown, 53 A. D. 486, 65 N. Y. S. 1049.

Similar rules apply to notes. Unless a note is paid at maturity there is no payment of the debt represented by the note, in the absence of a special agreement to the contrary. Where, however, the note of a third party is given by the debtor to the creditor, the presumption is that it was taken in absolute payment of the debt, provided the debt was contracted contemporaneously with the giving of the notes. If the note of a third party is given for a precedent debt, there is no such presumption, and the note must be paid before the debt is cancelled. 

Dibble v. Richardson, 171 N. Y. 131; 

Bank of Taiwan v. Schild, 258 N. Y. S. 331.

CARRIERS What Is a Common Carrier 

A common carrier is one who indicates to the public that he will, for a specified compensation, transport property from one place to another, for all persons that may see fit to employ him, up to the capacity of his facilities. 

Utica-Clayville v. Waite, 233 A. D. 297; 252 N. Y. S. 673.

Some examples of common carriers are: express companies, omnibuses, hackmen, railroad companies, ferries; porters, truckmen ; wagoners; cartmen; parties who undertake to carry goods for hire as a common employment. 

Jackson v. Hurlburt, 158 N. Y. 34, 37.

Common carriers never hold themselves out as such for other common carriers.

Railroad carriers are under a duty to furnish cars promptly on demand to transport goods when this can be done with reasonable diligence and without unfair discrimination. 

Walrath v. Amer. Ry. Exp. Co., 217 A. D. 83, 216 N. Y. S. 545.

A carrier may not unreasonably discriminate between its shippers. 

Root v. L. I. R. R. Co., 114 N. Y. 300.

Common carriers may refuse to carry goods if they are not of the kind carried by this carrier; or they are not tendered at the proper time and place as fixed by the carrier; or they are not safely packed; or the facilities are insufficient because of some unusual circumstance which exhausts the carrier's facilities. 

Liability of a Carrier in the Transportation of Goods 

Common carriers are insurers of goods upon their receipt for immediate transportation, so that if there is an accidental loss the carrier is liable. This rule does not apply where live animals and birds are shipped. The common carrier is not liable for injuries caused to such, arising from their nature and propensities, and which could not be prevented by foresight and care. 

Clay v. N. Y. C. R. Co., 224 A. D. 508, 231 N. Y. S. 424.

It is the obligation of the carrier to use reasonable effort and due diligence to carry and deliver the goods within a reasonable time from the date of receipt, and it is liable for failure to do so, except for certain specified causes. The breach occurs upon the expiration of a reasonable time from the delivery to the carrier. 

Geismer v. L. S. & M. R. Co., 102 N. Y. 563; 

Aristo Hosiery Co. v. Atlantic Coast Line Co., 129 Misc. 305. 53

The liability of the carrier for not delivering within a reasonable time is limited to such damage as may reasonably be presumed to have entered into the contemplation of the parties, or which has resulted from the delay after the carrier was informed of a special circumstance, e.g., the merchandise was to be used for the presentation of a particular theatrical performance on a stated day. 

Chapman v. Fargo, 223 N. Y. 32, 36; 

Rives v. Amer. Ry. Exp., 227 A. D. 375, 237 N. Y. S. 429.

The certain specified causes which will exempt a carrier from liability for delay or for any damage to the goods are these: (1) An act of God.-The damage in such a case must have been caused without the intervention of any human agency. If a district is overswept by a flood because some person broke a dam, it is not considered as caused by an act of God. But lightning causing a fire, or a snowstorm preventing the moving of trains, or the freezing of a river are acts of God. Note that if because of a snowstorm which prevents everyone from going about their business lights are not lit by the city employees, and because of this there is an accident, it would not be an act of God. There was human intervention, even though not the fault of the carrier's agents; and even though an act of God caused human intervention, the carrier is liable. If there is a special contract to deliver at a certain time, inevitable accident or an act of God is not a defense. (2) Acts of a nation (or its subjects) at war with this country. (3) Inherent nature of the goods. (4) Public authority, e.g., the goods are removed by valid legal process, and the bill of lading surrendered or impounded by the court. 

Harmony v. Bingham, 12 N. Y. 99; 

Michaels v. N. Ỹ. Cent. R. Co., 30 N. Y. 564.

Contracts which purport to totally exempt carriers from liability for negligence are void as against public policy. A carrier may limit its liability for damages caused even by its own negligence, if the parties agree that recovery is to be limited to an agreed valuation which forms the basis of the charges fixed by the carrier, or where the limitation is the consideration for other benefits to the shipper. 

F. A. Strauss v. Canadian Pac. Ry., 254 N. Y. 407; 

Kilthau v. International M. Marine Co., 245 N. Y. 361.

When a common carrier furnishes a bill of lading, this bill is a receipt, and prima facie is a contract to carry safely and deliver. 

Will v. Canadian Nat. Ry. Co., 216 A. D. 239, 214 N. Y. S. 513.

When a shipper nonfraudulently misstates the contents of a package which he ships by freight, and because of this misstatement a

CARRIERS 

55 lower rate is charged, it does not excuse the carrier for loss of the goods in transit.

In order that the company should be limited in its liability, the instrument on which its liability is mentioned must be of such a nature as to represent a contract. So an oral agreement, which is contradicted by a bill of lading given later by the company without the shipper's assent, would be binding despite the bill of lading. 

But an express receipt, or a bill of lading given without any previous agreement, is binding. So, also, if T checks his valise and receives a check on which is printed a limitation of liability, it is not binding. 

It is not a contract, but an identification ticket, unless T is put on notice. 

Bostwick v. B. & O. Ry. Co., 45 N. Y. 712.

When a person signs a statement for a carrier declaring the value of the goods shipped on his ticket, and which valuation determines the rate, the shipper is bound by the terms of the statement, even though he never read the statement when he signed it. 

Minturn v. N. Y. Cent. R. R. Co., 220 A. D. 222, 220 N. Y. S. 789.

The general rule is clear that any delivery of property by a common carrier thereof to a person unauthorized by the owner of the person to whom the carrier is bound by contract to deliver it constitutes a conversion, irrespective of the question of negligence or the exercise of due care. 

M. & T. Trust Co. v. Export Corp., 143 Misc. 1; 256 N. Y. S. 590.

A shipment of goods sent C. O. D. contemplates that the carrier will act as bailee and as agent to collect the amount specified, in cash. 

A carrier accepts a check at its peril. Should the check be dishonored, the carrier must pay the loss caused the shipper. 

Mogul v. Lavine, 247 N. Y. 20.

If a shipper orders the carrier not to deliver to the consignee, and gives the carrier ample notice, the carrier is negligent in delivering the goods to the consignee, and the shipper can recover. 

Bond v. Amer. Ry. Exp. Co., 124 Misc. 619, 208 N. Y. S. 643.

If a railroad is forced to deliver up a shipper's goods to a sheriff, it must notify the owner at once if it desires to be held free from liability. 

Where a freight agent who issued bills of lading against goods given to the railroad conspires with his dishonest friend to get up a bill and drafts showing that $10,000 worth of cars have been delivered to the railroad company, and these drafts are attached and delivered to a bona fide purchaser for value without notice, the railroad company is liable to the bona fide purchaser. This is the New 

York rule and the Federal rule. 

Josephy v. Panhandle & S. F. Ry., 235 N. Y. 306.

Connecting Carriers 

A carrier's liability as such ends with the safe delivery of the goods to the connecting carrier. If, however, the first carrier has expressly contracted to deliver goods to a certain destination, the first carrier remains liable for loss until the safe arrival of the goods at that destination. The fact that the bill of lading states that the goods will be delivered to a point beyond the first carrier's line, does not by itself mean that a contract exists for that transportation by the first carrier to that point, and that this first carrier will be liable even after it delivers the goods safely to a connecting carrier. For such a contract to exist, the language must be clear and definite. 

Wishnatzki v. Gt. Northern Ry., 225 A. D. 62, 232 N. Y. S. 165.

If the connecting carrier refuses to take the goods, the first carrier becomes a bailee from the time of the tender. If there is no tender, the first carrier remains liable as a carrier. 

If the shipment is an interstate shipment, the initial carrier is liable to the shipper for any loss caused by any of the connecting carriers. 

The initial carrier cannot contract so as to exempt itself from such liability. 

The Carrier as Bailee of Goods 

The goods arrive at their destination. The consignee is notified. 

After a reasonable time (a question of fact) the company becomes a bailee and is subject to the rules of bailment. The time of notification counts from the time of mailing the notice to the consignee. 

Should the consignee be unknown and so not notified, the company after such reasonable time is a bailee. 

Porter v. Penn. R. R. Co., 217 A. D. 49, 215 N. Y. S. 727; 

Rotundo v. Erie R. R. Co., 198 N. Y. 688.

If the consignee is known to the carrier, and the carrier fails to notify the consignee of the arrival of the goods, and sells them, it is a conversion.

If a transportation company has unclaimed freight or baggage (not live stock or perishable goods) in its possession for 60 days, it may, at its own option, deliver the same to any person engaged in the warehouse business in the State of New York. Upon taking the receipt the carrier is discharged from its liability. 

If the carrier holds the goods for a year, it may sell the goods at public auction after four weeks from the first application of the notice of sale. 

Erie R. R. Co. v. Kieser, 216 A. D. 500, 215 N. Y. S. 576;

Railroad Law, § 68. 

If the transportation was free, the carrier is not an insurer during transportation, but a bailee.

CARRIERS 

57 As to baggage carried by the passenger with him, the carrier is only a bailee.

A Carrier's Liability to Passengers 

A carrier must take reasonable care that a passenger shall have safe passage to the train, protecting him from any unnecessary danger or from a danger of which the railroad company is or ought to be aware. If the common carrier actually takes a passenger aboard, it is obligated to use care, fully commensurate with the situation and all the circumstances presented, in order to protect the passenger. If the passenger is a small child, that must be taken into consideration, and more care should be taken in watching him than in the case of an adult. 

White v. N. Y. Cent. R. Co., 228 A. D. 50, 239 N. Y. S. 90; 

Longacre v. Yonkers R. Co., 236 N. Y. 119; 

Sannucci v. N. Y. Cent. R. Co., 223 A. D. 517, 228 N. Y. S. 505.

In one case the plaintiff, a passenger on a subway train in the City of New York, was carried forward out of the train by a passenger behind her who was seeking to leave the train when the doors were opened at a station. Her foot sank in a space between the car and the platform, the passengers in the rear stepped over her, and she received injuries. There was no guard on the car or platform to regulate the crowd. It was held that the carrier was guilty of negligence, and the plaintiff recovered. 

Fidelman v. N. Y. Rapid Trans. Corp., 246 N. Y. 564.

If a carrier is negligent and a passenger is therefore hurt, the carrier is of course liable. This negligence may consist of a failure to use the best appliances. But if the carrier in selecting its appliances had done everything reasonable to get the best, and they turned out to be had, the carrier is not liable for injury because of the defect.

It is the absolute duty of a railroad to furnish safe locomotive boilers. The exercise of ordinary care in providing a boiler is not sufficient. 

Luce v. N. Y. C. etc. Co., 213 A. D. 374, affd. 242 N. Y. 519.

If an employee injures a passenger even maliciously, the carrier is liable. This rule differs from the ordinary rule of master and servant, for the company has the affirmative duty of carrying passengers safely, and it is responsible for the way the employees execute that duty. The only instance in which the company would not be liable would be if the passenger provoked the injury, e.g., by using profane language. This rule is brought out even more clearly in the case where a conductor assisted a passenger in alighting and

did so negligently. Even though it was not part of the conductor's duty to help people off, the company was held liable. 

Gillespie v. Brooklyn Heights R. R. Co., 178 N. Y. 347.

This liability exists even when the railroad employee was not actting within the scope of his employment. If, however, the plaintiff was not a passenger at the time of the injury, there can be no recovery unless the employee causing the injury was acting within the scope of his employment. Thus, where a ticketseller sells tickets to X, and then has X arrested for passing counterfeit money, of which X is innocent, the company is not liable, for the ticketseller was not dealing within his scope with a passenger, but rather as a private citizen. If he had held X before he had sold him a ticket, or immediately on selling the ticket and as part of the same act, the company would have been liable, as the agent would have then been acting within the scope of his employment. 

R is traveling as an invitee of the conductor. He is hurt through the brakeman's negligence. R cannot recover, for R was not a real passenger, as the conductor had no authority to invite him.

A common carrier may not in advance relieve itself, by stipulation or contract, from liability to passengers for hire for the consequences of its negligence or that of its servants. A private carrier may do so. A common carrier may, however, impose reasonable limitations upon its liability for negligence, e.g.: requiring as a condition of liability, the giving of notice of injuries within a specified reasonable time. 

Smith v. N. Y. Central R. R., 24 N. Y. 22; 

Murray v. Cunard S. S. Co., 235 N. Y. 162.

A contract exempting a common carrier from liability to a gratuitous passenger has been upheld. A common carrier has also been permitted to exempt itself from such liability where it has permitted one to ride for a reduced rate, and, in consideration of the reduced fare, released itself from liability for negligence. So if R was traveling on a free pass, or a reduced rate ticket, on which pass was an indorsement that the company was not to be liable in case of accident, caused by the negligence of the carrier, R cannot recover if he is hurt through the carrier's negligence. He is not protected as a passenger would be. 

Hester v. Man. Bridge Line, 214 A. D. 534, 212 N. Y. S. 292.

If a person traveling on a free pass exempts the carrier from liability for negligence, this does not exempt a joint tort-feasor. The release is interpreted as speaking of the future, as regards the carrier alone. The agreement, however, accrues to the benefit of all other companies recognizing the pass and whose facilities the plaintiff uses during the trip gratuitously, as part of the benefits of the free pass. 

Wilder v. Penn. R. R., 245 N. Y. 36.

CARRIERS 

59 Suppose one passenger hurts another. The company is liable only if the employee should have known of the threatened danger. This would be true if the passenger causing the damage had been intoxicated, or had openly threatened to start a fight.

Contractual Position of the Carrier 

The consignor is ordinarily liable for freight charges. The fact that the bill of lading stipulates that the consignee is to pay imposes no obligation on the carrier to insist on payment before delivery to the consignee. The carrier may neglect to collect from the consignee, and collect from the consignor. 

But the consignee may become liable for such charges by its own act. He becomes liable because of presumptive ownership, acceptance of the goods, and the services rendered and the benefits conferred by the carrier for freight charges. 

The consignee is not liable for transportation charges, under the Interstate Commerce Act, if the consignee is an agent only and has no beneficial title in the property, and he notifies the carrier of this in writing prior to the delivery of the property. 

Erie R. R. Co. v. Rosenstein, 249 N. Y. 241; 

N. Y. C. R. R. Co. v. Warren, 234 N. Y. 261.

Under the terms of the Sales Act, it is presumed that title to the goods is in the consignee. Therefore, unless this presumption is overcome, the right to sue for breach of contract of carriage is in the consignee. It has been held, however, that where the contract between the consignor and consignee fails to comply with the Statute of Frauds, the consignor may sue for breach of contract of carriage, since no actual transfer has taken place. The same is true if the contract has been rescinded during transit by mutual consent, so as to revest the title in the seller. 

Krulder v. Ellison, 47 N. Y. 36; 

Rosenberg v. Buffum, 234 N. Y. 338; 

Fein v. Weir, 129 A. D. 299, 114 N. Y. S. 426. 

Telegraph Companies

Ordinarily individuals and corporations engaged in quasi-public business cannot contract to absolve themselves from liability for their own wilful misconduct or gross negligence. If a telegraph company because of gross negligence fails to transmit an unrepeated message and the plaintiff sender is damaged thereby, he may recover the full amount of the damage. If the action is not based upon gross negligence and the message is not repeated, the damages are limited to the amount received by the telegraph company for sending the same, provided the contract so stipulates. An inadvertent or mistaken act of incorrectly transmitting the message, or a delay due to atmospheric

or other conditions to which telegraphic messages are subject, is not a case of gross negligence. But an absolute failure to deliver a message, unexplained by the defendant, is prima facie gross negligence as a matter of law. 

Krivitsky v. Western Union, 129 Misc. 431, 221 N. Y. S. 525. 

There seems to be a limitation upon the above rule. Where the terms of the telegram disclose the general nature of the transaction which is the subject of the message, the company is answerable for the natural consequences of its neglect in the matter. Where the terms of the message give no hint of the nature of the transaction, the liability is for nominal damages, or for the cost of carriage if the tolls have been prepaid. 

Kerr S. S. Co. v. Radio Corp. of America, 245 N. Y. 284.

Presumption That New York Law Applies 

In the absence of proof of what the law of a foreign jurisdiction is, in cases where such proof is required, there is a presumption that the common law of New York applies, provided that that foreign jurisdiction has adopted the common law from England. The presumption does not have any application in cases where it is necessary to prove the law of a country following the civil law. 

Gregg v. Gregg, 133 Misc. 109, 231 N. Y. S. 221; 

Gavin v. Malherbe, 261 N. Y. S. 373.

Where the law of a foreign jurisdiction is not proven, and the law of that jurisdiction is not derived from the English common law, the law of New York, both common and statutory, applies. 

Smith v. Compania Litagrafica de la Habana, 127 Misc. 508; 

Brassell v. John Hancock Mut. Life Ins. Co., 134 Misc. 274. 

When the Law of the Forum Applies 

When litigants air their dispute in a New York court, the laws of procedure of the New York courts will be followed, for it is the general rule that all remedial matters are decided according to the laws of the forum. What the actual rights of the parties are, i.e., whatever goes to the substance of the obligation and affects the substantive rights, is governed by the law of the state or country where the rights arose. The effect of this will be further discussed below. 

Rules of Evidence, Burden of Proof, Statute of Limitations, are all remedial points, and the law on these points is that of the state where the trial takes place. It is, of course, competent for parties to a contract to establish a shorter or different limitation to the right of action thereunder than that given by statute. The intent to do so must clearly appear from the contract itself. 

Reilly v. Steinhart, 217 N. Y. 549;

Whether the Statute of Frauds involves the remedial side of a litigation or the substantive rights of the parties is an unsettled question in this state. In one case the following situation arose: A and B had contracted in Pennsylvania concerning a sale of goods. 

The defense was a failure to comply with the Statute of Frauds. 

It will be seen (infra) that the law of the state where the contract is made usually controls the substantive rights of the parties. If the 

Statute of Frauds is a remedial matter, the New York law, i.e., lex 61

fori, would apply. The Court of Appeals deliberately avoided stating the New York law and decided the case on other grounds. 

Franklin Sugar Refining Co. v. Lipowicz, 247 N. Y. 465.

Domicile 

Frequently the domicile of a party is very important to determine the forum whose laws are to govern. One can have but one domicile, though he can have more than one residence. To constitute a domicile, there must be residence, plus the intention to have that place considered the sole domicile. Residence means living in a particular locality, but domicile means living in that locality with intent to make it a fixed and permanent home. 

In re Brown's Will, 133 Misc. 457, 233 N. Y. S. 145.

The essential requirements for a change of domicile are abandonment in fact of the former domicile and residence in a new locality with intent to remain there and make a new and sole domicile there. 

In re Beechwood, 142 Misc. 400; 254 N. Y. S. 473.

A woman upon her marriage takes the domicile of her husband by operation of law. If the husband elects to change his domicile, he may do so and the wife's domicile follows his. Her physical presence would not be required to effect the change. 

A wife, may, however, when justified by her husband's conduct toward her, in leaving him, make her home apart from him to establish a domicile.

Crimes 

In re Daggett's Will, 255 N. Y. 243.

A criminal prosecution must be brought in the county where the crime is committed. The New York courts will not enforce the infraction of the criminal laws of any other state. New York will hold the one charged with the crime for extradition. A person will be surrendered only where the accused was actually present in the demanding state at the time the act complained of was committed. 

Constructive presence is not sufficient. 

People v. McLaughlin, 247 N. Y. 238; 

People v. Enright, 217 A. D. 514, 217 N. Y. S. 288.

Only the Federal government may surrender a fugitive who has fled from a foreign country to one of the states. Such act is a part of the "foreign relations" controlled by the Federal government. 

A crime is committed where the last essential act is done. If S in State 1 sends his agent to New York to get money by fraud from New Yorkers, though the crime is begun in State 1, it is completed in New York, and S can be prosecuted in New York. It should be noted that, in the case of homicide, the crime is committed where the

fatal injury was inflicted, even though the death took place in another state.

New York will not enforce the penal laws of any other state. 

Suppose a railroad company was, according to the laws of State 1, to pay $10,000 if by its negligence it caused a death. If this railroad was sued for the $10,000 in New York, under the statute of State 1, there could be no recovery, for New York would not enforce another state's penal laws. 

West. Transp. Co. of Mich. v. Kelderhouse, 27 N. Y. 430.

A state court has no jurisdiction over crimes committed exclusively against the United States, nor will it assume jurisdiction of questions which have been adjudicated in the Federal courts. 

Madole v. Gavin, 222 A. D. 698, 225 N. Y. S. 407.

The tax or revenue laws of another state have no force in New York.

Torts 

In re Martins, 240 N. Y. S. 393; 

Beadall v. Moore, 199 A. D. 531, 191 N. Y. S. 826.

When one commits a tort against another's person, the latter gets a right to compensation, and each state, because of comity, will aid the injured to enforce his right. But no state will enforce such a right acquired under the laws of another state if to do so would violate its own public policy. 

Loucks v. Standard Oil Co., 224 N. Y. 99.

In personal injury actions, the lex loci (law of the jurisdiction where the injuries were inflicted) governs the rights of litigants, while the lex fori (law of the place where the trial takes place) controls matters relating to procedure. Where a gratuitous guest sues here for injuries received in an accident in Massachusetts, he must prove his own freedom from contributory negligence despite the fact that in Massachusetts the burden would be on the defendant to prove it. The rule governing proof of freedom from contributory negligence is a matter of procedure. 

Fitzpatrick v. Inter. Reg. Co., 252 N. Y. 127; 

Sapone v. N. Y. C. & H. R. R. Co., 130 Misc. 755; 225 N. Y. S. 211; 

Wright v. Palmison, 260 N. Y. S. 812.

One who is a New York resident when the action is brought can as of right bring his action in New York, even though the tort was committed in another state. The New York court may of its own choice entertain jurisdiction if a non-resident brings a tort action which arose outside of the state of New York. Generally speaking, our courts will as a matter of public policy refuse to retain jurisdiction in an action between nonresidents for tort arising outside the

state, unless special reasons are shown to exist which make the assumption and retention of jurisdiction necessary or proper. The 

New York courts do determine causes of action in tort between nonresidents arising within the state. 

Hunter v. Hosmer, 142 Misc. 382; 254 N. Y. S. 635.

If the act that caused injury to a person is not a tort in the state where the act is done, the injured cannot bring an action in New York, where the same act, if done in New York, would have been a tort and given rise to a cause of action. The converse is also true. 

So, if the injury in State 1 gave rise to a good cause of action in State 1, and the action is actually brought in State 2, the plaintiff as to remedy is governed by the law of the state where he brings the action, though as to the substantive part of the case he is governed by the laws of the place where the tort was committed. 

Only an executor or administrator may maintain an action for wrongful death, in New York. An individual cannot bring such an action, though he be the next of kin. The right to recover for wrongful death is purely statutory. Suppose X is killed through the negligence of the defendant, in Quebec. Under the law of Quebec (lex loci), an administrator has no cause of action upon which to sue. 

That right lies with the consort, ascendant and descendant of the deceased. The action was brought in New York by the plaintiff, as administrator of the deceased. It was held that, under the substantive law of the lex loci, an administrator had no cause of action and no legal capacity to sue and therefore the New York court dismissed the complaint. 

Sapone v. N. Y. C. & H. R. R. Co., 130 Misc. 755; 225 N. Y. S. 211.

Let us take this case: In New York there is no limit to the amount that can be recovered for death in a negligence case. Suppose State 1 by statute limits it, and X is killed there. The suit is brought in New York. The law of State 1 would govern, for since the amount is mentioned in the statute, it is part of the substantive law on the point and not part of the remedy, and the law of the state where the right arose governs. But if the statute of that state had mentioned no sum as the limit, the New York rules would govern, for then it would go to the remedy and not to the substantive rights, and there would be no limit as to amount. 

Loucks v. Standard Oil Co., 224 N. Y. 99.

Suppose the New Jersey law says that if an employer pays a sum to an injured employee under the Workmen's Compensation Act for an injury sustained in the course of his employment, the employer can get back any sum up to the amount paid, should the employee

recover in another action for the same injury. The employer pays $1,000 in New Jersey. Later the employee recovers $800 in a New 

York court. The employer can get from the employee the $800 in a New York court, because the right is a New Jersey right, and New York courts of equity will prevent the double recovery and enforce the substantive rights under the rules of another state. 

Contracts 

Hartford Acc. & Ind. Co. v. Chartrand, 239 N. Y. 36.

All matters bearing on (1) validity, (2) interpretation, and (3) execution of a contract, including (4) the competency of a party to act, are controlled by the law of the place where the contract is made, unless a contrary intention plainly appears. 

Napier v. Bankers Life Ins. Co., 51 Misc. 283; 100 N. Y. S. 1072;

Vanderhorst v. Kittredge, 241 N. Y. S. 302. 

A contract is made where the acceptance occurs. Thus, if an offer is mailed from State 1 to the offeree in New York, and the acceptance is mailed from New York, the contract is made in New York.

Suppose a valid contract is made in New York between H and Mrs. K., a married woman. The action is brought in State 2, where a contract with a married woman is invalid. State 2 would allow recovery, for the question involves rights and not remedies. Since it is a question of validity, the law of the place where the contract was made governs unless a contrary intent is shown. 

Franklin Sugar Refining Co. v. Lipowicz, 247 N. Y. 465.

All matters connected with the performance of a contract are regulated by the law of the place where the contract, by its terms, is to be performed. In one case A gave B a check for a gambling debt in Florida drawn on a New York bank and payable in New York. 

B sued upon the check, and it was held that, although it was to be presumed that the Florida law was the same as the common law, which held a gambling contract valid, yet the plaintiff could not recover, as the check was an executory contract, by its terms to be performed in New York, and the New York law holding a gambling contract illegal would be followed. 

Youssoupoff v. Widener, 126 Misc. 491; 215 N. Y. S. 24; 

Beadall v. Moore, 199 A. D. 531; 191 N. Y. S. 826.

If the plaintiff had sued on the contract made in Florida rather than on the check, it might have been presumed that the gambling debt was valid in Florida, and it could then be enforced in New York. 

Thuna v. Wolf, 132 Misc. 56; 228 N. Y. S. 658.

If no place of performance is mentioned in the contract, a presumption arises that the parties intend that it should be performed where it was made.

Vanderhorst v. Kittredge, 241 N. Y. S. 302. 

Where a contract is made in another state, to be performed in New York, the parties are presumed to know the law of New York State, even though they may be citizens of a foreign jurisdiction. 

De Witt v. Brisbane, 16 N. Y. 509, 514.

Where the place of performance is in different states or counties, the lex loci contractus (the law of the place where the contract is made) controls.

Smith v. Companio Litagrafica de la Habana, 127 Misc. 508. 

The validity of an assignment is determined by the law of the place where it is mailed to the assignee. 

Jackson v. Talmadge, 216 A. D. 100; affd. 246 N. Y. 133.

Bills and Notes 

A note is regarded as made where it is delivered, and if it is mailed, it is considered made where it is mailed. Ordinarily, then, the law of the place where the note is made governs. Thus, where a note is made outside of New York, bearing a rate of interest lawful in that state but unlawful in New York, the New York courts would hold the instrument valid. 

Hennenlotter v. De Orvananos, 114 Misc. 333; 186 N. Y. S. 488; 

Hibernia Natl. Bank v. Lacombe, 84 N. Y. 367.

The obligations of the drawer of a bill of exchange are determined by the law of the place where he delivered it. 

Amsinck v. Rogers, 189 N. Y. 252.

The indorsement of a negotiable instrument is an independent contract and is governed by the law of the place where it is made. 

Heidelburger v. Heidelburger, 171 A. D. 106; 155 N. Y. S. 993.

Marriage Contracts 

The validity of a marriage depends on the laws of the state where the marriage took place. Suppose Brown has been divorced because of his adultery in State 1. State 1, where he lived, declared that one who is divorced for his own adultery cannot remarry. This was a penalty on one divorced. So Brown moved to State 2 and remarried. State 2 had no such law. Later Brown moved back to 

State 1. He cannot be indicted in State 1, for he was validly married. The law of State 2 said so. But if it had been the law in State 

1 that neither divorced party could remarry, and it was based on public policy, rather than a penalty on the guilty party alone, the marriage in State 2 would not be recognized as valid in State 1.

There is no comity where the public policy in involved. In every other place but in State 1, the mariage would be valid. 

Real Property 

The law governing personal property is that of the state of domicile of the owner and not where the property is located. As to realty the law of the place where the land is situated governs. There are no exceptions to this rule as to real property. Thus a wife's right to dower must be determined by the laws of the state where the realty is situated. 

Lowe v. Plainfield Trust Co., 216 A. D. 72; 215 N. Y. S. 50.

It also follows that a judgment of a foreign court will not avail, of its own force, to transfer title to land in this state, despite the fact that a conveyance can be executed by any agent of the court in fulfilment of its mandate. Where, however, the owner himself executes a conveyance of land in this state, title passes even though the owner act under compulsion directed by the foreign court. 

It might be mentioned that if the conveyance in the foreign jurisdiction was compelled by the court in an insolvency proceeding dealing with the insolvency of the conveyor, the title of the conveyee would be subordinate to the rights of local creditors with executions or attachments against the goods of the creditor. Legitimacy 

Deschenes v. Tallman, 248 N. Y. 33.
