The status of a child as to legitimacy depends on the laws of his domicile. The domicile of an infant is that of his father (or of the mother, if the father is dead).

Conferring Jurisdiction by Stipulation 

Suppose the parties stipulate that the laws of state X are to govern in the transactions, no matter where the case is tried. This arrangement will control if it is made in good faith and if it bears on some element in the transaction, so that there is reason for that state being selected, and if the party who would ordinarily have the right to sue in New York is not being deprived of important rights under the arrangement. But if a state's laws are selected merely because they are most liberal or most stringent, and for that reason alone, the arrangement will not be recognized. 

Sliosberg v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 217 A. D. 685; 217 N. Y. S. 226.

An agreement by which a New York resident as a party to a contract undertakes to confer exclusive jurisdiction on a foreign tri-

bunal over controversies, is void as against public policy. This refers only to the jurisdiction of the court over the subject-matter. 

Sudbury v. Ambi Verwaltung, 213 A. D. 98, 210 N. Y. S. 154.

Foreign Judgments 

A foreign judgment is conclusive so far as to preclude a re-trial of the merits in New York, except (1) where the judgment is tainted with fraud; or (2) where the public policy of New York is involved; or (3) where the foreign court had no jurisdiction. 

Thus a contention that a judgment of another state is erroneous as a matter of law will not be maintained. The question should be disposed of in the state where the judgment was obtained. 

Franklin v. Lee, 233 A. D. 592; 253 N. Y. S. 620.

The law seems not to be definite on the question whether the fact that there is a lack of reciprocity between New York and the particular foreign jurisdiction involved, is material as regards the conclusiveness of the judgment. 

Cowans v. Ticonderoga Pulp & Paper Co., 219 A. D. 120; affd. 

246 N. Y. 603.

The foreign judgment may be attacked collaterally for want of jurisdiction, or for fraud perpetrated upon the court or upon a party. 

The fraud in such case is made up of the same constituents as is fraud in any other case. 

Hall v. Hall, 139 A. D. 120; 123 N. Y. S. 1056.

Wills and Decedent's Estates 

The rules of construction and the questions of revocation of a will are determined by the laws of the testator's domicile, if personalty is involved. As regards realty, the laws of the place where the land is situated govern. 

In re McCready, 259 N. Y. S. 512, 518.

The succession and distribution of personal property are governed by the law of the domicile of the decedent at the time of his death unless such law contravenes some rule of public policy. 

In re Bleicher's Estate, 142 Misc. 549; 255 N. Y. S. 368.

A will executed outside of New York State will be considered by the New York court to be legally executed and will be admitted to probate, provided it is in writing, subscribed by the testator, and meets the requirements of the place where it was executed or of the testator's domicile.

Decedent Estate Law, §§ 22a, 23.

Trusts 

The essential validity of declarations of trust or transfers in trust of personal property inter vivos is governed by the law of the settlor's domicile.¹ 

Ross v. Ross, 243 N. Y. S. 423. 

1For a discussion of the Statute of Limitations of foreign jurisdictions and its effect on causes brought in New York, see Sainer, "The Adjective Law of 

New York," 6th Ed., p. 55.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW Eminent Domain 

The power of eminent domain is the right of the state, inherent in the state as a sovereign power, to take private property for public use upon making just compensation. The "due process" clause of the Fourteenth Amendment of the Federal Constitution, as well as the provisions of the state laws, require that private property is not to be taken for public use without just compensation. 

This right may be delegated by the Legislature to a corporation or individual to whom has been delegated some public duty or function by some act of the Legislature, which act must state the specific purpose for which the delegation is made. If it is delegated to a municipality, the specific purpose need not be stated. 

People v. Pendergast, 220 A. D. 351; affd. 248 N. Y. 215.

The necessity or expediency of taking private property for public use is a legislative question, and the courts cannot interfere in its determination, nor review the determination of the question by the Legislature. Whether the use for which the property is proposed to be taken is a public one is a judicial question for the court to decide and as to this the determination of the Legislature may be reviewed. 

In re Cosgrove, 238 N. Y. 84; 

Little v. Ford, 223 A. D. 559; affd. 249 N. Y. 495.

Private property can never be taken by the state for any private use. Hence a railroad could not have property condemned so that it might erect a storage house for customers on it for that would be a private use. 

Matter of City of New York, Saratoga Ave., 226 N. Y. 128.

Where property has previously been devoted to a public use by a corporation having the power of eminent domain, it cannot be taken by another corporation to which has been delegated the power of condemnation, unless the authority so to do has expressly or by necessary implication been conferred by the Legislature, and this rule applies to municipalities as well as to public service corporations. 

But this rule has no application where the taking is by the sovereign. The rights to retake are inherent in its sovereignty, and are superior to Constitutions and statutes. 

In re Cruger Ave., 238 N. Y. 84; 

Board v. Ponda, 249 N. Y. 445; 

In re Elimination of Highway, 234 A. D. 129; 254 N. Y. S. 578. 70

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

71 

Fixing the Compensation 

The sum to be paid as compensation is fixed by the state, or by a jury, or by the Supreme Court, either with or without a jury. The jury decides by majority vote. Another method is by having at least three commissioners appointed by the court. The decision made in any of these ways is not reviewable by the courts.

The amount to be given is the fair market value of the property for all available uses and purposes. The fact that the property has not been used to best advantage does not militate against the owner's right to have the value assessed so that he may get the highest compensation. The owner is not entitled to swell the damages beyond the fair market value by any consideration of chance or probability that some time in the future the property may be used for some purpose to which it is adapted, unless it appears that the market value of the property is enhanced by the chance or probability. 

Cookingham v. Richfield Springs, 140 Misc. 760; 251 N. Y. S. 520.

If a grantee, holding under words of "limitation" and using the land as it was to be used under his grant, lost the land through its being condemned, the grantee gets the full award, and the grantor's heirs get nothing. The heirs have a mere possibility of reversion. 

N. Y. Cent. Ry. Co. v. Maloney, 234 N. Y. 208; 

In re Water Front on East River, 213 A. D. 187; 210 N. Y. S. 387;

Art. I, § 7, Constitution of New York. 

Where any realty under lease or other contract shall be taken by condemnation, all the covenants, contracts and engagements between landlord and tenant or any other contracting parties, respecting the same, or any part thereof, shall upon the vesting of the title in the city, cease and determine and be absolutely discharged. 

In re Allen St. & 1st Ave., 256 N. Y. 236.

A tenant is entitled to compensation for fixtures or structures annexed to realty, though these be the personal property of the tenant, whenever the city in taking the realty destroys the leasehold interest of the tenant. This compensation is required even where there is a clause in the lease stating that the tenant is to receive nothing for his leasehold interest in the event of a condemnation of the premises, and that he may remove his annexations at the end of the lease. 

In re Allen St. & 1st Ave., 256 N. Y. 236.

The award of the commissioners must be based on, and have support in the record and on whatever knowledge they may have acquired in viewing the premises. What the commissioners learn outside of the record is not to be taken into consideration in determining the award. 

In re Chittenango-Casenovia, 130 Misc. 516; 223 N. Y. S. 833; 

Board of Supervisors v. Beardslee, 252 N. Y. S. 10, 17.

Although the courts will not interfere with an honest compensation award, the Legislature may provide for a review by some one of the tribunals which might have been selected to award. 

In re School Site, 222 A. D. 554; affd. 250 N. Y. 588.

After a final order confirming report of commissioners in condemnation proceeding, the condemning party cannot discontinue the proceeding except on direction of the court on just cause shown. A just cause would be the proof of fraud, misconduct, irregularity, judicial error, or inequity in the proceeding, or some overpowering reason, as that the property for some reason became inundated, or that quicksand underlay it, thereby enhancing the cost of necessary foundations, or the state department of health should intervene and forbid selection of such a site for the preservation of public health. 

In re Municipal Garage, 140 Misc. 894; 252 N. Y. S. 18.

Police Power and the Due Process Clause 

The Constitution says that no person (and this includes corporations and municipalities) shall be deprived of life, liberty or property, without due process of law. But these rights cannot be claimed as superior to the public interest. 

Art. I, § 6, Constitution of New York. 

The state may establish regulations reasonably necessary for the general welfare, the protection, general prosperity and safety of the health and morals of the people, even though the rights of private property be thereby curtailed or the freedom of contract abridged. 

This is the state's "police power." Any law adopted by the Legislature must be reasonably calculated to effect such ends in order to be considered under the heading of police power. 

People v. La Fetra, 230 N. Y. 429; 

People v. Teuscher, 129 Misc. 94; affd. 248 N. Y. 454.

Under police power rights the state can reasonably regulate child and woman's labor conditions; employment agencies; moving pictures; material from which dwelling houses must be built, so that a law prohibiting frame houses in certain districts would be constitutional; size and bulk of homes; location and regulation of slaughter houses; use and preservation of wild forest land; zoning regulations; the sale of soft drinks; acts permitted on Sundays. Auction sales may be prohibited after sundown. 

Assn. for Protection of Adirondacks v. McDonald, 253 N. Y. 234; 

Wulfsohn v. Burden, 241 N. Y. 288; 

People v. Board of Appeals, 234 N. Y. 484; 

Cronin v. People, 82 N. Y. 318.

The Legislature is invested with a wide discretion in determining whether a business or occupation should be barred to the incompetent.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

73 The Legislature may prescribe qualifications for those who desire to practice architecture, as the work involves the public safety. 

Bowen v. Schenectady, 136 Misc. 307; 240 N. Y. S. 784.

A law limiting the price at which a speculator might sell theatre tickets was held to be unconstitutional. 

A statute prohibiting children from attending school unless vaccinated was held constitutional. 

Matter of Viemeister, 179 N. Y. 235.

A tax levied on an individual with discrimination, making him pay almost all the money necessary to support the village, would be void. 

But a tax levied as a special assessment because of a local improvement, by which the property of one or of a few alone benefited, could be made to be borne by these few. 

An ordinance prohibiting the operation of a business in one part of a town and allowing it in another is contra to the due process clause. It doesn't regulate the business all through the city, nor does it do so in other parts where the same neighborhood conditions exist. 

An ordinance prohibiting the construction of a gasoline tank within 1,000 feet of one specific dwelling house is unconstitutional. 

A zoning resolution which prohibited the use for business purposes of any structure, unless it had a frontage of at least 20 feet and a depth of at least 50 feet was held unconstitutional, because it was arbitrary and unreasonable and did not serve a purpose either useful or aesthetic, nor in any way promoted public health, morals, public safety or general prosperity. 

Oppenheimer v. Kraus, 221 A. D. 773; affd. 246 N. Y. 559.

Since a man has a duty to give evidence when called upon, he can be subpoenaed in New York to give evidence in a different state. 

This is not depriving him of liberty, for it is his duty. This applies only to criminal cases. In a civil case it does not apply because a deposition can be taken. A deposition cannot be taken in a criminal case, as every man accused must be confronted by his accusers. 

Allowing farmers to sell vinegar of a low standard, while others must conform to a higher standard, deprives those not farmers of means of competition, with discrimination, and thus deprives them of property in an unfair manner. It is an unconstitutional law. 

A right of review by appeal is not an essential element of due process of law. 

In re Foote, 129 Misc. 2; 221 N. Y. S. 302.

Construction of Statutes 

The courts favor such construction of a statute as will render it constitutional wherever possible. The judicial duty is to uphold rather than to nullify legislative enactments. The court assumes that the purpose of the legislature was legitimate. Whether the purpose was wise is not for the courts to consider. 

Schieffelin v. Goldsmith, 253 N. Y. 243.

The court will act only if an actual case is before it requiring a decision concerning the constitutionality of a statute. 

One who challenges the constitutionality of a statute, must show that he will be affected unfavorably if the statute is upheld and he must also show its unconstitutionality beyond a reasonable doubt. 

People v. Harnett, 224 A. D. 127; affd. 249 N. Y. 606; 

People v. Teuscher, 129 Misc. 94; affd. 248 N. Y. 454.

Any state law or any act of any department of the state which is contrary to, or in conflict with a Federal law, is invalid. 

An unconstitutional statute, even though it is finally declared to be wholly void, does not prevent the running of the Statute of Limitations, if one fails to act, relying on its validity. 

Interstate Commerce

The Federal Government has absolute power to regulate, prohibit, or burden interstate commerce. This does not mean that a state has no say over any articles or any situations which play a part in interstate commerce. A state may tax property which is used in interstate commerce where such property is within the geographical jurisdiction of the state. A state has been permitted to tax motor bus owners engaged in interstate commerce at the rate of one cent per mile for each mile of highway in that state over which such vehicle traveled, although intrastate buses were taxed upon a different basis. The classification was held to be reasonable and not discriminatory. 

It has also been held that a state ruling cannot interfere with the expression of Congress over an interstate matter simply because the state ruling is made in the interests of public safety, and in the exercise of the police power of the state. 

Intern. Busses Co. v. Blodgett, 19 Fed. (2d) 256; 

Hendrick v. Maryland, 235 U. S. 610; 

People v. Public Service Comm., 226 Á. D. 43; 234 N. Y. S. 342.

A common carrier passing through a foreign state, en route between termini in the originating state, is engaged in interstate commerce, unless the route is unreasonable or a subterfuge for the purpose of taking advantage of federal statutes. 

Bus Corp. v. Davis, 225 A. D. 182; 232 N. Y. S. 536.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

75 

Jury Trials in State Courts¹ 

A man is to be judged by his peers. An alien is not a peer, and the presence of an alien on a jury is sufficient ground for a new trial. 

People v. Bott, 121 Misc. 380; 201 N. Y. S. 47; 

Crouse Grocery Co. v. Valentine, 131 Misc. 571; 226 N. Y. S. 613.

In each of the following actions an issue of fact must be tried by a jury unless a jury trial is waived: (1) An action to recover judgment for a sum of money only.

In an action for an accounting, or an action to enjoin a nuisance wherein money damages are asked, there is no right to a jury trial. (2) An action of ejectment; for dower; for waste; for the abatement of a nuisance; to recover a chattel. (3) The statute gives the right to a trial by jury also in these cases: (a) If the defendant's answer in an action for divorce puts in issue the allegation of adultery, either side may demand a jury. (b) If an annulment is sought on a ground other than physical incapacity, the parties have a right to a trial by jury. (4) Action for partition. (5) Action by the Attorney-General to annul a corporation. (6) Action brought against one who is alleged to be a usurper in office.

Civil Practice Act, §§ 1142, 1023. 

A court sitting in equity may, on its own initiative, call in the aid of a jury. The court, however, is not bound by the jury's verdict in such case. 

Civil Practice Act, §§ 425, 430, 1023, 1142, 1149.

A surrogate may, as a matter of discretion, order a jury trial in cases where an absolute right to a jury trial does not exist. The statute gives an absolute right to a jury trial in these cases; contested proceedings for the probate of a will; where the question of heirship is the main matter and not a collateral issue. 

In re Boyle's Estate, 242 N. Y. 342; 

In re Bringold, 204 A. D. 101; 198 N. Y. S. 282.

In these cases there is no absolute right to a jury trial in the surrogate's court: in an objection to an account; in a proceeding to have the state treasurer pay over to one claiming to be an heir; in a collateral issue of kinship so as to determine the right to file objections to a will. 

In re Hamilton's Estate, 220 A. D. 536; 222 N. Y. S. 73. 

1For further discussion of trial by jury, see "The Adjective Law of New 

York," 6th Ed., pp. 112-114.

A jury trial may be waived in civil cases. "Civil" cases mean strictly judicial proceedings. Thus, in proceedings not strictly judicial, except in instances mentioned by statute, there is no right to a jury trial. Condemnation proceedings do not come under the head of judicial proceedings. 

People v. Smith, 21 N. Y. 595.

Though there is a right to a trial by jury for felonies there is no such right upon trials for misdemeanors. 

People v. McCann, 253 N. Y. 221.

In the courts of record in the City of New York, a jury is considered waived unless certain rules are complied with at the time the notice of trial is served.¹

The right to a trial by jury is not lost if issues equitable in their nature are injected during a trial, triable by a jury. The converse is also true, so that the mere injection of a side issue which is not an equity case would not give a right to a jury trial in the equity case. 

City of Syracuse v. Hogan, 234 N. Y. 457.

A man cannot be removed from the jury list merely because of bigotry on his part in having violent religious prejudices. 

Ford v. O'Bryne, 222 A. D. 50; 225 N. Y. S. 1. 

Constitutional Rights Which May or May Not be Waived

Under the Fifth Amendment to the Federal Constitution and also under the New York State Constitution (Article I, § 6) no person can be held to answer for a felony unless on presentment or indictment of a Grand Jury. Under this section a New York statute was held unconstitutional which permitted a defendant to waive an indictment or presentment before being tried for a felony. 

People v. Christian, 249 N. Y. 314.

This brings up the question which constitutional safeguards may or may not be waived. These can be divided into two classes: those which belong to the accused alone and which can be waived, and those which belong to the public as well and cannot be waived. A person may waive his right to a jury in civil cases; he may agree to testify against himself; he may waive compensation for his property taken for public use; he may consent to be searched without a warrant; he may waive his right to counsel; he may waive his right to be confronted by his accusers. 

One cannot waive the provisions in regard to freedom of religion; his right not to be twice put in jeopardy; his right to be tried by a court for a crime; or his right to be present at such trial. 

People v. Sugarman, 248 N. Y. 255. 

1See Sainer, "The Adjective Law of New York," 6th Ed., p. 114.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

77 

Double Jeopardy

The Constitution prohibits any one being tried twice for the same offense and thus placed in "double jeopardy." This rule applies to all indictable offenses and misdemeanors. 

An acquittal or conviction by a verdict of a jury, although not followed by judgment or sentence, bars another trial. 

People v. Cignarale, 110 N. Y. 23.

An indictment by a grand jury or an examination by a magistrate, or a trial which ends in a disagreement of the jury are not considered a trial so as to prevent a retrial. 

When a jury is arbitrarily discharged in a criminal case without the consent of the defendant, and where no circumstances exist calling for or permitting the exercise of a discretion by the court, the defendant is, by reason of the trial that thus comes to a sudden end, placed in jeopardy within the constitutional provisions referred to, and such discharge is a bar to another trial upon the same indictment. 

People v. Klein, 248 N. Y. S. 478.

In a recent case the court declared a mistrial and discharged the jury after its retirement before a verdict, because it had received information of the defendant's prior conviction of a crime which had the effect of turning the charge of the crime for which the defendant was then being tried, from a misdemeanor to a felony. It was held that the defendant was immune from further prosecution on the ground of former jeopardy. 

People v. Thrum, 143 Misc. 7; 256 N. Y. S. 586.

If X is convicted under the wrong section of the Penal Law and the conviction is therefore reversed, the state may have the defendant indicted under the proper clause. The conviction under the wrong clause is not reckoned as a jeopardy. 

People v. Smith, 177 A. D. 152; 163 N. Y. S. 283.

If a judge sets aside an acquittal, his decision must be reversed without any trial, for a trial would be double jeopardy.

Where an act is an offense against both the Federal and the state laws, the party guilty thereof may be punished in both the Federal and the state courts. The single act may thus constitute two crimes, and two trials may result.

Where, however, a single set of acts is done causing harm to several individuals, a criminal prosecution resulting in an acquittal based upon the acts which violated the rights of one of the individuals will serve as a bar to prosecutions based upon the same acts which violated the rights of other individuals. In one case ten lives were lost in a fire and it was alleged that all these deaths resulted from the same wrongful act of the defendant in failing to install automatic

sprinklers in the building where the fire occurred. The court in its opinion declared that if the defandant were acquitted after a prosecution which considered only the death of one of the individuals, he could not be tried for the acts resulting in the death of the others. 

People v. Barr, 259 N. Y. 104.

It thus follows that where a defendant is charged with burning 35 houses by the single act of setting fire to one building, an acquittal upon an indictment charging arson as to one of the buildings would be a bar to an indictment for burning any others of these houses. 

Woodford v. People, 62 N. Y. 117, 127.

Ex Post Facto Laws 

The Constitution of the United States forbids the passing of any ex post facto law either by the national government or by the states. 

An ex post facto law has been defined as follows: (1) Every law that makes an action, done before the passing of the law and which was innocent when done, criminal, and punishes such action. (2) Every law that aggravates the crime or makes it greater than it was when committed. (3) Every law that changes the punishment and inflicts a greater punishment than was annexed to the crime when committed. (4) Every law that alters the legal rules of evidence and receives less or different testimony than the law required at the time of the commission of the offense in order to convict the offender. 

Thus, if a crime is committed on the tenth day of December, the punishment for that crime is the punishment which the law, as it stood at that time, said should be meted out. If the penalty was increased by the Legislature on the twentieth, and the criminal is sentenced on the thirtieth, the law of the tenth governs. 

People v. Hayes, 140 N. Y. 484.

The ex post facto rule applies only to criminal cases, penal statutes, and vested rights. It does not apply to the rules of evidence which may add to the burdens of proof of a party on the trial; they may be changed by the Legislature (subject to the rules hereinbefore outlined) and be used at once, even at the trial of actions which were commenced prior to the change of the rules involved. 

Campbell v. Evening Post, 245 N. Y. 320.

Self-Incrimination 

A witness need answer no question, or produce any real evidence, if the answer or the real evidence will tend to make him liable to a punishment for any crime or to expose him to a penalty or loss of land. This rule applies even to questions put by a Grand Jury. 

Civil Practice Act, § 355.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

79 Accordingly, a defendant cannot be examined before trial in support of an allegation that "he negligently, carelessly and recklessly operated, managed and controlled a certain automobile, in violation of the ordinances of the City of New York," for the reason that a defendant may not be required, in violation of his constitutional rights, to testify to facts which may tend to incriminate him. Nor can a defendant be compelled to write out the details of his past. 

A forcible examination of the accused to determine whether he was drunk at the time of an automobile accident in which he participated, is compelling him to be a witness against himself. 

People v. Dennis, 131 Misc. 62; 226 N. Y. S. 689.

Where it is desired to identify a defendant he may be ordered to stand up.

His wig may be removed to show that he is bald. Such acts have been held not to be evidence tending to incriminate the defendant under any circumstances but merely a closer inspection of the object already before the court-the witness. An attorney, against whom charges have been brought in order to have him disbarred, is not privileged when the answer to a question would tend to make his disbarment more certain. Disbarment is not punishment for a crime. 

In re Becker, 229 A. D. 62; 241 N. Y. S. 369, 384.

Agricultural Lands 

A lease of agricultural land in which rent or service is reserved, for a period of more than twelve years is entirely invalid. The purpose for which the land was actually leased is entirely immaterial. 

The lease, to be considered valid, should have excluded the use for agricultural purposes. Thus, if A leases X's agricultural land only for the purpose of digging coal, for a period over twelve years, it is a valid lease, since the purpose of A's agreement is definitely stated not to be agricultural. 

Odell v. Durant, 62 N. Y. 524.

A lease of agricultural lands for a period of one's life for rent or services is invalid. The life might be longer than twelve years. 

Where two leases were executed to the same lessee simultaneously for agricultural lands, for eight and twelve years respectively, both were held void. If one lease for eight years had been given and then another for twelve, the second would be held void, since at the time of the execution there was a vesting in one individual for more than twelve years. 

Parthey v. Beyer, 228 A. D. 308; 238 N. Y. S. 412.

A lease for a fixed term with a covenant of renewal for twelve years was held void as to the renewal period, though original term may be valid. 

Hart v. Hart, 22 Barb. 606.

Voters

To vote in New York one must be a citizen of the United States for at least ninety days, and a resident of New York for a year, of the county for four months, and of the election district for thirty days before election.

Soldiers and sailors on active duty or those individuals who are away because of the necessities of their business (as traveling salesmen) may vote despite these "resident" provisions. Nor does one gain or lose residence for voting purposes while working for the Government, or while a student of a seminary, or while confined to a poorhouse or asylum in an election district which is not the one in which the citizen votes.

The bare intention of a student to change his residence to that of the seminary is not sufficient to constitute the change. If circumstances independent of his presence at the school evince an actual change of residence thereto, he can change his residence and vote in the seminary's district.

Art. II, §§ 1, 19, 3, Constitution of New York; 

In re Gardiner, 183 A. D. 491; 171 N. Y. S. 327.

The domicile of a married woman, for the purpose of voting and office holding, is established by the same facts and rules of law as that of any other person.

Domestic Relations Law, § 61. 

The right to vote is not a natural right of the citizen; it is a political privilege and emanates from the people of the state. 

O'Brien v. City of Saratoga Springs, 131 Misc. 728; 228 N. Y. S. 82. 

The Legislature

The Legislature of New York State plays a part similar to that of Congress in the Federal Government. It is composed of a Senate and an Assembly. A majority constitutes a quorum in either House. 

A military officer is ineligible to the Legislature. An officer of the 

Federal Reserve Corps is not considered a military officer. 

Any bill may originate in either house of the Legislature. In this respect it differs from the United States Congress, where revenue bills can originate only in the House of Representatives. 

Before a bill can be passed in the Legislature, it must be on the legislators' desks for three days, unless it be a bill of extreme importance in which case the Governor may send a certificate of public necessity and dispense with the three day requirement. 

Art. III, §§ 2, 10, 13, 15, Constitution of New York. 

A bill to become law must receive a majority vote of each branch of the Legislature, with a quorum present, unless the bill is a tax

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

81 measure or an appropriation bill in which latter cases the quorum consists of three-fifths of the members elected to each House, and not a majority as in other cases. Should the bill pass in both Houses, it goes to the Governor for his approval or veto. If he retains the bill for over ten days, Sundays excepted, the bill becomes law unless the Legislature has adjourned within these ten days. In case of such adjournment, the bill cannot become law unless the Governor actually signs the bill within thirty days after the adjournment. 

Art. III, § 25; Art. IV, § 9, Constitution of New York. 

If the Legislature wants to grant public (state) money or transfer property for local or private purposes, the assent of two-thirds of the members elected to each house is necessary. So an appropriation for improving the navigation of the Eoquet River from its mouth to the Falls is for a local purpose, as it promotes the interest of the people of the Falls and does not serve a general public service. So also is a bill to vest state land inside a city, in the city, local. Twothirds assent would be needed to pass such a bill. 

Art. III, § 20, Constitution of New York. 

Franchises to run a street railway, for example, are granted by the Legislature, or by the city so empowered by the Legislature, for the absolute control and ownership of the streets is in the state. 

The city is like a trustee of the streets. 

People v. Kerr, 27 N. Y. 188.

The Legislature cannot confer jurisdiction on the Court of Claims over a claim, if the claim would have been barred because of the Statute of Limitations had the claim been against a private individual instead of against the state.

No private or local bill shall embrace more than one subject, and this subject must be expressed in the title. So a title reading “An 

Act to Incorporate the Economic Power and Construction Co." is an insufficient title to include provisions allowing the corporation to use highways. 

A Legislature has no power to extend the limits of a municipality by adding territory to it, because the Legislature cannot pass any private or local bill (1) incorporating villages. In addition the 

Legislature cannot pass a private or local bill, (2) Changing the names of persons. A corporation is not a person under this rule. (3) Laying out, working or discontinuing highways, or draining swamps. (4) Locating or changing the county seat. (5) Changing the place of voting.

(6) Changing the fees of officers who do not receive fixed salaries. (7) Providing for a change of venue in civil or criminal cases. 

It does not apply to condemnation proceedings. (8) Providing for election of local supervisors. 

(9) Selecting grand or petit jurors. 

(10) Granting to a private corporation or individual any exclusive privilege or franchise or any exemption from taxation. 

Art. III, § 18, Constitution of New York. 

No law shall authorize the construction or operation of a street railroad, without the consent of the owners of one-half in value of the property bounded on the part of the highway through which the railroad is to go. The consent, once given, is irrevocable. In addition to these consents, the local authorities controlling the highway must consent. If the owners will not consent, the Appellate Division of the Supreme Court may, on application, appoint three commissioners, who, after hearings, may determine for the owners. 

The court's confirmation of the report is necessary. 

The Executive 

The executive power is vested in a Governor and Lieutenant- Governor, elected for two years, who must be thirty years of age and New York residents for at least five years next preceding election.

Art. IV, § 1, Constitution of New York. 

The Governor is commander-in-chief of the militia, which consists of all able-bodied male citizens between eighteen and forty-five. The militia officers are appointed by the Governor and their terms expire with that of the Governor.

Art. XI, § 4, Constitution of New York. 

The Governor has the power to call either House for a special session. He can pardon for all crimes except treason and impeachment. In the case of treason, the Governor can suspend execution of the sentence until the Legislature meets and acts on it. 

The Governor can commute a sentence for a crime, with any restriction. The commutation can provide that if the prisoner is later convicted of another felony he is to serve out the term for which he was conditionally pardoned. The reprieve or commutations of sentences may be revoked at any time prior to the actual discharge of the prisoner. The Governor may, therefore, revoke the commutation, even after it has been delivered to the warden of the prison, provided the prisoner had not yet been discharged. 

Art. IV, § 5, Constitution of New York; 

People v. Kaiser, 209 A. D. 722; 205 N. Y. S. 317.

People v. Lawes, 222 A. D. 680; 225 N. Y. S. 53.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

83 In case an appropriation bill has one or more items which are objectionable, the Governor can veto the objectionable items without affecting the rest of the bill.

Art. IV, § 9, Constitution of New York. 

The Lieutenant-Governor is president of the Senate and can vote only in the event of a tie. He becomes Governor on the death or disability of the Governor and acts as Governor during the Governer's absence from the state.

Art. IV, § 6, Constitution of New York. 

The State Judiciary 

The courts of New York State have no jurisdiction of crimes against the United States, unless the act constituting such crime is also a crime against the State of New York, in which case the state court would enforce only the state laws. 

People v. Conti, 127 Misc. 244; 216 N. Y. S. 442. 

The Supreme Court is the court of general law and equity jurisdiction. There are four judicial departments, and in each department an Appellate Division. The Appellate Division justices are Supreme 

Court judges appointed to the Appellate Division by the Governor for five years, or to the end of their term as Supreme Court judges if it is less than five years away. In case of need, additional judges may be appointed temporarily by the Governor. 

The term of a Supreme Court judge is fourteen years. If a vacancy occurs, a member is voted for at the next election, which must be at least three months away for a full fourteen-year term.

Until January 1 next after the election, the Governor appoints a judge with the advice and consent of the Senate, if then in session. 

If it is not in session he can appoint without it. (The same applies to vacancies in the Court of Appeals.)

Art. VI, § 4, Constitution of New York. 

No person can hold office of judge or justice of a court longer than the last day of December next after he shall be 70 years of age. This rule does not apply to the municipal courts. 

In re Moore, 125 Misc. 607; 211 N. Y. S. 655.

Judgments and orders rendered by a judge past the age of 70 are valid and cannot be attacked by private persons. The authority of the judge is open to attack only by the state. 

Sylvia Lake Co. v. Northern Ore Co., 242 N. Y. 144.

The Court of Appeals is the highest court in the state. It has seven judges elected for fourteen years. Five make a quorum and four are necessary to a decision. The Governor may appoint temporary judges if needed.

Art. VI, § 7, Constitution of New York.

Judges of the Supreme Court and Court of Appeals may be removed from office only by a two-thirds vote of both houses of the Legislature. Other judges, except justices of the peace, magistrates and judges of the municipal court of the City of New York, may be removed by two-thirds vote of the Senate alone upon the 

Governor's recommendation.

Art. VI, § 11, Constitution of New York. 

Compensation of Public Officials 

It is a well settled rule that a person accepting a public office, with a fixed salary, is bound to perform the duties of the office for the salary. He cannot legally claim additional compensation for the discharge of these duties, even though the salary may be a very inadequate remuneration for the services. Nor does it alter the case that by subsequent statutes or ordinances his duties within the scope of the charter powers pertaining to the office are increased and not his salary. Whenever he considers the compensation inadequate, he is at liberty to resign.

Salaries and terms of officials cannot be changed by the Legislature to be effective before the end of the term of the official whose salary or term is affected. This applies to the Governor, Lieutenant- 

Governor, Attorney General, Comptroller.

Art. V, § 1, Constitution of New York. 

State Debts 

The state can incur casual debts up to a million dollars, and the money must be used for the purposes for which it was borrowed. 

There is no limit on debts contracted for defense or for repelling invasion.

Art. VII, §§ 2, 3, Constitution of New York. 

Other debts must be repaid in fifty years through a tax provided for that purpose. The payment can also be made in equal installments. The law providing for the debt must be approved by the 

Legislature, and later by the people, at an election held at least three months after the passage of the debt by the Legislature, when no other bill is presented to the people. 

Art. VII, § 4, Constitution of New York. 

Even though the people approve the Legislature's proposition to borrow money, the state need not actually contract the debt. The 

Legislature may change its mind.

The state cannot lend its credit to any individual or corporation.¹ 

Art. VII, § 1, Constitution of New York. 

1As to the right of municipalities to contract debts, see infra, chapter on 

Municipal Corporations.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

85 

Amendments 

Any amendment may be proposed in either House. It must be passed not only by a majority in each House, but by two successive Legislatures. It is then submitted to the voters. If a majority are in favor, it is then a part of the constitution. 

In 1916 and at least every twenty years thereafter, and whenever the Legislature may at other times direct, the electorate decides whether there is to be a constitutional convention. The work of such a convention is submitted to the voters. If accepted, the constitution proposed by them becomes law. 

Art. XIV, Constitution of New York. 

A treaty, if in force, is the supreme law of the land, and supersedes all local laws inconsistent with its terms. 

Techt v. Hughes, 229 N. Y. at p. 240.

Assessments 

Before any assessments can be levied, notice must be given to the property owners. If it is not given, the assessment can be vacated.

Property owners may not be assessed for paving more than the original cost of paving. 

Segfried Const. Co. v. City of New York, 126 Misc. 689; 214 

N. Y. S. 385. 

Suits Against the State

As the State of New York is a sovereign power it cannot be sued without its consent. It may withdraw that consent pending suit, in the absence of statute. Neither the doctrine of estoppel nor that of laches can be made to apply against the state. The state is not governed by the Statute of Limitations. Its property cannot be acquired by adverse possession.

In re Certain Lands in City of New York, 127 Misc. 710. 

By statute, the state will not contest the right to the possession or title of real property where there has been adverse possession for forty years.

Civil Practice Act, § 31. 

Where there is a moral obligation on the part of the state to reimburse one who has suffered loss, an enabling act permitting the Court of Claims to pass upon such claims is constitutional. 

Cuvillier v. State, 250 N. Y. 258.

One state may maintain an action against another state and the state may maintain an action against an individul. 

A private individual cannot, without the consent of the state, interpose a counterclaim against the state in an action brought by the state.

Full Faith and Credit 

A judgment of a sister state may be assailed for want of jurisdiction over the person or the subject matter. As to defenses which might have been asserted at the time the action was brought, the judgment is conclusive.

Oral evidence of a defendant is competent to controvert jurisdiction of the person. It is not, however, competent to challenge the correctness, authenticity or conclusiveness of the judgment for a mistake of law. 

Phillips v. Griffin, 236 A. D. 209; 259 N. Y. S. 105.

OUTLINE OF THE CONSTITUTION OF THE UNITED STATES 

The purpose of the Constitution is stated in its preamble. It is (1) to form a more perfect union, (2) establish justice, (3) insure domestic transquility, (4) provide for the common defense, (5) promote the general welfare, and (6) to secure the blessings of liberty to ourselves and our posterity. 

The original constitution is divided into seven divisions called articles. Article I deals with the legislative power which is lodged in Congress, which in turn consists of (a) a Senate, and (b) a House of Representatives.

Each member of the House of Representatives must be at least 25 years old, 7 years a citizen of this country, and must be living in the state from which he is elected. The House is composed of members chosen every second year by the voters of the respective states. The number from each state is based upon the population of the state. The House chooses its own officers. 

The Senate is composed of two members from each state, elected by popular vote, and hold office for six years. It was originally so arranged that one-third are elected every second year. Each senator must be at least 30 years old, 9 years a citizen of the country, and must reside in the state from which he is elected. 

The presiding officer of the Senate is the Vice-President, who has no vote except to decide a tie. 

The Senate elects its own officers. It alone may try United States officials who are impeached, and at such trial the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court presides. A two-thirds vote of the Senators present is required for conviction, which conviction effects a permanent removal from office, and the offender may be further prosecuted by the courts.

Each branch of Congress decides the qualifications of its own members; punishes its own members for disorderly behavior; expels its own members; keeps its own records. A majority of the members is necessary for the transaction of business, except adjournment, or the compelling the presence of an absentee. Each branch must remain in session while the other branch is in session, except that an adjournment of three days or less is permitted. 

Congress must meet at least once a year, in January.

Members of Congress are paid by the government. They are exempt from arrest while attending their session, or in going to and 87

returning therefrom. This privilege from arrest does not exist in cases of treason, felony or breach of the peace. Members may not be arrested or questioned in any other place for any speech or debate made in either House. No member may accept any government office which was created during his term, or the salary of which was increased during his term; and no person holding any government office may at the same time become a member of either House. 

All bills for raising revenue must originate in the House, though the Senate may propose such bills. 

Every law before taking effect must be approved by both Houses and by the President, except that where the President declines to approve of any measure, it may still become effective if after such disapproval each House by a two-thirds majority again passes that measure. If a law is approved by both branches and sent to the 

President for his approval, and the President does nothing within ten days, Sundays excepted, the law becomes effective as if the 

President had approved. 

Congress has the following powers besides those mentioned: (1) provide for the raising of money by taxation and duties, which provisions must be uniform throughout the country; (2) borrow money on credit; (3) regulate commerce among the states and with foreign nations; (4) establish uniform rules on naturalization; (5) enact bankruptcy laws; (6) coin and issue money and punish for the counterfeiting thereof; (7) fix standards of weights and measures; (8) establish post offices and postal rates; (9) enact copyright and patent laws; (10) establish Federal courts inferior to the United 

States Supreme Court; (11) punish for crimes committed on the sea, and acts which are in violation of international law; (12) declare war, raise and support armies, though such appropriations of money shall be for no longer than a term of two years; (13) provide and maintain a navy; (14) make treaties with foreign nations; (15) govern the district which is the seat of the government, which is now Washington, D. C.; (16) make all laws necessary and proper for carrying into effect the foregoing powers. 

Congress is not permitted the following: (1) to suspend the writ of habeas corpus, except in the case of rebellion or invasion when the public safety is threatened; (2) take away the property of a person condemned to death (bill of attainder); (3) pass a criminal law to govern any acts committed prior to the enactment of the law (ex post facto laws); (4) levy a direct tax except in proportion to the last census taken; (5) tax any article exported; (6) give any preference to any port of any state over that of another, or to compel vessels doing an interstate business to pay duty; (7) grant any title of nobility (an office-holder may not accept any present, office, or title from any foreign land or its head without the consent of Congress).

OUTLINE OF CONSTITUTION OF UNITED STATES 

89 The states are prohibited from (1) making any treaty; (2) granting any letters of marque and reprisal; (3) coining money or issuing paper money on its credit; (4) providing for anything but gold and silver coin as legal tender; (5) passing any bill of attainder, ex post facto law or any law impairing the obligation of contracts; (6) granting any titles of nobility.

Each state is prohibited, unless permitted by Congress, from (1) laying any duty on imports or exports except what may be absolutely necessary for executing its inspection laws, and such collections shall be for the use of the United States Government, and under congressional control; (2) taxing tonnage of vessels; (3) keeping armies or warships during peace times; (4) making treaties with a state or foreign power, or engaging in war unless actually invaded, or in such imminent danger as will not permit of delay.

Article II deals with executive power vested in the President of the United States. He holds office for four years. The method of electing the President, and the Vice-President, is as follows: Each state elects electors, equal to its number of senators and representatives in Congress. These electors may not hold any office under the government. They vote for two persons, one as President, and the other as Vice-President. The person having the highest vote for the presidency becomes the President, provided he has a majority of the whole number of electors appointed. If he fails to have a majority, the House of Representatives being represented by at least two-thirds of the states, selects the President from the three names having the highest number of votes, this selection being by a majority vote; the representatives of each state vote as a unit, and each unit is one If the House fails to choose the President, the Vice-President acts as President. 

vote.

The Vice-President is also chosen by the electors, and the person chosen must have a majority vote. If he fails to obtain a majority of the number of electors, the Senate by majority vote selects the Vice-President from the two having the highest vote. At the time of the voting, two-thirds of the senators must be present. 

The time for choosing electors and the voting dates are chosen by Congress.

The President and the Vice-President must be born in the United States and have been a resident of the country for 14 years, and be 

35 years old.

The Vice-President becomes President if the President is removed from office, dies, resigns or is unable to perform his duties. The salary cannot be changed during the term. 

The President is the Commander-in-Chief of the Army and Navy; he may pardon or grant reprieves for offenses against the United 

States, except in cases of impeachment.

With the advice and consent of the Senate, he may make treaties. 

The necessary assent required is two-thirds of the Senators. With the approval of the Senate, he may nominate and appoint ambassadors, judges of the Supreme Court, cabinet members. 

The Constitution requires the President, from time to time, to give Congress information concerning the condition of the government; to recommend legislation; to call special sessions when necessary; to receive foreign representatives; to fix time for adjournment of Congress if the House disagrees; to execute the laws. The President and Vice-President, as well as other civil officers of our government may be removed upon impeachment, after conviction of treason or bribery, or other high crime or misdemeanor.

Article III deals with the judicial power which is lodged in the Supreme Court and the lower courts established by Congress. The judges thereof hold office for life during good behavior, and their compensation cannot be diminished while in office. 

The judicial power extends to law and equity cases which pertain to the Federal Constitution, the laws of the United States and its treaties, or which affect ambassadors or other public ministers, or which deal with controversies arising on the open seas in which the government is a party, or in controversies involving two states, or one state and the citizens of another state, or between citizens of different states, as well as cases between a state or its citizens and foreign states and its subjects. 

The Constitution requires a trial of all crimes excepting impeachment to be by jury, and to be held in the state where the crime was committed. Where a crime was not committed within any state, the trial is to take place as Congress by law directs. 

Treason is defined as the levying of war against this country, adhering to its enemies, giving them aid and comfort. Before a person can be convicted of such crime, the testimony of at least two witnesses, or the confession in open court by the accused, is necessary.

Article IV enumerates some of the rights of the states and the citizens thereof. Each state is directed to give full faith and credit to the laws, records and judicial proceedings of every other state. 

The citizens of each state are to have all the privileges and rights of the citizens of the other states. Provision is also made for extradition of persons charged with treason, felony or other crimes from one state to another. Each state is guaranteed a republican form of government and protection against invasion or domestic insurrection. 

Article V provides for amendments to the Constitution, which may be made whenever two-thirds of both Houses propose the amendments, or in the alternative, when the legislators of two-thirds of

OUTLINE OF CONSTITUTION OF UNITED STATES 

91 the various states, after a convention called for that purpose, approve of an amendment, provided the proposed amendment is ratified by three-fourths of the states or by conventions in three-fourths thereof. 

No amendment can be made which deprives a state without its consent of equal representation in the Senate. 

Article VI declares the Constitution, the laws of the United States made in pursuance thereof, and all treaties, to be the supreme law of the land. It also requires that no religious test shall ever be required as a qualification to hold any federal office. 

The last division consists of the amendments made to the Constitution subsequent to its ratification.

The first ten amendments, adopted within two years after the original Constitution, make up the Bill of Rights. These amendments are as follows: 

1. That Congress is to establish no religion or prohibit any individual from exercising his religious belief; or abridge the freedom of speech or press; or prevent the people from assembling peaceably; or from petitioning the government for redress for any grievances. 

2. That the right of the people to keep and bear arms shall not be infringed. 

3. That soldiers are not to be quartered in any house without the consent of the owner in times of peace, and in times of war only in accordance with the law. 

4. Unreasonable searches and seizures are prohibited, and provision made for warrants for search and seizure only upon sufficient evidence by sworn facts describing the place and things to be searched or taken. 

5. No civil person is to be held to answer for infamous crime unless upon a presentment or an indictment; no person is to be put in jeopardy for the same offense more than once; nor be compelled to testify against himself in a criminal proceeding; nor to be deprived of life, liberty or property without due process of law; nor his property taken for public use without just compensation. 

6. Speedy and public trials in criminal prosecution by an impartial jury are ordered, and the accused is to be confronted with the witnesses against him, and has the right to compel witnesses to come to court and to have the assistance of counsel. 

7. In lawsuits involving over $20, the right to a jury trial is preserved. 

8. Excessive bail or excessive fine or cruel and unusual punishment are prohibited.

92 9. The rights given under the Constitution are not to limit other rights and privileges the people might have. 

10. The rights not expressly given to the Federal Government nor prohibited to the States are left to the States and the people. 

12. This amendment changed the old method of electing the 

President and Vice-President to conform to the manner described above. 

13. Slavery was abolished within the United States. 

14. All persons born or naturalized in and subject to the jurisdiction of the United States are declared to be citizens of the United States and of the state of their residence; no state is permitted to deprive any person of his life, liberty or property without due process of law; each person within each state is entitled to the same protection of the law; representatives are to be apportioned according to the number of persons in each state, and provision is made for a proportional reduction from the representation of any state which deprives any person entitled to vote from voting. 

15. Gives the right to vote to all citizens regardless of race, color or previous condition of servitude. 

16. The tax on incomes is permitted. 

17. A change is made in the election of Senators so as to conform with the outline above presented. 

18. The manufacture, sale or transfer of intoxicating liquors. within the country or the importation or exporting thereof are prohibited. 

19. The right to vote is extended to females. 

20. The so-called "Lame Duck" Congress was established. The term of Congress begins the first week in January and the term of the President on January twentieth. 

21. (proposed) Repeal of eighteenth amendment.

CONTRACTS Unilateral and Bilateral Contracts 

Contracts are either unilateral or bilateral. If one party promises to do something in return for a specific act of the other party, we have the elements of a unilateral contract. Where one party promises to do something in exchange for a promise, we have the elements of a bilateral contract. The promises, of course, must not be merely formal words, having no probable value. 

Nassau Supply Co. v. Ice Service Co., 252 N. Y. 277. 

The Elements of a Contract 

It may be stated as the general rule that there are three elements necessary for the formation of a contract: (1) an offer; (2) an acceptance of that offer in its exact terms; and (3) mutual considerations. Each of these will be considered separately. 

The Offer 

Offers must be unqualified and definite. Often an invitation to negotiate is mistaken for an offer. "If you should be interested in my lot, would be glad to hear from you, size 20x100, price $1,000," was held to be an invitation to negotiate and not an offer to sell. 

Where a request is made of a merchant for prices, the sending of a catalog of prices is not an offer to sell any quantity at those prices; it is an offer to negotiate and looks for an offer. An auctioneer solicits offers from the bidders. The acceptance takes place when the hammer is knocked down. 

Cerreta v. Costello, 212 A. D. 687; 209 N. Y. S. 257; 

Patrick v. Kleine, 127 Misc. 88; 215 N. Y. S. 305; 

Smith v. City of New York, 10 N. Y. 504.

An offer to pay a "fair share of the profits," or "to convey 70 acres" not further described; to continue in the defendant's employ without stating the term, compensation or services to be rendered, would all be considered too indefinite to allow for an acceptance. 

An offer to purchase "a quantity" of merchandise cannot form the basis of a valid contract, as it is too indefinite, for there is no meeting of the minds as to quantity. Where a promisor retains an unlimited right to decide later the nature or extent of his performance, the promise is too indefinite for legal enforcement. 

Chiapparelli v. Baker, 252 N. Y. 192.

Although an agreement is uncertain and therefore invalid in the first instance because of the indefiniteness of the offer, it may be 93

rendered valid by subsequent acts, facts or by agreement of the parties which clarify the uncertainties. So although an acceptance of a promise to pay "a sum of money" for services to be rendered would not create a valid contract, the giving of notes for a definite sum of money would remove the indefiniteness. An agreement “to make a fair and reasonable settlement for personal injuries sustained by the plaintiff" is not enforceable where the amount to be paid is not set forth; but a promise to pay expenses of the plaintiff's illness is a definite promise. Agreements to sell or buy what would be "needed" or to lend an amount "sufficient to carry on your business" have been held definite enough to be enforceable. 

Habgood v. Van Dyke, 128 Misc. 884; 220 N. Y. S. 454; 

Wood v. Aetna, 224 A. D. 628; 232 N. Y. S. 53; 

King v. Krischer, 220 A. D. 584; 222 N. Y. S. 66; 

Stern v. Premier Shirt Co., 260 Ñ. Y. 201.

A promise that was originally too indefinite may by performance become definite, and as the other party to the bargain must be regarded as continuously assenting to receive such performance in return for his own promise, a valid unilateral contract arises on receipt of such performance. 

Grundt v. Shenk, 222 A. D. 82; affd. 248 N. Y. 602.

If goods are obtained under an agreement for their hire or sale and no price is fixed, it is presumed that a reasonable price is intended. A "reasonable" price is a definite price for the purposes of a contract, as the jury can fix that definite price. 

A promise or offer to buy or sell all the cans needed in a canning factory; all the coal needed for a foundry; all the iron refined during a certain period, is definite as to quantity, as the quantity can be determined 

Schlegel Mfg. Co. v. Cooper's Glue, 231 N. Y. 459.

The Acceptance 

An acceptance to be effective must be an acceptance of the offer in its exact terms. If the offer looks toward a bilateral contract, i.e., the offer requests a promise from the other side, the acceptance in such cases must consist of a binding promise. Commencement of the work which was to be promised is in itself not sufficient; the promise was what was asked, and that is what must be given. 

White v. Corlies, 46 N. Y. 467. 

Conversely, if the offer requests an act, i.e., unilateral contract, the promise to perform the act is not an acceptance of the offer; an act was asked for, and only that act in accordance with the offer can constitute an acceptance.

If the offeror desires that the offeree be bound because of some act of the offeree, by every part of the offer, the offer must be

CONTRACTS 

95 brought home to the notice of the offeree. An offer to reward a person who finds and returns a lost article is accepted when the article is returned by the finder who did so knowing of the offer of reward. 

Pierson v. Morch, 82 N. Y. 503; 

Fitch v. Snedaker, 38 N. Y. 248.

One who receives a ticket, on paying a consideration, for having his package taken care of for a time, or his car watched in an enclosure during a ball game, is not bound by a statement on the back of the ticket that the watcher is not to be liable for damage to the baggage or car, unless this notice is brought to the attention of the owner, so as to be made part of the contract. 

In some cases, however, although the "offeree" does not note or read the undertaking, he is considered as having accepted it. These cases are such where by usage and custom or by the actions of the party, he is considered as having "accepted." Thus, a person who buys a steamship ticket with conditions of the company's liability for damage to a passenger's baggage stamped on it, is a passenger subject to those conditions, even though he never reads them. Every part of a steamship ticket is a part of the contract. The fact that the purchaser did not read the ticket is immaterial. Curiously enough, this rule has not been applied to railroad tickets for trips of intrastate commerce; only if the conditions printed on the ticket are brought home to the purchaser, or are so printed as to be very conspicuous, is the purchaser held to be subject to the printed conditions. 

Where interstate commerce is involved, the ticket constitutes part of the contract, even though printed in fine print. 

Ellsworth v. Del. L. & W. R., 134 Misc. 680; 236 N. Y. S. 289; 

Madan v. Sherard, 73 N. Y. 329; 

Murray v. Cunard S. S. Co., 235 N. Y. 162; 

Hutchins v. Penn. R. R., 181 N. Y. 186. 

Ordinarily, one who signs an instrument is conclusively bound thereby. If he could read the instrument, not to have read it was gross negligence. If he could not read it, not to procure it to be read was equally negligent. In either case the writing binds him. 

If the signer is illiterate or blind, and the contents are misread or misrepresented to him by the other party or even a stranger, the writing is void, unless the signer be negligent. 

Pimpinello v. Swift & Co., 253 N. Y. 159.

Before an acceptance by a promise is binding upon the offeror, the acceptance must be received by the offeror. 

White v. Corlies, 46 N. Y. 467.

If the offer is made through the mails or sent by telegram, it is implied that there can be an acceptance in the same manner, and the moment the offeree puts his acceptance in the mail box, or sends a

telegram of acceptance, the contract is complete. This mail box may be that of the Post Office, or it may be the private mail box of the offeror. But if A sends an offer by telegram and B accepts by mail, or vice versa, it seems that the acceptance must be received in order to complete the contract, for the same method was not used in accepting as was used in offering. 

Webster v. Casein Co. of America, 206 N. Y. 506. 

The party may make it a condition that the proposed contract shall not be obligatory unless the acceptance is received in a specified time. This condition governs even though the offer and acceptance were both sent by mail. 

Where the offeror requires an acceptance "by return mail," he does not necessarily mean the mail immediately following the receipt of the offer, if the offeree lives in a city where there are several mails every day.

Even though both parties suppose that a binding contract exists between them, it is not conclusive, unless in fact such an agreement had actually been made. 

Machinery Utilities Co. v. Fry, 224 A. D. 392; 231 N. Y. S. 148. 

Rejection of the Offer

A proposal on the part of the offeree to accept the offer with a stated slight change or an intended acceptance subject to some condition is equivalent to a rejection of the offer. Upon such rejection. the parties are in the same position as if an offer were never made. 

Glens v. Ryerson, 195 A. D. 769; 162 N. Y. S. 427. 

Revocation of the Offer

An offer remains open for the time stated in the offer, or if no specified time is stated, for a period which is reasonable considering the subject-matter of the offer or the surrounding circumstances, or until the offer has been revoked by the offeror. Before the offer can be said to have been revoked by the offeror, the revocation must be received by the offeree. If a valid acceptance is mailed in acceptance of a mailed offer, the acceptance dates from the time it is mailed. 

A revocation dates from the time it is received. It thus follows that if the acceptance is mailed at 11 a.m. and received at 5 p.m., and the revocation mailed at 8 a.m. and received at 3 p.m., a valid contract exists, since the mailing of the acceptance occurred before the receipt of the revocation. 

Stein-Gray Drug Co. v. Michelson, 116 N. Y. S. 789.

Where the offer is made publicly through the newspapers, it may be revoked through the newspapers by giving the revocation similar publicity; and even though the one who read the offer has not read the revocation, the offer no longer exists.

CONTRACTS 

97 

Death or insanity of the offeror automatically revokes the offer if either occurs before valid acceptance. 

Mactier v. Frith, 6 Wend. 103.

Even though the offer states that it will remain open for a given length of time, the offeror, if he has received no consideration for his promise to hold it open, may withdraw the offer before the stated time has elapsed and thus render any subsequent acceptance ineffectual because there is no longer an offer to be accepted. If the offer is accepted before the withdrawal or its termination, it ripens into a binding contract. 

Eckstein v. Fur Co., 131 Misc. 167; 225 N. Y. S. 400.

An election once made by the optionee is binding and may not thereafter be rescinded even though the election is made before the time set for the termination of the option. It does not matter how long the option is to continue; once an election has been made the option ceases to exist. 

Galbraith v. Buffalo, 128 Misc. 79; 217 N. Y. S. 737.

If the offer is one to enter into a unilateral contract, it can be withdrawn at any time before the act requested has been performed. 

The offer may be withdrawn even though the offeree has begun to perform or is seen approaching with the intention of proffering performance, provided the withdrawal is made before actual performance. In one case a mortgagee agreed to discount $780 from the amount due on the mortgage maturing 5 years hence if the mortgagor would pay the unpaid balance before a stated date. On that day the mortgagor knocked at the door of the mortgagee and said, "I have come to pay off the mortgage." The mortgagee then said that he had changed his mind. It was held that there had been revocation before performance. 

Peterson v. Pattberg, 248 N. Y. 86. Consideration

Consideration, the third necessary element, is the doing or promising to do what one is not legally obligated to do, or the refraining from doing what one may legally do, for a promise or act which is the motive for the promise, the doing or refraining. The detriment to the promisee is not consideration merely because the promise induced the detriment. The promise and the consideration must purport to be the motive for each other. Thus, if A promises B the sum of $10,000, and in reliance upon that promise B purchases a home intending to use that money to pay for it, B cannot enforce A's promise merely because he has suffered a detriment. The promise and the detriment were not the motive each for the other.

Likewise, a voluntary payment by P of D's debt to X, does not require a payment by D. 

Olmstead v. Olmstead, 138 Misc. 839; 247 N. Y. S. 350. 

But where M says to his nephew, "If you don't smoke for three years, I'll give you $1,000," and the latter so refrains, he can recover upon the promise though it were possible to show that it benefited him not to smoke, and even if M could show that it was not worth $1,000 to him to have the promisee refrain. 

Hamer v. Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538; 

Alleghany College v. Bank, 246 N. Y. 369.

An agreement to withhold suit is a good consideration to support a promise to pay a debt, even though no set period is agreed upon. 

If no fixed time is set, the time must be reasonable. If the person who so agrees to withhold suit honestly and reasonably believed his claim to be at least doubtful, forbearance to sue is good consideration. 

Strong v. Sheffield, 144 N. Y. 392; 

Post v. Thomas, 212 N. Y. 264.

A promise to extend the time of payment of a debt is void, unless founded upon a good consideration; and a payment of a part of the debt or the interest already accrued, or, an agreement to pay interest for the future, is not a sufficient consideration for such a promise. 

The debtor would have been bound to pay interest for the period during which he withheld payment even if he had not promised to pay it. 

Parmelee v. Thompson, 45 N. Y. 58; 

Repelow v. Walsh, 98 A. D. 320; 90 N. Y. S. 651.

Past consideration is not consideration recognized in contract law. 

If A paints B's house without having been requested to do so, B is under no obligation to pay and a later promise by B to pay is unenforceable, since the consideration for B's promise was past, and this is not recognized as good consideration. The promise was not the motive for the detriment.

There are, however, some instances where past consideration would be a valid consideration. In all these instances the background is moral consideration, i.e., it seems just that a claim should be satisfied. But the elements of a good contract must have existed, though some defense created by law bars recovery on the contract. 

The additional promise for past consideration removes the bar which existed prior to the additional promise. These instances are: (1) The Statute of Limitations has already run to completion. 

A promise then made can be enforced if made in writing; or, in case of a debt, if part payment of principal or interest was made. The promise (or the payment) starts the statute running again.

Civil Practice Act, § 59.

CONTRACTS 

99 To make payments effective against a party to save a claim from the statute of limitations, they must have been made by him or for him by his authorized agent. 

Bklyn. Savings Bank v. Wechsler, 259 N. Y. 9. (2) A written promise by one discharged in bankruptcy to pay a debt which has been wiped out by the discharge will permit the enforcement of the promise to pay the discharged debt. Partial payment by itself is not sufficient to waive the debt. 

In re Clodgo, 131 Misc. 490; 227 N. Y. S. 690;

Personal Property Law, § 31. 

There is dictum to the effect that the written promise is effective even if made after the petition is filed and before the discharge. 

Meyer v. Price, 250 N. Y. 370.

The action in either of the above two instances may be maintained upon the new promise or upon the original cause of action. 

Herrington v. Davit, 220 N. Y. 162. (3) A promise by an adult to perform a contract made during his infancy is enforceable and infancy cannot be set up as a defense. 

This was also the rule at common law. (4) An endorser discharged because he received no due notice of dishonor waives the discharge by a clear promise to pay. The promise need not be in writing. 

Ross v. Hurd, 71 N. Y. 14. (5) Where a contract is unenforceable because it is oral, and therefore within the Statute of Frauds, it is enforceable when it is later put into writing.

If A plans to default in his contract with B, and C then promises A an additional sum if he does not break his contract, C's promise cannot be enforced. A has given up no legal right he cannot collect from C, for being already bound to carry out his contract with B, A, by promising not to do so, has not given any consideration for the additional promise of C. 

Vanderbilt v. Schreyer, 91 N. Y. 392.

The court in two sets of circumstances has held that A could collect from C, the second promisor. (a) In the first instance C was financially interested in the completion of A's performance. In this case the defendant contracted for the building of his house. The contractor sub-contracted the building of the foundation to the plaintiff. During the progress of the work the plaintiff told the defendant that he intended to abandon his contract as he felt the contractor was not financially responsible. 

The defendant thereupon promised to pay as he finished the work.

On default of payment by the contractor, plaintiff sued the defendant and recovered. 

Manetti v. Doege, 48 A. D. 567; 62 N. Y. S. 918. (b) In the second instance a marriage contract was involved. 

A was engaged to marry the daughter of C, and the latter promised A a certain annuity if he would carry out his contract. The court held that in such cases the other party to A's contract (the daughter in this case) was linked with A, and C's promise to A was to be read as follows: "If you and my daughter will not rescind nor modify your contract to marry each other, I will pay you an annuity of $10,000." Undoubtedly, if C promises A and B jointly to give them $10,000 to induce both not to rescind or modify their contract, C's promise can be enforced, for a legal right has been given up by 

A and B. 

De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 431.

A seal on an executory instrument is only presumptive evidence of consideration. It has the effect of placing the burden upon the defendant to prove that there was no consideration given. If the instrument contains no seal, the plaintiff must prove that consideration was given. A seal on a delivered release or executed contract is conclusive evidence of consideration. 

Stiebel v. Grossberg, 202 N. Y. 266;

Civil Practice Act, § 342. 

In order that a document be considered a sealed instrument there must be present something in the nature of a seal, some mark to indicate a seal affixed to the signatures. A mere intention to affix a seal, expressed or implied, does not make the paper a sealed document. A recital "Signed, sealed and delivered, etc.," does not make a sealed instrument, if, as a fact, nothing is affixed to the signatures which the law recognizes as a seal, e.g., a wafer, wax, the word "seal" or "L.S." opposite the signature. The fact that "L.S." does appear after the signature of a party to an instrument not required by law to be under seal is not alone sufficient to constitute the instrument subscribed a sealed instrument. Such a seal does not raise a presumption that the document is a sealed instrument unless there be a recognition of it in the body of the instrument by some such phrase as "witness my hand and seal," or "signed and sealed." In the absence of showing of an intention to have a sealed instrument, the character of the document becomes a question of fact. 

Drexler v. Paris, 259 N. Y. S. 601; 

Empire Trust Co. v. Heinze, 242 N. Y. 475.

The fact that a notarial seal and authentication is considered the equivalent of the seal of a party in the state where the seal was

CONTRACTS 

101 executed is immaterial. Such instrument is not a sealed instrument in this state. 

Kirsch v. Lubin, 131 Misc. 700; 228 N. Y. S. 94.

It is not necessary that every party to the instrument places his own seal upon it. One party may adopt the seal of another, and a single seal may suffice for all.

Bklyn. Pub. Library v. City of New York, 222 A. D. 422; affd. 

250 N. Y. 495.

A promise given for an illegal consideration is unenforceable. 

Therefore a promise in consideration of future illicit cohabitation or prostitution is void. 

Angresani v. Tozzi, 216 N. Y. S. 161; affd. 245 N. Y. 558. 

Mutuality Rule

When parties effect negotiations looking toward the creation of a bilateral contract, there must be mutuality of obligation to effect a binding contract. A promise by one party to perform cannot be enforced by the other party if the latter is left free to perform or not. If A writes B, "As long as your purchases amount to not less than $10,000, I will give you exclusive agency in New York",

B cannot enforce A's promise for the exclusive agency if A desires to terminate it, as there is nothing in the agreement compelling B to purchase anything. There was no mutuality of obligation. 

Smith v. Diem, 223 A. D. 572, affd. 249 N. Y. 590.
