Where the plaintiff, a jobber, entered into an agreement with the defendant by which the latter agreed to furnish plaintiff's requirements for resale during one year at a fixed price and there was no promise by the plaintiff to take any definite quantity, the court held the contract unenforceable for lack of mutuality. 

Schlegel Mfg. Co. v. Cooper's Glue, 231 N. Y. 459.

Even where a promise is made to purchase all the ice needed by the promisor, it would not create mutuality, if the promisor at the time of the promise was not in the ice business and had no requirements for ice. The promise to buy means nothing under such circumstances. If the promisor at the time were engaged in a going business requiring ice, a promise to take all the ice needed would be a binding promise and would allow for mutuality. 

Nassau Supply Co. v. Ice Service Co., 252 N. Y. 277. 

In one case the plaintiff agreed to sell certain black bread manufactured by the defendant, and no other, and the defendant agreed to furnish all the black bread needed by the plaintiff. The court held the contract enforceable, as there were mutual promises and obligations on the part of both parties-one to furnish all the black bread needed, and the other to sell no other kind of black bread and take all he would need from the other contracting party. 

Ehrenworth v. Stuhmer, 229 N. Y. 210.

A contract wherein a corporation agrees to buy back its stock from one who purchases or owns it is unenforceable because a corporation is permitted under the statute to buy its stock out of surplus only. Since it is problematical whether the corporation will have a surplus at the time of performance, there is the possibility that the corporation is making a promise to do what the law may not permit it to do a promise which may not be binding and for breach of which it could not be held liable. 

Topken, Loring & Co. v. Schwartz, 249 N. Y. 206.

In this connection it has been held that if the contract of repurchase was only part of an otherwise valid contract, e.g., a contract of employment, there would be mutuality. The contract then would be good unless it could be shown as a defense that the stock would have to be bought out of capital, there being no surplus. 

Richards v. Wiener, 207 N. Y. 59.

Subscription to Charities 

Where D promises to contribute a sum to a named charitable institution it would seem that there is lacking the element of consideration. The courts, however, have leaned toward enforcing these promises and have invoked the theory of promissory estoppel in the cases of subscriptions to charitable institutions. If the beneficiary, in the reasonable belief that the promise would be kept, incurred expense, the promisor is held to his promise. 

Barnes v. Perine, 12 N. Y. 18.

Where the court can spell out consideration in an implied promise on the part of the promisee, it will do so. Thus, where A promises a sum of money and requests that the fund bear his name or the name of some person, and the donee accepts a part of the gift or acts in conformity with the request, there is a direct detriment to the promisee, declared by the court to be the motive for the promise, and a bilateral contract exists without resort to the doctrine of promissory estoppel. 

Alleghany College v. Bank, 246 N. Y. 369; 

Keuka College v. Ray, 167 N. Y. 96.

Contracts of Employment 

A hiring is at will unless a definite period of service is specified. 

The master may discharge, and the servant leave, at any time. 

A hiring at so much per day, week, month or year, is an indefinite hiring, and no presumption arises that it was even for a day, but only at the rate fixed for whatever time the party may serve. 

Watson v. Gugino, 204 N. Y. 535.

If the servant seeks to make the hiring one of a definite term, the burden is upon him to establish it by proof.

CONTRACTS 

103 In an action to recover a salary for the remainder of a term of employment after the plaintiff's discharge, the jury must pass upon (a) whether the plaintiff made diligent efforts to secure other employment of a similar character, and (b) if he secured other employment, what the compensation was, and (c) whether the compensation was as much as he could have earned in good faith during the unexpired part of the contract. 

Schwed v. Kennedy, 220 A. D. 189; 221 N. Y. S. 179.

An employee, in the absence of an explicit promise or other evidence showing a contrary intent, is not entitled to recover additional compensation for extra services performed by him, unless they were clearly without the sphere of the work for which he was hired. 

Hett v. Barty Axle Corp., 241 N. Y. S. 609.

The Statute of Frauds¹-Contract for Services 

A contract for services which will be completed more than one year from the first day after making the contract, must be evidenced by a note or memorandum in writing, subscribed by the party to be charged or his lawful agent, otherwise it is voidable as a contract. 

Nothing short of full performance by both parties will take the contract outside of the operation of the Statute of Frauds. Even part performance will not bar the defense of Statute of Frauds. The part performer can recover only in quantum meruit. 

Personal Property Law, § 31; 

Sophie v. Ford, 230 A. D. 568; 245 N. Y. S. 470; 

Kelley v. Champlain Studios, 223 A. D. 388; 228 Ń. Y. S. 500.

In this respect it differs from a contract for the sale of merchandise of $50 or more. Then part delivery dispenses with the need of a written memorandum.

David Taylor Co. v. Fansteel Products, 255 N. Y. S. 270. 

If the contract is performable within a year, it is without the Statute of Frauds and need not be in writing. The courts have shown a marked tendency to consider a situation to be without the statute wherever possible. If the agreement may consistently with its terms be entirely performed within a year, it is not within the condemnation of the statute although it probably will not be performed within that time. So, an oral agreement to give a boy a sum in advance, sufficient to pay for two years of schooling, provided he would quit his job and complete the course, is binding because there is a possibility that the boy might take less than a year to complete the two-year course. In the same line of reasoning, an oral contract to build a house within 15 months; to account to the plaintiff for all 

1For a discussion of the Statute of Frauds as applicable to Sales of Personal

Property, see infra subject of "Sales." For its applicability to contracts for the 

Furchase of realty see infra subject of "Real Property."

proceeds from the sale of realty, provided the defendant should not be required to pay any sum due before a year from date; to pay upon the death of a third person; upon the determination of a suit; on the marriage of a third person; upon the restoration of health; to perform certain duties for the rest of one's life, were all held to be outside the statute as the contingency might occur within the year. 

Personal Property Law, § 31; 

Gallagher v. Finch, 211 A. D. 635; 207 N. Y. S. 403; 

Sherrill v. Bull, 129 Misc. 623; 222 N. Y. S. 1; 

Hempel v. Hecksher, N. Y. Law Journal, July 14, 1927.

An oral contract to make a written contract is invalid where an oral contract embodying the same terms as the intended written contract would be invalid because of the Statute of Frauds. 

Deutsch v. Textile, 212 A. D. 681; 209 N. Y. S. 388.

If we have an express contract of employment for a year, and the employer permits the employee to continue working after the beginning of the ensuing year, the law implies, as a proper inference of fact, that the employment is to continue for another year. This implication of fact arises, of course, after the beginning of a new year. Let us, however, suppose that during the first year of employment the parties discuss their relationship for the coming year and new terms are orally agreed upon to begin at the termination of the year's employment. Is this contract within the Statute of Frauds?

The court held in the affirmative, as the oral contract could not by its terms be terminated within a year from the making thereof. 

Lonsdale v. Migel, 222 A. D. 197; 225 N. Y. S. 593.

Where a verbal contract has been made which by its terms cannot be performed within a year, the circumstance that it is restated or confirmed within the year does not remove it from the operation of the statute. 

McGovern v. Madam Elise, 244 N. Y. S. 169.

Every agreement made in consideration of marriage must be in writing except mutual promises to marry.

Agreement to Settle-Accord and Satisfaction 

If A is indebted to B in a fixed sum which is undisputed, an agreement on the part of B to accept a lesser sum in full settlement is not binding on B if there was no consideration for his promise to take less, for when A promised to give less he did not promise to do anything he was not already bound to do. Even if a receipt of full payment were given for the lesser amount, B could still show that he did not get the full undisputed amount, unless the receipt were under seal, in which case B would be precluded under a technical

CONTRACTS 

105 rule of procedure from showing that there was no consideration, or the donee could prove a gift of the unpaid amount. 

Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266; 

Jaffray v. Davis, 124 N. Y. 164; 

Sexton v. Equitable, 130 Misc. 362; 224 N. Y. S. 63.

When the amount involved is unliquidated, a lesser sum paid may settle the claim. There are two forms of accord and satisfaction, (1) One is where there is a true assent to the acceptance of a payment in compromise of an honest dispute, or in extinguishment of a liability uncertain in amount. (2) The other is where the tender of payment is coupled with a condition making the use of the tendered money wrongful if the condition is ignored: 

Byrne v. Padden, 248 N. Y. 243, 249; 

Schuttinger v. Woodruff, 259 N. Y. 212.

If a creditor after an honest dispute retains for an unreasonable length of time or deposits a check marked "in full settlement," or so retains or deposits a check not so marked when the buyer says it is to be in full settlement of a disputed claim without any objection, it is a full accord and satisfaction. If the check was marked "in full settlement," the fact that the seller indorsed it "Received this check on account" does not change the situation; it is a full accord and satisfaction, and therefore binding. 

Kartingener v. Cohn, 129 Misc. 910; 223 N. Y. S. 465; 

Schnell v. Perlman, 238 N. Y. 362; 

Boosin v. Bird, 119 Misc. 701; 197 N. Y. S. 254.

Where A acting as P's agent for the sale of merchandise collects a sum of money, deducts 10% therefrom as his commission and returns the balance by check in full settlement, the acceptance of this check does not bar recovery by P of the sum deducted on the ground that there was nothing due to A, for there was no dispute at the time the check was sent; what was sent was due to P in any event; and finally the law will not suffer an agent to withhold moneys collected for a principal's account by the pressure of a threat that no part of the moneys will be remitted to the owner without the approval of deductions beneficial to the agent. Such conduct is a flagrant abuse of the opportunities and powers of a fiduciary position. 

Hudson v. Yonkers Fruit, 258 N. Y. 168. 

Suppose J owes K $1,000. his horse as full payment.

They agree that J is to give $800 and 

K tries to sell the horse, but can obtain no more than $100 for it. He then sues for the other $100. He cannot recover. Any consideration (the horse in this case) except "money for less money" is good and valid in law.

Novation 

To constitute a novation there must be an extinguishment of the original obligation and an executed mutual agreement among the parties to both the old and the new obligation whereby a new party is substituted for the old. The theory is that the new debtor contracts with the old debtor that he will pay the debt, and the new debtor also so contracts with the creditor. There is therefore a complete novation where P accepts the promissory notes of S for his (P's) entire claim against D, all three parties consenting. 

Henderson v. Sheppard, 248 N. Y. S. 89.

An extension of this idea is carried into a case where A has a disputed claim against B. They agree upon a settlement of $1,000. 

B fails to pay that sum. A may now sue B for $1,000 upon this substituted or new agreement. A cannot sue upon the original claim any more, as a new contract has come into being. This is not, however, a novation, as the original parties are involved in the transaction without any substituted party. 

Moers v. Moers, 229 N. Y. 294; 

Bandman v. Finn, 185 N. Y. 508.

If it had been A's intention to accept that sum in settlement, but that the case was not to be considered settled until the payment was actually made, it is submitted that no substituted contract had ever been made, and A should be allowed to sue on his original claim. 

Katz v. Bernstein, 260 N. Y. S. 13.

A creditor when advised, after he has entered into a contract with two or more, that one of them has retired from the contract and the others have assumed the debt, may (1) insist upon the continued liability of the retiring debtor, or (2) by continuing so to deal with the new debtors as to evidence his intention, he may enter into a new contract to accept them as solely liable. If he does the latter, he cannot hold the retiring party on the original contract. The assent of the creditor to the substitution must be established but the consent to enter into the new contract may be implied by conduct. Thus where the creditor of a partnership after dissolution thereof, knowing that one or several of the partners have agreed with the others to assume and pay the debts of the firm, takes the negotiable notes of those who should pay, in payment of the debts of the firm, he thereby cancels the claim against the firm and discharges the other partners. 

Schloss Bros. v. Bennett, 260 N. Y. 243.

Contracts of Infants 

The contracts of an infant, i.e., a person under 21 years of age, are valid but are voidable at the will of the infant. The fact that an infant has intentionally and fraudulently misstated his age and thus

CONTRACTS 

107 induced X to contract with him, does not estop him from setting up his infancy as a defense, for the courts will not permit an adult to enforce a contract by converting a cause of action in contract into one in tort. 

International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 188.

The disaffirmance must take place either before his attaining majority or within a reasonable time after attaining his majority. 

After disaffirmance, the infant is not entitled to be put in a position superior to such a one as he would have occupied if he had never entered into his voidable agreement. He is not entitled to retain an advantage from a transaction which he repudiates. If he has received a benefit, he must return it or its value. 

Joseph v. Schatzkin, 259 N. Y. 241. 

Casey v. Kastel, 237 N. Y. 305; 

Delano v. Blake, 11 Wend. 85.

The infant may recover the consideration with which he parted, if he makes a demand before bringing the action and tenders back before suit whatever he has received which is still in his possession or control. If the infant has squandered what he received, the adult is not aided in his defense and has no remedy. The infant may avoid the contract. 

Nathan v. Karp, 214 A. D. 490; 212 N. Y. S. 459.

This rule apparently does not apply where there has been an executed contract for the sale of personalty. In such case, the infant may recover the consideration paid by him less the value to him of the use of the property and the amount of depreciation. If he cannot return what he obtained, he cannot recover his consideration. So if an infant buys an article for cash and then desires to avoid the contract and gets back his money, he cannot do so unless he returns the article and accounts for the deterioration in value. But if realty had been sold by the infant, and the purchase money had been squandered, the infant can avoid the contract and obtain his realty, provided he avoids the contract within the statutory period of limitations (ten years), which commences to run on his attaining twenty- Mere delay in avoiding the sale is no ratification until the statutory period has passed. one. 

O'Donahue v. Smith, 130 A. D. 214; 114 N. Y. S. 536; 

Rice v. Butler, 160 N. Y. 578.

An infant can affirm his contract for the sale of realty after majority. His affirmance must consist of an actual act of confirmation with the intention to confirm. Mere passive acquiescence, even for a length of time but short of the period of limitations, is not sufficient. Where, however, an infant who had purchased realty continued in possession,

control, occupancy and management for a year after majority, the court held it to be an affirmance of the purchase. 

Whitman v. Home Guardian Co., 135 Misc. 598; 238 N. Y. S. 301.

A payment by an infant of part of a debt is not, as a matter of law, ratification of the infant's contract. 

International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 188.

An infant is liable for necessaries purchased by him during his infancy. What constitutes "necessaries" depends upon the station in life of the infant. Usually they include food, lodging, medical attendance, and common education. The infant is liable in quasicontract and so only for the reasonable value of the necessaries, if the plaintiff can show that the parent or guardian has not the means to pay or will not discharge his duty in furnishing the necessaries. 

Stetson v. Russell, 130 Misc. 713; 225 N. Y. S. 139.

The defense of infancy is sufficiently set up when the defendant alleges that he was an infant and that the articles purchased were not necessaries. 

Morgan v. Smith, 130 Misc. 412; 224 N. Y. S. 400.

An infant of eighteen can contract to marry. The marriage once made is valid. But if the infant refuses to go through with the marriage after the promise, he or she cannot be sued for breach of contract. Until the marriage the contract is voidable by the infant. 

Contracts of the Insane 

One already adjudged insane cannot make a valid contract. If he was not yet adjudged insane and the contract was made in a lucid interval, it is valid. If, however, the contract was made during an insane period and the insanity was unknown to the other party, the contract is voidable by the insane person, but what the insane person received he must return before the contract will be declared void. 

This is based on the theory of unjust enrichment. Mistake 

McCarthy v. Bowling, 182 A. D. 18; 169 N. Y. S. 463.

This section and the two following relate to the formation and not to the interpretation of a contract. 

Where parties deal face to face and the seller intends to transfer title to a person of good financial standing, a transfer of title to the person in front of him who falsely represents himself to be some other person, is a voidable transfer. The purchaser can pass good title by selling to a bona fide purchaser for value. 

If Jones, claiming to be Y, had personally obtained the goods, not upon credit, but by giving a forged check naming Y the maker,

CONTRACTS 

109 as the consideration, Jones would have been guilty of larceny. Jones would have obtained no title at all, and he could therefore pass no title even to a bona fide purchaser for value without notice. 

Amola v. Bernstein, 214 A. D. 469; 212 N. Y. S. 518.

If a vendor and vendee negotiate by letter, and the vendee, who is A, signs as B, there is no contract and no transfer of title, even though the goods are shipped. Nor would there be a contract if A had come in person and falsely said he was B's agent. In these cases, the person with whom the vendor thought he was dealing was not actually before him. A sale by A to a bona fide purchaser would pass no title. 

Phelps v. McQuade, 220 N. Y. 232; 

Brotchener v. Ullman, 141 Misc. 102; 252 N. Y. S. 244.

When the subject-matter is non-existent, unknown to both parties, there is no contract. So if T and S buy and sell a ship, which unknown to both had sunk the day before, no rights are conferred on either party. 

Duncan v. N. Y. M. Ins. Co., 138 N. Y. 88.

When there is a mutual mistake of identity, or quantity, or price, there is no contract. So if T and S buy and sell cotton from a ship called the "Peerless" and there are two such ships, and T means one, and S means the other, there is no contract. 

Misrepresentation and Fraud¹ "Misrepresentation" as used here refers to a quality and incident which do not involve the substance of the agreement. It is a false statement made by one who does not know of the falsity, but believes what he says to be true. If the representation is a component part of the contract, and is the essence of the agreement, it is a condition which justifies a rescission. But if the representation, though a component part of the agreement, is not of the essence, it is a warranty, and so justifies an action for damages and not rescission. 

Fraud to be actionable must be (1) Material to the inducement that brought about the contract; (2) About a fact and not an opinion; (3) With knowledge of the falsity of the fact, or in reckless disregard of its truth; (4) The statement must have been made with the intention that it be acted upon; and (5) It must have been acted upon so that damage resulted. 

Owens v. Waterhouse, 233 N. Y. S. 535. 

1For the remedies available to a party claiming to have been defrauded, see heading "Rescission of Contracts" in the subject "Equity," p. 180.

If X, while insolvent, obtains goods from P on credit, the fact that X omitted to mention his insolvency to P will not be sufficient to avoid the sale. If, however, there is an intent never to pay at the time the goods are purchased, title does not pass to the purchaser. 

Hotchin v. Third Natl. Bank, 127 N. Y. 329.

Duress 

"Duress" is a threat of violence by a stranger against, or imprisonment of, the person contracting or his near relative. It renders the contract voidable at the instance of the victim of the duress. 

O. P. R. R. Co. v. Forrest, 128 N. Y. 83.

A threat to take legal proceedings does not constitute duress. 

Crossways Apts. v. Amante, 213 A. D. 430; 210 N. Y. S. 346.

Contracts for the Benefit of a Third Party 

The tendency has been to allow P as beneficiary of a contract between A and B to recover although P has furnished no consideration for the promisor's promise. At the present time the law on the point can be summarized by saying that the beneficiary P can sue on a contract made for his benefit if one of these conditions exists: (1) If there was a pecuniary legal obligation running from the promisee to the beneficiary at the time the promise was made by the defendant for the benefit of the beneficiary. The fact that A, the promisee, owed an obligation to P, the beneficiary, links P up with A and makes P a privy to the consideration, given by A to B. 

Lawrence v. Fox, 20 N. Y. 268.

Suppose a broker gets a customer, X, for a tenant, to take over the latter's lease. The tenant is unable to get the landlord's consent to accept X and the deal falls through. Finally the landlord agrees to give a new lease to X, directly, and promises X to pay the broker's commission. The broker cannot recover from the landlord because there was no debt owing to the broker from tenant promisee, X. 

Freed v. Tishman, 119 Misc. 721; 197 N. Y. S. 259; 

Vroomon v. Turner, 69 N. Y. 280. (2) If the beneficiary P, at the time the promise sued upon is made to the promisee, stands in such esteem and affection in the eyes of the promisee as a child or parent would stand. The actual relationship between the beneficiary and promisee would not seem to be material, just so long as the attitude of one toward the other would permit the conclusion that a moral obligation to benefit the beneficiary should exist. A beloved niece, a wife, and an illegitimate child were permitted to recover as beneficiaries. This natural or moral duty takes the place of the legal pecuniary obligation. 

Seaver v. Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233; 

Buchanan v. Tilden, 158 N. Y. 109; 

Todd v. Weber, 95 N. Y. 181.

CONTRACTS 

111 (3) If the contract is a "public contract," as where a municipality seeks to protect its residents by contracts for their benefit. Two tests are applied by which the right to maintain the action is determined: (a) whether or not the contract was intended for the benefit of the plaintiff; (b) whether or not there was some obligation or duty owing from the city to the third party. 

In one case a Water Company agreed for consideration to furnish to the residents of the other party to the contract, the Village of Pelham Manor, a water supply at a certain rate. When the Company proposed to raise the rate, a resident of the Village, who was a customer of the Company, sought to restrain the collection of such increase. The court held for the plaintiff on the ground that he was the beneficiary under the agreement between the Water Company and the Village. 

Pond v. New Rochelle Water Co., 183 N. Y. 330.

In a very recent case, a taxpayer whose property was destroyed by fire was held not entitled to recover against a Water Company for a negligent breach of its contract with the City to furnish water for fire hydrants for a rental paid by the city. 

For the inhabitant of a city to be considered a beneficiary under a contract between the city and another, the benefit must not be merely incidental and secondary. It must be primary and immediate in such a sense and to such a degree as to show the assumption of a duty to make reparation directly to the individual member of the public. If a promissor undertakes to supply fuel for heating a public building, he is not liable for a breach of contract to a visitor who finds the building without fuel and contracts a cold. The service was toward the city as an entity, and not toward individual customers. 

Moch v. Rensselaer Water Co., 247 N. Y. 160.

It is not necessary that the beneficiary know of the existence of the promise when it was made. Whenever he learns of it, he can enforce it, and once he adopts it, the original parties can no longer rescind, in any of the above cases. 

Kessler v. Haile, 127 Misc. 413; 217 N. Y. S. 182. (4) If the promise runs directly to P, at the request of a party to the contract, although P does not furnish the consideration. 

Seaver v. Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233.

If A had an assignable contract with B, he can assign it to P, and P can sue B in his own name, regardless of the above rules. 

Note that it is an assignment to which this reference is made. Assignments¹

This brings up the question of assignments. An assignment may be made either for value or as a gift. In the absence of an agree- 

1For a discussion of the procedure in cases where the assignee is a plaintiff, see Sainer, "The Adjective Law of New York," 6th Ed., fp. 11, 62.

ment to the contrary, all rights to a sum of money arising after a breach of contract other than a contract to marry are assignable. An option is also assignable. 

Farone v. Hall, 128 Misc. 794; 220 N. Y. S. 1.

A bilateral contract for personal services cannot be assigned by either party as such a contract involves a personal relationship, and since performance cannot be delegated, the contract cannot be assigned. Thus, in one case it was held that an employee, who worked for X under a contract not to enter into similar services for another within a specified territory for eighteen months after leaving X's employ, could not be enjoined from so working for another by Y, who had purchased X's business, and this despite the fact that the employee worked for Y subsequent to the sale. 

Ave. Z Laundry v. Yarmush, 129 Misc. 427; 221 N. Y. S. 506.

The following are not assignable—all personal tort actions which die with the person, as libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault and battery, false imprisonment, and other actions for personal injuries which do not result in death. 

Injuries affecting estates, rather than persons, as conversion and deceit, are assignable.

A "public officer" may not assign in advance his salary or other pay to be earned and to become due; the reason for this rule being that such an assignment tends to prevent the office holder from having the interest in his work which he otherwise would have, to the public detriment. 

Diehl v. Sheehan, 251 N. Y. S. 254; affd. 258 N. Y. 624.

An assignment of something which has no present, actual, or even potential existence when the assignment is made, does not operate to transfer the legal title to that thing when it does come into existence. Such an instrument, if made in good faith for a valuable consideration and not void as against public policy, operates as an executory contract to transfer such after-acquired property and creates an equitable lien thereon. But the legal title remains in the assignor, and at law that title is not transferred until either the equitable lien is enforced by judicial decree or some new act intervenes by which the assignor puts the assignee in possession thereof. 

Black v. Sully, 138 A. D. 562; 123 N. Y. S. 371.

The expectancy of an heir in his ancestor's estate may become the subject of a contract for assignment, which, after the death of the ancestor, may be enforced in equity. 

In re M'Geehin's Will, 134 Misc. 334; 235 N. Y. S. 477.

The parties to a contract may always agree that rights thereunder are not to be assignable. It is to be observed, however, that it is one thing to agree that a chose in action shall not be assignable, and that

CONTRACTS 

113 if assigned no right of action shall vest in the assignee. It is another and a different thing if the owner of the chose in action merely covenants with the obligor that he will not assign. In the former instance no rights are vested in the assignee. In the latter case the assignment if made is good. The only consequence would be that the assignor would be liable for breach of his agreement not to assign. 

Sacks v. Neptune Meter Co., 258 N. Y. S. 254.

The assignee in every case takes subject to all the equities up to the time the debtor is notified or learns of the assignment. Whether or not the debtor assents to the assignment is immaterial. Therefore a bona fide purchaser for value does not acquire good title to a chose in action which he has bought from one who procured it by assignment from the owner by duress. 

Barry v. Equitable Life Assur. Soc., 59 N. Y. 587.

The vendee in a contract for the sale of real property is not liable to the assignee for the amount of the consideration for the assignment where the contract failed because the vendor was unable to convey a good title. The assignee bought what his assignor had. 

Sabel v. Harrison, 221 A. D. 879; 224 N. Y. S. 633.

It is not necessary for the assignee of a claim to notify the debtor in order to protect the assignment. Should the assignor assign the same credit to a second assignee, the latter would obtain nothing, because the assignor no longer had anything to assign. If, after the first assignment, the assignor or the second assignee received payment from the debtor, the recipient would be liable to the first assignee. 

All the moneys held would be held as a trust fund belonging to the proper assignee and the latter would be entitled to an accounting in equity. Who did or did not give the debtor notice, is legally immaterial.

The debtor, if he paid the second assignee in reliance upon notice from the assignor and without knowledge of the first assignment, would be discharged. 

Superior v. Zimetbaum, 221 A. D. 879; 212 N. Y. S. 473.

Transfers of choses of action are within the Statute of Frauds exactly in the same manner and under the same conditions as transfers of goods.

Contracts Implied in Law and Fact 

Implied contracts are of two classes. One class is evidenced by the acts of the parties and not by their words written or spoken. 

These are contracts resting upon an implied promise in fact. The second class consists of contracts implied by law where none in fact exists, i.e., quasi or contructive contracts created by the law rather than by the intentions of the parties.

The quasi contract rests upon the equitable principle that a person may not enrich himself unjustly at the expense of another. In reality no contract exists-there is only a law-created obligation. 

Miller v. Schloss, 218 N. Y. 400.

Thus, if A enters B's store and in B's view takes an article from the counter, then nods to B, receiving B's nod in return, we have a contract implied in fact. The actions of A implied the offer to buy at the regular price, and the actions of B implied the acceptance. 

Moreover, these actions express the real intention of the parties. 

If A controverts or steals something from B, we really have no contract between them. But the law permits B to waive A's tort, and B may bring his action in assumpsit. The offer and acceptance are fiction. This is a contract implied in law. 

A carrier can demand payment for freight charges from the consignee who accepts the goods despite the fact that the consignee is ordinarily liable for it. When the consignee accepts the freight in capacity of an owner, the law implies a promise on its part to pay. 

N. Y. C. R. Co. v. Warren, 234 N. Y. 261.

Implied Conditions 

In every contract there is an implied undertaking on the part of each party that he will not intentionally do anything to prevent the other party from carrying out the agreement on his part. Thus, if the contract calls for A to do some work with implements furnished by B, the other contracting party, there is an implied condition that the implements furnished by B are suitable for the purpose, and if they are not, B cannot recover for breach of contract. 

In re People, 250 N. Y. 410.

There are cases where the law inserts conditions of its own accord. 

The doctrine of implied conditions is asserted by the courts to overcome the hardships of the strict enforcement of the letter of contract law in certain cases and do justice. So where a situation arises which clearly was not to affect the contract and for which the parties naturally enough failed to provide, or contemplate, the law will think for them along equitable lines and will imply such conditions as would have been in the minds of all the parties, had they thought of them. In one case where the defendant agreed to run cars every half hour over land it sold to plaintiff, for a certain period, or in case of breach, to refund the purchase money, the court held that failure to adhere to the schedule during a snowstorm was not a breach sufficient to permit the plaintiff to demand his money. The court implied the condition that the defendant would be relieved from

CONTRACTS 

115 the consequences of a slight breach of non-performance under the above rule. 

Maidment v. Krause, 225 A. D. 492; 233 N. Y. S. 621; 

Stewart v. Stone, 127 N. Y. 500; 

Buffalo & L. Co. v. Bellevue Land Co., 165 N. Y. 247.

In every contract of personal service there is an implied condition that insanity, sickness or death shall be an excuse for non-performance. This rule, however, does not apply where the condition to be performed consists of the payment of money. Where a policy of life insurance contained a clause forfeiting it in case of non-payment of premium when it became due, it was held that the insanity of the insured did not effect a waiver of the forfeiture clause. 

Wheeler v. Connecticut Mutual Life, 82 N. Y. 543; 

Buscini v. Paterno Const. Co., 253 N. Y. 256.

Where repairs are to be made on the buildings of another, there is an implied condition on both sides that the buildings on which the repairs are to be made shall continue in existence. The destruction of them without the fault of either party will excuse complete performance. Thus, if M agreed to mend S's clocks, and the house and clocks burned, M not only is not liable, but he can recover for whatever work he did. He recovers the contract price for the finished work and in quantum meruit for those partly finished. 

Gabler v. Evans, 129 Misc. 911; 223 N. Y. S. 408; 

Whelan v. Ansonia Clock Co., 97 N. Y. 293. 

Where a plaintiff contracted to instal safety devices in the defendant's laboratory, for a sum to be paid on completion of the work, and a fire destroyed these laboratories before the work was completed, the plaintiff was permitted to recover for the equipment installed and for the equipment delivered on the premises but not yet installed, but recovery was not allowed for the equipment prepared and not delivered on the premises. 

Gabler v. Evans, 129 Misc. 911; 223 N. Y. S. 408.

If B engages to do an act lawful at the time, but which is unlawful on the date of performance, B need not perform. There is an implied condition that an unlawful act will not be required. In one case, one 

Mix was employed by the insurance company as a general agent under a contract by which he was to receive a specified annual salary for the term of not less than five years, and before the expiration of the five years, the insurance company was dissolved. The court said that there was no breach of the contract between Mix and the insurance company by either of the parties, as "what had happened was a dissolution of the contract by the sovereign power of the state, rendering performance on either side impossible.” 

Boer v. Garcia, 240 N. Y. 9.

A defense is insufficient which attempts to explain away a failure to manufacture and deliver goods on the ground that the merchandise mentioned in the complaint if manufactured by the defendant, would have infringed patents of other parties. The defendant, having undertaken to manufacture and deliver the product was impliedly required to obtain a license from the patentee. 

Planetlite Co. v. Gleason, 234 A. D. 304; 255 N. Y. S. 109. 

Conditions Precedent and Subsequent 

Contracting parties may stipulate that the contract is not to have any effect until the happening of a certain event or performance of an act. This stipulation is controlling and must be met before the contract can become effective. The parties can also agree that the contract is to be considered terminated upon the happening of an event. These express conditions are not of course limited to the creation and termination of contracts. The parties can agree on certain conclusions to follow on any conditions existing. So, for example, a clause can provide that the contractor shall be given an extension of his time for building the house if there is a strike among his workmen. This would mean that if the men went on strike (even if the contractor himself caused the strike) the contractor would be given more time.

If the parties have signed a full and complete contract, the fact that it provided that a more formal paper should be executed the following day does not make it less binding and effective. 

Hohauser v. Berwin, 125 Misc. 834; 211 N. Y. S. 580. 

Before a plaintiff can recover, he must allege and prove that he has performed or tendered performance of his part of the agreement, except as may have been prevented by waiver or some other fact excusing non-performance. 

Rosenthal v. Natl. Folding Box, 226 N. Y. 313, 322.

If a promisor himself is the cause of the failure of a condition upon which his own liability depends, he cannot take advantage of the failure. A party cannot insist upon a condition precedent when its non-performance has been caused by himself. 

Aimes v. Wesnofske, 255 N. Y. 156.

It is the general rule that in contracts of purchase and sale, the conditions with respect to payment and conveyance are concurrent. 

But where part of the price is to be paid prior to conveyance, the covenants are not concurrent, but independent. If payment is to be made upon a certain day and the consideration for the money is to be performed at a later date, the performance of the consideration is not a condition precedent to the right to recover the money payment. 

Where, however, the person entitled to payment in advance of the

CONTRACTS 

117 performance of the consideration neglects, upon the default of the payor, to sue for such payment until after the time set for performance of the consideration, the obligations which at first were independent become dependent and concurrent. It would in the latter case be a defense to the action for the money that the plaintiff has not performed his part. 

Loliver v. Palladino, 226 A. D. 391; 235 N. Y. S. 243; 

Kennelly v. Shapiro, 222 A. D. 488; affd. 250 N. Y. 523.

Very frequently building contracts contain a clause that no payment is to be made to the contractor unless a certain named architect or engineer gives his certificate of approval. Despite this condition precedent to payment, recovery can be had where the certificate has been wrongfully or unreasonably refused. 

Nolan v. Whitney, 88 N. Y. 648.

If, however, a contract for the sale of goods is involved and the contract provides that the approval of a certain named expert, not the buyer's agent, must be obtained before the buyer need accept the goods, an unreasonable refusal by this expert to give his approval is sufficient excuse for non-acceptance by the buyer. Only collusion or dishonest control of this party by the buyer can permit the seller to recover for this non-acceptance. 

Van Iderstine v. Barnet, 242 N. Y. 425.

Where A is employed under a contract which states that A is to do work which must meet with the satisfaction of his employer and A is discharged before the termination of the contract, a defense that A failed to satisfy would fail if the employer was unreasonable or arbitrary in his dissatisfaction. But if the contract was one involving fancy taste and judgment, the unreasonableness of the employer plays no part. The only question is whether the dissatisfaction was feigned or genuine. 

Applegate v. McFadden, 214 A. D. 221; 212 N. Y. S. 67.

When a person by express language engages absolutely to do an act not impossible or unlawful at the time, an unavoidable accident not foreseen will not excuse non-performance. The party should have protected himself by appropriate excusatory stipulations in the contract. Thus, where one contracts to build an entire structure upon the land of another and it is destroyed by fire so that the builder cannot deliver on the date set, he is not only unable to collect for what he has done, but unless he replaces the building before the due date, he is liable for damages for non-performance. The owners of the land may even recover back money they had advanced on the contract.

If A is bound by a contract with B for a definite period of time, and C deliberately and unjustly induces A to breach the contract,

C is liable to B for damage. The rule is different where there is no employment for a definite time, or definite purpose, so that either party may leave the employ of the other at will, under the terms of the contract. 

Campbell v. Gates, 236 N. Y. 457; 

Vail-Ballou v. Casey, 125 Misc. 689; 212 N. Y. S. 113. Waivers

A waiver is the voluntary abandonment or relinquishment by a party of some right or advantage. It is applied by the courts in order to defeat forfeitures. For that reason no consideration for the waiver is required, nor any prejudice or injury to the other party. 

Fisher v. U. S. Casualty, 138 Misc. 307; 245 N. Y. S. 406. 

Contracts in Restraint of Trade 

Suppose D owns a dairy store and sells it to E. E, desiring to protect himself, has a stipulation put in the contract of sale that D is not to engage in the dairy business anywhere within five blocks of the store. This contract, even though it is unlimited in time and is in restraint of trade, would be an enforceable contract, because the protection that E gets is a fair protection for his particular contract, and does not interfere with the interests of the public. 

Lappono v. Marmone, 204 A. D. 496; 198 N. Y. S. 433.

For similar reasons a provision in a contract for the employment of an inventor that he should not enter the employment of a rival company for a year following the termination of his employment is a valid provision.

A contract, however, by an ordinary workman not to enter other like employment for eight years after leaving his employer, when there is no element of secret or valuable information which he could impart, would be a contract in restraint of trade, unreasonable in its scope, and therefore void. 

Clark v. Stenacker, 236 N. Y. 312.

Illegal Contracts 

If a statute declares certain undertakings to be void, then no action can be maintained for failure to perform such undertakings. Similarly, where the statute requires the obtaining of a license before certain acts are done, no action will lie for the rendition of services without first having the license. A real estate broker, for example, must have a license to practice his profession. If he effects a sale without having such license, he can recover nothing. 

Gambling, wagering and usurious contracts are illegal. Where there is an agreement to pay more interest than 6% per annum, the contract is usurious and any money advanced cannot be recovered.

CONTRACTS 

119 However, demand loans of $5,000 or more made upon collateral may bear any rate of interest agreed upon in writing. 

General Business Law, § 379. 

The defense of usury is not available to corporations or their guarantors or indorsers.

General Business Law, § 374. 

A contract made between A and B cannot be enforced when its performance would require A to defraud X or to betray the confidence of X, or which would require A to make another contract with X which he had every intention of breaching with X and other parties with whom X had other contracts. In a recent case (A), a newspaper man contracted with news agency (B) to send radio messages while a passenger on the Graf Zeppelin (X) in reply to messages sent to him by his friends. The news agency made arrangements to collect the messages for publication as news of the flight. Newspaper man (A) at the time he engaged passage on the 

Graf Zeppelin (X) agreed that he would give no interviews and send no reports of the flight while en route and for eight days after the completion of the flight. The owners of the Graf Zeppelin (X) had contracted the exclusive news rights of the flight to a third party (Y). Newspaper man (A) apparently had full knowledge of contract for exclusive news rights. When the newspaper man sought to collect under his contract with the agency, relief was denied. The court held that the contract was born in tort. 

Reiner v. North American Newspaper Alliance, 259 N. Y. 250.

A seller cannot recover the price of goods sold where he has paid a commission to the purchaser's agent. agent recover a promised commission. and is now part of the statutory law. 

Penal Law, § 439; 

Nor can the purchaser's

This was the common law 

Sirkin v. 14th St. Store, 124 A. D. 384; 108 N. Y. S. 830.

P and L enter into and execute an illegal contract. P then sues D for money which P gave D under this illegal contract. P cannot recover. The rule is that where money has been paid on an illegal contract, which has been executed, neither party can recover from the other for both parties were in pari delicto. 

Where the parties are not equally in guilt, or when it benefits the public interest to give relief to the more innocent of the two contracting parties, the contract will be set aside and the injured party will be restored to status quo as far as possible. In one case money paid to a marriage broker was recovered, even though the contract to find the plaintiff a husband was illegal. The purpose was to discourage the business of marriage brokers. Similarly one who has lost money to a bookmaker at a horse race may recover

back such money. The bookmaker cannot recover back money he has lost. 

Duval v. Wellman, 124 N. Y. 156; 

Watts v. Malatesta, 261 N. Y. S. 51. 

Since neither the Eighteenth Amendment to the United States

Constitution nor the Enforcement Act prohibits the purchase of liquor, there is nothing immoral in its purchase, nor, for that matter, in its consumption within proper bounds. A buyer of alcoholic beverage committed no unlawful act in purchasing thereof, and therefore could maintain action against druggist as seller for breach of implied warranty that beverage was fit for consumption. 

Karpel v. Sands, 258 N. Y. S. 746.

CORPORATIONS Forming the Corporation 

In this discussion we exclude banking, insurance, railroad corporations, municipal corporations and law corporations. The last named is prohibited. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 7. 

A corporation is formed by filing a certificate of incorporation with the Secretary of State in Albany, and paying the necessary fee and tax. This certificate contains (1) the name, (2) the purpose of the corporation, (3) the amount of stock, common (with par value and amount without par value) and preferred, including the dividends to be paid on the latter, (4) the city and county in which the principal office is to be, (5) the intended duration of the corporation, (6) the names and addresses of the directors (who must be at least three in number), the names and addresses of the subscribers to the certificate and the number of shares the latter take, (7) a statement that (a) at least two-thirds of the subscribers to the certificate are adult U. S. citizens, (b) at least one is a N. Y. resident, and (c) at least one director is both a U. S. citizen and a N. Y. resident.

Stock Corporation Law, § 5. 

The certificate is signed and separately acknowledged by each incorporator.

A corporation other than a railroad corporation, which shall not organize or commence the transaction of its business, or undertake its corporate business within two years from the date of its incorporation, may be dissolved in an action by the attorney general. 

General Corporation Law, § 36. 

The Corporate Entity 

The corporation is an entity separate and apart from its stockholders. The owner of even all the capital stock of a corporation does not thereby become the legal owner of its property, but title to the latter is vested in the corporate entity. 

Brock v. Poor, 216 N. Y. 387.

While a corporation will ordinarily be regarded as a legal entity, quite separate and apart from its stockholders, officers or directors, or from other corporations with which it is connected, the law will not permit this fiction to be carried to such an extent as to become 121

a cloak for fraud or illegality. The courts will look beyond the fiction of corporate entity, and hold two corporations, in legal contemplation, to be but one unit, where one is so organized, related to or controlled by the other as to be its mere instrumentality, or alter ego. 

While it is difficult to reconcile all the cases, there is a broad rule that where one corporation acquires sufficient stock in another to control it, the corporate entity of the two organizations will be preserved where such acquisition is resorted to for the purpose of participating in the business and affairs of the owned corporation in the normal and usual manner. But where such stock ownership is acquired with the design of so dominating and regulating the subsidiary company that it becomes a mere dummy or department of the holding company, the court will not permit the owner to escape liability for the acts of its alter ego, and will deal with the real party in interest, as if the dummy or substitute did not exist. 

Pagel v. Herman Paper Co., 258 N. Y. S. 168. 

In one case the plaintiff sought to hold the defendant liable for personal injuries received through the negligence of a motorman who was operating a car on the tracks of the Forty-Second Street, Manhattanville & Saint Nicholas Avenue Railway. Each company had the same executive officers, and practically the same board of directors. The Third Avenue Railway Company owned substantially all the stock and second mortgage bonds of the Forty-Second Street Railway Company, and a large block of its first mortgage bonds, and made large loans to it from time to time. All the cars which ran over the tracks of the two companies were marked "Third Avenue System." Cars were purchased by the Third Avenue Railway Company and leased to its various subsidiaries. The wages of the motormen and conductors running on the tracks of the Forty- Second Street Railway Company were paid by that corporation, as well as many expenses for maintenance and repair. Yet the corporate entity of the two corporations was recognized and upheld, and the plaintiff was denied a recovery against the dominant company. 

Berkey v. Third Ave. Ry., 244 N. Y. 84.

Corporate Duties 

The corporation must keep at its office a stock book containing the accounts and a list of stockholders which must be open daily for inspection, and from which extracts can be made by any of the corporation's judgment creditors or by any stockholder who has been such for at least six months. Anyone holding a written authorization of the owners of at least five per cent. of the outstanding shares may examine the books. The corporation must forfeit to the people $50 for every day it fails to so keep its stock book.

CORPORATIONS 

123 If an officer of the corporation wilfully violates this duty, the officer and the corporation must each pay to one injured thereby not only the resulting damage but an additional $50. (The corporation's defense to this can be that the one suing has within two years past offered for sale the stock book information of any corporation merely for the gain derived from the selling of it.) But even if the failure to keep the books open was not wilful, the corporation must pay the state a fine of $50 for every day the book is not so kept open. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 10. 

Every foreign stock corporation having an office for the transaction of business in this state must keep a stock book therein. An office leased by a foreign corporation and used as headquarters for its sales force within the state, under conditions almost identical to the instant case, does not constitute "having an office for the transaction of business in this state" so as to require the keeping of a stock book. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 113; 

Butts v. Valeris Const. Co., 259 N. Y. S. 94.

The certificates of stock issued by the corporation must have (1) the signature of the president or vice-president, (2) that of the secretary or treasurer or their assistants, and (3) the corporate seal. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 65. 

The certificate as a legal concept is but evidence of title to the property right which its owner has in the corporate stock. Nevertheless, they are considered property for all practical purposes. A conversion of a certificate of stock is, therefore, a conversion of the stock itself. 

Pierpont v. Hoyt, 260 N. Y. 26.

A corporation, unless it be one organized for political purposes only, must not contribute to any political party. To do so makes the stockholders who participate guilty of a misdemeanor. No one can be excused from testifying in such a case on the ground that the evidence will tend to incriminate him. But he will be given immunity if he testifies.

General Corporation Law, § 44. 

Ultra Vires Contracts 

A corporation can rightfully engage only in transactions which are necessary to the carrying out of its purpose. If it has been organized to manufacture gunpowder, it cannot manufacture furniture. It can also engage in welfare work if it is in connection with the business in which such a corporation engages. It may borrow for corporate purposes and with the consent of two-thirds of the capital stock, mortgage its property to secure the debt. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 16.

Any contract made which is not within the corporate purpose is considered ultra vires (beyond its powers). Whether the courts will enforce an ultra vires contract depends upon how far the transaction has gone. If the contract has been executed and performed on both sides, the court will not interfere, but will leave the parties as it found them. If neither side has performed, so that the contract is entirely executory, the court will not aid either party in the enforcement of the contract. But if the corporation alone has performed, the court will give the corporation relief, for whatever harm can be done has already been done. If the other party alone has performed, the corporation cannot be held unless it has received a practical benefit from the other party's performance. If both sides have partly performed, there can be a recovery on the basis of quantum meruit. 

Whitney v. Barlow, 63 N. Y. 62; 

Jemison v. Bank, 122 N. Y. 135; 

Appleton v. Bank, 190 N. Y. 417; 

Leinkauf v. Lombard, 137 N. Y. 417.

When, however, an ultra vires act affects only the interests of the stockholders, it may be sanctioned by the assent of the stockholders. 

Behrman v. Zelman, 130 Misc. 846; 225 N. Y. S. 385.

Some corporations are allowed to obtain land only up to certain amounts. Others, particularly educational institutions, are limited in the amount of personalty they can have. Suppose G presents such a corporation with a $10,000 gift, and this gift brings the holdings above the permitted amount. Since the gift is complete, the courts will not aid G in any effort on his part to get back the money, should he regret having given the gift. If, however, he had only promised to give that sum, but had not as yet given it, the court will not enforce the gift in a suit by the corporation.

As a general rule, a corporation may not guarantee the debts, or indorse notes for the accommodation of a third person when its own business interests are not involved. Notwithstanding the general rule stated, a corporation is bound if it makes or indorses commercial paper for the accommodation of another in respect to a bona fide holder who discounts it before maturity on the faith of its being business paper. 

Chase Natl. Bank v. Rosenbaum, 254 N. Y. S. 593.

Stockholders who consent to an ultra vires act cannot thereafter be heard to complain concerning the act of which they themselves approved. The consent may either be a positive act or neglect to act promptly to condemn the unauthorized act. 

Santos v. National Bank, 130 Misc. 348; 223 N. Y. S. 817.

A corporation may guarantee the bonds of another corporation, provided:

CORPORATIONS 

125 (1) The other corporation is engaged in the same business, and (2) The holders of all outstanding shares give their unanimous consent in writing or unanimously vote to do so at a meeting called for that purpose. If, however, the guarantor corporation owns a majority of the other corporation's voting shares, the holders of only two-thirds of the outstanding shares need so agree in their voting. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 19. 

Corporate Rights 

(1) Collection of Subscription Moneys.—If a note is given for shares to be issued later, it is unenforceable unless at least ten per cent of the amount subscribed for is paid in cash; if for stock previously validly issued, it is an enforceable note. However, a later course of dealing between the parties may make the unenforceable note enforceable. A payment of interest on it by the maker or a transfer to a bona fide purchaser, by which the corporation receives the ten per cent makes the note enforceable. 

Furlong v. Johnston, 239 N. Y. 141; 

Backus v. Hutson, 240 N. Y. S. 610. 

(2) Changing Bonds to Stock.-With the consent of two-thirds of the capital stock, the directors may allow bondholders to change their bonds into stock. If there is not enough stock to supply the demand, the capital stock may be increased to meet the demand. 

(3) Right to Change Its Name.—A corporation may change its name by filing a certificate giving the old name and the new one. The holders of all outstanding shares must sign and acknowledge their signature on the certificate filed for that purpose with an affidavit of the secretary or assistant secretary authenticating their authority but the president or vice-president and secretary or assistant secretary may do so if they are so authorized by a majority of the votes of all outstanding shares at a meeting called for that purpose. 

General Corporation Law, § 40. 

(4) Right of Merger.-Any stock corporation, authorized to do business in New York, that owns the stock of another corporation authorized to engage in a similar business in this state, may merge with the latter corporation. All the property becomes vested in the possessor corporation and it is liable for the obligations of the merged corporation. The combined corporation may assume the name of either corporation.

Stock Corporation Law, § 85.

(5) Purchase of Stock.-A corporation may buy its own stock but only out of its surplus and not out of its capital stock.¹ 

Cross v. Begullin, 226 A. D. 349; affd. 252 N. Y. 262.

A stock corporation may own, sell or pledge stocks, bonds or notes of any other corporation if the certificate of incorporation permits, or if the corporation whose stocks, bonds or notes are acquired is engaged in a similar business or is engaged in work necessary or useful to the business of the other corporation. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 18. 

A stock corporation can with the consent of two-thirds of the outstanding shares, at a meeting called for that purpose, sell all its property, privileges and rights to any corporation, but its franchise can be sold only to a domestic corporation. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 20. 

(6) Right to Consolidate-A corporation may consolidate with another corporation organized under the laws of this state. A certificate must be filed with the secretary of state giving facts about the new corporation, e.g., location, directors, terms of consolidation, etc.

The certificate is signed in a fashion similar to that of the certificate of change of name.

Stock Corporation Law, § 86. 

If one of the stockholders objects to such consolidation within 20 days after such a meeting and he wants payment for his shares, he can apply to the Supreme Court to have three people appointed to appraise the value of his stock. The notice of the application to the court must be served within 60 days after the corporation's meeting, and the corporation must be given eight days' notice. The corporation must buy his stock at the appraised value, subject to the confirmation of the appraisal by the court, provided the consolidation is actually accomplished. 

Stock Corporation Law, §§ 21, 87; 

In re O'Hara, 133 Misc. 184; 231 N. Y. S. 60.

The new corporation gets all the rights and privileges of each of the consolidated parties, and succeeds to all the debts of both, but the stockholders are only liable to the same creditors that they were liable to before the consolidation.

Stock Corporation Law, §§ 88, 89, 90. 

(7) Right to Mortgage Its Property-Every stock corporation other than a moneyed corporation shall have the power to borrow money and contract debts when necessary for the transaction of its business, or for the exercise of its corporate rights, privileges or franchises, or for any other lawful purpose of its incorporation; and 

1For a discussion of contracts for the repurchase of stock being unenforceable for lack of mutuality, see ante p. 102.

CORPORATIONS 

127 it may mortgage its property and franchises to secure the payment of such obligations, or of any debt contracted for such purposes. 

The consent to the execution of such mortgage, except a purchasemoney mortgage, by the holders of not less than two-thirds of the total number of shares outstanding entitled to vote thereon, given either in writing or by vote at a meeting of the stockholders called for that purpose, shall be required.

Stock Corporation Law, § 16. 

Without such consent a corporate mortgage is deemed invalid and ineffectual to create a lien on corporate property. 

Cohn v. Gersh Realty Co., 137 Misc. 245; 242 N. Y. S. 671.

Undoubtedly, if money is advanced to a corporation with the understanding between that corporation borrower (its stockholders to the requisite two-thirds participating in the understanding) on the one hand, and the lender on the other, that the loan shall be secured by a corporate mortgage, equity would not deny the lender the mortgage which should have been given him as security for his loan. 

Maryland Cas. Co. v. Schaefer, 141 Misc. 629; 253 N. Y. S. 709. (8) Right to Issue Stock.-A corporation may issue stock for property or money or services. But neither a promoter, nor anyone else, can get stock for services rendered before the corporation actually existed. The directors, if there is not any fraud, are the final judges of the value of the property or the services. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 69. 

Amer. Macaroni Corp. v. Saumer, 174 N. Y. S. 183.

Liabilities of a Corporation 

A corporation is liable in the same manner as a natural person. 

It is liable on its contracts, for its torts, and for its crimes. In its crimes or torts it does not matter whether the corporation was engaged at the time in ultra vires acts or not. It seems hard to see how a corporation can be liable for a crime, when intent is part of the crime, for a corporation, being an artificial body, cannot have intent. Yet the courts have said that if the authorized officials commit a crime in the corporate name, the corporation is liable to a fine (you cannot imprison the corporation) or the charter may be taken away. The agents of the corporation who personally participate can be imprisoned.

A corporation is not bound by agreements between promoters made prior to its formation, unless there is an adoption of this agreement after incorporation.

A corporation is prohibited from interposing a defense of usury in any action.

General Business Law, § 374.

Stockholders' Rights 

The stockholder whom the law recognizes is not the indorsee of the stock or the actual holder. It is the holder of record. The stock certificate, when rightfully issued, is an affirmation of ownership of the specified amount of stock by the person designated therein or by his assignee. 

Holbrook v. New Jersey Zinc Co., 57 N. Y. 616. (1) Right to Dividends.-If the directors act unreasonably in refusing to declare a dividend, the stockholders can force them to declare dividends out of the surplus. The action to recover a dividend can be maintained against the corporation after it has been declared. 

Jones v. Van Heusen, 230 A. D. 694; 246 N. Y. S. 204.

Upon the death of a stockholder, his personal representative becomes the owner of the stock and entitled to dividends, and to have such ownership noted on the books of the corporation. 

In re Starbuck, 251 N. Y. 439.

When a dividend has been declared out of the earnings of a corporation, such dividend belongs to the owner of the stock at the time the dividends were declared. If the stock is sold after the dividends were declared but before they became payable, the dividends do not pass with the stock to the purchaser. 

In re Booth's Estate, 248 N. Y. S. 264.

If a stockholder is indebted to the corporation, the directors may refuse to consent to a transfer of his stock until such indebtedness is paid, provided a copy of the contents of Section 66 of the Stock Corporation Law-the substance of which is the statement in this paragraph-is written or printed upon the certificate of stock. Dividends belong to the owner of the stock, although the corporation may retain the amount of the dividends and consider it in partial payment. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 66; 

In re Starbuck, 251 N. Y. 439. (2) Lost Certificates.-Suppose a stockholder loses his certificate of stock and the corporation refuses to give him another. The owner may ask the Supreme Court, after showing that he lost it, to order the corporation to issue a duplicate. Before the court will do so, it will have the owner file a bond to indemnify the corporation should it have to pay out any money to one who in good faith has a claim under the lost certificate. This section applies only to domestic corporations. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 76; 

In re Bernhard, 209 A. D. 808. (3) Right to Financial Statement.-A stockholder who owns at least three per cent. of the capital stock may demand a financial

CORPORATIONS 

129 statement from the treasurer. This statement must be forthcoming within 30 days after the demand, or $50, plus $10 for each day until the statement is furnished, must be given to the stockholder who made the demand. A copy must be kept open for inspection for a year. The treasurer is not required to furnish more than one such statement a year.

Stock Corporation Law, § 77. 

(4) Right to Inspection.—A stockholder may inspect the books and papers of the company where it appears that the inspection is sought to disclose something which it is the right of the stockholder to know, and not merely to annoy the corporation or for some other purpose than the protection of the stockholder's interests. 

In re Rehe, 136 Misc. 136; 239 N. Y. S. 41. (5) Right to New Stock.-A stockholder has a right to purchase a proportionate share of new stock issued for money only, so that he may retain his proportionate interest in the corporation. This right can be waived by him. 

Dunley v. Avenue M Garage Co., 253 N. Y. 274. (6) Misconduct of Directors.-The right to bring an action based upon the misconduct or mismanagement of directors rests in the corporation. A stockholder may not maintain such an action unless (a) demand has been made upon the corporation to bring the action and it has refused. In such case the allegation of the demand and refusal is an essential averment in the complaint, or (b) the control of the corporation is in the hands of directors whose acts are questioned so that a demand to bring the action would be futile. No allegation of demand and refusal is necessary. 

Jones v. Van Heusen, 246 N. Y. S. 204.

If a stockholder accepts a stock certificate from the corporation with knowledge of a by-law printed on the face thereof requiring a stockholder to offer the stock to the corporation before selling it to others, he is estopped from questioning the validity of the by-law, and the fact that he believed that the corporation was in no financial condition to purchase the stock from him is immaterial. 

Hassel v. Pohle, 214 A. D. 654; 212 N. Y. S. 561. (7) Right to Appraisal of Stock.-A sale not in the ordinary course of business, by a corporation of a substantial part of its business, "so integral as to be essential for the transaction of its ordinary business", entitles dissenting stockholders to have their stock appraised and paid for by the corporation. The statement that such a sale constitutes a "practical dissolution" of a corporation has been used, not to indicate that the corporation as such had ceased to exist, but as a justification for holding that such a sale

could not be made over the protest of dissenting stockholders without giving them the right to have their stock appraised. 

While the sale of the assets of a corporation changes the character of the business of the vendor corporation, and establishes the right of a dissenting stockholder to have his stock appraised, it does not amount to an actual dissolution. 

In re Clark's Will, 257 N. Y. 487.

The method of arriving at the appraisal figure is the same as outlined in the case of a consolidation (see supra p. 126).

Stock Corporation Law, § 21. 

Liabilities of Stockholders 

When a person enters into a subscription agreement with a corporation and pays ten per cent or more of the par value of the stock for which he subscribes, the statute makes him liable to creditors for the full amount of unpaid calls on it, provided the debts were incurred by the corporation in transactions which were not ultra vires. 

It does not matter that the certificate of stock has not been issued to him.

Stock Corporation Law, § 70; 

Allen v. Ryan, 219 A. D. 634; affd. 246 N. Y. 609.

The liability of the stockholders to creditors for the amounts unpaid on their capital stock is joint and not several. 

Schnitzler v. Tartell, 130 Misc. 565; 224 N. Y. S. 339.

A stockholder cannot make a legal contract with the corporation which will relieve him from such liability as regards the creditor. 

Granger v. Allen, 214 A. D. 367; affd. 244 N. Y. 587.

If a corporation owes Jones money, can Jones collect at once from stockholders who could be subject to creditors' claims? No. 

He must first try to collect from the corporation, and if execution is returned unsatisfied, he can proceed against the stockholders who were such when the debt was incurred. 

Note, however, the "two-year" condition of the debt that must exist before the stockholder will be liable in any event: (1) It must be for a debt which is payable within two years from the time it was contracted. (2) The action against the corporation must be started within two years after the amount claimed became due. (3) An action against a stockholder to recover upon unpaid subscriptions is barred, where over two years have expired since he has ceased to be a stockholder, or since the corporation has become hopelessly insolvent and has not and could not transact business and a receiver or trustee in bankruptcy has been appointed. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 73; 

Granger v. Allen, 214 A. D. 367; affd. 244 N. Y. 587.

CORPORATIONS 

131 In any case, even if the stock is fully paid and non-assessable, stockholders of domestic corporations¹ are jointly and severally liable to laborers and employees of the corporation, provided the laborer within thirty days after the work is done notifies the stockholder that should there be an execution against the corporation returned unsatisfied, he will hold the stockholder liable personally. The stockholder will be personally liable, provided that an action is begun against him within thirty days after the return of the unsatisfied execution. Lawyers and officers of the corporation are not employees under this rule.

Stock Corporation Law, § 71; 

Bristor v. Smith, 158 N. Y. 157; 

Bogardus v. Fitzpatrick, 139 Misc. 533; 247 N. Y. S. 692.

A pledgee of stock is not liable as a stockholder. The pledgor is. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 71; 

Bristor v. Smith, 158 N. Y. 157.

Meetings and Votes 

Notices of meetings signed by the president, or vice-president, or secretary, and containing the purpose and place of the meeting, must be served in writing, personally, or by mail, on each stockholder, no less than ten nor more than forty days before the meeting. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 45. 

If the notice of a meeting is technically defective but no stockholder is misled, the court will not interfere with the results of the meeting. 

Langan v. Francklyn, 20 N. Y. S. 404.

Unless the certificate of incorporation or an amendment thereto provides otherwise, every shareholder of record is entitled to one vote for each share.

General Corporation Law, § 23. 

A pledgor of stock who is the holder of record is prima facie entitled to vote. 

Elyea v. Lehigh Salt Mining Co., 169 N. Y. 29.

The inspectors of elections may keep the election open, within a reasonable discretion, as long as is necessary to receive the vote of those present.

An election is not vitiated because the polls were kept open after the time prescribed by the constitution and by-laws. 

Young v. Jebbelt, 213 A. D. 774; 211 N. Y. S. 61.

There may be voting by proxy at corporate meetings, except in religious corporations. An officer or clerk of a banking corporation 

1The applicability of this rule to domestic corporations alone was made in the Bogardus case by the Supreme Court. A decision in the Municipal Court of the City of New York (Spector v. Brandriss, 259 N. Y. S. 558) disagreed with the Bogardus case and held that the rule applied to foreign corporations as well.

cannot vote the share of a stockholder by proxy. The proxy, where allowed, must be in writing and may be good for any time specified. 

If no time is specified, the proxy is good for eleven months. But whether specified or not, it can always be revoked. The vote by proxy is as binding upon the stockholder as his own vote, except where the vote is cast in furtherance of a conspiracy or by collusion. 

General Corporation Law, § 19; 

Wellington v. Morris, 132 Misc. 509; affd. 235 N. Y. S. 906.

A stockholder, by agreement in writing, may transfer his stock to a voting trustee, thus permitting the trustee to vote for him. The right to vote is thereby separated from the beneficial ownership of the stock. The right to vote is irrevocable for the definite period of the voting trust. A record of this voting trust agreement must be kept in the stock book of the corporation. The agreement must be for a period not exceeding ten years.

Stock Corporation Law, § 50; 

In re Morse, 247 N. Y. 290. 

Directors and Officers

The management of the business of a corporation is vested in its board of directors who constitute the mind and body of the corporate entity. This authority, however, is conferred upon the directors, not in their individual capacity, but only when acting together as a board. Acting singly, a majority, or even all of the directors, cannot bind the corporation. Whatever is done must be the deliberate action of the body, and not of its individual members. 

Unless otherwise provided, a majority of the board at a meeting duly assembled shall be necessary to constitute a quorum for the transaction of business and the act of a majority of the directors present at such a meeting shall be the act of the board. 

General Corporation Law, § 27; 

Knapp v. Rochester Dog Protective Assn., 257 N. Y. S. 356.

The court will not regulate their conduct in the reasonable exercise and performance of their duty, even though by their conduct they may render the corporation liable in damage. Nor will the court permit their hands to be unduly tied. An agreement among stockholders whereby directors are bereft of their power to discharge an unfaithful employee is illegal as against public policy. 

Fells v. Katz, 256 N. Y. 67.

If an officer in full charge of the business which the corporation was organized to transact, and representing the corporation, does an act apparently on behalf of the corporation, the act is prima facie an authorized act. If this officer should act in an unauthorized matter, the corporation, which is like a principal in agency, is not chargeable with the knowledge and acts of the unauthorized agent. Should

CORPORATIONS 

133 the managing agent or any officer be permitted, under the by-laws, to contract up to $1,000, and he contracts for goods of the value of $2,000, the corporation must make good on the contract because the corporation is like a principal. Private instructions (and by-laws are that) do not limit the agent's authority as to third persons. 

Spitser v. Born, 194 A. D. 739; 185 N. Y. S. 875.

Under Section 55 of the Stock Corporation Law, each director is required to be a stockholder unless otherwise provided in the certificate of incorporation or in a by-law. 

In re Haecker, 212 A. D. 167; 207 N. Y. S. 561.

Directors may at their own discretion declare dividends but only out of surplus. They must never pay dividends from any part of the capital stock. If they do so, they become personally liable to the corporation and its creditors. The liability is the amount that the dividends wrongfully paid out exceeds the surplus profits.
