The cause of action survives the death of a party defendant. "Capital" means property capital. Those directors whose names appear on the minutes as having been opposed to the declaration of dividends out of the capital, or who were absent from the meeting at which the dividend was declared, are not liable. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 28; 

Cox v. Leahy, 209 A. D. 313; 204 N. Y. S. 741.

Courts will not interfere with the exercise of discretion as to declaration of dividends unless the bad faith of the directors is first shown. Thus, a contract engaging the directors unrestrictedly to declare dividends can have no legal force. 

Lindgrove v. Schluter & Co., 256 N. Y. 439.

If stock of another corporation be held, which constitutes a part of the surplus, it is legally distributable as a dividend, the same as if it were cash. 

Liebman v. Auto Strop Co., 241 N. Y. 427.

When the corporation has spent money for improvements or when it has surplus profits equal to or greater than the amount of a paid dividend, it may borrow an equivalent sum for the purpose of dividends, if it has not ready funds sufficient to pay the dividend. 

Gilbert v. Prankard, 204 A. D. 83; 198 N. Y. S. 25; 

Bankers' Trust Co. v. Dietz, 157 A. D. 594; 142 N. Y. S. 847.

The directors have no right to lend the corporation's money to a stockholder. If it is done and a loss is suffered, the creditors can hold the directors personally responsible for that amount. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 59. 

The directors may lend money to the corporation if they honestly believe it to be solvent. They may also get an assignment of securities, the same as any other person. All that is needed is good faith. 

Converse v. Sharpe, 161 N. Y. 571.

A contract between a director or officer with his corporation for the sale or purchase of any property one to the other is voidable at the option of the corporation even though the transaction be favorable to the corporation. The courts will refuse to set it aside on proper application when, and only when, it was fairly and openly made for an adequate consideration and the corporation was adequately represented by other officers or directors. The presumption is against the validity of the purchase and the burden of showing its validity is on the director. Should there be an increase in the capital stock authorized by stockholders the directors and officers may, in the absence of fraud, issue stock to themselves. 

Gilbert v. Prankhard, 204 A. D. 83; 198 N. Y. S. 25; 

Hine v. Lausterer, 135 Misc. 397; 238 N. Y. S. 276.

The directors and officers are not entitled to any compensation for their work as such, unless provision is made for it before performance. However, when services are rendered by a director or officer which are outside the scope of the ordinary duties of the office, compensation may be recovered as upon an implied contract if it was an understanding that he was to be paid for such services prior to the rendition of the services. Ordinarily, a decision of the parties as to the amount of compensation is final. The court will interfere only where fraud is shown, or where the vote of the interested director has been necessary for the passing of the compensation, or his presence necessary to make a quorum. 

Bagley v. Carthage, 165 N. Y. 179; 

Wellington v. Morris, 132 Misc. 509; affd. 235 N. Y. S. 906.

The general rule is that a director of a corporation is qualified to vote or act at a meeting of the directors upon any resolution in which he is personally interested. 

Munson v. S. G. & C. R. R. Co., 103 N. Y. 58, 73.

A director of a corporation may be a director in other corporations.

If directors are not elected on the day designated in the by-laws, the old directors are considered as remaining in office until successors have been elected.

General Corporation Law, § 28. 

If the date for the election of new directors has arrived, and the directors fail to call a meeting, any stockholder may call a meeting after a month has passed. He must give two weeks' notice personally or by mail, and he must publish a notice of this meeting in a newspaper in the county once a week for two weeks. 

General Corporation Law, § 29. 

Since directors have to do the managing, every director has an absolute right to examine the corporation's books, even though it

CORPORATIONS 

135 can be shown that the particular director was hostile to the corporation, and was engaged in competition with it and desired to harm it. 

Wilkins v. Asher, 207 A. D. 168; affd. 237 N. Y. 574.

Suppose two-thirds of the stockholders file a certificate of dissolution. The directors automatically become the trustees for the creditors of the corporation. The directors are jointly and severally liable to the extent of any assets which come into their hands. 

General Corporation Law, § 35. 

If the corporation divides its property among the shareholders, and the executions issued by the corporation's creditors against the corporation are returned unsatisfied, the creditors can follow the property into the hands of any of the stockholders, in the nature of a creditor's bill. 

Hazard v. Wight, 201 N. Y. 399.

The attorney-general on behalf of the people, the corporation itself or a director, creditor, receiver or trustee in bankruptcy, may bring an action against a director to compel an accounting by him of his management and disposition of the property in his charge; to compel him to pay to the corporation the value of any property kept by him or transferred or lost through neglect; to enjoin a transfer of property when there is good reason to believe the transfer to be contrary to law. The attorney-general may also bring an action to suspend a director from exercising his office for an abuse of trust. 

General Corporation Law, § 60. 

Upon dissolution of the directors of a corporation are charged with the duty of winding up its affairs and for that purpose may sue and be sued. They are trustees first for the creditors and second for stockholders with full power to collect assets and pay debts. 

O. G. Orr v. Fireman's Fund Ins. Co., 235 A. D. 1; 256 N. Y. S. 79. 

Insolvent Corporations

An insolvent corporation cannot transfer any of its property to an officer, director or stockholder unless the property so transferred is paid for in cash. If property is wrongfully transferred, those of the corporation involved are individually liable to the extent of any less sustained by the claimants. If the transfer is in payment to a creditor, even under an attachment obtained by the creditor (except a laborer creditor), it is a preference and is void, and the transferee must account for the property he receives. But if a bona fide purchaser buys the property for value, he is protected. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 15. 

Suppose A, B and C are employed by the Famous Corporation. It is consolidated with the Jensen Corporation, under the latter's name. 

Then A, B and C are discharged with their contracts having another

year to run. Whom must they sue? Only the Jensen Corporation.

The non-existence of the Famous Corporation is a defense to the latter.

Stock Corporation Law, § 90. 

De Facto Corporations 

If three or more persons (1) in good faith attempt to organize a corporation, (2) under an existing law which authorizes incorporation, and (3) these persons have acted as a corporation under this law, they are as a group regarded as a de facto corporation, even though there be defects in its organization. Its existence can be questioned only by the state in a direct proceeding brought for that purpose, called a quo warranto proceeding. 

Munter v. Ideal Peerless Laundry, 241 N. Y. S. 411.

A de facto corporation has a substantial legal existence as a corporate entity. It may take and convey property, can sue and be sued to the same extent as a de jure corporation. 

Sacks v. Anne Realty Co., 131 Misc. 117; 225 N. Y. S. 370.

A third person who dealt with them believing them to be a corporation can hold the members only as stockholders of a corporation and not as partners. By dealing with the association as a body corporate, he admits its legal existence as a corporation, and he is estopped to deny the corporate existence. The de facto corporation cannot, even if it so desires, deny its corporate existence. It is estopped from so doing. The proceeding taken to form a corporation and its attempted corporate acts are enough to hold it. 

Comm. Bank of Keokuk v. Pfeiffer, 108 N. Y. 242; 

Joint Stock Co. v. Natl. City Bk., 210 A. D. 665; 206 N. Y. S. 476.

Those who were stockholders of record at the time the corporation was merely de facto and incurred liabilities and participated in its acts of user as a corporation, are liable as if the corporation de facto were a de jure corporation. 

Eaton v. Aspinwall, 19 N. Y. 119.

In a recent case a plaintiff sought to hold an individual, C, liable on a contract made between the plaintiff and the X corporation. 

His argument was as follows: A, B and C had contracted to form the X Corporation and filed a certificate. A and B were minors, and since the General Corporation Law requires that the incorporators be of full age, the certificate was void. Therefore, he said, C was liable. The court held that the corporation was de facto and that C was not liable individually.

Foreign Corporations 

A foreign corporation may do business in New York, provided it obtains a certificate of authority from the Secretary of State in Albany. In order to obtain this certificate, the foreign corporation

CORPORATIONS 

137 must (1) present to the Secretary of State a corporate statement, signed and acknowledged by its president, or vice-president, or secretary, or treasurer, or managing director, or attorney in fact, setting forth (a) the state or country of its incorporation, (b) its place of office in New York, (c) its proposed business, (d) its designation of the Secretary of State as its agent to receive service of process; (2) a certified copy of incorporation, or the law under which it was incorporated, if it has no certificate of incorporation. 

General Corporation Law, § 15. 

As soon as it gets this certificate the corporation can do business and sue on any contract made after it received the certificate. Neither the corporation, doing business in New York, nor its assignee can sue on a contract made in this state before it got the certificate. But it can be sued on a contract (and in such action can put in a counterclaim) even if the contract had been made before it obtained the certificate. 

Stock Corporation Law, § 110; 

Stephenson v. Weltsee, 223 A. D. 41; 227 N. Y. S. 230.

By "doing business" is meant a regular and systematic course. 

The foreign corporation must maintain and carry on business with some continuity of act or purpose and not a business of a temporary character. Merely soliciting orders in New York through a selling agent who sends the orders out of the state for approval by the foreign corporation, would not be doing business in New York. 

Allison v. Sarandrea, 134 Misc. 566; 236 N. Y. S. 265. 

Individual isolated sales do not constitute "doing business." 

Inter. Fuel & I. Corp. v. Donner, 242 N. Y. 224.

A complaint that shows upon its face that the plaintiff is a foreign corporation (other than a moneyed corporation) doing business in 

New York, trying to recover upon a contract made in New York, must allege that the plaintiff has obtained a certificate of doing business. If it fails to do so it may be dismissed for failure to state a cause of action. If the complaint does not state that it was doing business here, and it develops upon the trial that it was not authorized so to do, a notion by the defendant at the end of the plaintiff's case to dismiss the complaint, should be granted.

Western Felt v. Modern Carpet Co., 252 N. Y. S. 697. 

The foreign corporation may cease to do business in New York by filing with the Secretary of State a statement in its corporate name, signed by its president, or vice-president, or secretary, or treasurer, or managing director, or attorney in fact, stating (1) the name of the corporation, (2) the state or country in which it was organized, (3) when it received authority to do business in New York, (4) that it consents that the Secretary of State shall receive process for the corporation for any claims against it which were created prior to the filing of this certificate of surrender.

General Corporation Law, § 16-e.

CRIMINAL LAW 

Attempts

An attempt is an act done with intent to commit a crime, but failing to effect the commission because of some physical situation. 

If G tries to burn a building and actually puts a light against the wall, he is guilty of an attempt to commit arson, even if the wall happens to be fireproof and therefore will not burn. But if the reason for the failure to commit a crime is the lack of a legal element, there can be no attempt, even though intent was present. Suppose 

G is suspected of buying stolen goods. A supposed friend of his, really a detective, tells G that he has a stolen diamond ring, and G "buys" this ring. G cannot be indicted for attempting to buy stolen goods, because one of the elements of that crime is the buying of goods knowing them to be stolen. That legal element was missing, for G could not possibly know the ring to have been stolen. 

Penal Law, § 260. 

If the act is merely in the way of preparation, or too remote to constitute the crime of attempt, the crime of attempt is not committed. There must be such nearness of accomplishment that in all reasonable probability the crime itself would have been committed but for timely interference or obstruction. In a recent case A, B and C planned to rob X, known to have in his possession a large sum of money. The three started out for the building in which they figured X to be. Three policemen followed and as they entered the building and before they saw X, they were arrested. They were held not guilty of attempted robbery, as the act did not come so near the commission of the crime of robbery that there was reasonable likelihood of its accomplishment but for the interference. At the time of the arrest, the most that could be said was that the defendants were seeking an opportunity to commit a crime. 

People v. Rizzo, 246 N. Y. 334.

One who solicits another to commit a crime is guilty of an attempt. 

Effect of Intoxication 

An act committed by a person in a state of voluntary intoxication is not deemed less criminal because of the intoxication. Where, however, a particular or specific purpose, motive or intent is a necessary element to constitute a particular degree of crime, e.g., murder in the first degree, the jury should be permitted to consider the question of intoxication in determining the existence of such par- Sollecild 138

CRIMINAL LAW 

139 ticular intent. The jury may find guilt of a lesser crime though voluntary intoxication does not call for an acquittal. 

People v. Koerber, 244 N. Y. 147.

Homicide-Murder 

There are two degrees of murder. In either of these there must be a design to kill, except in two instances. (1) When the one kills another while committing an independent felony. In such case the crime is murder in the first degree, even if the killing was never contemplated when the criminal started to commit the independent felony, or even if he struck his victim without intending to kill. Thus if D, while assaulting A, not intending to kill A, kills B who has rushed to the defense of A, D is guilty of murder in the first degree. The independent felony was the assault upon A. If D, however, assaults A not intending to kill A, but does kill A, the assault becomes merged with the homicide and we have not a case of a killing plus an independent felony. 

People v. Wagner, 245 N. Y. 143.

If A, in prison indicted for a felony, attempts to escape, the attempt constitutes a felony. If A kills a keeper while thus attempting to escape, he is guilty of murder in the first degree, as he killed while committing an independent felony. 

People v. Weiner, 248 N. Y. 118. (2) The other instance is when a person recklessly disregards the life of others. If D drives his car through a crowded street at a very high rate of speed, it is a murder in the first degree if any one is killed by him. So, also, if a man fires a gun into a crowd of persons or wrecks a train and a passenger is killed. There need be no intention in such cases to kill anyone. 

People v. Darragh, 141 A. D. 408; 126 N. Y. S. 522;

Penal Law, § 1044; 

People v. Jernatowski, 238 N. Y. 188.

If there is a design to kill plus a premeditation of an appreciable space of time, however short, it is murder in the first degree. If there is the design to kill, but no premeditation, it is murder in the second degree (except in the two instances mentioned). 

People v. Guadagnino, 233 N. Y. 344.

It thus follows that if the defendant inflicted the fatal wound in a sudden transport of passion, excited by what the victim had said, which for the time disturbed his reasoning powers and deprived him of the capacity of reflect, or while under the influence of some uncontrollable emotion, it is not murder in the first degree. 

People v. Caruso, 246 N. Y. 437.

Manslaughter 

The next grade in homicide is manslaughter. The main difference between murder and manslaughter is that in the latter there is no design to kill (always remembering the above mentioned exceptions). 

There are two degrees of manslaughter. 

Where one beside himself, in a blind rage, kills with a dangerous weapon, the crime is manslaughter in the first degree. If the instrument used had not been a dangerous weapon, e.g., a chair, or piece of wood, it would be manslaughter in the second degree. 

People v. Caruso, 249 N. Y. 302.

The statute has arbitrarily declared certain acts which result in death to be manslaughter in the second degree: (1) A doctor who while drunk treats and kills a patient; (2) One who keeps gunpowder unlawfully, or keeps a dangerous animal, and death results; (3) One who overloads a vessel which transports passengers and death results; (4) A woman who deliberately produces a miscarriage upon herself.

Penal Law, §§ 1050, 1052. 

A wilfull killing of an unborn quick child is manslaughter in the first degree, unless done to save the woman's life.

Penal Law, § 1050. 

If A kills B while A is committing a misdemeanor, it is manslaughter in the first degree. If A kills B while A is committing a civil trespass, it is second degree manslaughter. 

Taking one's own life is not a crime, but helping another to take his own life by giving advice or actual assistance is manslaughter in the first degree.

Penal Law, §§ 2301, 2304. 

A killing is excusable if committed by accident or misfortune in doing a lawful act by lawful means with ordinary caution, without unlawful intent. This rule is limited to this extent: if the homicide is committed as a result of culpable negligence-not amounting to manslaughter in the first degree--the defendant is guilty of manslaughter in the second degree. Culpable negligence means the disregard of the consequences which may ensue from the act and indifference to the rights of others. 

People v. Angelo, 246 N. Y. 45.

Whenever intent becomes material—and it is material in cases of homicide the quality or persistence of the intent is for the jury.

CRIMINAL LAW 

141 The jury is to be charged concerning the various degrees of murder and manslaughter. 

People v. Moran, 246 N. Y. 100.

Attacking a Person-Assault 

This crime is divided into three degrees: 

First degree.-There must be intent to kill with the use of poison or a dangerous weapon. The fact that the person attacked was not actually touched does not matter. There need be no battery, the assault being enough. So if A shoots at B with intent to kill, and misses, we have a case of assault in the first degree. 

Penal Law, § 240. 

Second degree.-If one intending to injure or commit a crime administers a drug to another; or intending to commit a felony he assaults some one; or with intent to injure he assaults and succeeds in injuring; or to keep from being arrested he assults some one; or he wilfully and wrongfully assaults another by the use of a weapon or thing likely to produce grievous bodily harm, e.g., a blackjack, (actual contact not being necessary), it is second degree assault. Intent to kill does not enter into this crime, in the second degree.

Penal Law, § 242; 

People v. Wagner, 245 N. Y. 143. 

Assaults in the first and second degree are felonies.

Third Degree.-This constitutes all other assaults and is a misdemeanor. By a recent law it is third degree assault to drive, or allow another drive, one's car so negligently that a person is hurt. 

Penal Law, § 244. 

It has been held to be assault in the third degree to strike a person with an automobile when driving the car against the direction permitted by a traffic light.

Rape 

People v. Saroff, 227 A. D. 114; 237 N. Y. S. 73.

Forcing a woman not one's wife to sexual intercourse, when through unsoundness of mind or by reason of mental or physical weakness, or because her resistance has been overcome by physical strength or by drugs administered, she is unable to resist, constitutes the commission of the felony "rape, in the first degree." 

Having intercourse with a girl who is under 18, and not one's own wife, she being willing, is second degree rape. A girl under 18 is incapable of giving consent to such intercourse. The fact that she appeared to be over 18, and said she was over 18, does not matter. 

Penal Law, § 2010.

It should be noted, however, that the female's testimony alone will not convict the defendant of rape. The material facts necessary to be established are (1) the commission of the crime and (2) the connection of the defendant with it. 

The corroboration of the fact of penetration need not be direct; it may be circumstantial. 

People v. Haskall, 125 Misc. 628; 210 N. Y. S. 337. 

People v. Deitsch, 237 N. Y. 300.

Proof of opportunity for the commission of the offense does not suffice as the necessary corroboration. 

The rule that the female's evidence is not sufficient by itself does not apply to a case of attempted rape.

Seduction 

People v. Brehm, 218 A. D. 266; 218 N. Y. S. 469.

When a female above 18 agrees to sexual intercourse, only because the man promised to marry her, he is not guilty of rape because she allowed the intercourse. But it would constitute the crime of seduction, provided the female had never had any intercourse before, lawful or otherwise. As in the case rape, the woman's testimony alone would need corroboration. The corroboration here would have to be as to the promise of marriage and the intercourse, but not as to her chastity.

Penal Law, § 2175; 

People v. Nelson, 153 N. Y. 90.

If two years go by before an indictment is found against the defendant, the prosecution for seduction is barred. Should the man marry the woman involved before the two years are over, there also cannot be any prosecution.

Penal Law, § 2176. 

Abandonment

A man who abandons his wife while she is pregnant and in destitute circumstances is guilty of a felony. 

Bigamy 

The person having a husband or wife living, and marrying another, is guilty of bigamy. Bigamy is a felony. The fact that he believed he was validly married to the second, or divorced from the first, whereas he was not (knowing, however, that his wife was alive), does not relieve him. The consort in the second marriage would also be guilty, if she knew that the man was entering into a marriage of a bigamous character.

Penal Law, § 340.

CRIMINAL LAW 

143 Robbery To constitute robbery in any degree, there must be (1) force or the causing of fear and force or injury to the person or to property, even in the slightest degree, of any one present at the time, or of a member of his family. The force must be used either to take or retain possession. If the force or the threat be not used either to take or retain possession, it is not robbery, whatever else it may be. Thus using force to escape, after one has been caught pickpocketing, is not robbery. (2) The property taken must be taken from one's person or in the presence of a person. Taking it from a room having no occupants is not robbery. 

What is the distinction between the degrees? If the elements that make up robbery are present, and more than one robber engage in the affair and are present, and by their presence or otherwise aid in causing fear or supplying the force, it is always robbery in the first degree. But suppose one person alone robs: if he is armed with a dangerous weapon or uses a motor vehicle to aid him, or actually injures someone present at the time of the robbery, it is robbery in the first degree.

Penal Law, § 2124. 

If violence is used, but no injury is done, and the lone robber has no dangerous weapon or motor vehicle, it is second degree robbery. 

Penal Law, § 2124. 

A person who robs another under circumstances not amounting to robbery in the first or second degree is guilty of robbery in the third degree. Example: A takes personal property from X by means of "fear of injury in the future.'

Extortion 

People v. Thompson, 198 N. Y. 396;

Penal Law, § 2128. 

Extortion is the obtaining of property from another (including corporations) with his consent, by means of force, fear or under the color of official right. Where the intended victim pays money in apparent response to threats, but really is acting with the police to lay a trap for the accused, the extortion is not committed, but it amounts to an attempt to commit extortion. 

People v. Gillette, 200 N. Y. 276.

The threats may be against the person or property of the one threatened or against his relatives, or they may be threats to accuse one of these of a crime or to expose a secret or kidnap any of them. 

Compelling the victim to sign, alter or destroy a paper which affects the legal rights of a party is extortion. 

Penal Law, §§ 852, 853.

Extortion is a felony, but if a public officer or one pretending to be one unlawfully and maliciously and under color of official authority detains another against his will, or deprives him of property or of his rights, he is guilty of misdemeanor. 

Penal Law, §§ 854, 855. 

Robbery differs from extortion in that to constitute robbery the property must be taken against the will and without the consent of the possessor. In extortion consent is obtained. Bribery

Penal Law, §§ 850, 851; 

People v. Hagen, 212 A. D. 879; 208 N. Y. S. 235.

Bribery of a public officer or an attempt to bribe a public officer is a felony. If the bribe is offered and accepted, the offeror is not privileged from testifying upon the prosecution for receiving the bribe. But if the offerer testifies that he offered and gave the bribe, he is granted complete civil and criminal immunity. 

The crime of bribery was committed in a case where the bribe was given to deter a police officer from interfering with the sale of liquor, despite the fact that such interference was technically beyond the official powers of the police officer, the prohibition law being a federal law and not a state law. The gist of the crime of bribery is the wrong done to the people by corruption of public servants and the act in the case bore some official relation to the duties of the officer, to wit, upholding the peace and dignity of the state. 

People v. Lefaro, 250 N. Y. 336. 

If a public officer receives free transportation from a railroad or free service from a telegraph or telephone company, he is guilty of a misdemeanor. The company is also guilty of a misdemeanor, but if it testifies to the giving, it gets civil and criminal immunity. Larceny 

Larceny can be committed in three ways: (1) By taking property of any value from another without his consent with intent to steal (distinguish from robbery, where force and fear are the elements). (2) By getting money or property under false pretenses. The indictment or information which should label the crime "larceny”there is no such crime in the penal law as "obtaining goods under false pretenses"-must set out the intent, pretense, the fraud and the reliance thereon, and must negative the truth of the pretense. 

It is insufficient to state that "the defendant falsely asserted, etc."; the indictment must state by distinct averment that the pretense was false. 

People v. Williams, 135 Misc. 564; 238 N. Y. S. 712; 

People v. Cooper, 224 A. D. 145; 229 N. Y. S. 748.

CRIMINAL LAW 

145 Any person who, with intent to defraud, issues a check or draft for the payment of money, knowing that he has not sufficient funds with the drawee for the payment of such check or draft, is guilty of a misdemeanor. If money or property is obtained thereby, the drawer is guilty of larceny. The fact that the check was given for an indebtedness past due, is no defense.

Penal Law, § 1292a; 

People v. Humphries, 234 N. Y. S. 688. 

The fact that the check was post-dated is a defense. 

People v. Mazeloff, 229 A. D. 451; 242 N. Y. S. 623. (3) By appropriating property to one's own use that was given to him to be kept as bailee or trustee or executor or guardian or other fiduciary, i.e., embezzlement.

So if one finds an article which he knows to be Smith's, and he keeps it, he is guilty of larceny.

Penal Law, §§ 1290, 1300, 1302, 1303. 

It is not a defense to a criminal prosecution for larceny that the owner whose property was taken was a party to an illegal purpose. 

Tench v. Lawson, 225 A. D. 198; 232 N. Y. S. 449.

Not all personal property can be subject of larceny. Property to be the subject of larceny must be detectable or measurable by the senses or by some mechanical contrivance so far as its physical presence, quality or quantity are concerned. Thus electricity and gas can be subjects of larceny. But the right to produce oil, or the good will of a business, cannot be the subjects of larceny though they are personal property. 

People v. Ashworth, 220 A. D. 498; 222 N. Y. S. 24.

Asportation is an essential element of larceny. By asportation is meant any appreciable changing of the location of the property with felonious intent. The taker at some particular moment must have obtained complete, independent and absolute possession and control of the property, adverse to the rights of the owner. The intent must exist to deprive the owner, not temporarily, but permanently, of his property. This intent to steal is a necessary part of the crime of larceny. In one case the defendant secretly and wrongfully made use of machinery belonging to another. The state could not prove asportation of any concrete articles of personal property. As a result the charge was dismissed. 

People v. Ashworth, 220 A. D. 498; 222 N. Y. S. 24.

There is one exception to this rule. By a statute, the mere operation or use or taking by the defendant of an automobile or motor vehicle without the consent of the owner, constitutes larceny. The fact that the person who so took the motor vehicle intended soon to return it, does not relieve him. Under the terms of the statute a boy who

borrows his father's car without permission is guilty of a felony. 

If the father does not report the fact to the police, he is guilty of a felony as an accessory after the fact. The intention of the boy is immaterial. It is an absurd situation, but this result is due to the wording of the statute.

Penal Law, § 1293a; 

People v. Simmons, 130 Misc. 821; 226 N. Y. S. 397, 405;

Schenectady Varnish Co. v. Auto Ins. Co., 127 Misc. 751. 

Grand Larceny in the First Degree.-If the market value of the property which is the subject of larceny is over $500, it is always larceny in the first degree, no matter in which one of the three abovementioned ways it is obtained. If a negotiable instrument is stolen, its value is the amount which is still due on it. 

In amounts of $500 and less the property must be stolen or appropriated at night either from (a) the person of another or (b) from a dwelling house or railway car. In the case of (b) the property must be worth more than $25. In the case of (a) it may be of any value. 

Penal Law, § 1294. 

Second Degree.-If property is taken from the person of another by day, it is larceny in the second degree if $500 or less. If taken in any way other than the above-mentioned ways, and is valued over $100, it is always larceny in the second degree, no matter when it is taken.

Penal Law, § 1296. 

Petit Larceny. The first and second degrees of larceny are felonies. Any other larceny, as stealing a box of tooth-paste from a counter, or $100 and less from a dwelling house, is called petit larceny, and is a misdemeanor. Burglary

Penal Law, § 1298. 

There are three degrees in burglary. 

First degree.-All these elements must exist: (1) The act must take place at night. (2) The burglar must break in by opening a closed door or closed window, or go through the chimney, or be let in by an arrangement with someone inside. (3) He must "enter" by putting either a part of his body or an instrument inside to frighten the people in the house or in order to take something out. (4) The place entered must be a dwelling house in which there is a human being at the time.

CRIMINAL LAW 

147 (5) The burglar must be armed with a dangerous weapon, no matter whether he has it when he gets to the house, or gets it inside; but if he has a confederate, or he gained entrance or exit by assaulting someone, it would be an element which would dispense with the need of the dangerous weapon. (6) There must be intent to commit a crime at the time the entry is erected. What the burglar actually does when he gets inside is entirely immaterial.

Penal Law, § 402. 

Second degree. If the entry took place in the daytime and the elements under subdivisions 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 were present, or if elements 1, 2, 3, 4 and 6 were present (5 was missing), it would be burglary in the second degree. 

Penal Law, § 403. 

Third degree. If one breaks into any building not a dwelling house containing an occupant, with the intent to commit a crime; or if he enters a building when the door is open (so as not to constitute a "breaking in"), with no intent to commit a crime, but while inside he commits a crime and then opens the door which he had shut, in order to get out, he is guilty of burglary in the third degree. 

If the door had been open when he wanted to get out, it would not have been a breaking out, and so not burglary. 

Penal Law, § 404. 

A building includes a railway car, booth, tent, shop, or other erection or enclosure. It does not include a gasoline pump, containing no storage compartment and separated by a driveway from a building.

Arson 

People v. Lamphere, 219 N. Y. S. 390.

This crime is a felony and is divided into three degrees. 

First degree.-It can be committed only at night. The building wilfully burnt must be a dwelling house which has a human being in it, whether the incendiary knows it or not, or it can be any other building not a dwelling house in which the incendiary knows there is a human being.

Penal Law, § 221. 

The fact that the incendiary warns the occupants that he is going to set fire to the building is no defense to an indictment for arson in the first degree. 

Woodford v. People, 62 N. Y. 117.

Second degree. If the same conditions existed but the fire was wilfully started during the day instead of at night, it is arson in the second degree. But it is possible to have arson in the second degree

even though the burning be at night. the dwelling house or structure had no human being in it. 

Such a case would be when

It is arson in the second degree if a building is set on fire so as to defraud the insurer.

Penal Law, § 222. 

Third degree.-Burning a structure with no human being in it during the day is arson in the third degree.

Penal Law, § 223. 

To constitute a burning there need not be a flame. Charring is enough. But the mere scorching or discoloration is not enough. Forgery

It should be clearly understood that copying another's signature is not the only kind of forgery. Any material alteration of an instrument, or putting together different parts of genuine instruments, with intent to defraud, or knowingly disposing of a forged instrument-all these also are forgery. 

Suppose a paper is signed "John Brown." A man who did not sign that paper, but whose name happens to be John Brown, passes if off as his, after finding the paper. John Brown is a forger. 

Penal Law, §§ 880, 883. 

First degree. If a person, intending to defraud, forges any instrument by which any right or interest in property is or purports to be affected, or the acknowledgment to such an instrument is so forged by him, he is guilty of forgery in the first degree.

Penal Law, § 884. 

If a notary public takes an acknowledgment of an instrument which may be recorded, and wilfully certifies falsely that it was executed by the parties, or that the execution was proved, he is guilty of forgery in the first degree.

Penal Law, § 885. 

Second degree. If a person intending to defraud forges the seal of this state, or of a court of record, or of a state officer, or of a corporation, or of any other state or county, or forges a record of any instrument or judgment or court decree, or a certified copy thereof, or an entry in the book of records or accounts of a state, city, village, or town officer, or forges any bank notes, he is guilty of forgery in the second degree.

Penal Law, § 887. 

Third degree.-An officer or employee of a corporation or partnership who falsifies or unlawfully alters or erases or destroys any writings of the firm, or any person intending and succeeding in injuring the good name of any person by altering or aiding in altering any written communication, is guilty of third degree forgery.

CRIMINAL LAW 

149 If a person intending to defraud forges a ticket of a railroad or other public conveyance, or sells a forged ticket knowing it to be forged, he is guilty of third degree forgery. 

If one forges a postage or revenue stamp of the United States or of New York, or knowingly sells such a stamp, he is guilty of third degree forgery.

Perjury 

Penal Law, § 889. 

A person who under oath as a witness in an action or a special proceeding (even though he later proves to have been an incompetent witness), or in an affidavit in such action or special proceeding, wilfully and knowingly testifies or states a material matter to be true, when he does not know it to be true, is guilty of perjury. 

What is a material matter? It must have some bearing on the main fact about which the person has testified. The fact that the ultimate decision was decided upon an entirely different point, about which this witness was asked nothing, does not mean that the false testimony given would not constitute perjury. His testimony need not have affected the trial. It is sufficient if it might have affected it. The question of the materiality is for the jury. 

Penal Law, §§ 1620, 1626 

It is perjury to knowingly falsely swear or affirm that an affidavit is true, which contains material matter affecting title to property, including an assignment or satisfaction of a mortgage, upon which reliance is placed.

Penal Law, § 1620. 

If A induces or procures P to commit perjury, he is guilty of subornation of perjury. If P should then not try to commit perjury, A would be guilty of attempted subornation. To have a case of subornation of perjury, perjury must have actually been committed. 

Note that if A attempts to have P say something under oath, which even if said would not be perjury, A is not guilty of even attempted subornation of perjury.

People v. Teal, 196 N. Y. 372. 

No civil action exists in favor of the defeated party to a litigation against a witness who gave material testimony on the trial, for his false swearing or his fraud in testifying. 

Morgan v. Morgan, 129 Misc. 212, 221 N. Y. S. 117.

Conspiracy-Accomplices 

If two or more agree to commit a crime, or to have a third party falsely indicted or arrested, or to prevent one from exercising a lawful trade, and one of them actually does an overt act toward the furtherance of the agreement, they are all guilty of a conspiracy.

in The prosecution can take place either in the county in which the agreement was made or in which the overt act was done. Physical presence in the place of execution is not necessary. Thus, if D, order to carry out a conspiracy, mails letters from New York county containing false reports, addressed to Oneida county where the letters were delivered, the prosecution may take place in New York county or in Oneida county. An overt act was committed in Oneida county when the letters were received. 

People v. Levy, 123 Misc. 845; 206 N. Y. S. 857.

If the crime agreed upon is a felony to be committed upon the person of another, or is arson, or burglary, there need be no overt act committed to constitute the agreement of conspiracy. 

Penal Law, §§ 580, 583; 

People v. Collins, 234 N. Y. 355.

The general test as to whether one is an accomplice is whether he himself could have been convicted for the offense, either as principal or accessory.

If an accomplice confesses, any other person he accuses cannot be convicted as an accomplice on this confession alone. There must be corroborated evidence, which evidence must show not only that a crime has been committed, but that the one accused was in some way connected with it. But where X was indicted for receiving stolen goods, he could be convicted on the uncorroborated testimony of the thief, though the thief was merely an accomplice. 

Code Criminal Procedure, § 399; 

People v. Enlid, 214 A. D. 277; 212 N. Y. S. 77.

A failure to prove an alleged alibi was deemed sufficent corroboration of the testimony of an accomplice. 

People v. Gorsk, 236 N. Y. 673;

A refusal by the court to charge that if the jury find X an accomplice his testimony must be corroborated before there can be a conviction of the defendant, is material error, except, of course, where the defendant is on trial as a "fence." 

People v. Youilo, 243 N. Y. 519.

Suppose a confession involves only the person who does the confessing. Is the confession enough to convict the confessor? No.

But if it is shown that the crime has been committed, the confession would be enough to send the case to the jury.

Accessories In the case of felonies there are no accessories before the fact. 

These are considered principals. Only accessories after the fact are considered accessories. And these accessories after the fact cannot be convicted unless the principal is first convicted.

CRIMINAL LAW 

151 In cases of misdemeanors all accessories, whether before or after the fact, are considered principals. 

Penal Law, § 2. 

Infants and Crimes 

A child under 7 years of age is legally incapable of committing a crime. A child over 7 and under 12 is presumed incapable, but this presumption may be removed by showing that he had sufficient capacity to understand the act or neglect charged against him. 

A child under 16 cannot be tried for a felony. If what he did would be a felony if done by one older, it is called juvenile delinquency. Crimes punishable by death or life imprisonment are excepted.

It thus would follow that a killing by an infant under 16 during the commission of what would amount to a robbery by an adult, would not be murder in the first degree as there would be absent the independent felony. The acts amounting to robbery would only be juvenile delinquency on the part of the infant. 

People v. Roper, 259 N. Y. 170.

Statute of Limitations¹ 

A prosecution for a felony other than murder and seduction must be commenced within five years after its commission. There is no limitation which may bar a prosecution for murder. A prosecution for seduction is subject to a two-year statute of limitations. 

A prosecution for a misdemeanor must be commenced within two years after its commission. The magistrate must issue a warrant or the indictment must be found within that time. 

Code of Criminal Procedure, §§ 141, 142. 

If the defendant is without the state when the crime is committed, the statute begins to run after his return to the state. If, after the commission of the crime, the defendant leaves the state and does not stay for his regular attendance to business, or remains in the state under a false name, the period of absence or staying under a false name is not considered part of the period in calculating the time of the running of the statute.

Code of Criminal Procedure, § 143. 

If for any reason the indictment is dismissed and the case resubmitted, the time during which the prosecution was pending is not part of the time of the running of the statute, provided a new 1For a complete discussion of the rules of evidence and procedure in criminal matters, and the liability of infants for crimes, see "The Adjective Law of New 

York," 6th Ed., pp. 220 et seq., under the heading "Criminal Procedure.”

prosecution for the same offense is commenced within sixty days after the order is entered.

Code of Criminal Procedure, § 144a. 

The "ex post facto" rules apply to the Statute of Limitations. If 

A commits the crime of seduction in 1912, an indictment could not be found against him in 1915, even if the legislature had increased the period to 5 years in 1913.

Marriage DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

charged overall

Neither a formal solemnization of marriage nor a ceremony is a requisite of a valid marriage. The marriage may be proved from cohabitation, reputation, acknowledgment of the parties, reception in the family, words, actions and the lives of the parties evidencing their understanding and intent. It is a status and so does not need a formal contract for its validity. 

In re Smith's Estate, 136 Misc. 863; 242 N. Y. S. 464, 470.

Common law marriages are valid in this state and are sufficiently proved if it is established that legally competent parties in praesenti intended to become husband and wife and thereafter lived and cohabited as such. In a recent case A and B, incompetent to marry because of a living undivorced spouse, entered into a ceremonial marriage. This "marriage" was not intended to be meretricious but was, of course, illegal. The respective spouses of A and B died, and the latter continued to live as man and wife. It was held that the continuance of the relationship after the impediments to their marriage were removed constituted a valid common law marriage. 

In re Haffner's Estate, 254 N. Y. 238.

If A and B had originally intended to enter into immoral and illegal relations, there would have been no common law marriage, for a cohabitation which is illicit in its origin is presumed so to continue until a change in its character is shown by acts and circumstances strongly indicating that the connection has become matrimonial. 

Hill v. Vrooman, 242 N. Y. 549; 

In re Erlanger's Estate, 259 N. Y. S. 610, 618.

The state considers itself a party to every marriage contract, since it considers the contract the foundation upon which rests the perpetuation of society and civilization. Thus, regardless of motives inspiring the marriage, parties to a marriage contract make such contract subject to the scrutiny of the law, and its requirements, obligations and duties must be met. For example, marital intercourse. so that children may be born, is an obligation of the marriage contract and this obligation may not be modified by private agreement. 

In one case, a marriage was annulled when it was found that the wife had entered into the marriage with the intention of not submitting to marital intercourse and of not having children, and that the husband married with the belief that the wife would submit to marital intercourse. 

Miller v. Miller, 132 Misc. 121; 228 N. Y. S. 657. 153

The Husband's Responsibilities 

A husband, by force of the marriage, has the duty to maintain and support his wife according to his means. Her wealth plays no part in relieving him of any responsibility to support her, or in determining in what manner he is to support her. Nor is his obligation diminished by the commission of any adultery on the part of either, so long as the marriage contract is in effect. 

Likewise, the husband and wife can make no contract with each other absolving him from the duty to support his wife, which would not be subject to inquiry by the court. The obligation to support grows out of the marital status. And while agreements of separation are recognized, yet they are open to attack where their provisions are inadequate, inequitable, unjust, unconscionable, or where the contract was improvidently entered into. A husband also continues civilly liable for his wife's support even where there is an agreement providing for the payment of a fixed sum to the wife for her support for the rest of her life, and the sum is later exhausted. 

Tirrell v. Tirrell, 232 N. Y. 224; 

Harding v. Harding, 203 A. D. 721; 197 N. Y. S. 78; 

Gregg v. Gregg, 133 Misc. 109; 231 N. Y. S. 221; 

Hamlin v. Hamlin, 129 Misc. 263; affd. 227 N. Y. S. 819.

A husband is liable for legal services rendered to his wife where such services are necessary for her protection in her subsisting marital rights. This includes legal services rendered in an action for separation brought against her or by her including expenses necessary to protect her to help her obtain the separation decree, e.g., to find her husband who had left her without provision for her support. 

Lanyon Detective Agency v. Cochrane, 240 N. Y. 274; 

Turner v. Woolworth, 221 N. Y. 425.

Where during the action counsel fees have been awarded the wife and the order therefor was filed, that allowance fixes the husband's liability for the expenses of the suit. 

Sheer v. Foley, 260 N. Y. S. 252.

Whether legal services rendered to a wife, who is the defendant in a divorce action, are necessaries, seems to be undecided, but on principal such services should be necessaries. 

A husband is not liable for the services of a detective employed by a wife to discover his infidelity in order to maintain her action. for a divorce. Nor can an attorney maintain an action against a husband for services rendered to the wife in her action for divorce. 

It is never necessary for the safety of a wife, as such, to obtain a divorce. The Civil Practice Act (§ 1169) provides for counsel fees to enable a wife to meet the expenses of a defense.

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

155 Legal services procured by a wife in order to defend herself against a charge of assault and grand larceny were held a necessary obligation for which the husband would be liable. 

Elder v. Rosenwasser. 238 N. Y. 427.

A wife can buy necessaries on her husband's credit if she does not receive money from him for such necessaries. This right is only a prima facie right and can be rebuttal. If the wife buys such necessaries with her own money, believing her husband's claim that he had none of his own, she can recover such sums from the husband or his estate when she learns that he had sufficient funds. 

In re Kiley's Estate, 132 Misc. 582; 230 N. Y. S. 311.

The theory of the husband's liability rests on the fiction of agency. 

As to basic necessaries the wife is impliedly authorized to pledge her husband's credit.

Edgar A. Levy Leasing Co. v. Cohen, 261 N. Y. S. 145. 

If goods are sold to the wife on her own credit, the husband is not liable even for necessaries. 

Wilder v. Brokaw, 141 A. D. 811; 126 N. Y. S. 932.

The duty to support a wife and pay for her necessaries does not cease with the institution of a divorce action. It is only when she is the unsuccessful defendant in a divorce action that she loses her right to support.

The amount fixed by a court as alimony fees fixes the amount of the husband's liability to the support of his wife and children. She or he may make application for change of the amount. The wife, however, is under no obligation at any time to ask for alimony. If she makes no application, the husband's common-law liability continues. When the court has determined the extent of the husband's liability to support his wife and children, the obligation cannot be increased except by the court upon proper showing. Such decree is a complete bar to an action for necessaries for anything purchased, or expenses incurred over and above the amount allowed as alimony. 

The decree has stated in dollars and cents just how much can be spent for what now can be called "necessaries."

Suppose before marriage G contracts to give B, her future husband, $5,000. After the marriage, those who were creditors at the time of the contract sue B for $20,000 due them from G, the wife. 

The creditors can recover from B no more than $5,000, as a husband is liable for a wife's ante-nuptial debts only to the extent of the property which he received from her.

Domestic Relations Law, § 54. 

The Parent's Rights and Responsibilities 

A father is required to support minor children, both legitimate and illegitimate, until they reach their majority or are emancipated, un-

less there is a showing of their physical or mental disability in which case the liability continues. But although this is so, the child cannot buy necessaries and charge his father; nor can a stranger supply the infant with necessaries. It is only when the father has failed or neglected to support his child that the former is liable for the reasonable value of the necessaries supplied to the infant. The theory underlying this is that in such case any person who supplies such necessaries is deemed to have conferred a benefit on the delinquent parent, for which the law raises an implied promise to pay on the part of the parent. Of course, the infant is also liable for these purchases. 

Domestic Relations Law, § 120; 

Van Valkenburg v. Van Valkenburg, 13 Johns. 480; 

Laumeier v. Laumeier, 237 N. Y. 357; 

Halsted v. Halsted, 228 A. D. 298; 239 N. Y. S. 422.

A divorce decree does not absolve the father of his duty to furnish necessaries for his child. 

Dumay v. Dumay, 217 A. D. 773; 216 N. Y. S. 740.

Though the wife obtains custody of their child by divorce decree, it is still the duty of the father to support that child. If he fails to support the child, he may be also convicted of being a disorderly person. 

Code Criminal Procedure, § 899. 

The estate of a deceased testator is not obligated to support infant orphans. And the will of the father may entirely cut off any and all of the infants. 

Rice v. Andrews, 127 Misc. 826; 217 N. Y. S. 528.

The father is entitled to any money earned by the children if he » gives thirty days' notice in writing to the employer. If the employer, despite the notice, pays the minor, he must make good to the parent all earnings subsequent to the notice.

Domestic Relations Law, § 72. 

The father is also entitled to compensation for loss of services arising from personal injuries to a child caused by the negligence of a defendant. The right to maintain an action of this character is based upon the relationship of master and servant. Under the common law the father originally had exclusive right to the services of his minor children. But a mother may, even during the lifetime of the father, bring an action for loss of services of an infant child. where it was shown that she was practically the head of the family, paid the bills, and by direction of the father was entitled to and did, receive the earnings of the infant, she being responsible for its maintenance and support. 

Hagopian v. Samuelson, 260 N. Y. S. 24.

The parent is entitled to the services and earnings of a minor child while it is in the parent's custody or control and has not been eman-

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

157 cipated. A parent may relinquish his rights or emancipate the child expressly or by implication. A minor, who with the consent and approval of his parents was receiving his own wages and paying his own way, was held to be emancipated. 

Crosby v. Crosby, 230 A. D. 651; 246 N. Y. S. 384.

An emancipation or a failure to support the child carries with it the loss to the parent of the child's earnings and also the parent's right of action against a person whose wrongful act has injured the child. 

Cuming v. Brooklyn City R. R. Co., 109 N. Y. 95. 

If an infant marries he is emancipated, and for all purposes of relationship between parent and child, the child is considered an An emancipated child can recover from his father on a conadult. tract. 

Crosby v. Crosby, 230 A. D. 651; 246 N. Y. S. 384.

A parent may not recover damages against an unemancipated minor child for his tort, but he may recover such damages from an emancipated child. 

LoGalbo v. LoGalbo, 138 Misc. 485; 246 N. Y. S. 565.

If an infant is an employee, his parent's rights for wages are the same as those of the infant. 

Turner v. Kouwenhoven, 100 N. Y. 115, 119.

If an infant has completed his term of service and his parent sues for his wages, the employer is not entitled to an affirmative judgment against the child's parents because of conversion by the infant, unless the employer can show that the parent himself was a party to the conversion. 

Werner v. Weinberg, 125 Misc. 393; 211 N. Y. S. 48.

A stepfather need support a minor stepchild only if he is of sufficient ability to do so, and the child is or is liable to become a public charge. At common law there is no liability at all. 

Public Welfare Law, § 125; 

People v. Fermoile, 259 N. Y. S. 564. 

A father need not support his stepchildren.

If a man has an adult daughter who is physically incapable of supporting herself, he must support the daughter though she is no infant, otherwise he will be subject to criminal prosecution. 

Must a grandparent support a grandchild? Yes, if the parent is unable to do so and the grandparent has the means. 

People v. Ramm, 197 N. Y. S. 234. 

A mother must support her children if the father is dead. Where the child, however, has an estate from which the child can be supported, the estate must support both child and mother if the surrogate should so direct. This is not so in the case of the father.

The Wife's Rights 

A wife can contract in her own name, and if she works she can keep all she earns. The husband cannot sue for what she earns. 

She can go into business with her husband as partner, and is liable to third parties just as a partner. But for work rendered by her for her husband in connection with his business which are not of an extraordinary nature, she is not entitled to be paid as a matter of right. In a recent case a wife's services in managing her husband's rooming houses were not held of such character as to warrant a finding of an implied agreement to pay her compensation. 

Domestic Relations Law, §§ 51, 60; 

Martin v. Bronx County Trust Co., 260 N. Y. S. 344.

If a wife saves money from her weekly allowance, the amount so saved belongs to her husband unless given to her as a gift. 

A wife may sue for tort against her in her own name without joining her husband in the action, but she may not maintain an action against her husband in tort. A complaint in an action for malicious prosecution (a tort) was dismissed where it appeared that the defendant was the spouse of the plaintiff. This rule applies to negligent and wilful torts. 

Allen v. Allen, 246 N. Y. 571; 

Schubert v. August Schubert Wagon Co., 249 N. Y. 253. 

Wadsworth v. Webster, 261 N. Y. S. 670

A wife may maintain an action for criminal conversation against her husband's paramour. 

Oppenheim v. Kridel, 236 N. Y. 156.

She can also be sued for torts committed by her. 

Domestic Relations Law, § 57. 

Void Marriages 

(1) Marriages between ancestor and descendant, brother and sister, uncle and niece, or aunt and nephew, be they even of half blood, are void. The children are, however, legitimate. (2) A marriage is void if one of the parties is married at the time of the second marriage. Even though the polygamous marriage is contracted in a foreign jurisdiction where it is valid, it would be void in New York.

Domestic Relations Law, § 6. 

A court of another state lacks jurisdiction to declare the marriage relations of a citizen of this state dissolved, where the divorced citizen actually resides in New York during the divorce proceedings, makes no voluntary appearance in the other state's courts and receives no actual notice of such proceedings. Accordingly, that decree is no defense to a prosecution for bigamy.

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

159 A marries B while B had a spouse (C) living. A did not know of the other living spouse. B obtains a divorce from C, thus making 

A and B competent to be husband and wife. Subsequent to the decree, A continued to live with B. It was held that B was not barred from having his marriage to A annulled, inasmuch as the marriage was bigamous at its inception. 

Earle v. Earle, 141 A. D. 611; 126 N. Y. S. 317. (3) A marriage in which the male is under 16 or the female under 

14 is prohibited.

Domestic Relations Law, § 15a. 

Terminating a Valid Marriage State 

These cases fall into two classes: first, where the marriage state automatically ends, and second, where the parties must complete a legal proceeding to end it. 

In the first class we have the case where a man is finally sentenced to life imprisonment. As soon as he is so sentenced the marriage state ends. The sentence "life imprisonment with possibility of 20 years for good behavior", would end the marriage state. 

The possibility of its being for life has the effect of life imprisonment. A pardon does not automatically permit the resumption of the marital status whether or not the other spouse has remarried in the meantime, nor is he entitled to the guardianship of any previous offspring. 

Gargan v. Sculley, 82 Misc. 667; 144 N. Y. S. 205;

Domestic Relations Law, § 58. 

In the second class we have these cases: (1) Enoch Arden Law; (2) The 18-year Rule; (3) Lunacy; (4) Impotency; (5) Duress; (6) Fraud; (7) Divorce laws. (1) Enoch Arden Law.-If one spouse has not been heard from for more than five years and the other spouse desires to remarry, he or she may do so upon compliance with Section 7a of the Domestic Relations Law. A verified petition must be filed in the Supreme 

Court showing the five years' absence, and also that a diligent search has been made and that the spouse is honestly believed to be dead. 

The court orders a notice of the presentation and object of such petition to be published in two newspapers once a week for six successive weeks, directed to the absent spouse. At least twenty days after the last publication, the court may end the marriage state after a hearing and proof taken. The spouse's return after this would have no effect. Though the statute contains no specific provisions under which the court acquires jurisdiction, it has been held that the court has jurisdiction under this section only (1) if the marriage sought to be dissolved was performed within New York, or (2) if

the parties had a marital domicile here both at the time of filing the petition and for five years prior thereto. 

In re Santos, 132 Misc. 670; 230 N. Y. S. 395.

In a recent case, the wife, who had married the petitioner in Massachusetts, abandoned him in Portugal, and the petitioner then came to New York to live, and five years later sought to dissolve the marriage under this section. The court held that when a wife abandons her husband without the latter's fault, the husband may carry the marital status with him when he changes his residence, and the New York court therefore had jurisdiction. It was held not necessary in this case for the wife to be living with her husband in order to establish a marital domicile. 

Dodge v. Campbell, 223 A. D. 471; affd. 252 N. Y. 590. (2) The 18-Year Rule.-If the parties were New York residents and one of the parties was under 18 at the time of the marriage, that party or his parent, guardian or friend might sue for annulment of the marriage. Whether or not the annulment is to be granted is in the discretion of the court. The fact that the parents of the infant agreed to the marriage, or the fact that the marriage took place in a state where a marriage by this infant under 18 would be valid, does not mean that in New York this party cannot annul the marriage. If there had been a cohabitation after the infant had reached 

18, there can be no annulment on the ground of "under age."

Civil Practice Act, § 1133; 

Smith v. Smith, 129 Misc. 503; 221 N. Y. S. 672. 

Where a girl under eighteen compels a man to marry her because she is about to become the mother of his child, and her intention is simply to legitimatize the child and then dissolve the marriage, she will not be aided by the court. She cannot grasp the benefits of a marriage contract with no intentions to live up to its obligations, and then ask for a destruction of the status created by her. 

Smith v. Smith, 129 Misc. 503; 221 N. Y. S. 672;

Civil Practice Act, § 1133. 

Since the laws of the place where the marriage takes place govern the validity of the marriage, if two non-residents marry in a jurisdiction where infants under 18 may marry, that marriage is valid and cannot be set aside even in New York. The case mentioned in the second preceding paragraph has reference to a situation where New York residents go to such a jurisdiction only so as to avoid the marriage laws of New York. 

Simmons v. Simmons, 208 A. D. 195, 203 N. Y. S. 215. (3) Lunacy. (a) An action to annul a marriage on the ground that one of the parties was a lunatic at the time of the marriage may be maintained by a relative of the lunatic during the continuance

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

161 of the lunacy, or after his death in that condition, and during the life of the sane spouse. The action may also be maintained by the sane party during the continuance of the lunacy, provided the plaintiff did not know of the lunacy at the time of the marriage. 

Civil Practice Act, § 1137. 

(b) A marriage may be annulled if a party has been incurably insane for a period of five years. Before the rendition of the judgment, three doctors, recognized as authorities on mental diseasesone of whom must be the superintendent of a state hospital for the insane if the party is not confined in a state hospital for the insane, or a doctor of the hospital if the party is so confined, and the other two appointed by the court-must all agree that the party is incurably insane. If the marriage is being annulled because of the insanity of the wife, the court, before rendering judgment, must exact sufficient security to provide for her suitable care during the joint lives of the parties.

Domestic Relations Law, § 7; 

Genauer v. Genauer, 134 Misc. 594; 236 N. Y. S. 340. (4) Impotency.-If one party to the marriage contract was impotent at the time of the marriage, and the impotency was incurable, an action to annul the marriage can be maintained by the other party. The action is to be brought within five years after marriage. 

If the defendant fails to plead the Statute of Limitations as a defense, the plaintiff is entitled to a decree, although the evidence shows that more than five years have elapsed since the marriage. 

Civil Practice Act, § 114; 

Katz v. Katz, 240 N. Y. S. 68. 

Sterility is not a ground for annulment. 

(5) Duress. If one of the parties was forced to marry under duress practiced by the other party to the marriage, the marriage contract may be set aside. The duress, if practiced by a third party, is not sufficient ground for setting aside the marriage. (6) Fraud.-A marriage contract is voidable if fraud was practiced upon the plaintiff, provided the fraud went to the essence of the marriage contract. The language of the statutes permitting annulment on the ground of fraud is broad enough to justify the statement that a marriage may be annulled for any practiced fraud or deception which goes to the very essence of the marriage contract. 

The following have been held to be cases of fraud permitting the annulment of a marriage: The defendant lied about or knowingly concealed a loathsome or dangerous disease, as syphilis, leprosy, or tuberculosis; or that he was subject to epilepsy, rendering cohabitation extremely dangerous though a party's merely being subject to epilepsy, or to fainting spells, has been held not to furnish ground

for an annulment action; or concealed, on inquiry, previous unchastity; or held himself out as a person of good character, concealing from the plaintiff criminal activities in which he had been engaged; or falsely represented that the plaintiff was the father of the defendant's child; or stated that she was a widow whereas she was a divorcee; or was induced to enter into the marriage contract by a promise on the part of the defendant to join the plaintiff's religious faith, which promise the defendant never had intended to keep, and there had been no consummation of the marriage. 

Civil Practice Act, § 1139; Blank v. Blank, 109 N. Y. 91; 

Aufiero v. Aufiero, 222 A. D. 479; 226 N. Y. S. 611; 

Rutstein v. Rutstein, 221 A. D. 70; 222 N. Y. S. 688; 

Lapides v. Lapides, 254 N. Y. 73; Svenson v. Svenson, 178 N. Y. 54; 

Rubman v. Rubman, 140 Misc. 658; 251 N. Y. S. 474.

Concealing one's characteristics, temper or lack of wealth, does not by itself justify an annulment. That plaintiff's consent to the marriage was obtained by the defendant's fraudulent representation that she had sufficient money to enable him to enter into a certain business enterprise, it could not be said as a matter of law that this was not a ground for an annulment. The misrepresentation might be of the essence of the contract. 

Shonfeld v. Shonfeld, 260 N. Y. 477. 

McGill v. McGill, 179 A. D. 343; affd. 226 N. Y. 673; 

Griffin v. Griffin, 122 Misc. 837; 204 N. Y. S. 131.

And it has also been held that a marriage will not be annulled because one or both parties were untruthful to each other in their mutual declarations of undying love for each other. 

Schaeffer v. Schaeffer, 160 A. D. 48; 144 N. Y. S. 774.

Cohabitation, with knowledge of the fraud, constitutes a waiver, and the defrauded party is estopped from asserting the fraud. 

Watkins v. Watkins, 197 A. D. 489; 189 N. Y. S. 860.

In the last five instances (Nos. 2 to 6, inclusive, excluding, however, 3b) the marriage state is ended when its nullity is declared by a court of competent jurisdiction, but for all purposes the effect is as if there never was a marriage, except, however, that the offspring of the parties born subsequent to the marriage contract are considered legitimate under the statute. In the case of 3b there is merely a termination of an existing marriage. 

Rubin v. Joseph, 215 A. D. 91; 213 N. Y. S. 460; 

Sleicher v. Sleicher, 251 N. Y. 366.

In one case a child was born one day before the marriage of the parents. The marriage was later annulled upon the ground of duress. It was held that the marriage did not legitimatize the child, as the legal effect of the annulment was to render the status of the parents the same as they were just prior to the marriage. 

Moncrief's Will, 234 N. Y. 390.

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

163 (7) Divorce. The statute permits one spouse to divorce the other and terminate the marriage relationship with certain limitations under conditions outlined in the discussion which follows. 

Under § 1147 of the Civil Practice Act, one of the parties to the marriage may procure a judgment in New York, divorcing the parties and dissolving the marriage by reason of the defendant's adultery, where one of the four following conditions exists: (a) Both parties were New York residents when the adultery was committed. (b) The parties were married in New York. (c) The plaintiff was a New York resident when the adultery was committed and when the action was commenced. (d) The adultery was committed in New York and the plaintiff was a New York resident when the action was commenced. 

There must be at least one credible witness to the adultery. A confession by the wife is not sufficient. A prostitute or a "professional witness" in divorce cases, or one whose testimony shows him to be shameless in the matter of sex, is not a credible witness, and the testimony should have corroboration. Justices and jurors are justified in looking with suspicion upon such evidence. 

Witchley v. Witchley, 131 Misc. 115; 226 N. Y. S. 95; 

Elsworth v. Elsworth, 221 A. D. 875; 224 N. Y. S. 620.

Opportunity and inclination to adultery, e.g., a woman and a man not her husband registered as man and wife at a hotel and stayed in the same room overnight, make out a sufficient case of adultery, and the witnesses to these are enough without their having seen the actual intercourse. 

Miller v. Miller, 212 A. D. 114; 208 N. Y. S. 113.

In either of the following cases the plaintiff is not entitled to a divorce even though the adultery is established: (a) When the offense was committed with the procurement or the connivance of the plaintiff. (b) Where the adultery has been forgiven by the plaintiff. Voluntary cohabitation with the knowledge of the adultery is equivalent to an affirmative forgiveness.

The knowledge which must exist must be sufficiently substantial upon which to base a belief, and usually there must be some legal proof of the commission of the offense before condonation will be implied from cohabitation. (c) Where the action was not commenced within five years after the discovery by the plaintiff of the offense charged. (d) Where the plaintiff has also been guilty of adultery, so that the defendant would have been entitled, if innocent, to a divorce. 

Civil Practice Act, § 1153.

Suppose a plaintiff wife, in an action for divorce, had committed adultery in 1922. The action for this adultery on the part of the defendant husband was commenced by the husband in 1928. The adultery of the wife had never been forgiven or condoned by the defendant husband. Is the adulterous act of 1922 (of over 5 years' standing) a defense to defeat the plaintiff's action? The court held that it was no defense, since the adulterous act of 1922 would not entitle the defendant to a divorce if innocent. 

Ryan v. Ryan, 132 Misc. 339; 229 N. Y. S. 511.

Although adultery is a crime, the fact of adultery in a civil action may be proved by only a fair preponderance of the evidence. 

Burgess v. Burgess, 173 N. Y. S. 505.

The defendant in a successful divorce suit cannot remarry in New York without permission of the court. This permission is not usually granted before three years have passed, during which time the defendant is on his or her good behavior. He or she can, however, marry in another state, because the provisions of the order of the courts forbidding the remarriage have no extraterritorial effect. The subsequent marriage of the guilty party in a sister state, if valid in that state, will be recognized here as a lawful marriage. 

Fisher v. Fisher, 250 N. Y. 313.

A divorce granted by a rabbi in New York, in accordance with the rabbinical laws of Russia, where the marriage took place, is not a valid divorce.

The New York courts will not recognize the validity of a foreign decree of divorce obtained by one spouse, who moved to another state and brings suit there, based on constructive service of the defendant spouse, who continues to live in New York. A citizen of 

New York has on one occasion been enjoined from proceeding with a divorce action instituted by him in Mexico, against his wife. 

Ackerman v. Ackerman, 200 N. Y. 75; 

Greenberg v. Greenberg, 218 A. D. 104; 218 N. Y. S. 87.

If the defendant has been personally served with the summons in a foreign jurisdiction or appears in the action, New York will recognize the foreign decree under the full faith and credit clause of the 

United States Constitution. 

Parson v. Parson, 230 N. Y. 141.

It would seem, however, that where the parties have established a matrimonial domicile in a foreign jurisdiction, and which is their domicile at the time of the commencement of the divorce proceedings in that state, full faith and credit would be given to a judgment of the court of that state even without a personal appearance by the defendant or personal service upon the defandant. In one instance the New York court recognized a divorce decree obtained in

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

165 California by the husband, a New York resident. He had been abandoned by his wife prior to his removal to California and he thus carried the marital status with him. Since the wife could not acquire a domicile apart from her husband by her abandonment, his domicile was hers. A divorce granted in the state of his domicile upon constructive service, without her appearance, was held valid. 

North v. North, 111 A. D. 921; 

Rocco v. Rocco, 131 Misc. 867; 228 N. Y. S. 405; 

Richards v. Richards, 132 Misc. 551; affd. 231 N. Y. S. 868.

The decree in an action for divorce speaks as of the time of the rendition of the decree. It has therefore been held that a plaintiff in an action for divorce may be permitted by the court to serve a supplemental complaint setting forth adulterous misconduct of the defendant occurring after the commencement of the action. 

Otto v. Otto, 220 A. D. 130; 220 N. Y. S. 513.

Before a final judgment is entered annulling or dissolving a marriage, an interlocutory judgment is entered. The death of either party after the entry of the interlocutory judgment and before it has become final, abates the action. For legal purposes, such as fixing dower rights, or entitling one to letters of administration, the parties were husband and wife at the date of the decease. Alimony 

Grotsch v. Hassey, 133 Misc. 373; 231 N. Y. S. 469.

The court may not award counsel fees or alimony where the wife brings an action for annulment, since such action relates back to the contract of marriage and is equivelent to rescission. The right to counsel fees or alimony is predicated on the husband's duty springing from a valid marriage contract. The wife may obtain this award where she is the defendant in the annulment suit. 

Heafey v. Heafey, 142 Misc. 147; 254 N. Y. S. 82; 

Sweet v. Sweet, 132 Misc. 386; 230 N. Y. S. 317.

Kiel v. Kiel, 261 N. Y. S. 162. 

If a divorce decree in favor of the wife fails to include a provision for maintenance and support of minor children of the husband, who are to remain with the wife, this does not absolve the father from the duty to provide for them. 

Manice v. Randolph, 221 A. D. 570; 224 N. Y. S. 496.

Where allegations of adultery in a wife's complaint were not supported by any proof, and were in fact denied in the husband's answer and affidavit, a motion by the wife for alimony pendente lite and for counsel fees should be denied, as no reasonable likelihood of success in the action was shown. 

Cassese v. Cassese, 223 A. D. 843; 228 N. Y. S. 172.

Parties to an action for divorce may adjust between themselves the amount required for the future maintenance of the wife. This

agreement, if fair and made in good faith, will be accepted by the courts in lieu of any other provision for her support. 

Hamlin v. Hamlin, 224 A. D. 168; 230 N. Y. S. 51.

The remedy for enforcement of alimony is by contempt against the husband, by sequestration, or by reduction of accrued alimony to judgment and issuance of execution on that judgment. 

White v. White, 224 A. D. 355; 231 N. Y. S. 146.

An application to the court for a reduction of alimony is permitted where circumstances have changed.
