Usually the husband will not be heard if he is in arrears at the time of the application, but where the applicant has for a long time obeyed the order and is still trying to do so to the best of his ability, the court in its discretion, may hear him. Especially so if the wife has remarried. 

Kelly v. Kelly, 144 Misc. 302; 258 N. Y. S. 367.

A wife who has obtained a divorce decree ordering the payment of alimony may later ask for an increased allowance if circumstances warrant it. Such increase may be allowed nunc pro tunc, as of the date upon which the motion to increase was made returnable even though the actual decision is made later. 

Harris v. Harris, 259 N. Y. 334.

Separation Agreements 

A husband and wife can make a valid contract for separation and support, provided that they are either living apart at the time they enter into the separation agreement or they intend to separate immediately after the signing of the agreement.¹ 

Marks v. Marks, 127 Misc. 416; 216 N. Y. S. 341.

A valid separation agreement is binding upon the parties until it is set aside by the court, but whatever effect the agreement may have upon the question of alimony, it does not oust the court of jurisdiction to entertain a separation action. Furthermore, if the husband has breached the agreement, the wife may maintain an action for separation if legal grounds exist, and in that event the court may determine de novo, the amount of alimony to which she may be entitled. 

Zysman v. Zysman, 140 Misc. 617; 251 N. Y. S. 355. 

1A very recent case held that a separation agreement is void, if made while the parties are living together, even though they may intend to separate immediately after the signing of the agreement. This is squarely in conflict with the Marks case, but is not controlling, as the two courts were of practically equal jurisdiction. The law on this point is unsettled. The preponderance of the cases holds in favor of the Marks case. For such holdings, see Fives v. 

Fives, 122 Misc. 657, 203 N. Y. S. 585; Weeks v. L'Ecluse, 177 N. Y. S. 284; 

Leary v. Leary, 136 Misc. 13, 242 N. Y. S. 65. 

Stewart v. Stewart, 130 Misc. 59; 223 N. Y. S. 603.

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

167 The agreement can only be set aside by an action in equity where it is shown that the provisions are inequitable, unjust or improvidently entered into. 

Hamlin v. Hamlin, 129 Misc. 263; affd. 227 N. Y. S. 819.

Where there is a valid existing separation agreement at the time the wife sues for divorce, which agreement provides for the support and maintenance of the wife, an order granting alimony and counsel fees is unauthorized.

In the absence of proof showing the invalidity of the agreement or the inadequacy of its provisions, the later enrichment of the husband is of no consequence, and the amounts set out in the separation agreement to be paid by the husband can not therefore be increased. 

Crowell v. Crowell, 135 Misc. 530; 238 N. Y. S. 44.

To rescind a contract of separation under which the wife received a certain amount weekly or monthly, it is not necessary for the plaintiff wife to offer to refund the payments made to her thereunder, because the payments constituted but a partial performance of a duty which the defendant owed in any event, and the ordinary rules applicable to rescission of contracts do not apply. 

Hamlin v. Hamlin, 129 Misc. 263; affd. 227 N. Y. S. 819.

Actions for Separation 

An action for separation may be maintained in either of the following cases: (1) Where both parties are New York residents when the action is commenced. (2) Where the defendant is not a resident at the time the action is commenced, then the plaintiff may maintain the action, if he is a resident and the marriage took place in New York. (3) If the marriage took place without the state, provided the parties became New York residents and continued so for at least one year.

In every case, however, in order to maintain a separation action, the plaintiff must be a resident of the state at the commencement of the action.

Civil Practice Act, § 1162. 

The action may be maintained by a duly authorized representative of an incompetent person. Whether an incompetent may maintain an action for divorce through a guardian ad litem, is an open question in New York. 

Kaplan v. Kaplan, 256 N. Y. 366. 

The grounds for separation are: (1) Abandonment or desertion; (2) failure to support; (3) cruel treatment; (4) conduct rendering cohabitation unsafe or improper.

Abandonment contemplates the voluntary separation of one party from another without justification, with the intention of not returning. The intent is to be determined as of the time of departure. 

Pompilio v. Pompilio, 129 Misc. 207; 221 N. Y. S. 594.

It is not necessary that the plaintiff allege and prove acts of violence to be entitled to a separation. Cruel and inhuman acts are sufficient. A continual ignoring by a husband of his wife, and his refusal to converse or communicate with her, constitute cruelty. 

Causing four unjustifiable arrests for non-support within eight months, was held to be cruel and inhuman treatment, giving the plaintiff the right to a judgment of separation. 

Bergman v. Bergman, 138 Misc. 335; 245 N. Y. S. 439.

A wife's refusal of sexual intercourse is a defense to her action to compel support, even though the parties had agreed that the marriage was not to be consummated until after a religious ceremony, and the husband refused to go through with such religious ceremony. 

Mirisio v. Mirizio, 242 N. Y. 74.

A wife who deserts her husband, loses her right to support, but she may change her mind and come back, provided her desertion has not ripened into abandonment. Should the husband refuse to take her back, she may maintain a separation action. 

If a wife leaves her husband and the absence has not ripened into an abandonment, it is the husband's duty to take her back if she offers to return. If her conduct is persistent and obstinate so that it amounts to an abandonment, the husband may maintain his action for a separation despite an offer on the part of the wife to return. 

Just what length of time a wife must remain away to be considered as having abandoned her husband depends on circumstances. It was held that absence for over 5 years by the wife was not, as a matter of law, abandonment, when the wife had maintained a lawsuit, based upon reasonable grounds and with sincerity, and upon losing the lawsuit she immediately offered to return to her husband. 

Bohmert v. Bohmert, 241 N. Y. 446; 

Mirisio v. Mirisio, 248 N. Y. 175.

Cohabitation after acts of cruelty does not act as a condonation in the sense in which it would after an act of adultery. Endurance of unkind treatment in an effort to overcome its practice does not condone a course of inhuman conduct. 

Fisher v. Fisher, 223 A. D. 19; affd. 250 N. Y. 313.

Where the defendant's acts on which the action for separation is based occurred more than ten years prior to the institution of the suit, the suit is barred by the Statute of Limitations. 

Guerin v. Guerin, 127 Misc. 745; 217 N. Y. S. 1.

In an action by one spouse for a separation, the defendant may counterclaim in divorce, even though the defendant could not have

DOMESTIC RELATIONS 

169 commenced the divorce action because of failure on his part to meet any of the requirements of § 1147 of the Civil Practice Act.¹ 

Rolle v. Rolle, 201 A. D. 698; 194 N. Y. S. 661.

The fact that the adultery relied on did not occur until after the commencement of the separation action should not affect the defendant's right to counterclaim in divorce or to insert a supplemental answer, if an answer had been served. 

Weiss v. Weiss, 135 Misc. 264; 238 N. Y. S. 36.

In an action for separation the conduct of the plaintiff subsequent to the acts complained of are material to the issue, for a plaintiff may have forfeited her right to relief by subsequent misbehavior. 

Vernes v. Vernes, 232 A. D. 707; 247 N. Y. S. 798.

No judgment for a separation can be made by the default of the defendant or by consent. The plaintiff in every case must present his side to the court so that the testimony should justify the decree. 

Greenfield v. Greenfield, 215 A. D. 504; 213 N. Y. S. 643.

A judgment of separation may be revoked by the court upon the joint application of the parties, upon satisfactory evidence of reconciliation. No agreement of the parties has any effect on the decree unless ratified and made effective by judicial sanction. It thus follows that a reconciliation and cohabitation subsequent to a separation judgment providing for alimony do not deprive the wife of the alimony under the decree should the parties again separate after the reconciliation. 

Schatzberg v. Schatzberg, 229 A. D. 214; 241 N. Y. S. 684.

Where a wife has left her husband without justification, and he is ready to take her back, he is not liable even for necessaries furnished to her while she lived apart from him. If they are simply living apart, she has no authority to pledge the credit of her husband for necessaries, and the burden is upon the one seeking to hold the husband for necessaries furnished to the wife to show that the separation was due to the fault of the husband. 

McDermott v. Books, 128 Misc. 17; 217 N. Y. S. 181.

A final judgment of separation in the wife's favor does not relieve the husband of his legal obligation toward his wife's support, even if she has enough property to support herself for a while. 

Stevens v. Stevens, 125 Misc. 451; 211 N. Y. S. 192.

If a separation action is decided in the husband's favor, it does not bar the granting of alimony to the wife in a subsequent divorce action, where she would otherwise be entitled thereto. 

Saltsman v. Saltsman, 228 A. D. 788; 239 N. Y. S. 381.

A judgment fixing the custody of children is incident to a judgment for divorce or separation. If a divorce or a separation is 

1See supra, p. 163.

refused, jurisdiction over the adjudication of the custody is lost. The remedy of habeas corpus or a petition to a court of equity would have to be used. 

Finlay v. Finlay, 240 N. Y. 429.

If a wife gets alimony, it lasts until her husband's death or her death, whichever comes first. In determining the amount of alimony to be paid, the fortune of the husband together with the earning capacity are to be considered. The fact that he has enough to suit him, and therefore does not use his capabilities, does not limit the wife's recovery to a sum based only on the income of the property he has. If the husband will not exert himself, his capacity for earning must be estimated and added to his income. 

Faye v. Faye, 131 Misc. 388; 226 N. Y. S. 729.

The estate of the husband is liable for any alimony which is due and not paid up to the time of his death. Bankruptcy of the husband and later discharge do not relieve him of the duty to pay alimony. 

The wife cannot assign alimony. It is to be paid to her and to no one else.

Alimony cannot be subject to claims of creditors when such claims antedate the allowance of the alimony. Alimony is subject to claims of judgment creditors arising after allowance of alimony, particularly for necessaries. Of course, a court of equity might interfere in a proper case to the extent necessary to protect the sustenance of wife or children even as to a claim arising subsequent to the allowance. 

Tappe v. Battelle, 140 Misc. 49; 249 N. Y. S. 589.

If a wife is divorced because of her adultery, she loses her right to support.

The legal domicile of an infant whose mother has been divorced for her adultery is that of his father. On the father's death the infant's domicile becomes that of the mother. The fact that the mother had been divorced does not prevent her from applying for letters of guardianship over the infant, in the country of her domicile. 

In re Thorne, 240 N. Y. 444. 

Illegitimate Children

Such children are legitimatized if the parents marry at any time subsequently. But if any interest had been vested before this marriage, the legitimatized children cannot affect it in any way.¹

Domestic Relations Law, § 24. 

1For a complete discussion of "Adoption", the reader is referred to Sainer, "Adjective Law of New York”, 6th Ed., p. 234 et seq.

EQUITY The Injunction and Equity Jurisdiction (a) Nuisances 

As a general rule, an owner is at liberty to use his property as he sees fit, without objection or interference from his neighbor, provided such use does not violate an ordinance or statute. There is, however, a limitation to this rule. That one must so use his property as not to injure that of another is deeply imbedded in our law. Sic utere tuo ut alienum non laedas. An owner will not be permitted to make an unreasonable use of his premises to the material annoyance of his neighbor, if the latter's enjoyment of life or property is materially lessened thereby. 

Equity will enjoin a nuisance if the plaintiff can show that he is suffering special damage and that the remedy at law is inadequate. 

We shall observe that running through the entire scope of the jurisdiction of equity over the injunction is the thought that equity will intervene where it appears that damage will result which cannot in good justice be measured by dollars and cents. 

A condition is a nuisance when it clearly appears that the rightful enjoyment of property is wrongfully materially lessened and the physical comfort of persons in their homes is materially interfered with thereby. Whether the particular use to which one puts his property constitutes a nuisance or not is generally a question of fact, and depends upon whether such use is reasonable under all the surrounding circumstances. What would be deemed a torment in one locality would be unnoticed in some other place; a condition which would cause little or no vexation in an industrial district might be extremely tantalizing to those living in a beautiful residential zone. Each case is unique. No hard and fast rule can be laid down which will apply in all instances. The inconvenience, if such it be, must not be fanciful, slight, or theoretical, but certain and substantial, and must interfere with the physical comfort of the ordinarily reasonable person. 

Boye v. Donner-Hanna Coke Co., 236 A. D. 37; 258 N. Y. S. 229; 

McCarty v. Natural 'Carbonic Gas Co., 189 N. Y. 40. 

If the defendant erects a "hot dog" stand in a beautiful residential section, the presence of which stand induces many to surround, overrun and litter the lawns of the plaintiff, and to park their cars along the street and disturb the rest and sleep of the plaintiff and 171

other residents of the locality, the plaintiff is entitled to an injunction. 

The relief at law would be inadequate. 

Andrews v. Perry, 127 Misc. 320; 216 N. Y. S. 537.

The operation in a residential district of a boxing arena seating 20,000 persons, wherein boxing bouts are held one night a week, which draws crowds, autos, buses, etc., constitutes a nuisance which can be enjoined by the property owners. The operation of a large bakery at night, creating noises which disturb the sleep of the neighborhood and produced actual physical discomfort and annoyance to persons of ordinary sensibilities, is a nuisance, even though it is caused by conducting a trade or business in a city. 

Dillon v. Cortland, 224 A. D. 303, 230 N. Y. S. 289;

Russell v. Nostrand Club, 240 N. Y. 681, Case 2. 

The fact that an annoyance is intermittent and not constant does not affect the right of the plaintiff to protection. Contamination of the atmosphere by noxious odors, fumes and refuse from the burning of garbage, if done frequently enough to cause discomfort and annoyance to those living in the neighborhood, and to prevent them from enjoying their property, may be deemed a nuisance. 

Nicoll v. Ossining, 128 Misc. 848; 220 N. Y. S. 345.

If, however, the protection of a legal right would do the plaintiff but comparatively little good and would produce great public or private hardship, equity will withhold its hand and remit the plaintiff to his remedies at law. Equity will balance conveniences. 

McCann v. Chasm Power Co., 211 N. Y. 301.

The fact that the plaintiff purchased his own property long after the nuisance was present, and knew of its existence, does not affect the plaintiff's right to an injunction. 

Andrews v. Perry, 127 Misc. 320; 216 N. Y. S. 537. 

(b) Compelling or Preventing Personal Service 

In two kinds

Equity will not, as a rule, compel personal service. of cases equity may compel service, though personal. One is where not to do so would be aiding fraud. E.g.: Jones conveys a strip of land to a railroad, and the latter promises to build a station near his home and stop there every twelve hours. Should the railroad company refuse, equity would compel the railroad company to carry out its agreement. The second class of cases includes cases where the public is involved. If a railroad defendant promises to do something which would be of great benefit to the public, the other party to the contract can get equity to compel the defendant to perform. 

Equity will enjoin one whose services are unique, or of special value, e.g., such as a great singer, or very popular actor, from performing for anyone else during the period of his contract. The court

EQUITY 

173 will not compel performance of the contract for that would compel the defendant's personal service, and this the equity court will not do. Even though a negative covenant not to appear for anyone else may be lacking in the contract such will be implied and enforced against the parties and against the threatening third parties. 

Rogers v. Comstock, 225 A. D. 34; affd. 252 N. Y. 561.

Before any equitable relief will be granted, the plaintiff must show that his services cannot be replaced. 

Merl v. Janks, 125 Misc. 318; 209 N. Y. S. 530.

A clause in a contract stating that for a breach of the negative covenant the plaintiff might obtain injunctive relief, is not conclusive upon the court. It is merely a concession that the parties recognized the defendant's service to be of a unique and exceptional character. 

Hammerstein v. Safronie, N. Y. Law Journ., July 19, 1928, p. 1734. 

An employee will be enjoined from disclosing trade secrets or confidential communications which he learnt or obtained while in the employer's employ. (c) Specific Performance

Equity will compel specific performance of a contract, the subject matter of which is unique and not to be replaced by money. Land is unique as a matter of law. Thus equity may compel specific performance of an option or a contract to buy and sell land. The remedy at law would be inadequate. So, also, equity may issue an injunction restraining one from leaving the jurisdiction with a unique chattel, which the plaintiff claims. An altar piece, a race horse, a family heirloom, a painting by Titian, stock having a special value (e.g., control of the corporation and not being obtainable in the open market), have been held sufficiently unique to justify specific performance. In such case the question as to whether equity will grant relief rests in the sound discretion of the court, and it cannot be demanded as a matter of right. 

Butler v. Wright, 186 N. Y. 259; 

Waddle v. Cabano, 220 N. Y. 18; 

Schaefer v. Thompson, 237 N. Y. 55.

Suppose the agreement was to convey land in California. Would an equity court in New York compel specific performance? Yes, if it had jurisdiction of the person named as defendant. 

A contract to leave property to a person by will gives rise to a right which can be enforced in equity. The beneficiary, if a child of one of the contracting parties, may sue upon the contract, although not a party to the contract itself. Specific performance may be maintained. 

Thomas v. Hens, 219 A. D. 627; 220 N. Y. S. 785.

Specific performance will not lie to compel the making of repairs under a covenant in a lease. 

Beck v. Allison, 56 N. Y. 366.

Equity will compel a licensee, who received a free license to store material on the licensor's land, to remove it on the day promised. 

A remedy at law would be inadequate, for it would give damages and not the land. But if J contracted to deliver fifty tons of coal, and instead he delivered only ten and refused to deliver any more, there can be no specific performance. The law remedy is adequate, for the coal is not unique. 

Wheelock v. Noonan, 108 N. Y. 179.

Equity will not enforce any contract where both parties are not bound. But let us take this case: A memorandum is signed only by 

Hite, in a contract which is required to be in writing. Brown desires specific performance, but Hite puts up the defense that, since Brown. would not be bound as he did not sign, there is no mutuality of obligation and Hite should therefore not be bound. But the rule is that this contract will be enforced if all the other elements necessary to give equity jurisdiction are there. Brown, by asking equity to give him relief, had put himself under the jurisdiction of the court and subjected himself to its orders, and therefore there is mutuality.¹ 

The time that the parties need to be bound in order to have mutuality is when the decree is to be given. So if a vendor of realty did not have title at the time he made the contract, he could still be compelled to convey if he had obtained the title before the decree was to be given. There was mutuality then. 

If a party seeks specific performance in a situation when he knew it had become impossible at the time the action was begun, e.g., the defendant had conveyed the land in dispute to another, equity has no jurisdiction and the plaintiff has only a legal remedy in a court of law. If, however, the plaintiff, in ignorance of the truth, sought relief in good faith, then equity would assess damages. 

Saperstein v. Mechanics, etc., Bank, 228 N. Y. 257.

Though the assignee under a contract for the sale of realty may get specific performance if he is ready, willing and able to perform the terms of the contract as therein provided, the mere assignment of a bilateral executory contract may not be interpreted as a promise. by the assignee to assume performance of the assignor's duties so as to be liable to be compelled to perform. The assignee is under no personal obligation because of the assignment, unless he has expressly or impliedly bound himself to perform. Where the assignee 

1For a further discussion of "Mutuality" see supra, p. 101.

EQUITY 

175 invokes the aid of the equity court against the original party to the contract, he impliedly binds himself to perform.¹ 

Epstein v. Gluickin, 233 N. Y. 490; 

Langel v. Betz, 250 N. Y. 159.

Although equity will not ordinarily enforce a sale of land when there is an incumbrance or unmarketable title, it will do so if the vendee requests it. Of course there will be a decrease in price. 

It should be noted, however, that not every purchaser under a contract of sale of real property is entitled to specific performance with an abatement, if the facts are not as stated in the contract. This privilege applies if the deficiency is in the subject-matter of the contract. Thus, it would not apply if the seller misrepresented the income of the property. If the purchaser would not take the property as is, he could only sue for damages for breach of contract. 

Radel v. 134 W. 15th St. Bldg. Co., 222 A. D. 617; 226 N. Y. S. 560; 

Chesebro v. Moers, 233 N. Y. 75; 

Feldman v. Lisansky, 239 N. Y. 81.

Specific performance of a contract to convey land, which does not satisfy the Statute of Frauds, will not be granted. 

Life Savers' Club v. Mosher, 125 Misc. 341; 209 N. Y. S. 741.

Payment alone, even though in full, will not take the case out of the statute. In such a case the vendee has his action at law to recover the purchase price or such part as he has paid, when the vendor refuses to convey; and thereby the complaining party can be restored to the condition he enjoyed before making the contract. The acts and facts that will justify an exercise of the equitable powers of the court are those which constitute fraud or are the equivalent of fraud, and where the victim cannot be fully compensated in money, or otherwise restored to his former condition. Thus where one buys land and pays for it, makes improvements that are substantial and permanent, and that are induced by the contract; where land is purchased pursuant to a contract with the money of one but in the name of another; where a confidential relation exists, and the confidence has been abused; where there was, at the time of making the contract, a preconceived intent to violate it-these and similar acts are such as will induce a court of equity to intervene. In fact, fraud in any form, whether actual or constructive, or the equivalent, which brings injury to the victim and which is not remediable at law, may result in such a part performance as will induce a court of equity to protect the innocent party. It is the fraud or its equivalent, that gives rise 

¹In the restatement of the Law of Contracts (Sec. 164) it is declared that in the absence of circumstances showing a contrary intention the assignment of a contract, wholly or partially executed on both sides, binds the assignee to perform. This is not the law in this state.

to equity jurisdiction and not the oral contract, which is void under the statute. 

Zelser v. Yorkville Park Co., 141 Misc. 190; 252 N. Y. S. 626.

Let us take this case: F makes an oral contract with S where, in consideration of the agreement on the part of S to board and care for F as long as F should live, he agrees to devise certain realty to S. 

F died and left no will. Can S, who carried out her bargain most faithfully, enforce this contract for the transfer of realty, in a court of equity? The court said "Yes," because the following elements, all of which are necessary in cases of this kind, were present: (1) the contract was definite as to parties, consideration, and the property involved; (2) the plaintiff proved her case by a preponderance of the evidence; (3) the plaintiff's recovery would not be inequitable nor unjust, because she had faithfully performed; and (4) her performance was referable unequivocally to the contract. 

Cf. Canute v. Minor, 232 A. D. 325; 249 N. Y. S. 680. 

Salem v. Finney, 127 Misc. 387; 215 N. Y. S. 553; 

(d) Clouds Upon Title 

Equity will act to remove a cloud upon title if the deed is valid on its face and the defendant is not in possession. If the defendant is in possession, the plaintiff should use the remedy of ejectment, which would try the title. 

Equity will restrain a defendant from proceeding in an illegal action against the plaintiff's land, which action, if completed, will cast a cloud upon the plaintiff's title. 

De Witt v. Van Schoyk, 110 N. Y. 7.

Equity will enjoin a continuous trespass and thus prevent a multiplicity of suits for the recovery of damages. (e) Unfair Competition

Where two stores were located near each other, and did business of a similar character, and the plaintiffs had opened in 1919 under the name of "Louis' Restaurant," and the defendant in 1928 under the name of "Lois' Restaurant," equity issued an injunction enjoining the defendant from using the name "Lois' Restaurant," when the plaintiffs clearly proved their case by showing that they had created a good-will, and that confusion had resulted, or probably would result, because of the close similarity of the business and names. 

Louis' Restaurant v. Coffey, 132 Misc. 690; 230 N. Y. S. 82; 

Gotham Silk Hosiery Co. v. Reingold, 223 A. D. 260.

The fact that the second user acts innocently does not aid the defendant, if the plaintiff's design or mark was in fact stimulated. 

Gotham Silk Hosiery Co. v. Reingold, 223 A. D. 260; 228 N.Y.S.9; 

Albany Packing Co. v. Crispo, 227 A. D. 591; affd. 253 N. Y. 607,

Even in cases where family names are involved, a person may not give his name to a corporation and thereby take from another his

EQUITY 

177 good will and rights which he has acquired by the use of such name, especially where the purpose is to mislead the public as to the identity of the establishments. 

Gabriel v. Niederstein, 230 A. D. 716; 243 N. Y. S. 472.

In one case the plaintiff used the name "Tecla" in a certain script. 

The defendant used the same name similarly written. The plaintiff sold jewelry, the defendant cosmetics. Although the parties were not competitors the defendant was enjoined from writing or printing the word "Tecla" on any advertising matter in the characteristic script as used by plaintiff. 

Tecla Corp. v. Salon Tecla, 249 N. Y. 157.

If one party sends to the customers of a competitor false or misleading circulars or reading matter which would deceive the public and thus take away the competitor's business, the latter can successfully ask equity to enjoin the circularizing of the false material. 

Old Investors', etc., Co. v. Jenkins, 133 Misc. 213; affd. 233 

N. Y. S. 845.

One who has sold the good-will of his business has no right to solicit trade from old customers, even though the absence of a covenant to refrain from competition may permit him to carry on a competing business. Former customers of the firm to which the seller belongs may of their own accord transfer their trade to him. 

Tierney Sons v. Tierney Bros., 130 Misc. 428; 224 N. Y. S. 144.

The damages recoverable for such breach are limited strictly to losses which the buyer sustains, including loss of profits and diminution in value of the property purchased. 

Present v. Glazer, 225 A. D. 23; 232 N. Y. S. 63. 

(f) Restrictive Covenants 

Equity will enforce restrictive covenants. The law governing this subject may be summarized through the following examples: (1) Suppose X owns a whole block of lots. He prepares a building scheme, under which the tract is divided into lots to be sold to different purchasers for separate occupancy, by deeds containing uniform covenants restricting the use which the grantees may make of their premises. X sells the lots to various buyers showing them the building plan, and each buyer covenants for himself, his heirs and his assigns, not to use the premises for anything but residences. 

These covenants for uniform developments "run with the land" and future grantees of these buyers are bound by the covenants in the deeds. 

Rose v. Jasima, 218 A. D. 646; 219 N. Y. S. 222; 

Krekeler v. Aulbach, 51 A. D. 591; 64 N. Y. S. 908.

The covenants are not only enforceable by X and his successors, but they are enforceable by any grantee as against any other, upon

the theory that there is a mutuality of covenants and consideration which binds each and gives each the appropriate remedy. 

Besch v. Hyman, 221 A. D. 238; 223 N. Y. S. 231. (2) One person selling a tract of land may, for the benefit of his remaining land and also for the benefit of his grantees, impose any restrictions upon the land granted that he sees fit, which are not against public policy. These restrictions are enforceable in equity by the grantor or his assigns in favor of the property benefited and retained by the grantor against the property burdened. The restrictive covenants run with the land and bind subsequent purchasers. 

The fact that others have violated this restriction and that other actions have not been brought to enjoin and restrain such violations, is immaterial, McCain v. Aylesworth, 128 Misc. 408; 219 N. Y. S. 59.

Suppose, however, Jones owns four lots in a residential section and he sells them with restrictive covenants of a similar nature binding the heirs and assigns, of course. Years later the character of the neighborhood changes and becomes a manufacturing district. assignee of the title of one of these lots wants to change his house into a factory. Will equity enjoin him at the suit of his neighbors and enforce the restrictive covenant? No. The covenant has become of little value to the residential people, and equity will not enforce such a worthless covenant. The party aggrieved has his remedy at law. 

Amerman v. Deane, 132 N. Y. 355.

It seems, then, that in enforcing restrictive covenants equity will consider the balance of conveniences, and an injunction will be withheld when the injury is not serious or substantial, and the defendant would be subject to great inconvenience and loss in comparison to the inconvenience and loss which might be suffered by the plaintiff. 

Forstmann v. Joray, 244 N. Y. 22.

A railroad grantee in a deed covenanted to pay for any damages caused by sparks or cinders from the railroad, to the ice in the lake of the grantor, his heirs or assigns. It was held to run with the land. 

Morgan Lake v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R., 245 N. Y. S. 116.

A covenant running with the land will enure to the benefit of the grantor and his heirs and assigns, even though the original agreement did not mention "assigns" of the original parties. 

Real Property Law, § 257; 

St. Regis v. Powers, 219 A. D. 321; 219 N. Y. S. 684.

A court of equity may, by injunction, prevent the defendant's lessee from using a building for a purpose which violates the restrictive covenants of the lease. 

Lyon v. Bethlehem Eng. Corp., 253 N. Y. 111.

EQUITY 

179 A duty to speak does not arise except when the owner's property is being improved, or appropriated by another. One may not sit by passively and allow his property to be sold or conveyed, nor may he silently suffer it to be improved with the expensive structures and later receive the aid of equity. But, where the neighbor is building structures on his own property and there is no actual invasion of the plaintiff's property, there arises no duty to speak. (g) Crime 

Noble v. Echo Lake Tavern, 142 Misc. 427; 254 N. Y. S. 662.

A court of equity takes no jurisdiction over the prosecution, punishment or pardon of crimes or misdemeanors. The jurisdiction does, however, exist to restrain pending or threatened criminal prosecution under an unconstitutional enactment, provided the prevention. of such prosecution is essential to the safeguarding of rights and property, and jurisdiction also exists to restrain criminal acts which threaten to destroy property rights. Where there is proof of a threatened wrong which the courts have power to enjoin, there may be room for the exercise of a sound judicial discretion in determining whether that power should be exercised. 

I. R. T. v. Lavin, 247 N. Y. 830; 

Monroe v. Quigley, 130 Misc. 357; 223 N. Y. S. 830. (h) Negotiable Documents

Equity takes jurisdiction to enjoin the transfer of unmatured negotiable instruments to the end that a defense of the maker may not be lost to him. Thus equity will act to enjoin the transfer of a usurious note in order to prevent its sale to a bank, which latter could and might collect despite the usury under Banking Law, § 114. 

Weissman v. Naitove, 125 Misc. 647; 211 N. Y. S. 740.

If an equity decree is disobeyed, the person so disobeying can be jailed for contempt of court. (i) Equitable Estoppel

The doctrine of equitable estoppel, or estoppel in pais, is that a party may be precluded by his acts and conduct from asserting a right to the detriment or prejudice of another party who, entitled to rely on such conduct, has acted upon it. 

Draper v. Oswego, 190 N. Y. 12.

Before relief will be given it must be shown either (a) that there was actual fraud practised by the plaintiff, relied upon and acted upon by the defendant, or (b) that the plaintiff led the defendants into making an outlay in order to do the very thing to which the plaintiff now objects, by leading the defendant to assume that he had a right to do it, when the plaintiff knew that he had not. Where, a riparian owner (plaintiff) saw the owner on the opposite side of

a stream building a factory upon his own land and digging a ditch which the plaintiff knew would divert the water to a point below the plaintiff's land, the plaintiff's failure to object did not constitute a ground for estoppel barring an action to recover for the diversion. There was silence, but there must be a positive duty to speak, before a failure to do so can estop the owner. 

N. Y. Rubber Co. v. Rothery, 107 N. Y. 310.

Where a defendant makes use of the statute of frauds to effectuate a fraudulent purpose, under circumstances where to permit a guilty defendant to rely upon that statute would be to help in the perpetration of the fraud, equity may forbid such misuse of the statute. 

Wagner v. Mfrs. Trust Co., 261 N. Y. S. 736.

Rescission of Contracts 

If P, because of the false representations made to him by D, enters into a contract with D, P upon the discovery of the fraud has 

(a) He may rescind the contract absolutely in equity and sue in a law action to recover any consideration with which he has parted. 

He need neither allege nor prove damage, or that the alleged misrepresentations were knowingly made or made with fraudulent intent. 

It is sufficient if the representations were false, for the fraudulent intent or damages are important only in tort actions for deceit. 

Sedgefield v. 440 W. End Av. Corp., 228 A. D. 138; 239 N. Y. S. 178. (b) He may bring an action in equity to rescind and in that action have full relief. It is sufficient in this case for the plaintiff to offer in his complaint to return what he has received and to make tender of it on the trial. (c) He may retain what he has received and bring an action at law to recover damages sustained. This affirms the contract. 

Garage, 226 A. D. 235; 234 N. Y. S. 

Comm. C. Co. v. Third, etc., 463. 

The representations to warrant rescission, must not be mere expressions of opinion or promissory in their nature. A representation that the premises are worth upwards of $7,000 is an expression of opinion; a representation that certain roads leading to the premises would be filled in is promissory. So, too, is the representation that the defendant would secure for the plaintiff a building loan mortgage to enable the latter to commence the erection of a building on the premises. 

Brang v. Stachnick, 235 A. D. 591; 257 N. Y. S. 671.

When an action is brought solely for rescission, the plaintiff must tender everything that he has received, including all profits realized from the articles obtained, e.g., dividends from stock. There cannot

EQUITY 

181 be partial rescission. If part of the goods received had been sold before the discovery of the grounds entitling him to rescission, the plaintiff may tender what remains immediately on the discovery of the grounds and tender a money allowance for what has been sold. 

Friedman v. Richman, 213 A. D. 467; affd. 215 N. Y. 576; 

Marr v. Tumulty, 256 N. Y. 15.

Where, however, profits have been accepted with knowledge of the facts, he will be held to have waived the fraud and to have ratified his purchase. 

Brennan v. National, 247 N. Y. 486.

One who is induced by fraud to enter into a contract is not bound, as a condition of rescission, to restore what has been received, where the other party is indebted to him out of the same transaction in a larger amount. 

Brennan v. National, 221 A. D. 658; affd. 247 N. Y. 486.

A rescission will be granted if P enters into a contract where D accepts an offer of P consisting of a list of articles for a total price, each item marked with a certain price and the items added up incorrectly to a wrong total. Another case would be where the buyer is led by the fraud of the seller to believe he is buying the whole plot of land, when the actual agreement is for part only, and this fraud induced the buyer to make the purchase. The remedy is to rescind in equity or an action at law in deceit. 

Weigel v. Cook, 237 N. Y. 136.

In such a case evidence of prior conversations can be put in, because in an action for fraud statements made before the signing of the contract are admissible. The plaintiff is not obliged to investigate so as to ascertain the truth. It is enough that he relied on the defendant without being negligent in so doing. 

There is a distinction between a rescission for fraud, which goes upon the theory that a contract is treated to be non-existent for lack of true assent, and rescission for abandonment, which goes upon the theory that a contract is avoided for non-performance by the other party, though it was valid in its origin. In the first case, notice of rescission must follow promptly upon the discovery of the fraud. In the other case, notice may be given at any time within the period of the Statute of Limitations, unless delay would be inequitable. 

Rescission for abandonment is, in the strict sense, not really a rescission. What is meant is that the injured party elects to disregard the contract and demands restitution. 

Schochet v. Public Natl. Bank, 220 A. D. 201; 221 N. Y. S. 154.

When the deception induced by misrepresentation relates to the very nature of the contract which the plaintiff is induced to execute, and produces such essential error that the person tricked may be said to have never intended to make any contract whatever of the nature

alleged, the so-called contract is deemed void ab initio, and rescission is not the remedy. Equity need not be called upon to relieve the plaintiff. 

Telford v. Metropolitan Life, 223 A. D. 175; affd. 250 N. Y. 528.

Every contracting party has a right to rely on the express statement of an existing fact, the truth of which is known to the opposite party, and unknown to him, as the basis of a mutual engagement, and he is under no obligation to investigate and verify the statements to the truth of which the other party to the contract, with full means of knowledge, has deliberately pledged his faith. 

Rucksttuhl v. Healy, 222 A. D. 152; 225 N. Y. S. 570.

Equity takes jurisdiction in divorce and annulment cases, since originally the jurisdiction was in the Court of Chancery. The 

Supreme Court received the jurisdiction as successor to the Chancellor. The courts have no common-law jurisdiction over the subject of divorce. 

Ryan v. Ryan, 132 Misc. 339; 229 N. Y. S. 511; 

City of N. Y. v. Kaiser, 125 Misc. 637; 210 N. Y. S. 598.

Equity and Reformation of Contracts 

If there is a mistake of fact made by both sides, or a mistake of fact on one side and fraud by the other, equity can reform the contract to conform to the ideas both sides were supposed to have had. 

An action to reform a written agreement rests upon the theory that the parties came to an understanding, but in reducing it to writing, through mutual mistake, or through mistake on one side and fraud on the other, omitted some provisions agreed upon or inserted something not agreed upon. The object of the action is to so change the instrument as written as to make it conform to the agreement as made, by inserting the provision omitted, or striking out the one inserted by a mutual mistake.

A mistake on one side without fraud may be a ground for rescission or for refusing to enforce a specific provision, but it cannot be a ground for altering its terms. 

Curtis v. Albee, 167 N. Y. 360; 

Metzger v. Aetna Ins. Co., 227 N. Y. 411, 417.

Ordinary negligence of a plaintiff in failing to read an instrument is no defense to an action for reformation, unless it can be shown that the negligence was so gross as to amount to a violation of a positive legal duty. Thus, where an attorney drew a mortgage and made it payable in five instead of ten years as agreed between the parties, it was held that the mortgagor could have it reformed, despite the mortgagor's failure to read the instrument before he signed and the absence of artifice to prevent his reading it. 

Andrews v. Gillespie, 47 N. Y. 487.

EQUITY 

183 Where a vendee agreed that the sale was subject to a lease held by Z, who was in possession, and the scrivener omitted to mention Z's lease, the vendor can get reformation of the agreement. The buyer cannot get damages because possession was refused him. There being no misrepresentation, rescission is not the action, but reformation. 

The mistake was one of fact, made by the scrivener. So where there has been no mistake in the agreement, but a mistake in reducing it to writing, an action may be maintained for reformation. 

Avery v. Equit. Life Assur. Co., 117 N. Y. 451; 

Baird v. Erie R. R. Co., 210 N. Y. 225; 

Unger v. Weisner, 129 Misc. 363; 221 N. Y. S. 489.

Where the parties agreed upon a sale of stock at its actual value as shown by the books of the corporation, and in writing the contract the term "face value" was used upon the representation of one party that this meant actual value, reformation was held proper relief. 

If there had been a mistake of law, i.e., a mistake as to the legal import of the contract made, as where both thought that what they signed was valid, whereas it was not, equity will give no relief. 

A man is presumed to know the law, and for that reason he is not aided because of a mistake of law. This is carried to such an extreme that it has been held that one is supposed to know that a law is unconstitutional, so as to be held responsible for that knowledge, even before the court declares it unconstitutional. The statute so declared unconstitutional is presumed to have been known by all to be a nullity from the time of its enactment, even though the fact that it was unconstitutional is not decided until a long time afterwards. 

In cases where there has been a mistake of law on one side, and either positive fraud or unfair conduct on the other, equity will afford relief. The existence of a confidential relationship such as husband and wife, or attorney and client, may found the basis of a charge of constructive fraud and the court may give equitable relief. 

Haviland v. Willets, 141 N. Y. 35.

Equity will not enforce or reform any contract if the petitioner was not absolutely fair and honest in his own dealings in the transaction. He must come into equity with clean hands. Thus, if A transfers property to X so as to keep it out of the hands of A's creditors and it is understood that the property is to be redelivered at the end of the year, A cannot get equity to compel a reconveyance to himself if X refuses. A was not fair and honest in his dealings. 

Knowlton v. Congress, etc., Spring Co., 57 N. Y. 518; 

Dressel v. Hander, 101 Misc. 574; 168 N. Y. S. 542.

Equity will not aid a plaintiff if he has attempted to recover in a law action on the same facts; he had one choice, and he made it. 

Where a reformation of the instrument would operate to the actual prejudice of the intervening rights of bona fide purchasers or

incumbrancers, a court of equity, as a general rule, would not exercise its jurisdiction. 

Burlingham v. Hanrahan, 140 Misc. 512; 251 N. Y. S. 55.

Equity and Trusts-Accountings 

Equity's jurisdiction covers the subject of trusts. Equity will impute an intention on the part of trustees to carry out their obligations as trustees. 

Equity has stretched this jurisdiction to cover situations even where there is a remedy at law, which remedy, however, is not the best remedy. Where one receives stolen goods knowing them to have been stolen, he holds the same ex maleficio for the benefit of the cestui que trust, despite the fact that the plaintiff owner has a remedy in conversion for the value of the merchandise at the time and place of conversion. The equity court has seen fit to give the benefit of its jurisdiction in such a situation and not to leave the plaintiff to a mere money judgment for the value. Thus, if the wrongdoer resells, the true owner is entitled to follow the res, if possible, and impress the trust; or if this cannot be done, he is entitled to an accounting of the profit made as well as for the value. 

The basis for an equitable action for an accounting is the existence of a fiduciary or trust relation with reference to the subject matter of the controversy. A bare agency is not sufficient. It must appear that the person from whom the accounting is required has been intrusted with the custody and control of the property of another, with all the powers incident and necessary thereto, and in pursuance thereof has exercised such powers for the owner. No particular words are necessary to create the relation. 

Pelkey v. Pelkey, 258 N. Y. S. 562.

Likewise, equity takes exclusive jurisdiction in actions for accounting where there are involved a trustee and cestui, guardian and ward, partnership, other joint enterprises including a relationship of trust and confidence, not generally, however, employee and employer. 

If the balance between the parties has been struck and the joint matter completely wound up, a court of law is competent to act. 

Sloane v. U. S. Syndicate, 135 Misc. 365; 238 N. Y. S. 91.

Equity ordinarily will not aid a volunteer, i.e., one who has not given any consideration. An exception to this rule exists in the case of a completed trust. If a trust is completely created, equity will enforce it even at the instance of a volunteer. 

In actions in equity the rights of the parties are determined as they exist at the date of the decree. In actions at law the rights of 1

EQUITY 

185 the parties are determined as they existed at the date of the commencement of the action. 

Bardel v. Standard Oil Co., 218 A. D. 145; 218 N. Y. S. 36; 

Wappler v. Woodbury Co., 246 N. Y. 152.

Rights of Heirs and Representatives 

Suppose A on the 10th contracts to convey land to J on the 30th of the month. On the 20th A dies, the heirs then getting title. J desires to compel a conveyance. The statute permits the executor or administrator of A to make a deed reciting the contract and convey-Car ing the land. The transfer becomes effective upon confirmation by the court on notice to the persons interested. But the personal representatives take the money left by the deceased; and since a vendor of realty becomes the owner of the purchase money, the money that J pays goes to A's representatives. 

Surrogate's Court Act, § 227; 

Waxson Realty Corp. v. Rothschild, 255 N. Y. 332.

But what if the one who died (A in this case) had been the buyer? 

Then the personal representatives would have had to pay for the land and the heirs would have received it, as the contract vendee of realty becomes the equitable owner of the land. The land descends as real estate. 

Klapp v. Dealy, 213 A. D. 523; 211 N. Y. S. 22; 

In re Campbell's Estate, 131 Misc. 912; 229 N. Y. S. 301; 

In re Kelleher's Estate, 133 Misc. 581; 232 N. Y. S. 680.

Assume now that A had been a lessor and owner, and J the lessee, with an option to buy the land on payment of $5,000. A dies before the exercise of the option. The heirs have now become the lessors. 

They take the land as owners, since there is no outstanding contract to convey. But they take it subject to the lease. Should J exercise his option, the money would go to A's heirs.

The proceeds of the real estate of a minor sold during his minority pass as realty at the death of the minor before the age of 21. If the realty is sold under a decree of foreclosure, or in a partition action, the fund is considered realty and passes to the heirs of the infant. 

This is statutory, the objects of the statute being to preserve and continue the character of the property during the infant's minority and to avoid giving the infant a power of testamentary disposition which he does not otherwise possess. 

In re Charbonneau, 129 Misc. 356; 221 N. Y. S. 449;

Civil Practice Act, § 1402. 

Equitable Conversion 

A positive direction by a testator that his realty is to be converted into cash indicates that the conversion is to be made as of the time of the testator's death and the realty is treated as personalty for all purposes under the will. If, however, the conversion of the

realty was directed in order to carry out a certain purpose, and the purpose could be carried out without effecting a conversion, or that purpose was illegal or unlawful, no conversion is effected. 

In re Hanis Will, 213 A. D. 487; affd. 242 N. Y. 536.

The fact that an executor has discretionary power to sell the realty during the existence of a life estate held by the testator's widow is not enough to convert the proceeds into personalty for the purposes of distribution under the will. The fund retains its character as realty for the purposes of distribution. 

Hoyt v. Hoyt, 125 Misc. 95; 210 N. Y. S. 155.

Where, however, the conversion of realty into personalty is not merely an incident to the devise or merely a convenient arrangement, but it was really the testator's intention to distribute personalty, a valid equitable conversion will be held to exist. 

The fact that the time and manner of the sale of the realty are left to the judgment of the executor does not mean that there is no equitable conversion. Thus a provision in a will leaving the family residence to a widow for life, with provision that after her death the premises should form part of the residuary estate to be divided among the children, was held to effect an equitable conversion. 

In re Grefe's Will, 140 Misc. 134; 250 N. Y. S. 269.

When there has been an equitable conversion, a partition action cannot be maintained. 

Allison v. Coffin, 125 Misc. 615; 211 N. Y. S. 545.

Conflicting Equities 

If S contracts with B1 for the sale of a piece of land, and the former makes a similar contract with B2, B1 would prevail because his equity was prior to that of B2. The same rule would apply if a trustee of land contracted to convey it to a third party. The cestui would keep the land because his equity was a prior one. 

But suppose that instead of contracting to sell to B2, S actually sold the land to him. B2 gets a good title, for he was a bona fide purchaser for value and got the legal title rather than an equity. 

Whenever the legal title conflicts with the equitable, the legal prevails. 

But where the purchaser has paid no consideration or has actual or constructive notice of any outstanding equity, even though he receives legal title, he nevertheless takes subject to such equities. 

Jayne v. Cortland, 107 A. D. 517; 95 N. Y. S. 227.

In New York the rule also is that a bona fide first mortgagee's rights take preference over the equitable rights of one who contracted to buy the land, even though the mortgage was made after the contract to convey. And this despite the fact that a mortgagee of realty does not have the legal title.

EQUITY 

187 X owned lots 1, 2 and 3. He mortgaged the three lots. He sold lot 1 to A, lot 2 to B, both subject to the mortgage, and he kept lot 3. 

The mortgagee of the three lots attempted to foreclose for non-payment. The mortgagee cannot pick the lot to be first foreclosed. The lot not sold (lot 3) must be first foreclosed. After that the lots must be foreclosed in the inverse order of their sale, i.e., lot 2 and then lot 1.

Laches and the Statute of Limitations There is a ten-year Statute of Limitations in equity. But a plaintiff who has been guilty of laches may be barred, even though the ten-year period has not elapsed, and even though the defendant does not plead the laches of the plaintiff. 

The rule of laches is applied in the following manner. If the plaintiff has negligently failed to press his claim in equity within a reasonable time and the delay has occasioned loss or hardship to the defendant or misled him to his injury, the plaintiff will be barred in equity, though the period allowed by the Statute of Limitations may not yet have ended.

The hardship may consist of loss of evidence, change of title, intervention of equities, incurring expenses in improving the property which is the subject of a contract or sale, or a considerable rise in the value of the property. 

Seligson v. Weiss, 222 A. D. 634; 227 N. Y. S. 338.

Where there is concurrent jurisdiction in law and equity, equity is bound by the Statute of Limitations of the law remedy. 

Minion v. Warner, 238 N. Y. 413, 418.

Insurable Interest

INSURANCE 

A life insurance policy upon the life of another is not valid, (1) unless obtained with the acquiescence of the person insured, and (2) unless he had an insurable interest in the person insured at the time the policy was issued. In order to collect on a fire, marine or personal property damage policy, there must be an insurable interest at the time the loss occurs. 

Miller v. Stuyvesant, 131 Misc. 770; 227 N. Y. S. 324.

The discussion of insurable interest must take into consideration a difference between life policies and other policies. (1) Life insurance policies.-Everyone has an insurable interest in his own life. The fact that one makes another the beneficiary is immaterial.

One spouse has an insurable interest in the other spouse. In this case and in the case of an employer taking out a life policy covering his employees collectively, and in the instance where a person is liable for the support of a child, a life policy may be obtained without the consent of the insured. 

Hilmes v. Gilman, 138 N. Y. 369.

A relative has an insurable interest in the life of another relative, provided the person who takes the policy has a pecuniary interest in the life of the relative whose life is insured. 

One has an insurable interest in the life of a fiance or fiancee. 

Bogart v. Thompson, 24 Misc. 581; 53 N. Y. S. 622.

A creditor has an insurable interest in a debtor's life, even after the Statute of Limitations has run against the debt, but the creditor's insurance must not be very disproportionate to the amount of the debt. The payment of the debt by the debtor after his life has been insured does not affect the policy. It is in full force, and when 

J dies D can collect, for he had had an insurable interest when the policy was taken out. So, also, if a wife takes out a policy on the life of her husband, she can collect on the policy at his death, even though they had divorced each other after the issuance of the policy. 

Reed v. Provident S. L. A. Socy., 190 N. Y. 111.

Except in cases similar to the debtor-creditor relationship, there is no limit to the amount for which one can insure a life. (2) Fire insurance policies.-The owner of property has an insurable interest in his own property. His recovery is not limited to his loss, but rather by the value of the property destroyed. Thus, 1 188

INSURANCE 

189 an owner on whose land a building is being erected has an insurable interest to the full value of the building, even though he may compel the contractor to stand the loss. 

Foley v. Mfgr's Fire Ins. Co., 152 N. Y. 131.

A mortgagee also has an insurable interest in the property of which he is the mortgagee, limited to the amount of the mortgage debt. A tenant, and one who is a remainderman (either vested or contingent), also has an insurable interest. 

Kent v. Aetna, 84 A. D. 428; 82 N. Y. S. 817; 

Modern v. Concordia, 222 A. D. 764; 226 N. Y. S. 63.

Analogous to the case of a contingent remainderman is the case of one who is the contract vendee of a piece of property. He has an insurable interest, even though he has not yet obtained full legal title. 

One who has already contracted to sell a house maintains an insurable interest until delivery.

If the contract of sale makes no mention of insurance, the vendor may collect on any policies if the property burns after the making of the contract and before title passes. The loss falls upon the vendee. 

Brownell v. Board of Education, 239 N. Y. 369; 

Reife v. Osmers, 252 N. Y. 320.

A carrier or other bailee has an insurable interest in the goods which it carries or holds. Any legal or equitable interest is enough to give an insurable interest. 

Symmers v. Carroll, 207 N. Y. 632. 

A bailee may insure against loss by theft or fire of any property in his hands up to the full value thereof, and may recover, as trustee for the bailor, the actual loss sustained. 

Mord v. N. Y. Indemnity Co., 216 A. D. 252; affd. 244 N. Y. 589.

If one insures when he has no insurable interest, he gets nothing. 

If he gives notes for the premiums, the company cannot collect on the notes, because they were based on a void policy. 

Valued Policy

A valued policy is one in which a definite valuation is placed, by agreement, upon the subject-matter of the insurance and written in the face of the policy. Such valuation, in the absence of fraud or mistake, is conclusive upon the parties.

Thus, in case of a total loss under a valued policy, the amount recoverable is the amount of the policy, without regard to the depreciation or other elements entering into the market value of the loss. 

A loss is total only when the property has sustained such damage that repairs would not be reasonably practicable. 

Lee v. Hamilton, 251 N. Y. 230.

Statute of Frauds 

A contract of insurance being a contract of indemnity, i.e., to pay on the happening of an event and not if another default, it is valid though not in writing.¹

The Binder 

1 A binder is intended to be superseded by a formal policy. It is a present contract of insurance issued to protect the assured temporarily while the assurer investigates the risk and finally determines whether or not to issue a permanent policy. It includes, however, all the obligations according to the terms of the policies in ordinary use by the insurer. If the form of the policy is fixed by the statute, then the statutory provisions are held to be included in the binder. 

Ell Dee Clothing Co. v. Marsh, 247 N. Y. 392.

Hostile and Friendly Fires 

A fire which does not pass beyond the limits in which it is supposed to be confined, e.g., a stove or furnace, is called a friendly fire, and the insured is not entitled to recover for damages caused by the smoke or heat escaping from the proper confines of the fire. If the fire extends beyond the place in which it properly belongs, it is a hostile fire and the insured is entitled to collect for damages resulting, provided the fire was not purposely caused by the insured. 

Warranties, Representations and Conditions A warranty is a basic part of the insurance contract. A breach of warranty destroys the rights under the contract. A representation, if it is to avoid the policy, must concern itself with a material part of the policy. Since policies are written in the language of the insurer, all ambiguous and doubtful clauses will be construed against the insurer. The tendency is to construe a clause as a representation rather than a warranty, wherever possible.

By statute all statements of the insured in life policies only, unless put in by his fraud, are representations and not warranties. This, as stated above, means that the statement to void the policy must go to a material part of the policy. The rule also applies to an application for reinstatement of a policy after a default in the payment of the premium. A material false statement in such application will as effectually void a policy as a similar statement in the original application. 

Schrader v. John Hancock, etc., Ins. Co., 232 A. D. 644; 251 

N. Y. S. 169. 

1See (infra) the discussion of the Statute of Frauds in Suretyship contracts.

INSURANCE 

191 If the insured had misstated, though in good faith, that he was not suffering from cancer, whereas he was, and he died of cancer, this is so material a misrepresentation that, although not a warranty, it avoids the risk and the company need not pay. Note again that this rule applies to life policies. 

Eastern v. Travelers, 234 N. Y. 441;

Insurance Law, § 58. 

In one case the insured denied in his application that he had ever been treated for tuberculosis. As a matter of fact he had been so treated. The beneficiary was not permitted to recover under this policy, because the denial influenced the issuance of the policy and was therefore a material misrepresentation. 

Klapholtz v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 218 A. D. 695; 219 N. Y. S. 64.

It has been held, however, that a question in the application following a list of questions asking whether the assured had had consultations with doctors about specific diseases related to a consultation concerning some ailment which pertained to her general good health and not to a temporary upset. It was not incumbent upon the insured, if then in good health, to recall visits for medical advice for such minor ills as constipation or common colds readily relieved by simple remedies. Where, however, the first question about health is "Have you ever consulted a physician" and there then follows a list of questions as to specific maladies, the question plainly indicates that the insurer wished to know the details of any professional treatment in order to decide whether it would issue a policy. A false answer in such case denying professional treatment for a discharge from ears would avoid the policy. 

Nowak v. Brotherhood, 226 A. D. 123; affd. 252 N. Y. 465;

Insurance Law, § 58; 

Jenkins v. Hancock Mut. Life, 257 N. Y. 289.

If a serious condition in the state of the applicant's health develops (e.g., the necessity of a major operation) after the application for insurance has been submitted, a failure to disclose the situation prior to the delivery of the policy constitutes a deliberate fraud and avoids the policy, despite the fact that every statement in the application is true. 

Armand v. Met. Life Ins., 134 Misc. 357; 235 N. Y. S. 726.

A life policy and papers attached to it must contain the entire contract. No unattached paper can be incorporated by reference. 

When an insured receives a policy, it is his duty to read it and if an application incorporated therein does not contain correct answers to the questions asked by the medical examiner, it is his duty to have it corrected. A recovery by the insured is not permitted because the medical examiner incorrectly recorded the applicant's an-

swers or because the insured was unable to read or neglected to read the policy.

Minsker v. John Hancock Life Ins., 254 N. Y. 333. 

The rule that the life policy consists only of the papers attached applies only to the original policy, but not to an application for a reinstatement of a policy which had lapsed for nonpayment of premiums. In the latter instance, the insurer can defend on the ground of misrepresentation, even though the application for reinstatement was not attached. 

Insurance Law, § 58; 

N. Y. Life Ins. Co. v. Rosen, 227 A. D. 79; affd. 255 N. Y. 567.

If a policy contains false material representations, the insurer is relieved from any liability under the policy. Although the applicant's nondisclosure of a fact concerning which he has not been asked will not avoid a fire or life policy, if the applicant is aware of the existence of some circumstance which he knows would influence the insurer in acting upon his application, good faith requires him to disclose that circumstance, though unasked. 

Sebring v. Fidelity Ins. Co., 255 N. Y. 382.

In one case an insurer had executed a contract of fire insurance with an individual who had previously been insured by other companies, had twice been burned out, was in bad repute with underwriters, and would not be insured by informant's company. With such information in its possession and without disclosure, the insurer shortly thereafter effected a contract of reinsurance with another company. The court held that the duty existed to disclose this information and failure to do so rendered void the policy of reinsurance. 

N. Y. Bowery Ins. Co. v. N. Y. Fire Ins. Co., 17 Wend. 359.

The question of materiality of the representation is for the jury. 

This is the test: If the underwriter, with full information, would have refused to accept the risk, then the concealment is material. 

The undisclosed fact need not be of such a nature as to have increased the risk or contributed to any loss or damage. 

Sebring v. Fidelity Ins. Co., 255 N. Y. 382.

Life policies frequently contain a clause that they are to be incontestable after the insurance company has received the premiums for two years after the first premium was paid; then the company cannot for any reason contest the validity of the policy; except if the insured (1) fails to pay his premiums, or (2) enlists in the army or navy. If the insured should die before the two years are over, the insurer may still contest the policy even after the two years have passed, if the policy read that it was to be incontestable

INSURANCE 

193 after it had been in force for two years from its date of issue. 

The ground for this holding is that since the insured died before the two years were over, the policy was not in force for two years. 

In order that the policy be considered "in force," the insured must be alive. 

Van Saun v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 135 Misc. 855; 239 N. Y. S. 698. 

McKenna v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 220 A. D. 53; 220 N. Y. S. 568.

Where the policy read that it was to be incontestable except for non-payment of premiums, provided two years have elapsed from the date of issue, it was held that the insurer cannot set up any defense except non-payment, after two years from the date of issue, even though the insured may have died before the lapse of two years. 

Sexton v. Eq. Life Assur. Soc., 130 Misc. 362; affd. 227 N. Y. S. 902; 

Marowitz v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 122 Misc. 675; 203 N. Y. S. 534.

The ground for the latter holding was the failure of the clause to have the words "in force" included. It should be noted, however, that the time elapsing betwen the death of the assured and the appointment of a representative for the deceased against whom the insurer can commence an action, is not part of the two-year period.¹ 

Jensen v. Met Life Ins. Co., 251 N. Y. 336. 

It may also be noted at this point that, even though the beneficiary may sue to collect under the policy before the two years are over, the defense of the insurer cannot be inserted in the answer if it is served after the two-year period, even though it may have been served within 20 days after the service of the complaint. In one case the complaint was served March 16, 1927; the noncontestability period expired April 1, 1927. The answer was served after April 1, 1927, setting up a defense of fraud. It was held that the defense was inserted too late, as the contest takes its start when the insurer serves an answer disclaiming liability. 

Wolpin v. Pru. Ins. Co., 223 A. D. 339; 228 N. Y. S. 78; 

Killian v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 251 N. Y. 44.

If an insured misstates his age, in his life policy, in order to pay lower premiums, he would not get the full value, but only the amount owing the insured, based on the actual premiums paid, had the insured stated his correct age. A misstatement of age is not affected by the two-year incontestability clause, and all the insured collects is what he is rightfully entitled to. 

Murphy v. Trav. Ins. Co., 134 Misc. 238; 234 N. Y. S. 278. 

1The insurance statute now requires that life insurance companies shall contain a clause that the policy is to be incontestable after it has been in force during the lifetime of the insured for a period of two years from its date of issue except for nonpayment of premiums. This became effective in 1921. 

But a policy containing other clauses would be interpreted as outlined above 

Insurance Law, § 101.

A case of breach of condition that would end the policy would be this: The policy provides that it will become void if the insured factory is left uninhabited for twenty days or more at a time. The owner departs for Washington on business, and the factory is shut down for a month. Later he returns, and from a cause in no way connected with its having been vacant, the factory burns down. The company need not pay the insurance. A material condition subsequent has been violated, and the fact that the breach was in no way connected with the loss is of no consequence. 

If the owner had leased it for three months, and the lessee, without the owner's knowledge, had permitted it to become vacant for a month, the policy would nevertheless be terminated. 

The standard fire policy in New York contains a clause that the insurer shall not be liable for loss while the hazard is increased by any means within the control or knowledge of the insured. This means that the company would not be liable for loss if occasioned while the increased hazard existed, even though the fire was not caused or affected by this new hazard. Once the hazard is removed, the liability of the insurer exists again. 

Taverna v. Pal. Ins. Co., 228 A. D. 33; 238 N. Y. S. 389; 

Filards v. Nat. U. F. Ins. Co., 224 A. D. 136; 229 N. Y. S. 682.

A policy that declares that it is to be void if the insured die by his own hand becomes void if the insured committed suicide. But if he were insane at the time he committed suicide, or killed himself accidentally, it would not be affected. Where the policy read, "The

Company is not liable if the insured should die by anything taken or inhaled," and it appeared that gas had accidentally escaped in the room, which A inhaled causing his death, the court held that death was not caused by the inhaling of gas within the meaning of the policy. It was held that the policy contemplated a voluntary and intelligent act by the assured, not an involuntary and unconscious act. 

Paul v. Travelers' Ins. Co., 112 N. Y. 472.

If a fire or accident policy has expired and no fire (or accident) has occurred, the insured cannot recover his premiums paid by him on the ground that the policy has all the time been invalid because of his breach of warranty, where no question of the validity of the policy had previously arisen. 

Proof of Loss

If the policy requires the assured to notify the insurer of any event against which the insurer insures within a specified time, such notice must be given if liability is to attach. Where, however, an insurer, with full knowledge that proper notice has not been given, undertakes to defend the interests of the assured in an accident, it is considered a waiver of the right to disclaim liability.

INSURANCE 

195 If the insured agrees in writing to permit the insurer to defend subject to its right to disclaim, no waiver is spelled out. If, however, after the insurer has so acted as to have waived the breach, a subsequent signing of a non-waiver agreement by the insured is, by itself, unsupported by consideration and unenforceable. 

269 Canal St. Corp. v. Zurich Gen. Acc. Co., 252 N. Y. 603; 

Killeen v. Gen. Acc., etc., Co., 131 Misc. 691; affd. 229 N. Y. S. 875; 

Farrell v. Merchants', etc., Co., 203 A. D. 118; 196 N. Y. S. 383. 

The giving of "immediate” notice, or notice "forthwith," does not require the giving of such notice instantly. It means that the notice is to be given within a reasonable time, considering all the circumstances of the situation. A delay of 10 days was held, in the absence of proper explanation, to be unreasonable as a matter of law. 

Greenwich Bank v. Hart Ins. Co., 222 A. D. 219; affd. 250 N. Y. 116; 

Reena v. U. S. Cas. Co., 228 A. D. 108; affd. 256 N. Y. 537.

The liability of an insurance company to a mortgagee who is the beneficiary even by the words "loss, if any, payable to—as interest may appear" is different from its liability toward the owner with respect to presentation of proofs of loss, and the short period of limitations does not apply to a mortgagee. 

Miller v. Stuyvesant Ins. Co., 223 A. D. 6; 227 N. Y. S. 326. 

Waiver and Estoppel

If a general agent (not a mere soliciting agent) waives a breach, it is a valid waiver. This applies even if the policy specifically says that the president alone can waive and that the waiver must be in writing. 

Where the insured falsely stated in a burglary policy that he never before had suffered a burglary loss, it is a breach of warranty which voids the policy, if the company has not waived. 

Drilling v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 234 N. Y. 234.

A clause in a life insurance policy requiring the policy to be delivered during the good health of the insured is waived when the agent for the company delivers the policy with knowledge of the illness of the insured. 

McClelland v. Mut. Life Ins. Co., 217 N. Y. 336; 

Brown v. John Hancock, etc., Ins. Co., 142 Misc. 632; 255 N. Y. S. 257. 

The soliciting agent who is employed to secure insurance is usually regarded as the agent of the insured and not of the insurance company. 

Condon v. Exton-Hall, 80 Misc. 369; 142 N. Y. S. 548.

The extent of the agent's authority is not determined by his being called "general agent" or "special agent." The question is whether

the particular act is within the scope of his actual or apparent authority. Where, however, the agent, who solicited the insurance, made out and had the application signed, received the policies from the insurer and delivered them to the insured, he was held to be an authorized agent, clothed with authority to waive a condition precedent. The knowledge of this agent is the knowledge of his principal, the insurer. 

Glagowski v. Erie, etc., Ins. Co., 216 A. D. 654; 216 N. Y. S. 64; 

McClelland v. Mut. Life Ins. Co., 217 N. Y. 336.

This rule applies where the limitation of authority of this agent against waiving provisions is apparent for the first time in the policy itself. If the limitation appeared in the original application executed by the insured, such soliciting agent would have no authority to waive a condition precedent. 

Manson v. N. Y. Life Ins., 229 A. D. 670; 243 N. Y. S. 579; 

Bible v. John Hancock, etc., Ins. Co., 256 N. Y. 458.

If the insurer company by unfair conduct leads the insured to believe that a warranty relied upon is not part of the contract, it would be estopped from alleging the breach of warranty.

There is no waiver of any breach if the knowledge of facts relied upon comes to a company representative who is not authorized to act for the insurer. Thus, even though the agent and doctor of the insurer knew that the insured fraudulently stated that he had never been in a hospital, the beneficiary cannot collect in an action on the policy. And even if the insured had informed the company's doctor that he recently had been sick and the agent deliberately kept that information from the company, the beneficiary cannot collect, even though he could not read or write English. 

Stanulevich v. St. L. Life Assn., 228 N. Y. 586; 

Grubick v. John Hancock, etc., Co., 212 A. D. 126; 208 N. Y. S. 279; 

Malchak v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 134 Misc. 640; 236 N. Y. S. 300; 

Emanuele v. Met. Life Ins. Co., 242 N. Y. S. 715.

It has not been decided in this state whether evidence would be admissible, in an action to reform the contract of insurance, to show that the answers contained in the written statement were never given and that the defendant insurer, through its agents, took unfair advantage of the applicant's disability to read English. 

Kiviatkouski v. Brotherhood, 243 N. Y. 394.

Where a policy of automobile insurance was entered into by mail, directly with the company's office, and the terms of the policy were stated in the letters, the insured is justified in taking the final policy without reading it, and in relying on the company not inserting representations which were not made. If these representations so inserted prove to be false, the company is estopped from urging a defense of misrepresentation based on its own wrong. 

Abell v. Cornwall Ind. Corp., 241 N. Y. 318.

INSURANCE 

197 

Subrogation 

There is no subrogation under a life policy. The result is that if an insured is killed by A's negligence, the beneficiary gets the amount of the policy and the insurer company has no claim against the tort-feasor. The estate of the insured can sue the tort-feasor in addition to collecting on the policy.
