An insurer who pays the claims of the insured under an accident or fire policy is entitled to be subrogated to the rights which the insured would have had against such third party for its default or wrongdoing. If the assured receives the damages from the wrongdoer before payment by the insurer, the amount so received will be applied pro tanto in discharge of the policy. The insurer can sue in his own name and need not join the insured as a party. 

If the insurer has paid out money in settlement of the claim, one trial court held that no right of action remains in the assured, nor can he by any act defeat the right of such insurer. The acceptance of payment from the insurer operates as a virtual assignment of the cause of action to the insurer, and a part payment operates as an assignment pro tanto. A release, therefore, executed by the assured to the tort-feasor, after the assured has been paid by the insurer, was held not to prevent the insurer from maintaining his action against the tort-feasor under its right of subrogation. 

Camden F. Ins. Assn. v. Bleem, 132 Misc. 22; 227 N. Y. S. 746.

The Appellate Term in New York City held, however, that where the insured settles with the tort-feasor after having been paid by the insurer under the policy, the insured is liable over to the insurer for the money received from the tort-feasor, in an action for money had and received, but that the right of subrogation was destroyed when without knowledge of the insurer's claim the tort-feasor obtained a release from the insured. 

Met. Cas. Ins. Co. v. Badler, 132 Misc. 132; 229 N. Y. S. 81.

If the insured releases his claim against the tort-feasor before the insurer has made any payments, the right to subrogation is lost, but the insured cannot collect on his policy. 

Ocean Acc., etc., Co. v. Hooker, 240 N. Y. 37.

In a recent case the insured received $2,000 from his insurance company under a policy when the Susquehanna R. R. Company destroyed his automobile. The Railroad Company, not knowing of this policy, paid a sum to the insured in satisfaction of his claim for property damage and personal injuries. The court held that if the insured collected any money in satisfaction for the damage for which the insurer had paid, he holds such money for the benefit of the insurer to the extent of the sum paid by the insurer to the insured. 

If the insurer desires to collect from the insured because its right of subrogation has been lost, it can do so by showing that it might have

recovered against the tort-feasor and the insurer has lost this because of the release. 

Hamilton Fire Ins. v. Greger, 246 N. Y. 162.

Where a mortgaged building is restored after a fire, the mortgagor who pays the cost is subrogated to the rights of the mortgagee under a fire insurance policy. 

Savarese v. Ohio Farmers Ins. Co., 234 A. D. 248; 254 N. Y. S. 683. 

Forfeiture of a Life Policy 

The standard life policy requires the company to notify the insured of the amount of premiums due, where payable, and that the policy will be forfeited if the premium is not paid. The notice must be mailed not less than fifteen nor more than forty-five days before the premium is due. If the company does not do so it cannot declare the policy void for failure to pay the premium until one year after default. If the company does mail such notice and the insured defaults, he is given two years' time to bring any suit under his now forfeited policy. This arrangement does not apply to policies payable in weekly or monthly installments.

If there is a default, the insured can take advantage of a clause which every life policy contains. He can within three years after default reinstate the policy if the cash surrender value has not been paid and if he can give satisfactory evidence of insurability and if he pays all premiums due, with interest. 

Cancellation of Fire Policies Section 122 of the Insurance Law of the state of New York makes it mandatory for fire insurance companies, upon request of the assured, to cancel the policy and return the unearned premium; but the mere mailing of a fire insurance policy to an insurer, in fire insurance policy cases, with a letter requesting that it be canceled, does not operate to cancel the policy; it must be received by the insurer in order to make the cancellation effective. So, where a policy is mailed to an insurance company for cancellation, and the property insured is destroyed. by fire prior to its receipt by the agent, the company remains bound on the policy. 

Lofaro v. John Hancock Mut. Life Ins. Co., 259 N. Y. S. 47. 

Reforming a Policy 

Policies are subject to reformation under the same circumstances as would be applicable in the case of any other contract. 

Schenectady Varnish Co. v. Auto Ins. Co., 127 Misc. 751; 

Paskie v. Con. P. Ins. Co., 223 A. D. 603; 229 N. Y. S. 121, 126.

There is no merit to the contention that by receiving and retaining the policy the assured waives any objections thereto. The court of equity has consistently refused to deprive the assured of relief

INSURANCE 

199 when asking for reformation of an insurance policy when it was drawn by the insurer and contained terms other than were intended and agreed upon.

The Beneficiary and Creditors 

Under the Insurance Law creditors of an insured cannot reach the proceeds of insurance money payable to a third party beneficiary even though the insured paid the premiums himself and even though the insured had reserved the right to change the beneficiary, unless there had existed an intent to defraud the creditors at the time the policy was taken out. The interest of the beneficiary is free from the claim of any creditor of the insured. If payments of premiums are made in fraud of creditors, the amount of premiums paid, with interest thereon, enures to their benefit from the proceeds of the policy. The insurer is discharged from liability if it pays all to the beneficiary, unless before such payment the company shall have written notice from a creditor claiming a specified amount from the proceeds of the policy. This rule applies even where a wife is the beneficiary, regardless of the amount of the insurance carried. 

Insurance Law, § 55a; 

Chatham-Phoenix v. Crosney, 251 N. Y. 189.

Whether dividends on a life policy which is payable to a beneficiary may be attached by a judgment-creditor of the insured is undecided in this state. It has been held that dividends used to obtain additional insurance is not attachable. 

N. Y. Plumbers Specialty Co. v. Stein, 140 Misc. 161; 250 N. Y. S. 220. 

If the beneficiary is to take only if he survives the insured, the proceeds of the policy belong to the estate of the insured if the beneficiary predeceases the insured. If, however, the insured murdered the beneficiary, the estate of the latter would be entitled to the insurance moneys. The court will not allow the estate of the insured to profit by his own crime. If the beneficiary murders the insured, the estate of the latter collects the insurance money, provided always that the policy was validly issued. If the policy was procured in furtherance of a conspiracy between assured and beneficiary to defraud the insurer, the policy has no validity, and the estate of the insured cannot take if the insured has been murdered by the beneficiary. It seems that where the policy has been validly issued and the assured has been murdered by the beneficiary, the estate of the insured can collect on the policy because of an implied intention read into the policy that, if the beneficiary cannot take, then the insurance should be paid to those who would take in the absence of a beneficiary. 

Goldstein v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 225 A. D. 642; 234 N. Y. S. 250.

If a wife is the beneficiary under a policy insuring the life of her husband, the wife cannot make an irrevocable transfer of the policy

while her husband is alive. She (and no one else) may recover the insurance money falling due upon her husband's death, in spite of a previous assignment by her. Her creditors cannot avoid an assignment previously made by the wife, despite her inability to make an irrevocable assignment. 

Jackson v. Tallmadge, 214 N. Y. S. 528; affd. 246 N. Y. 133.

Change of Beneficiary 

Where a policy contains a clause that a change of beneficiary can be effected only by an indorsement by the insurer covering the change, such act must be done. In one case an insured attempted to leave the insurance money to his wife by will. It was held that the beneficiary could collect, and not the wife, as no indorsement had been made by the insurer as required by the policy. In another case the insured died after the request for a change but before the policy was presented at defendant's office as required by the terms of the policy to effect a change, and no recovery was allowed to the substituted beneficiary. 

Martinelli v. Cometti, 133 Misc. 810; 234 N. Y. S. 389.

It should be noted that under the recent decisions the defense that no change had been indorsed by the insurer is available only to the latter. Where the insured has requested the change and delivered the policy to the insurer and has thus done all that he could possibly do, the new beneficiary is entitled to the proceeds if the insurer does not object.

Chatham Phenix Trust Co. v. Travelers' Ins. Co., 232 A. D. 598; 

251 N. Y. S. 43.

A beneficiary unconditionally designated as an irrevocable beneficiary in a policy of life insurance has a present or vested right to receive, whenever the policy matures, whatever sum may become payable thereon in accordance with its terms. 

If therefore, while a policy is in force, the insured, insurer, or both, without the consent or authorization of the beneficiary, take any action with respect to the policy that would defeat, impair, or materially change the rights of the beneficiary thereunder, such action so far as the beneficiary is concerned, has no force and effect and is void. When premiums are not paid by the assured, there is no obligation on the part of the insurer to notify such beneficiary even though the policy lapse as a result. 

Ruckenstein v. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co., 144 Misc. 154; 258 

N. Y. S. 162.

Accident Policies 

By statute, if a person insured against liability for causing damage to the person or property of others by means of his automobile, injures X or damages his property, X may recover against the insurer

INSURANCE 

201 (1) after he gets judgment against the insured, and (2) after the time to appeal from such judgment has expired, and (3) after execution is returned unsatisfied. 

The insolvency or bankruptcy of the person insured does not relieve the insurance carrier. 

Insurance Law, § 109; 

Schroeder v. Col. Cas. Co., 126 Misc. 205; 213 N. Y. S. 649.

If the injured party sues the insurer under § 109, the insurer may set up any defense that it could have set up against the insured. So, if the insured had failed to give the insurer proper notice of the accident as specified in the insurance policy, or had failed to cooperate with the insurer in the defense, thus releasing the insurer, the insurer may set up this defense against the injured party. 

Hermance v. Globe Ind. Co., 221 A. D. 394; 223 N. Y. S. 93; 

Coleman v. New Amsterdam Cas. Co., 247 N. Y. 275; 

Seltzer v. Indemnity Ins. Co., 252 N. Y. 330.

Section 211a of the Civil Practice Act permits one joint tort-feasor, who has paid a judgment recovered against him and others, to recover from the other joint tort-feasors their pro rata shares of such judgment. Let us suppose that A and B were joint tort-feasors. 

X sued both and recovered a judgment which A's insurer pays. By this payment A's insurer became subrogated to A's rights. May A's insurer recover the pro rata share from B under Sec. 211a of the 

Civil Practice Act? The court held "No" on the ground that Sec. 

211a became law after Section 109 of the Insurance Law and that it would be a forced construction to decide that section 109 of the Insurance Law applies to a right to maintain an action authorized by a subsequent enactment. 

Metropolitan Casualty v. Union Indemnity, 141 Misc. 792; 253 

N. Y. S. 324.

The rule is settled in the law of insurance that the cause nearest the loss is to be considered and that distant causes are to be disregarded. Let us suppose that the plaintiff bank is insured by the defendant company against loss through larceny or theft only. A depositor of the plaintiff knowingly deposits to his own account a forged check wherein the maker is a fictitious person. Thus far the depositor is guilty of forgery. Then the depositor draws out some money against this check, the bank paying against uncollected funds. 

The depositor, having obtained money by false pretenses, is guilty of larceny. The loss thus sustained may be collected from the insurer, as the cause nearest the loss was the larceny. 

Trade Bank v. U. S. Fid. & G. Co., 223 A. D. 584; 229 N. Y. S. 92.

It has been held that where one acts as aggressor in an attack, under circumstances which would render a homicide likely as the

result of his own misconduct, death was not accidental within the meaning of clause in a policy providing for payment in case of accidental death. Similarly one judicially executed for a crime does not die accidently. 

Manno v. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co., 139 Misc. 848; 249 N. Y. S. 471.

The Co-Insurance Clause 

Since most fire insurance policies carry an 80% co-insurance clause, such clause will be used to explain its workings. Under a co-insurance clause the insured is obligated to carry insurance to an amount equal to 80% of the value of the insured property. 

Let us assume the value of the property insured to be $10,000. 

The 80% clause of course, requires that $8,000 insurance be carried. 

Should the insured carry only $6,000 insurance and the fire loss amount to $4,000, the insured can get only 75% of his loss since the insurance carried was only 75% of what the co-insurance clause required him to carry. Putting it in a different way, the insured gets only that percentage of his loss which the amount of insurance carried bears to the amount which should have been carried.

Statute of Frauds 

Leases or assignments of leases which are to run for the period of more than a year from the time the term commences must be subscribed by the lessor or his agent.

Real Property Law, § 242. 

When the lease begins does not matter. It may begin the next month to run for a year or less from that date, and yet be valid though oral. It thus differs from a contract of employment, which must be in writing if it is to terminate later than a year from the next working day. The counting of the year in such latter contract begins from the weekday following the making of the contract. 

Ward v. Hasbrouck, 169 N. Y. 407.

A contract for leasing for a longer period than one year must be subscribed by the lessor or by a person by him lawfully authorized. 

A mere signing is not enough. This does not mean that a lessor may enforce an oral executory contract against an alleged lessee merely by subscribing a memorandum of the bargain. The lessee must indicate his assent to the transaction, though his signature may not be necessary. 

300 West End Ave. Corp. v. Warner, 250 N. Y. 221;

Real Property Law, § 259. 

The fact that an oral lease agreement is voidable does not excuse the lessee from paying the reasonable value for the use and occupation of the premises. The lessee is bound on a valid lease for a year if he had made any payment at all as rent on the lease, since a lease for a year need not be in writing. 

Real Property Law, § 220. 

If the unexpired term of a lease does not exceed one year, parol evidence is admissible to prove an agreement for a surrender of that lease. 

Gorlin v. Hroatsky, 126 Misc. 570; 214 N. Y. S. 45.

Under the common law a landowner had to recover a judgment in ejectment before he could recover from a trespasser for the use and occupation of the premises. The damages recoverable in the ejectment were nominal. The statute now permits the landowner to recover from a trespasser for use and occupation in a direct action. 

City of N. Y. v. Fink, 130 Misc. 620; 224 N. Y. S. 404. 203

Duty to Repair 

10-4 5-0 8-hu Unless there is a stipulation in the lease to the contrary, a landlord need make no more repairs than are necessary to keep the house in "good repair." He is, however, bound to take reasonable care to keep the common halls and stairways reasonably safe for passage of tenants and those lawfully using the same even though there be no lease. The degree of care owed to a guest of a tenant is the same as that which the landlord owes his tenant. 

Schabel v. Onseyga Realty, 233 A. D. 208; 251 N. Y. S. 280.

It is to be noted, however, that a covenant or duty by a lessor to repair, does not impose upon the lessor a liability in tort either to the lessee or his invitees. It is only by statutory enactment that the liability of a lessor has been enlarged so that he is liable in tort for damages resulting from his failure to repair a defective part of a tenement house. The statute imposes a responsibility to keep a tenement house, including walls and ceilings of apartments, in good repair. This duty cannot be delegated and an owner is not relieved from liability by having leased the tenement to one person with privilege of subletting.¹ 

Cullings v. Goetz, 256 N. Y. 287; 

Altz v. Lieberson, 233 N. Y. 16; 

Morris v. City of New York, 261 N. Y. S. 228.

The statute also requires that in every tenement house a proper light shall be kept burning by the owner in the public hallways, near the stairs, upon the entrance floor, and upon the second floor, above the entrance floor of said house, every night from sunset to sunrise throughout the year.

Tenement House Law, § 76. 

A tenement house is defined as a building occupied as the home or residence of three or more families living independently of each other, and doing their own cooking upon the premises. 

Villari v. Duggan, 141 Misc. 316; 253 N. Y. S. 81.

A clause providing that the landlord should not be liable for damage from leakage whether due to his negligence or not, relieves him from any obligation to make repairs where the roof leaked. 

Kirshenbaum v. General Outdoor Adv. Co., 258 N. Y. 489. 

The fact that a tenant remains in an apartment having a defective ceiling for a long period does not, as a matter of law, make the 1The City Court of the City of New York held in a very recent opinion that a landlord under the common law is liable to tort in violating a contract to repair premises over which he retained control, so that he would be liable in tort to a tenant of even a two-family house, i.e., not a tenement, where injuries resulted from a breach of covenant to repair. 

Goldberg v. Engelhard, 261 N. Y. S. 560. 1

tenant guilty of contributory negligence. That is a question of fact for the jury.

Constructive and Partial Con Enchor 

If the premises become uninhabitable through the fault of the lessor so as to amount to a constructive eviction, the lessee may terminate the lease, provided he speedily vacates the premises after the commencement of the causes constituting such constructive eviction. Eviction as a defense to a claim for rent does not depend upon a covenant for quiet enjoyment, either express or implied. The obligation of payment is suspended because it involves a failure of the consideration for which it is paid. 

City of N. Y. v. Cokenes, 242 N. Y. S. 374.

Where noises and leaks amount to constructive eviction, a tenant remaining in the premises is not absolved from paying rent because a clause in the lease prepared by him provides that he is to live rent free in the case of noises and leaks. The court will construe the clause as a penalty. The tenant will be limited to an action for damages on the covenant. 

19 John St. Corp. v. M'Namara, 136 Misc. 844; 242 N. Y. S. 407.

An eviction cannot be predicated on acts or conduct, however wrongful or distressing, unless committed, encouraged or connived at by the landlord. He is not responsible for the conduct of other tenants acting within their rights in their own apartments. If defendant's grievance was a substantial one, he could secure redress, not against his landlord, but against the offending tenant in the premises above. For even where the landlord might have the right to terminate the lease because of a tenant's noisy and boisterous conduct, his failure to exercise such a privilege would not necessarily support the claim of constructive eviction. 

Tudor City v. Perkett, 143 Misc. 209; 256 N. Y. S. 395.

Since in the absence of fraud or a covenant a lessor does not represent that the premises are tenantable and usable for the purposes for which they are intended, the lessee has no cause of action when he goes out because of deterioration of the premises. The lease merely is at an end. Should the lease cover the situation by its terms these terms would control. 

Alta v. Leiberson, 233 N. Y. 16; 

Mulligan v. Fioravera, 228 A. D. 270; affd. 255 N. Y. 559.

Causes which would constitute grounds for terminating the lease or constructive eviction are, among others: numerous bedbugs which cannot be gotten rid of; failure to furnish heat during the cold weather, the means of furnishing such heat being in the landlord's control; failure to provide elevator service seriously interfering with

the tenant's use of the premises; destruction by the elements; use of machinery which caused great vibrations; presence of offensive odors not there to the lessee's knowledge when the lease was made. What is and what is not a good cause is in most cases a question of fact. 

Bliss v. Clark, 104 Misc. 543; 172 N. Y. S. 112;

Real Property Law, § 227; 

Read v. Levy, 101 Misc. 547; 167 N. Y. S. 568; 

Ess. Ebb. Realty Co. v. Buttenheim, 125 N. Y. S. 401; 

Fleckner v. Douglas, 136 Misc. 57; 239 N. Y. S. 121.

It has been held that where the use of the premises became illegal because of a new law, the tenant was justified in abandoning and that such abandonment will support a plea of eviction, although there was no actual entry or physical disturbance of defendant's possession. 

Hizington v. Eldred Refining Co., 235 A. D. 486; 257 N. Y. S. 464.

A tenant must pay any rent which falls due while he is in possession, even though the acts constituting the eviction are then taking place and even though he vacates the premises immediately thereafter. 

Harperly v. Joseph, 187 N. Y. S120; 

50 W. 160th St. Co. v. Henderson, 216 N. Y. S. 9.

If a partial eviction exists, the tenant need not vacate the entire premises. If the partial eviction is actual and is the act of the landlord, the entire rent which becomes due during the eviction is suspended, because the landlord is not permitted to apportion his own wrong. If the eviction is the act of a stranger by force of paramount title, the rent will be apportioned and a recovery permitted only for the value of the premises retained. 

Fifth Ave. Bldg. Co. v. Kernschan, 221 N. Y. 370; 

Friedman v. Garage D. Corp., 130 Misc. 266; 223 N. Y. S. 839.

The tenant, in cases of partial eviction, is not entitled to damages. 

If the tenant pays the rent, or admits liability for it, he may claim damages. 

Davies v. Schatzen, 124 Misc. 170; 207 N. Y. S. 217; 

524 West End Ave. v. Rawak, 125 Misc. 862; 212 N. Y. S. 287; 

Peerless v. Halbreich, 125 Misc. 889; 213 N. Y. S. 49.

These are examples of a partial eviction: an arbitrary refusal to give the tenant the key; the lack of the use of a lavatory; an actual ouster by a landlord of a tenant from space in a rental garage, which can be occupied by six automobiles, regardless of the size of the garage; interference by a tenant's occupation of premises by the landlord's entry upon the demised premises without permission from the tenant, in order to install a new hot water system. 

City of N. Y. v. Pike Realty Co., 247 N. Y. 245; 

Kermacoe v. McKenna, 132 Misc. 293; 229 N. Y. S. 498.

The lessor, in making a lease, impliedly affirms that he has such title as to enable him to give an unencumbered lease for the term

therein stated. If the lease contains a covenant of quiet enjoyment, the possession of the lessee is not to be disturbed by a superior title during the term of the lease. This is not dependent upon the continued title of the lessor, or upon subsequent payments of mortgages which were or became liens upon the premises. Thus the appointment of a receiver in foreclosure proceedings commenced because the lessor mortgagor or his conveyee failed to meet the obligations under a mortgage which was a lien at the time the lease was made, does not make void lease despite the fact that the lease may contain a covenant of quiet enjoyment. When the sale under the foreclosure takes place, the tenant is constructively evicted. Where the tenant is ousted he may recover from the original lessor for breach of covenant. 

Ganz v. Clark, 252 N. Y. 92; 

Knickerbocker Oil Co. v. Richfield Oil Co., 234 A. D. 199; 254 

N. Y. S. 506.

Without allegation and proof of actual or constructive eviction no damages can be claimed for breach or covenant of quiet enjoyment. 

Grattan v. Tierney, 226 A. D. 811; 234 N. Y. S. 399.

Where a remainderman evicts the lessee of a life tenant after the latter's death, the tenant can get back from his lessor's estate only what he has paid. If he has not paid in advance, he get only nominal damages. He can recover nothing for the loss of the lease or for improvements he has made on the property unless the life tenant had been guilty of fraud in leasing the premises. 

Real Property Law, § 247; 

In re Hunt's Estate, 120 Misc. 174; 197 N. Y. S. 633. 

Destruction of the Premises

Upon the destruction of the leased premises by fire or the elements, neither the tenant nor the landlord need rebuild, in the absence of covenants in the lease requiring it to be done. The tenant, if the fire was not caused by his negligence, may declare the lease at an end, provided he moves and he is not liable to pay rent for the time subsequent to the surrender.

Real Property Law, § 227. 

The landlord, in the absence of a covenant to rebuild, cannot enter upon the premises to do repair work, but if the tenant makes no objection to such a proceeding, it would be deemed a license to the landlord to enter for the purpose of rebuilding. The tenant can then remain under the terms of the original lease, regardless of how much more valuable the premises may have become because of the rebuilding. 

Smith v. Kerr, 108 N. Y. 31. absurd Postios

If the tenant elects to terminate the lease, he divests himself to any claim to emoluments in the absence of a provision that the tenant under such circumstances might take growing crops. 

Ferruci v. McDermott, 138 Misc. 438; 246 N. Y. S. 22.

Assignments and Subleases A tenant can assign no less than all of the remaining part of the lease of the entire place leased, but he can sublet all of the leased premises for part time or a part for all the remaining time, unless there is a contrary provision in the lease. 

Sandford v. Ambassador Restaurant, 139 Misc. 3; 247 N. Y. S. 655.

If the lease requires that the permission of the landlord be obtained before the tenant may sublease, and the tenant subleases without that permission, the landlord is considered to have waived that provision of the lease by accepting rent from the new tenant with knowledge of the assignment. But should the new tenant fail to pay rent, the original lessee is liable, for nothing short of a novation will transfer the lease to the new tenant. 

If the landlord gives his written consent to an assignment, the covenant against assignment in the lease is cancelled and the assignee can then assign without any permission. 

Costallat v. Diamond, 226 A. D. 755; 234 N. Y. S. 152; 

Lynch v. Joseph, 228 A. D. 367; 240 N. Y. S. 176.

As between the paramount landlord and a subtenant, a sublease of the entire remaining term of the original lessee operates as an assignment and creates privity between the landlord and the subtenant. This means that the landlord is entitled to proceed for the rent reserved in his lease against the subtenant, even though a different rent has been contracted for by the subtenant, and the assignee, so long as he continues to hold the estate, is liable directly to the original lessor on all covenants in the original lease including the covenant to pay rent. 

Stewart v. L. I. R. Co., 102 N. Y. 601, 608.

A bona fide sublease of a portion of the demised estate is not an assignment and for that reason there is no privity between the subtenant and the paramount landlord. 

Midway Hotel v. Belleclaire Syndicate, 138 Misc. 401; 246 N.Y.S. 155.

Likewise, a sublease of the entire premises which sublease expires before the original lease, even as little as one day, absolves the subtenant from liability for rent. 

Sandford v. Ambassador Restaurant, 139 Misc. 3; 247 N. Y. S. 655.

The fact that the landlord gives his consent to an assignment and a new tenant goes into possession does not, in the absence of a

rr release to the assignor or proof of surrender and acceptance, relieve the prior tenant from his liability. The original lessee remains liable on the covenants, even though privity of estate is destroyed. 

Gillette v. Aristocrat Restaurant, 239 N. Y. 87.

A lessee who assigns his lease and is obliged to pay the landlord for the default of the assignee acquires a cause of action against the assignee, not by virtue of a privity of contract or estate, as neither exists, but merely for the reason that the lessee is a surety and the assignee is the principal obligor. Where, however, the assignee has assigned to a subsequent assignee, and the lessee is obliged to pay for the default of this subsequent assignee, the lessee can recover only from the subsequent assignee and not from the first assignee, for there is no longer any surety agreement between the lessee and the first assignee and there never was privity of contract or estate. 

The principal obligor is the second assignee. 

Schlessel v. Sherman, 130 Misc. 633; 224 N. Y. S. 593.

Where a sub-tenant remained in possession without permission of the landlord after the expiration of the lease of the original tenant, it was held that the landlord could hold the original tenants as holdover. 

Meyerowitz v. Horowitz, 129 Misc. 215; 220 N. Y. S. 681.

On the sale of leasehold interest the vendor must show: (1) that he owns the lease; (2) that he has authority to sell it; (3) that the original lessor had power to create the term. 

If a lessee buys the building in which the leased premises are, the lease automatically terminates, even if the tenant buyer resells at once.

Termination of the Lease-Holdover Tenants¹ 

A tenancy at will and a tenancy from month to month (where the tenant's term is indefinite, although rent is paid monthly) can be terminated by thirty days' notice in writing served on the tenant personally or by delivering the notice to one of suitable age and discretion upon the premises, or if one cannot be found, by affixing the notice to the door. After thirty days the landlord may reenter, maintain ejectment or remove the tenant by summary process. There is no statutory provision requiring a like notice by the tenant to terminate the tenancy, but the courts hold that the notice is reciprocal and the obligation rests on the tenant also. 

Boyer v. Wallenberg, 132 Misc. 116; 228 N. Y. S. 358; 

Hand v. Knaul, 116 Misc. 714; 191 N. Y. S. 667;

Real Property Law, § 228. 

¹For a discussion of the rights of a tenant in Condemnation Proceedings, see ante, pp.

If a tenant of indefinite tenancy, i.e., not a lessee for a term, says that he will leave on a certain date and he does not, he must pay double the amount of the rental for the period during which he holds over. (If sickness was the cause of the holding over, the rule does not apply.) If a lessee, not having given notice of quitting, holds over, the landlord can assume that the lease which just terminated is renewed for another year. 

Real Property Law, § 229; 

Lerner v. Wolf, 121 Misc. 114; 200 N. Y. S. 368.

In order to give the landlord a right to consider a tenant a holdover under an implied lease, there must be an actual retention of possession of the premises by the tenant, as against the landlord, so that the tenant would by virtue of his possession become a trespasser. 

Merely leaving property on the leased premises after the expiration of the lease may or may not entitle the landlord to treat the tenant as a holdover liable for another year's rent. The determination is one of fact. Not only the value of the goods which are left over by the tenant must be considered, but the ratio of that value to the amount of the rent is also material. If a tenant for a year or years, in possession of a store, should leave his entire stock and fixtures after the expiration of the lease, the court would be required, as a matter of law, to hold that he had retained actual possession and that the landlord could elect to hold the tenant for another year. 

If a tenant merely leaves litter, filth and worthless articles which tenants are accustomed to leave behind them, there is no such holding over which can permit the landlord to consider the tenant liable under an implied lease. 

Canfield v. Harrison, 222 A. D. 326; affd. 248 N. Y. 541.

If a tenant vacates the premises and the landlord comes into possession of them, the relationship of landlord and tenant comes to an end. A reletting by a landlord after abandonment by the tenant works a surrender and acceptance by operation of law, in the absence of consent to such reletting. Where the lease permits the landlord to relet for the remainder of the term, the landlord must not relet for a longer period. If he does relet for a longer period, there is a surrender and acceptance. 

Bonsignore v. Koondel, 134 Misc. 344; 235 N. Y. S. 453; 

Moore v. Lynch, 130 Misc. 385; 224 N. Y. S. 1.

Under a lease giving the tenant a privilege of renewing on the same terms and conditions and not requiring a notice of election to renew, the tenant's mere remaining in possession and the landlord's failure to object for a length of time after the expiration of the lease (in one case five months was held to be a sufficient period, although

no rent was due till then) are equivalent to an exercise of the privilege to renew. 

Fritz v. City of N. Y., 125 Misc. 296; 210 N. Y. S. 717.

Upon the termination of the tenancy the landlord is entitled to receive full and complete possession of the demised premises from the tenant. If there be an undertenant in possession, the tenant must get him out, else his possession will be regarded the possession of the tenant and the latter will be considered a holdover tenant. 

Goodwin v. Humbert, 216 A. D. 295; 215 N. Y. S. 20.

When a lease is made to two or more tenants jointly, with covenants for a renewal of the privilege of an additional term, it cannot be exercised by one of them without the other. 

Goldberg v. Himlyn, 121 Misc. 580; 201 N. Y. S. 837.

If a summons to recover title or possession to the property occupied by the tenant is served on the tenant by a person other than the landlord, he must forthwith notify the landlord. If he fails to do so, he forfeits three years' rental to the landlord. 

Real Property Law, § 225. 

There can be no recovery for use and occupation unless it is shown by the plaintiff that the conventional relation of landlord and tenant existed between the parties. There must have existed some contract, express or implied, to pay for the use and occupation. 

Thus, in one recent case, the defendant was not required to pay rent in the following circumstances. He entered into possession of the property as a purchaser at a defective sale under a mortgage foreclosure. By stipulation the sale was set aside and a second sale took place. The suit was for use and occupation of the premises by the defendant between the first and second sales. 

Thackray v. Ritz, 130 Misc. 403; 223 N. Y. S. 668.

If a lease requires a tenant to pay all taxes assessed against the demised premises during the term when due, it is construed to mean that the tenant pay all such taxes assessed during the term, even those payable at a date after the expiration of the term. 

Walker v. Stein, 222 A. D. 22; 225 N. Y. S. 209.

Eviction by Summary Proceedings 

When a lease expires because of a conditional limitation, the landlord may remove the tenant by summary proceedings. If the lease ends because of a condition subsequent, the landlord must proceed in ejectment to remove the tenant. 

There is much confusion in the cases where the inquiry is whether the language of a particular lease operates as a conditional limitation or condition subsequent. If the term has expired and the tenant is

a holdover, the tenant may be removed by summary proceedings. 

Likewise where a lease provides for its termination by a sale of the premises at the option of the landlord and upon giving of notice. 

The test of a conditional limitation seems to be whether the lease evinces a clear intention that an event, though its occurrence is optional with the landlord, shall, when it transpires, end the lease as if it by its terms had been limited to that time. Thus, summary proceedings may be maintained under the following clause after notice given: "If default be made in the payment of rent. . . the landlord may, if he so elects . . . terminate this lease by giving to the tenant 5 days' notice in writing of his intention to do so and this lease and the term thereof shall expire and come to an end on the day fixed in such notice as if said date were the date originally fixed in the lease for the expiration thereof."1 

Paul v. Levine, 137 Misc. 82; 242 N. Y. S. 729; 

Reisberg v. Ownit, 133 Misc. 156; 231 N. Y. S. 42.

If the lessee uses the building for unlawful purposes, the lease becomes void. The landlord may evict not only the tenant who may have been convicted for keeping a disorderly house, but the undertenants of such tenant as well, though they may have been guiltless. 

Costa v. Poppas, 236 A. D. 175; 258 N. Y. S. 293;

Real Property Law, § 231. 

Although the tenant had ceased to violate the law before the commencement of summary proceedings to remove him on the ground that the premises were used for illegal purposes, all the proceedings for the summary removal of the tenant can still be taken. 

A landlord need not give his lessee possession. The lessee must get that for himself. And the lessee cannot bring dispossess proceedings. Only the landlord can do that. All the lessee can do is to bring ejectment. In his complaint for ejectment three allegations are essential: (1) that the plaintiff is seized of the lease; (2) that he is entitled to immediate possession; and (3) that the defendant is in possession thereof and withholds the same. 

Borrelo v. Brown, 198 N. Y. S. 236.

If a landlord accepts rent after the due date, he is deprived of his right to exercise his election to terminate the lease because of the non-payment of the rent on the due date. 

Levinsky v. Thompson, 125 Misc. 897; 211 N. Y. S. 690.

If a landlord commences summary proceedings by service of a precept and petition, the lease is at an end, from the time the war- 

1But compare Ross v. Aaront, 260 N. Y. S. 387, where the court said that a conditional limitation must operate eo instante, i.e., upon the happening of a specific contingency the demise expired ipso facto, and where the lease ends at the option of the landlord to be exercised after the happening of the event, there is a condition subsequent and not a conditional limitation.

rant for the removal of the tenant is issued, or upon the voluntary removal of the tenant and the resumption of possession by the landlord. 

Cromwell v. Sanford, 222 N. Y. 248; 

Basirico v. Zahn, 260 N. Y. S. 630.

The landlord cannot claim any more rent. All he can claim are damages for breach of lease. Even though the lease contains a clause that the landlord may relet as the agent of the tenant, it means that the reletting shall be evidence of the damages sustained by the breach of the lease resulting in the eviction by summary proceedings. There is no right belonging to the landlord to sue every month for loss of rental. The claim, as a subject of suit, exists at the termination of the time mentioned in the lease. 

Of course, a damage clause can be drawn to make a tenant responsible for monthly deficits after reentry by the landlord, and this even without charging the landlord with a duty to account for any surplus. But unless this is plainly contained in the lease, the cause of action does not accrue until the end of the term. 

Hermitage Co. v. Levine, 248 N. Y. 333; 

McCready v. Lindenhorn, 172 N. Y. 400.

If a tenant is ejected in summary proceedings upon an allegation that he is holding over after a default in the payment of rent, and the unexpired term of the lease exceeds five years when the warrant is issued, the lessee, within one year after the execution of the warrant, shall have the right to redeem. This right may, however, be waived, at the signing of the lease or subsequently. 

Civil Practice Act, § 1437. 

If the landlord, between the issuance of the warrant and the time of expiration of the right to redeem, has leased the premises to a third party, such new lessee may hold the premises until noon of May 

1st following the redemption.

Civil Practice Act, § 1439. 

Ordinarily an assignee of rents may not bring summary proceedings unless right to possession has been granted by the landlord. 

Goodman v. Shached, 144 Misc. 905; 260 N. Y. S. 883.

Disposition of Security Furnished by Tenant 

When a tenant deposits moneys with a landlord under a lease as security for his performance of the terms thereof, in the absence of a specific agreement to the contrary, the relation between the tenant and landlord is that of debtor and creditor and the landlord may use the money. Of course they may provide by agreement that the money is to be held in a fiduciary relation. 

After a landlord evicts a tenant by summary proceedings, he cannot retain a deposit, though the terms of the lease state that

he has a right to retain the deposit in case of a breach of the covenants. The damages for the breach are the amount unpaid at the time the summary proceedings were had. These damages are absolutely fixed by the lease, and no other damages accrue after the summary proceedings, for these proceedings terminate the lease. 

Bloomberg v. F. G. W. Const. Co., 144 Misc. 17; 257 N. Y. S. 657.

An assignment of a lease does not include the security deposited by the tenant thereunder, unless it is specifically included in the assignment. 

Shattuck v. Buck, 158 A. D. 709; 143 N. Y. S. 1045.

Nature of a Mortgage MORTGAGES 

A mortgage has its inception when first it is delivered to somebody with the intention of having it become a binding lien, upon which the one to whom it is delivered could sue in case the conditions of the mortgage were not performed. 

The failure on the part of the owner of the mortgage to record it, does not affect its validity. It is good against all but subsequent lienors who act in good faith without knowledge of the unrecorded mortgage.

Real Property Law, § 291; 

Ochenowsky v. Dunaj, 137 Misc. 674; 244 N. Y. S. 267.

An instrument purporting to be a mortgage, which is left blank as to the name of the mortgagee, is absolutely void. However, if authority is expressly granted to fill in blank spaces left in a mortgage, or if the granting of such authority is by a legitimate factual inference, the mortgagor in respect to innocent third parties may be bound by the words inserted. 

Hilburt v. Walker, 258 N. Y. 8.

Let us suppose that A gives to B a mortgage for 3 years, in the amount of $800. The intention of the parties is that B is to assign that mortgage to X for $600, which sum B is to return to A. This mortgage has its inception, not when A delivered it to B, but when B delivered it to X. Therefore, the mortgage is usurious and is void. 

A mortgage of real property does not imply a covenant for the payment of the sum intended to be secured; and where such covenant is not expressed in the mortgage, or a bond or other separate instrument to secure such payment has not been given, the remedies of the mortgagee are confined to the property mentioned in the mortgage.

Real Property Law, § 249. 

A contract to give a mortgage, or to cause another to give it, is a contract for a transfer of an interest in realty, which must be in writing.

Mortgages given to secure future advances are a recognized form of security. The question arises whether the security of the mortgage is effective from the date of each particular advance or whether there is a lien when the agreement for the future advances is made. This is the rule: Where the mortgagee has bound himself to make ad- 215

vances, such advances when made relate back, and the mortgage is a valid lien for advances made against subsequent incumbrancers having notice of the mortgage. Where there is no obligation on the mortgagee, but such advances are optional with him, and he has actual notice of a subsequent incumbrance or conveyance of the mortgaged premises before any advances are made, the lien is not good as against a subsequent incumbrancer.

The rights and liabilities of the assignees of the mortgagee and mortgagor are of course the same as that of their assignors, unless there exists a contrary provision. A purchaser at a foreclosure sale purchases only such right, title and interest as the mortgagee had in the premises. 

Horowitz v. Welt, 224 A. D. 37; 329 N. Y. S. 519.

When the owner of a senior (prior) mortgage is seeking to enforce collection by a foreclosure, the holder of a junior mortgage may compel an assignment of the senior mortgage to him, provided he first tenders the amount due, with interest and costs. 

In re Ryan, 216 A. D. 619; 215 N. Y. S. 571.

Where a second mortgagee pays city taxes for the owner of the property, he is not the owner of the rights of the city so as to have a lien prior to the first mortgagee in the amount so paid. The taxes were not the first mortgagee's debt and he is not affected by the payment. 

Lawyers' T. & G. Co. v. Claren, 260 N. Y. S. 847.

Property "Subject to" the Mortgage 

Where property is sold by the mortgagor "subject to a mortgage of" a certain sum, the property remains as security for the mortgage, and the vendor, not the vendee, remains personally liable on the bond. If, however, the vendee assumes the payment of the mortgage, the vendee becomes, as between the parties, the principal debtor, and the grantor or vendor becomes a surety for the payment of the mortgage indebtedness. It therefore follows, in accordance with the rules of suretyship, that if a holder of a pond and mortgage, which contains a clause that the "whole of the principal sum shall become due after default in payment of interest," extends the time of payment for a grantee who has assumed that mortgage, the grantor is discharged to the extent of the value of the land. The same result follows if the holder releases the grantee. 

Wagner v. Brady, 221 A. D. 405; 223 N. Y. S. 99; 

Tousey v. Barber, 132 Misc. 861; 231 N. Y. S. 133; 

Hoffman v. Piccone, 242 N. Y. S. 707.

Let us now suppose that A owns a piece of property and gives a bond and mortgage to X. A then sells the property to B, who takes the property subject to the mortgage. B sells the property to C, who

MORTGAGES 

217 assumes the payment of the mortgage. C fails to pay the instalments on the mortgage, and X forecloses. Is C liable for any deficiency? The answer is No. C's title was derived from B, who was not at all liable personally under that mortgage. Consequently he could not become a surety for one who himself was not at all liable personally under that mortgage and who, therefore, was not a debtor. 

There can be no surety if there is no debter. If B had assumed the payment, then C in our case would have been liable for the deficiency. 

Vrooman v. Turner, 69 N. Y. 280.

A purchaser taking subject to a mortgage cannot set up the defense of usury against such mortgage. He has bought the equity of redemption merely. 

Sherling v. Gallatin, 261 N. Y. S. 747.

Merger of the Fee and the Equity in Property It is the general rule at law that if the ownership of the equity of redemption and of the mortgaged premises coincide in the same person, the mortgage debt is extinguished. The doctrine of merger has never been favored in equity and the estates will be held separate when justice requires it and when such was the intention of the parties. The intent of the holder of the two estates will be gathered from the situation and the effect which a merger of the title would have upon his interest. Equity will prevent a merger whenever it will result in an injury or injustice to the owner, and in the absence of some intervening right or equity in a third person, he may treat the estates as merged or not, as he deems fit. 

Dunkum v. Maceck Bldg. Co., 256 N. Y. 275.

Where an owner of mortgaged land inherits the mortgage he has a reasonable time to decide whether the merger shall occur. He is not required to act before he knows what the situation is. Less than 

60 days' delay in manifesting intention whether or not the merger should result is not unreasonable. 

Tharburn v. Wende, 235 A. D. 424; 257 N. Y. S. 186.

At law, merger will operate, even though one of the estates be held in trust, and the other beneficially, by the same person; or if both the estates were held by the same person on different trusts. 

Equity, however, would interpose and prevent the merging, if the justice of the case required it. As a general rule, an equitable estate would merge in the legal title subsequently acquired by the cestui que trust. In equity, the rule would be modified by the intention of the party and the requirements of justice, so that the equitable estate, if necessary, might be kept in existence. 

Burton v. Klein, 135 Misc. 571; 239 N. Y. S. 103.

Foreclosure of Mortgages 

The foreclosure of the mortgage is the legal process for realizing upon the lien. When, under a foreclosure judgment, the property is

sold, the sum paid by the purchaser takes the place of the land. 

The mortgage indebtedness is paid therefrom and subsequent liens foreclosed are transferred from the land to the remaining balance, distribution following in the order of priority of the liens. 

A defendant who was not a party to the bond and mortgage may not seek any affirmative relief on the ground that the mortgagee exacted from the original mortgagor a usurious rate of interest. 

The subordinate lienor may plead usury only as a defense. 

Vom Lehm v. Egbert, 259 N. Y. S. 487.

Proceedings to obtain any portion of the surplus money are called surplus money proceedings. Judgments over ten years old cease to be liens upon realty and therefore are not payable out of a surplus in surplus money proceedings wherein the surplus moneys stand in place and stead of the real estate. 

Douglas v. Chisholm, 142 Misc. 869; 255 N. Y. S. 15.

Where land owned by a husband and wife as tenants by the entirety has been sold under a mortgage foreclosure, the surplus over the mortgage debt is constructively real property still held in entirety by both. 

In re 115th & Vistula Ave., 242 N. Y. S. 6.

If the sum obtained will not satisfy the mortgage debt on which foreclosure is based, a deficiency judgment as to the unsatisfied balance, if asked for, results.

The title acquired by the purchaser at the foreclosure sale is free from the lien of the mortgage upon which the suit was based and of subsequent liens held by parties to the action. 

Sautter v. Frick, 242 N. Y. S. 370.

Even though the terms of a mortgage permit the holder in an action to foreclose, to have a receiver appointed for the premises if the owner fails to maintain the premises in reasonably good repair, the court will not appoint a receiver unless it can be shown that the failure to repair impaired the security. 

W. I. M. Corp. v. Cipulo, 216 A. D. 46; 214 N. Y. S. 718.

An owner of property is entitled to the rents until there is a default under the mortgage. Then the mortgagee has an equitable claim to all unpaid rents. When a receiver of the rents is appointed in a foreclosure action under the mortgage, he does not stand in the shoes of the owner; he has the rights of the mortgagee under the mortgage.

The receiver represents only the rights of the mortgagee. He cannot sue a tenant for rent unless the tenant attorns to him. Upon failure to attorn, the receiver may apply to the court which appointed him, to fix the reasonable value of the use and occupation of the

MORTGAGES 

219 1 premises and it generally is fixed in the sum the tenant agreed to pay under the original agreement of hiring. Then suit may be brought for the fair and reasonable value of the use and occupation. 

O'Brien v. Brody, 260 N. Y. S. 500.

The expenses of a receiver appointed at the instance of the plaintiff mortgagee in a foreclosure action, are chargeable only on the fund collected. If the receiver spends any more than he has collected, he cannot charge the expenses to the plaintiff. 

Handman v. Madonick, 235 A. D. 47; 256 N. Y. S. 188.

A receiver may not incur any indebtedness which exceeds the sum he collects, nor may he spend money for any purpose other than the physical preservation of the property. 

Mfrs. Trust Co. v. Roerich Museum, 261 N. Y. S. 206.

In the absence of fraud, it seems that the receiver may not disregard an agreement previously made by the landlord with the tenant. 

The receiver is entitled to receive and the occupants are required to pay the agreed rental rather than the reasonable value or the use and occupation. The receiver has no authority to accept the surrender of a lease. 

Lynch v. Harrar, 261 N. Y. S. 565.

After a foreclosure judgment to which the tenant has been a party, if he has lost his rights under his lease by the judgement, he is privileged after the sale to vacate the premises. Until the actual sale there is no eviction. The sale constitutes the eviction and until that occurs, the tenant remains liable to the landlord on his contract. 

Klasko Finance Co. v. Bellaire Hotel, 257 N. Y. 1; 

Prudence Co. v. 160 W. 73rd St. Co., 260 N. Y. 205.

If a lessee who has been made a party to the foreclosure action remains in the premises after the sale and continues to pay the rent to the mortgagee purchasing at the sale, in accordance with the lease, the lessee has thus attorned to the mortgagee for the remainder of the term. 

Jacob Reich Inc. v. Fordon, 234 A. D. 110; 254 N. Y. S. 453.

A purchaser at a foreclosure sale acquires the property subject to leases of tenants who have not been made parties to the action. He may enforce the covenants of the lease but he has no other rights. 

He is bound by such covenants at least in so far as they afford the tenant a defense or offset to his claim for rent. In such a case it makes no difference whether the purchaser is a mortgagee himself or a stranger. Though the mortgagee was not bound by the terms of a lease subordinate to his mortgage, yet in his character as a purchaser at the foreclosure sale, he can have no greater rights than any other purchaser. 

Stellar Holding Co. v. Berns, 143 Misc. 781; 257 N. Y. S. 369.

If a clause in a mortgage reads that "the whole of said principal sum shall become due after default in payment of any instalment of principal or of interest for thirty days," it means that if any part of the principal sum due is not paid on the due date, the whole sum becomes payable at once and the mortgagee may foreclose. The filing of the summons, verified complaint and lis pendens constitutes a valid election to consider the entire amount due though the summons has not yet been served and no other declaration of election made. 

Albertina Realty v. Rosbro Realty, 258 N. Y. 472.

Before the whole sum will become due for non-payment of interest, the interest must be overdue the full thirty days. The thirty days of grace apply only to default in the payment of interest. 

If the mortgagee continuously since the making of the mortgage receives instalments of principal as well as interest at a period subsequent to the due date but still within the grace period for the payment of interest, thus, by their continued dealings, leading the defendant into an honest belief that the grace period applied to both interest and instalments of principal, a finding that the plaintiff should be estopped from foreclosing in the circumstances would be warranted. 

Moore Realty v. Mootchnick, 232 A. D. 705; 247 N. Y. S. 712.

An unintentional, but negligent, delay of the mortgagor in making interest payment until one day after the 20-day acceleration clause had expired was held no ground for relief for the mortgagor, and the entire amount of the mortgage was held to be due. The facts there were that the president of defendant corporation, who was the mortgagor, was the only person authorized to sign checks in its behalf. Prior to his departure for Europe a clerical assistant computed the interest due on the next instalment on the mortgage, but made an error in calculating it. A check for this amount was signed by the president. After his departure the assistant discovered the error, notified the mortgagee of the shortage, assured him of remitting the balance upon the president's return, and forwarded the check as drawn. On the president's return, through forgetfulness of the clerk, he was not informed of the deficiency in the interest payment. 

At the expiration of 21 days from the interest-payment date, the mortgagee brought an action to foreclose. The mortgagor at once tendered the deficiency, and, the tender being refused, he paid the money into court.

Graf v. Hope Building Corp., 254 N. Y. 1. 

In a recent case the defendant failed to pay an instalment of interest which was to be paid on March 17th. A twenty day grace period expired April 7th. On April 3rd the defendant mailed a check for the amount due to the plaintiff. On that day the bank account of the 1

MORTGAGES 

221 defendant was insufficient to pay the amount of the check, but on the same day and the next day other checks in sufficient amount were deposited for collection. On April 8th the check of the defendant was returned uncollected for the reason it was drawn against uncollected deposits. It was held under the authority of the Graf case that the plaintiff could foreclose and equity would not intervene. 

Weirfeld Holding Co. v. Pless, 257 N. Y. 536.

Notice of intention to exercise the option to declare the principal amount of a mortgage due under an acceleration clause, where given in behalf of only one of two or more holders of a mortgage does not inure to the benefit of the co-obligees, and therefore the mortgagor's subsequent tender of interest is valid and the mortgagee cannot foreclose because of the alleged default. 

Seligman v. Burg, 233 A. D. 221; 251 N. Y. S. 689.

Under Section 150 of the General Corporation Law, a receiver can only be appointed in an action to foreclose a mortgage upon property of a corporation where it appears (1) that the mortgage debt or the interest thereon has been unpaid for thirty days after payment demanded; and (2) that either the income of the property is specifically mortgaged or the property itself appears to be insufficient to pay the debt.

Under Rules of Civil Practice section 179, where a receiver has been appointed, his appointment is extended to any subsequent suit or proceeding relating to the same property. Where, however, a first mortgagee commences a suit to foreclose after a suit to foreclose has been started by a second mortgagee and a receiver appointed all unbeknown to the first mortgagee, and a receiver is appointed in the second action, the latter supplants the first receiver despite the rule. The first receiver should account to the second. 

Nankin v. Lorran, 259 N. Y. S. 930.

Extension of a Mortgage 

Let us assume that A the owner of property, gives B a bond and mortgage. Before their expiration A grants to X who takes subject to the mortgage. On the due date X and B enter into an agreement for valid consideration to extend the time for the payment of the principal. The assent of A is not obtained. This serves to release A of his obligation on the bond to the extent of the market value of the property at the time of the extension. If at that time the market value of the realty equalled or exceeded the amounts due upon the mortgage, the value thereof being sufficient to cancel the mortgage debt, then A would be wholly released. If at the time ot the extension the market value were less than the debt, then A would be liable for the difference between such market value and

the amount of the debt. A would also be relieved to the amount of any depreciation in value between the time of the extension and the new date of maturity of the mortgage. Thus when an extension is relied upon as a defense, it must be shown that at the time of the extension the realty was of sufficient value to cancel the debt, or else as a partial defense that the property had depreciated in value since the extension. The burden is upon the defendant. 

Meldola v. Furlong, 142 Misc. 562; 255 N. Y. S. 48.

Where a bond and mortgage are valid in their inception their validity is not affected by a usurious agreement for the extension. The extension is void. 

Sherling v. Gallatin, 261 N. Y. S. 747.

MUNICIPAL CORPORATIONS 

Definition A municipal corporation includes a city, town, county, village, school district, sewer district, water district, lighting district, or any other district or territory authorized by law to issue bonds or having powers of local government.

General Corporation Law, § 3. 

The Legislature and the Municipality

The authority of the Legislature over the boundaries of subdivisions of the state is absolute. It may consolidate, add to, or take from the territory of a municipality or district, without the consent of the municipality or district affected. The fact that persons and property in the territory annexed may be subject to taxation to pay bonds and obligations theretofore voted, without having had any voice in creating the liability, does not render the act of annexation void. 

Adriaansen v. Board of Education, 226 N. Y. 145.

The right to levy and collect taxes is a power delegated to the municipality by the Legislature. The municipality has no power to grant exemptions from taxations, nor to make any contract looking to that result. 

Troy Union R. Co. v. Troy, 132 Misc. 534; affd. 238 N. Y. S. 577.

Torts A municipal corporation possesses two kinds of powers, one "governmental and public" and the other "private." In the exercise of the latter power, a municipality is like a private corporation and is liable for its torts. But when it is acting as a political division of the state, the corporation is not liable for its torts. It is difficult to determine when a city, through its agents, is acting in one capacity, and when in the other. The distinctions are not clear. 

The following activities have been held to be governmental, and no liability for tort exists in the performance of those activities: the exercise of the duties of policemen and firemen; of inspectors of election; the care and erection of buildings and apparatus used solely for governmental purposes, e.g., a court house, city hall, office of the city treasurer, prisons, workhouse, and their appurtenances, including the sidewalk in front of them; furnishing water for fire purposes. 

If a public building is used both for activities governmental and private, the municipality would be liable for damages resulting from negligence in the care of the building. 223

77 The following activities have been held to be corporate and private: the work of the street cleaners; furnishing water for drinking purposes for a price; management of municipal markets; cleaning of sewers; selling gas; construction and operation of subways; the establishment and maintenance of municipal parks; the collection of garbage; maintenance of municipal playgrounds. 

Canavan v. City of Mechanicville, 229 N. Y. 473;

Flatbush Waterworks v. People, 220 A. D. 784; 222 N. Y. S. 665; 

Harman v. City of Lauderdale, 134 Misc. 133; 234 N. Y. S. 196; 

Herman v. Board of Education, 234 N. Y. 196; 

Finkelstein v. City of New York, 183 A. D. 539; 169 N. Y. S. 718; 

Wilcox v. City of Rochester, 190 N. Y. 137; 

Boutet v. City of New York, 199 A. D. 835; 192 N. Y. S. 608; 

O'Brien v. Saratoga Springs, 131 Misc. 728; affd. 229 N. Y. S. 613.

By statute, every municipality is liable for the negligence of an appointee of the municipality who operates a municipally owned vehicle upon the public highway in the discharge of a statutory duty imposed on the municipality, provided the appointee was within the scope of his employment at the time. This liability exists even though the operator is performing a governmental duty. Thus a plaintiff injured by the negligent operation of an engine responding to a fire alarm may recover against the city. 

Snyder v. Binghamton, 138 Misc. 259; affd. 233 A. D. 782;

Highway Law, § 282g. 

No liability can attach to a city for the negligent operation of fire wagon, police wagon or ambulance although the operator has been given permission to use it, unless he was acting within the scope of his employment at the time of the accident. The rule of an owner's liability under Section 59 of the Motor & Vehicle Law (see ante page 8) does not apply to such cases.

Attempts have been made to distinguish between public and private powers. When the power exercised has relation to public purposes and is for the public good, it is to be classified as governmental in its nature. When it relates to the accomplishment of private corporate purposes, in which the public is only indirectly concerned, it is private in its nature. 

A Board of Education is not liable for the torts of those whom it appoints to carry out the functions for which it was created. Thus, the Board of Education would not be liable for the negligence of its teachers. The Board must provide instruction. Its duty is performed when it appoints teachers. It does not itself teach, and the teachers are not the agents of the Board. Such employees, like policemen of a city, are personally liable for torts committed by them in the course of their employment. It, however, remains liable for its own negligence. Therefore, a pupil in a high school recovered for injuries received while operating a dangerous buzz saw as part

MUNICIPAL CORPORATIONS 

225 of his school work. The Board of Education, as such, participated in the appropriation for the dangerous machinery. The Board of 

Education was liable for negligent maintenance of a school building, for such duty is imposed directly upon the Board. Its duty is not complete when employees are selected to take care of the buildings. 

Lessin v. Board of Education, 247 N. Y. 503; 

Herman v. Board of Education, 234 N. Y. 196; 

Katterschensky v. Bd. of Education, 215 A. D. 695; 212 N. Y. S. 424. 

If a municipality delegates a governmental function to a private corporation, this latter corporation is not immune from liability for wrongs committed during the performance of these acts. 

The agents of the municipality are of course personally responsible for torts committed by them whether or not the municipality is liable.

Before one can maintain his action against a municipality for personal injuries, he must comply with certain statutory requirements which include usually, notice to a designated official of intent to sue to be given within a period stated in the City Charter. Physical or mental inability to prepare this notice may be a ground for the allowance of additional time. Whether the ability is present in the plaintiff, is a question of fact for the jury. 

Clawson v. City of Ithaca, 214 A. D. 333; 212 N. Y. S. 433.

As a matter of law the strict requirements of the statute are not enforced against an immature infant of approximately ten years or less. They are enforced against an infant of riper years, of ordinary physical and mental capacity. Between these two periods lies a middle period where the infant may or may not be held to the provisions of the statute under the facts of the case as the jury may determine. No exact limitation of these periods by age can safely be made and the classification may depend to some extent on the facts in a given case, but it is the age and capacity of the infant, rather than the conduct of its parents and guardians which control. 

Russo v. City of New York, 258 N. Y. 344. 

Care of Streets

A municipality is only required to use ordinary care to keep its streets in a reasonably safe condition for travel, and is liable for damages resulting to a person injured as a result of its failure to do so, provided the municipality had actual or constructive notice of the defect. Permitting a hole 12 inches in diameter and 6 inches deep to remain in the sidewalk was not a violation of its duty when there was left ample space on the walk and there was nothing to catch and hold the foot of a pedestrian. A municipal corporation is not liable,

however, for defects in the highway when they are of a trivial nature and are slight depressions made by constant use. 

Moore v. City of Lockport, 228 A. D. 162; 239 N. Y. S. 190.

The fact that the abutting owner is also liable does not relieve the city of its liability.

This duty of the municipality cannot be shifted except by the Legislature. Thus, a city may be liable for damages caused by a defective bridge substituted for a sidewalk over which people walk, or for damages caused by a defective shed under which they walk, which shed was put up during the erection of a building, provided, of course, the city had actual or constructive notice of the defects. 

If a shed, inappropriate for carrying material, had stored upon it tons of bricks, making it unsafe for pedestrians, and this condition had existed for months, during which time inspectors were at the place, the city may be charged with notice of such condition. 

Meteroth v. City of New York, 241 N. Y. 470.

Where a municipality maintains a sufficiently wide highway in good condition, it is not responsible for accidents caused to or by people who leave the traveled portion of the highway. One should keep to the traveled track of the road. 

Contracts 

Horton v. City of N. Y., 213 A. D. 172; 210 N. Y. S. 433.

A municipality can make contracts the same as any other corporation, subject to its charter provisions, and it can be held liable for any breach. The purpose of these contracts made by the city must be municipal. Municipal purposes are such purposes as are public in character, for the common good and welfare of the municipality, c.g., to furnish free band concerts, free recreation piers, free colleges; celebrations of the city's anniversary; aid of the poor as a class; auditing city's books; care of highways, docks and bridges; construction of sewers, airports; care of parks, etc. The Legislature can determine what is and what is not a municipal purpose, but its determination is subject to review by the courts. 

Schieffelin v. Hylan, 236 N. Y. 254; 

Hesse v. Roth, 224 A. D. 344; affd. 249 N. Y. 436.

It follows from the above that the plea of ultra vires is available to a municipality. 

Troy Union R. Co. v. City of Troy, 227 A. D. 351; 238 N. Y. S. 577. 

It is illegal for a municipal corporation to sell water to persons for consumption outside of its city limits except by authority of the Legislature. The mere illegality of the act, however, does not give a taxpayer the right to enjoin the city, unless the taxpayer can show that the illegal acts are injurious or dangerous to the public interest. 

Western N. Y. Water Co. v. City of Buffalo, 242 N. Y. 202.

MUNICIPAL CORPORATIONS 

227 Once the city makes a contract for a stipulated sum it cannot pay the other party a greater sum, even if such a sum was promised during the performance of the work. 

Suppose several municipalities are co-operating for a number of years in a public project (as building a highway) on which a large amount of money has already been spent. One of the municipalities cannot withdraw on the ground that its representatives made a mistake of judgment when they started the project. 

An employee of a city cannot contract with the city, even though the employee gains no benefit therefrom.

One dealing with the agent of a municipal corporation has no right to presume that the agent is acting within the scope of his authority. It is the duty of one so dealing to ascertain the nature and extent of the agent's authority to contract. One may go even further and say that any fair, reasonable and substantial doubt as to the power of a municipality must be resolved against its existence. 

Pierce v. Board of Education, 125 Misc. 589; 211 N. Y. S. 788; 

American Surety Co. v. Loomis, 132 Misc. 62; 229 N. Y. S. 62.

A municipality cannot be authorized by the Legislature to pay the expenses of a police officer in successfully defending himself in a criminal action based on acts committed by him as police officer. 

It is not for a city purpose but would be a gift. 

Schieffelin v. Henry, 123 Misc. 792; 206 N. Y. S. 172.

PARTNERSHIP When a Partnership Exists 

If A receives a share of the profits of a business, he is prima facie a partner. But this is rebutted if it was (1) in payment of a debt owed to A; or (2) A's wages; or (3) rent to A, who was a landlord. 

In re Rosenberg's Will, 213 A. D. 167; 209 N. Y. S. 315;

Partnership Law, § 11; 

Sloane v. U. S. Syndicate, 135 Misc. 365; 238 N. Y. S. 91.

If A had invested money in the firm and had risked losing it in whole or in part, and was to share in any profits, and in addition had a right to participate in the management of the firm, there would be present the elements of the existence of a partnership. They would not be conclusive, however. The surrounding circumstances and the intention would have much to do with the determination. 

If A lends B certain machines to use in B's factory, for which A is to get one-fifth of the profits of the business, A is not therefore a partner. But if he had loaned money to B, for which he was to receive one-fifth of the profits, and he had the option of allowing his money to stay in the business or drawing it out, he would be a partner. There was a community of profits and a specific interest therein.

A may be a partner for all legal purposes, even though he may not have invested any money. He may be a partner by estoppel. 

Instances of a partner by estoppel would be: (1) A falsely states that he is Z's partner. L, hearing A, extends credit to Z. L can hold A as Z's partner for the purpose of this debt, and A would be estopped from denying the partnership. (2) The same result would follow if Z told people that A was his partner, and A, knowing of those statements, deliberately failed to deny them. L, lending money to Z on the supposition that he was 

A's partner, could hold A. 

A partnership is not like a corporation, which is a legal entity having certain rights and subject to defined liabilities. It has no independent existence. It has a name by which individuals conduct a joint business, and in which their accounts as such are kept, and through which certain established equitable rights in marshaling assets are acquired. 

Matter of Peck, 206 N. Y. 55.

It should be noted, however, that persons contracting with the individuals constituting a partnership, deal with these individuals 228

PARTNERSHIP 

229 collectively. For example, A and B as partners, made a contract of insurance with the X Insurance Company. B sold his interest to C and the insurer was not notified. A claim under the policy then arose. The partnership of A and B was not permitted to recover. 

As the original partnership of A and B no longer existed A's presence in both is immaterial. The contract of insurance was between the insurer and the now terminated partnership. 

Ardolino v. Ierna, 225 A. D. 439; 233 N. Y. S. 477.

Partners as Agents of Each Other 

A partner is the agent of every other partner for carrying on the business. If a partnership does an act which is apparently partnership business, the partnership is bound, even though the partner had no authority thus to act for the partnership, unless the person with whom the partner dealt knew that there was no authority. So a partner can borrow money in the partnership name. Also, an admission made by a partner concerning partnership affairs, within the apparent scope of authority, is evidence against the partnership. 

If the act done by the partner was not apparently partnership business and unauthorized, the partnership is not bound. Acts which are not apparently part of the partnership business, are (a) an unauthorized assignment of the partnership property in trust for creditors; (b) confession of a judgment against the partnership; (c) submission of a partnership claim to a referee or arbitrators; (d) binding the firm by a contract of surety for the accommodation of a stranger; (e) any act which would make it impossible to further carry on the partnership business.

Partnership Law, § 20. 

If a partner, acting in the ordinary course of business, commits a tort or breaches a trust, the partnership as well as each partner is liable. 

Model Bldg. v. Reeves, 114 Misc. 437; 186 N. Y. S. 759.

The liability of the partnership is by statute so extensive as that of the partner committing the tort. Therefore, a partnership would not be liable for the tort of a partner committed by that partner against his wife. The partner himself not being liable to his wife under the doctrine of Allen v. Allen (see page 158) the partnership would not therefore be liable. 

Wadsworth v. Webster, 261 N. Y. S. 670.

If a summons is served upon one of several defendants alleged to be jointly indebted on a contract (e.g., a partnership contract), the plaintiff on recovering may take judgment against all the defend-

ants. This does not apply in the case of several defendants jointly liable for a tort.

Civil Practice Act, § 1197. 

If the title to realty is in the partnership's name, any partner may convey the property if the partner so conveying could be reckoned as acting as the partnership's agent. If the buyer is not a bona fide purchaser and the partner who sells it is trying to defraud the partnership, the partnership can recover the property. 

Partnership Law, § 21. 

The partnership can acquire any estate in realty and it can as well convey in the partnership name.

Partnership Law, §§ 12, 51. 

The Partnership and Its Creditors 

Where money is loaned to a partner for a partnership purpose, the partners are liable jointly (not jointly and severally). 

Partnership Law, §§ 24, 25, 26. 

A partner has an equal right with his partner to use for partnership purposes all partnership property. This right to specific partnership property is not subject to attachment or execution, if one partner should happen to be liable for his individual debt. But the partner-debtor's intangible interest in the property can be levied on in such a case. The intangible interest consists of his share in the profits and surplus.

Partnership Law, § 51.
