If the partnership itself is the debtor, then the specific property can be levied upon.

Suppose there are creditors of both the partnership and the individual members. Can the creditors of the partnership issue execution against the property of individual members? First, partnership creditors must issue execution against partnership property and individual claimants against individual property. If one group, for instance, the creditors of the partnership, is not satisfied, it can issue execution against the property of the individual members if there is any individual property left over. 

In re Seybel's Will, 124 Misc. 297; 207 N. Y. S. 765.

Individual contract claims against a deceased partner's estate cannot be preferred over tort claims against the estate or against the partnership; all share pro rata. 

In re Seybel's Will, 124 Misc. 297; 207 N. Y. S. 765.

A conveyance of partnership property where the partnership will be thereby rendered insolvent, is fraudulent as to partnership creditors, if without fair consideration, though made to a person not a partner. 

Liebowitz v. Arrow Roofing Co., 259 N. Y. 391.

PARTNERSHIP 

231 Rights of Partners as Among Themselves Where several persons agree on terms of a partnership to begin at a future date, and in order to be ready to transact business on the day in question, they proceed with the plans necessary for that purpose, the partners who have been authorized to incur liabilities or expend money in making such preparations may bring an action for an accounting against the other members of the partnership and obtain judgment for the proportionate share the other members of the partnership must pay. 

Sivin v. Jones, 236 A. D. 483; 260 N. Y. S. 91. (1) Suppose a partner lends money to the partnership over and above the amount he is required to contribute. Even if no mention is made of payment of interest, it is implied that the partner is to get interest from the date of the advance.

Partnership Law, § 40. 

(2) A partner is not entitled to special compensation for services though they be extraordinary unless there was an agreement to pay for these services at the time they were rendered, or unless the services were performed under an employment or special agency altogether distinct from the powers and duties as a partner. 

Levy v. Leavitt, 257 N. Y. 461. (3) Neither joint venturers nor partners can sue each other at law for anything relating to the concerns of the joint enterprise, until after a settlement and balance struck and an express promise to pay, i.e., only after there has been an account stated between them. Before such account stated, the partners should seek their remedy in an accounting in equity. 

Perlman v. Perlman, 139 Misc. 396; 247 N. Y. S. 453.

Where one partner fraudulently misrepresents the value of certain stock to the other and induces him to purchase the stock from him at a high price, an action for damages in deceit should be brought in a law court. It is not an equity action, but rather one for fraud, and had nothing to do with the partnership. 

Langford v. Dalalle, 136 Misc. 62; 239 N. Y. S. 107; 

Ludlum v. Wagner, 212 A. D. 779; 209 N. Y. S. 540; 

Arona v. Fraser, 125 Misc. 333; 209 N. Y. S. 756. (4) If one partner sells any partnership property, he holds the amount received for the partnership. Every partner is a strict fiduciary toward the other. The same rules apply to joint adventurers. 

Greenslete v. Ferguson, 191 A. D. 745; 182 N. Y. S. 198.

When A, who held a lease in his own name for himself and B as a joint adventure, obtained a renewal of that lease before its expiration without B's knowledge, it was held that B was entitled to an interest in the new lease, as A was a trustee for B. The facts that

the new lease covered the adjoining premises together with the original premises, was held immaterial. 

Meinhard v. Salmon, 249 N. Y. 458.

When a partnership for a fixed term is continued after that term without any express agreement, the rights and duties of the partners remain the same as they were at the termination, so far as is consistent with the new partnership at will. 

Partnership Law, § 45. 

Dissolution of the Partnership

A partnership is dissolved by the death, determination and act, or bankruptcy of one of the partners, or by agreement of the partners, or by termination of the definite term or particular undertaking specified in the agreement. No one can be forced to continue as a partner against his will. He may be liable to the other partner for breach of contract, and nothing more.

Partnership Law, § 62. 

The will or intent of any of the partners to dissolve a partnership, or the mutual consent of all the partners to dissolve, may be shown by conduct as well as by words. 

Bayer v. Bayer, 215 A. D. 454; 214 N. Y. S. 322.

If one of two partners dies, it becomes the duty of the surviving partner to wind up the business. The surviving partner assumes the character of a trustee for the estate of the dead partner. 

If some transaction is necessary in order to aid the partnership in getting a large surplus, the surviving partner may carry through the transaction. Thus, if at the time a partner died a lot of 5,000 dresses was left unfinished and so almost worthless, the surviving partner may have the work completed and so have finished dresses which can bring a fair price. Should there be a lack of funds with which to pay the workmen, the surviving partner could even mortgage the factory to prevent a ruinous liquidation. ness. 

Losch v. Marcin, 251 N. Y. 402.

The surviving partner can be required to account for the firm transactions and for transactions required to wind up the firm busi- If the surviving partner fails to account and continues the business, the representatives of the deceased partner are entitled, at their option, to receive the profits attributable to the use of his rights in the property of the dissolved partnership. 

Cahill v. Haff, 248 N. Y. 377.

Although ordinarily no partner is entitled to any compensation for acting in the partnership business, a surviving partner is entitled to reasonable compensation for his services in winding up the partnership affairs.

Partnership Law, § 40.

PARTNERSHIP 

233 Suppose a partner transfers his interest in the partnership to another person. Since a partnership is a personal affair, it would seem that the partnership would be at an end. But this transfer does not, of itself, dissolve it. Nevertheless, the assignee cannot participate in the partnership's affairs or management or even inspect the partnership's books as a partner. All that he is entitled to is the assignor's share of the profits, if there are any. Of course, if the remaining partners decide to dissolve, they can do so. The assignee can then require an accounting of all the accounts since the last accounting.

Partnership Law, § 53. 

All those third parties who had extended credit to the partnership after the dissolution, but knew only of the fact of its existence and not of its dissolution (and the dissolution had not been advertised in a paper of general circulation), can hold the partners liable. 

The Retiring Partner

If the retiring partner publishes the fact that he has retired, in the newspapers, he is not liable to any who may become future creditors of the partnership. Future creditors here include even past customers, but who have heretofore been dealing on a cash basis. But such a publication is not enough to keep him from being liable to past creditors, i.e., those who have in the past been creditors, who have not read the notice and who continue to give the partnership credit. The retiring partner should have given these creditors actual notice. The notice must be received.

If the retiring partner has been a silent one, he need give no notice to anyone, for since no one was supposed to know that he was a partner, no one gave the partnership credit because of him. 

Is a retiring partner liable for partnership debts contracted before he left? Yes, even as to the customers that get notice, but only as a guarantor of collection. The creditors must exhaust their remedies against the partnership first. The same rule applies to the case where a partner dies. His estate is surety, and if the creditor fails to collect his debt against the survivor he can resort to an equity action against the representative of the deceased partner.

If one member of a partnership sells the entire assets of the firm to a corporation of which he is the principal stockholder, and for a non-existing debt, the sale will be set aside at the instance of the other partner, and it is such a fraud as would justify a dissolution. 

Where, upon the dissolution of a firm, one of the copartners purchases and succeeds to the business, the exclusive right to use trademarks belonging to the firm passes to this purchaser, although no express mention is made of them in the deed of assignment. 

Merry v. Hoopes, 111 N. Y. 415.

If a partner is declared a lunatic in a judicial proceeding, or in any other way is declared insane, so that it is not reasonably practicable to carry on the business with him, any other partner can apply to the court for a decree of dissolution. 

What are the effects of dissolution? If the dissolution was caused by the death, bankruptcy or any act of a partner, a partner who had incurred any liability while acting for the partnership, without knowledge of the dissolution, can get from each partner his share of the liability. 

It is a general rule that books should be kept open to the inspection of any partner at all times, even after dissolution. But this right in the partnership books may be transferred by a retiring partner like his right to any other bit of property, by express or implied agreement. 

Sanderson v. Cooke, 256 N. Y. 73.

The Incoming Partner 

An incoming partner is liable for debts contracted by the partnership before he became a partner. But this liability is satisfied only out of partnership property. He cannot be held personally liable for these debts as he can for those that are contracted after he became a partner.

Partnership Law, § 28. 

Limited Partnership Suppose A wants to be a partner in the X Radio Company, but he wants to be liable for no more than the amount of capital invested by him, and not be personally liable beyond that amount, as other general partners are. What must be done to effect such a result?

He must invest money or goods (services alone will not do). A certificate sworn to by the general partners, as well as by the special partner, must be filed in the office of the County Clerk in the county where the partnership's principal place of business is located. The certificate must state the name and address of the firm, and the names and addresses of each partner, the contributions of the limited partner, the nature and life of the partnership. After being filed, the certificate must be published in two of the county's newspapers once a week for six consecutive weeks. If a false statement is put in the certificate, the special partner becomes liable as a general partner. 

Partnership Law, § 90. 

The name of a special partner cannot be used in the firm name. 

If it is used with his permission, the special partner becomes a general one.

The general partners control and manage the business, but the special partner may transact business with the partnership, and in all such transactions he is treated as if he were a general creditor.

PARTNERSHIP 

235 Though claims of creditors have priority over those of special partners, the claims of the latter have priority over claims of general partners. 

Partnership Law, §§ 98, 99, 103, 112.

The special partner cannot withdraw any money he originally put in, unless the filed certificate so provides. If he withdraws any such amount, he is liable as a general partner to the extent of the amount so withdrawn until he returns it. 

If a special partner withdraws his contribution from the partnership at a time when the partnership is insolvent, the special partner can be made to respond to the extent of his withdrawal after the legal remedy against the firm has been exhausted. 

The special partner may also be so charged, even though at the time of his withdrawal there are sufficient assets left with the general partners to discharge the then outstanding liabilities, if before satisfaction of such liabilities the firm becomes insolvent. The contribution withdrawn is treated as a fund to be used for the discharge of liabilities existing when the special partner withdraws. 

Partnership Law, § 105; 

Kittredge v. Langley, 252 N. Y. 405.

A special partner can assign his interest. The assignee gets the assignor's right in the income and a right to dissolution. If the other partners agree to substitute him for the other, he may become a full-fledged special partner.

Partnership Law, § 108.

Tenure 

REAL PROPERTY 

friends Citizens and alien (friends can take real property in New York by gift, devise or purchase. A sale to a lunatic is void, unless it is for his benefit, made in good faith, and the seller cannot be put in status quo. 

Real Property Law, § 10; 

In re Valentine, 10 Hun 83.

All except a minor and an insane person can transfer interests in realty. Deeds of minors and of persons not adjudged insane are voidable. A deed of an insane person before officially so found is not even voidable as against a bona fide purchaser for value without notice of the incompetency. 

Goldberg v. McCord, 251 N. Y. 28.

The deed of a person confined to a State Hospital under a summary commitment by the county judge is a voidable deed. Once a person has been adjudged insane or a committee appointed to care for the property of the incompetent, a subsequent deed is void. 

Finch v. Goldstein, 245 N. Y. 300.

Should an infant desire to sell realty and give a perfectly valid deed, he can apply to the Supreme Court for permission. 

Real Property Law, § 11. 

Before a deed becomes effective as a grant there must be delivery and acceptance, with the mutual intent that title pass. The recording of the deed raises a rebuttable presumption of delivery. 

Estates 

Buszozak v. Wolo, 125 Misc. 546; affd. 220 N. Y. S. 921. 

A "fee" is the greatest interest that can be granted in real estate. 

It implies a proprietary ownership. A fee is conditional if it is limited upon the happening of some event of such character that it may never happen.

Fee tails do not exist in New York. Therefore, a conveyance to 

A and the heirs of his body gives A absolute title (a fee simple). If land is conveyed to A and the heirs of his body, remainder to B and his heirs (a common-law fee tail, with a fee simple remainder), what does B get if a fee tail is now equal to a fee simple? If A dies without issue, B gets the fee.

Real Property Law, § 32. 

The rule in Shelley's case is also abolished: So A to B for life, remainder to his heirs, gives B a life estate and his heirs take a vested 236

REAL PROPERTY 

237 remainder. B does not take a fee simple, for the life estate is not merged.

A life tenant may enjoy the estate in such a reasonable manner that the land shall pass to the remainderman as nearly as practicable unimpaired in its nature. He may only do what is required for the general use and enjoyment of the estate as he received it. He may not destroy existing buildings even to erect better ones, for this would be a material change and would render it impossible for the life tenant to restore the same premises substantially, at the end of the term. 

Brokaw v. Fairchild, 135 Misc. 70; 237 N. Y. S. 6.

A life tenant must pay the ordinary taxes upon the premises. If he fails to pay these taxes, the remainderman may pay and recover a judgment in damages and, if necessary, sell out the estate on execution, or else have a receiver appointed of the rents and profits, sufficient to satisfy the judgment. 

Keely v. Clark, 125 Misc. 541; 211 N. Y. S. 391.

The statute permits remainders, both vested and contingent. They are descendible, devisable and alienable, and this applies to personalty as well as to realty. A remainder is vested if there is a person living who would take at once, if one who then had the estate (less than a fee) died. But if the person or event upon which it is to take effect is not existent or is uncertain, as the case may be, it is contingent. The law recognizes three varieties of remainders. First, absolutely vested remainders in which the taking by a specified person is directed without any limitation whatever, e.g., to A for life, with remainder to B. Secondly, remainders vested subject to being divested, e.g., to A for life, with remainder to B if B survive A, otherwise to C; or to A for life, with remainder to B if C dies without issue, otherwise to the issue of C. Thirdly, contingent remainders, where the gift is to a person who may answer a certain description at some future time, e.g., to A for life, remainder to the issue of B, who is then childless. 

In re Terwilliger's Will, 135 Misc. 170; 237 N. Y. S. 390, 405;

Real Property Law, § 40; 

In re De Puy's Will, 194 A. D. 796; 185 N. Y. S. 817.

In one case X was given a life estate and upon the death of X the estate was to go to the children of X, and in case X should die without issue, then to F. F predeceased X, who left no issue. It was held that F's interest went to his heirs. The remainder, though strictly a contingent one, descended by virtue of the statute. The court refused to imply a condition that F should also be living at the death of X. 

Hennessy v. Patterson, 85 N. Y. 91; 

Guaranty Trust Co. v. Curry, 134 Misc. 99; 234 N. Y. S. 329.

Upon the death of one seized of real property the title to such real property vests in the devisee by virtue of the will itself. 

Milliner v. Morris, 219 A. D. 425; 219 N. Y. S. 166.

There cannot be created a remainder on any estate per autre vie unless this remainder is in fee. This applies to personalty also. 

Real Property Law, § 44. 

Estates for years are chattels real. Estates at will or sufferance are chattel interests, but they cannot be subject to sale on execution. 

Real Property Law, § 33. 

A lease is personal property and the right to renew a lease is personal property. Easements 

Rodrack v. New Moon Theatre, 121 Misc. 63; 200 N. Y. S. 237.

An easement is an interest in and right to the use of land and is created (1) by deed or (2) adverse use, open and notorious, for the prescriptive period (15 years). It is an incorporeal hereditament and being an estate or interest in real property, it cannot be created by a parol agreement. 

Rochester Post Adv. Co. v. Smithers, 224 A. D. 435; 231 N. Y. S. 315; 

Ehrle v. Sutton Place Apts., 137 Misc. 122; 241 N. Y. S. 386.

The estate for the benefit of which the easement exists is called the dominant estate. The land subject to the easement is called the servient estate. It is not necessary that the dominant and servient estates be contiguous.

An "easement in gross" is a mere personal interest in the realty of another. The principal distinction between an easement proper, that is, an easement appurtenant, and a right in gross is found in the fact that in the first there is, and in the second there is not, a dominant tenement. Easements in gross are not assignable or inheritable. 

Whether this is an appurtenant easement or an easement in gross is to be determined mainly by the nature of the right and the intention of the parties in creating it. The surrounding circumstances are of importance in determining that intention. As a rule of construction, courts favor the appurtenant easement. An easement or servitude created by a deed is never presumed to be personal or in gross when it can be fairly construed to be appurtenant to some other estate; and when the reservation naturally operates to enhance the value of the other adjacent land of the grantor it is a strong circumstance to indicate that it was intended to be appurtenant to the estate and not merely personal to the grantor. An easement is never construed to be in gross when it can fairly be construed to be appurtenant. 

Saratoga, etc., Corp. v. Pratt, 227 N. Y. 429, 442; 

Cody v. Springfield, etc., Co., 134 N. Y. 118; 

Weigold v. Bates, 258 N. Y. S. 695.

REAL PROPERTY 

239 A right of way by prescription cannot be acquired to wander generally and at random over the property of another. Such right can only be acquired by use of a definite and well defined route, not necessarily, however, certain definite tracks. 

Olofson v. Malpede, 127 Misc. 813; 216 N. Y. S. 695.

If a party claiming the easement shows an open and uninterrupted enjoyment for the prescriptive period, to disprove it must show that such use was licensed or permitted, or that it was restrained or limited in point of time, in order to defeat the claim. 

Niesz v. Spencer, 213 A. D. 476; 211 N. Y. S. 3.

If one sells a part of his land, he does not automatically have any easement (say, of passage) over the sold portion, even though the seller has been accustomed to pass and repass over that portion for for any number of years before the sale. The conveyor impliedly grants all those apparent and visible easements which at the time of the grant were used by the owner for the benefit of the part granted. 

If he had wanted to keep a right to pass, he should have reserved it at the time of sale. But this reservation could not have been made for any one but for himself and his land, or for one to whom he later sold his dominant estate. 

Scrum v. Davis, 141 Misc. 46; 251 N. Y. S. 746.

Suppose X and Y are tenants in common of Lot 1. Y floods the lot for thirty years, the flooding being the result of Y's placing a dam in a bordering stream. Y sells the dam and the machinery near to it to A. Lot 1 is sold to B. Can B prevent A's flooding the lot? 

Yes; A has not gained any prescriptive right because his grantor used the land for more than fifteen years, for the reason that one tenant in common cannot create an easement in land held in common. 

Crippen v. Morss, 49 N. Y. 63.

If A has an easement to cross B's land to go to Jamestown, and A goes to Jamestown, but only so as to go to Elizabethtown further on, he has become a trespasser ab initio. He is liable in an action, but the easement is not thereby lost. The next day A can cross the land to go to Jamestown, and not be a trespasser in so doing. 

Suppose W has a right of way over B's land. B blocks the passageway. W, unable to use his right of way, passes over another part of B's land. W is liable in trespass. He should have removed the obstruction and sued for damages.

If an easement is created by grant, mere non-user of the easement by the grantee for any length of time does not destroy the easement. 

O'Neill v. O'Hare, 254 N. Y. 186.

It may be lost by an abandonment for 15 years continuously or an actual adverse user for that period. 

Scrum v. Davis, 141 Misc. 46; 251 N. Y. S. 746.

To establish any abandonment there must be a definite unequivocal act showing a clear purport to abandon. 

Easement by Necessity

If a grantor conveys a portion of his property to another, leaving other land to which he has no access, except by passing over the land conveyed, an easement is reserved by implication. Actual necessity for an easement must exist. Mere convenience will not suffice. The necessity speaks as of the date of the conveyance and not as of some later point of time. Once the grantor acquires another right of way, the easement of necessity is lost. 

Smith v. N. Y. Central R. Co., 257 N. Y. S. 313.

The way permitted under a right of way by necessity is the route which is most reasonable and most convenient. No subsequent grantee is exempt from the easement of the right of way of necessity which might be reasonably determined upon as the most convenient under all the circumstances. 

Falcone v. Benjamin, 129 Misc. 143; 221 N. Y. S. 190. 

Dedication of Highways

Before there can be a dedication of a street or highway by a private owner to the public, the owner must show by act or declaration a deliberate, unequivocal and decisive intention to permanently abandon his property to the specific public use. To constitute acceptance there must be (1) a user by the public, (2) the tract must be kept in repair, and (3) taken in charge by the public. 

People v. Sutherland, 252 N. Y. 86.

In one case, a so-called street was used for 10 years, was policed by a city policeman, a gas main was laid therein, street lamps were installed, and sign posts were erected. The court held that there was not sufficient adoption by the public authorities to impose expenses of a city "street" upon the public.

The Profit 

Matter of Wallace Ave., 222 N. Y. 139; 

Lynbrook Homes v. Frey, 217 A. D. 164; 216 N. Y. S. 351.

An easement, as we have seen, is a right to do something on the land of another. A profit is a right to take something from the land of another.

A profit, and an easement, are both lost by unity of seisin. When a dominant and a servient estate become merged in a single owner, an easement or profit is extinguished upon the theory of merger. 

Let us suppose that A owns Lot 1 and X owns Lot 2. A has an easement or profit in X's land, Lot 1 being the dominant and Lot 2 the servient. A buys Lot 2 and he immediately sells the lot to Smith. 

A, although he still owns Lot 1, has lost his easement or profit in

REAL PROPERTY 

241 Lot 2, for once the same person owns the dominant and servient estates, any easement or profit is ended. 

Naccash v. Hildansid Realty, 252 N. Y. S. 383.

A profit is also lost if the dominant estate is split up among several new owners or the servient burden increased because of an act of the dominant tenant. If the servient estate is split into parts, the the dominent estate is required to take a proportionate share from each of the new servient parts, unless the product to be taken is practically entirely in one particular part of the servient estate. In such a case the dominant party may take all from that part. So if 

A has a profit to get coal from B's land, and one-half of B's land is sold to Z, should Z's part contain all of the coal, A is not limited to one-half from Z, but can get the whole share from him. 

The License 

A license in respect to real property is an authority, revocable at the will of the licensor, to do particular acts upon the land of the licensor, which would amount to a trespass without such permission. 

It may be given orally or in writing. 

Meers v. Munsch, 217 A. D. 541; 217 N. Y. S. 256.

If A, the licensor, barred up the road which the licensee, B, had made at great expense to the latter and had used for a long period, even forty years, B could have no recourse against A. 

A theatre ticket is a mere token or evidence of a license. Another case is where A allows B to open a road on A's land. 

Harris v. Jack Theatre, 139 Misc. 111; 246 N. Y. S. 396.

A mere license creates no right in the licensor's property and it will not be protected by an injunction.

The exception to the rule that licenses are revocable at will is the license coupled with an interest. C sells hay to J, and J is to come and get it. When J comes, C will not let him on his land. J still has a license, for the license J had is one coupled with an interest, and it is irrevocable. This applies to personalty as well. 

Adverse Possession

There are six elements to constitute an effective adverse possession. (1) The possession must be hostile. (2) The possession must be under a claim of right. (3) It must be actual and not merely constructive possession. (4) It must be exclusive. (5) It must be known by the person against whom the adverse user is claimed, or it must be so visible, open, or notorious as that knowledge of such use or claim will be presumed. (6) It must be continuous for at least 15 years. All must exist.

Civil Practice Act, § 35.

One in possession without title of any kind is not in adverse possession if during the period of possession negotiations were carried on in which it was sought to determine the question of right to possession. But where A, through mistake or inadvertence, possesses, uses or improves the land of B as owners are accustomed to do, such possession has been said to be hostile and under claim of right. B is charged with notice by the physical fact alone. 

Smith v. Egan, 225 A. D. 586; 233 N. Y. S. 582; 

Hintley v. State, 234 N. Y. 309.

Where a landowner opens up a way on his own land for his own use and convenience, the mere use thereof by another under circumstances which do not injure the road nor interfere with the owner's use of it, will not in the absence of circumstances indicating a claim of right, be considered as adverse, and will not ripen into a prescriptive right no matter how long continued. 

Sewall v. Fitzgibbon, 233 A. D. 70; 251 N. Y. S. 599.

Open and notorious use for the prescriptive period throws upon the owner of the used premises the burden of showing that the user was not under a claim of right. 

Olofson v. Malpede, 127 Misc. 813; 216 N. Y. S. 695.

Where the occupant, or those under whom he claims, entered into possession under a claim of title, based upon a written instrument or upon the decree or judgment of a competent court, and there has been a continued occupation and possession of the premises for fifteen years under the same claim, the premises are deemed to have been held adversely, by statute. Where the land is a tract divided into lots, the possession of one lot is not deemed the possession of any other lot.

Civil Practice Act, § 37. 

By "possession," as used in the preceding paragraph, we mean either (1) Where the land has been usually cultivated or improved; (2) where it has been protected by a substantial enclosure; (3) where it has been used for the supply of fuel or of fencing timber. 

Civil Practice Act, § 38. 

Where there has been a continued occupation under a claim of title, not founded upon a written instrument, the premises actually occupied, and no others, are deemed to have been held adversely. 

Civil Practice Act, § 39. 

By "possession," when the claim is not founded upon a written instrument, we mean either of the following, and no others: (1)

Where the land has been usually cultivated or improved; (2) where it has been protected by a substantial enclosure. 

Where premises have been bounded by a fence for upwards of the prescriptive period, the law presumes that the fence is the true

REAL PROPERTY 

243 boundary, and will not disturb this line, even where it does not correspond with the line of the premises as shown upon the map according to which they were conveyed. 

McMahon v. Morse, 135 Misc. 233; 237 N. Y. S. 361.

Tenants in Common and Joint Tenants Every estate granted or devised to two or more, unless expressly declared to be a joint tenancy, is a tenancy in common. But if these grantees or devises happen to be executors or trustees in their representative capacities, it is a joint tenancy, though not so stipulated. 

This applies to personalty as well. 

Real Property Law, § 66; 

In re Deming's Will, 106 Misc. 133; 174 N. Y. S. 172.

The essence of a joint tenancy is that the joint tenants take and hold as though they together contituted one person. The four unities of (1) time-that they acquire their interests at the same moment, (2) title that they acquire their interest by the same deed or will, (3) interest-that each have an interest identical with the interest of each of the other cotenants, and (4) possession-that each be entitled to the common possession of the entire property, must all exist. 

A tenancy in common requires only the unity of possession. They must all be present in a joint tenancy. 

Moore Lumber Co. v. Behrman, 144 Misc. 291; 259 N. Y. S. 248.

A tenant in common may convey his rights, and the conveyee becomes a tenant in common just as the conveyor was. If a joint tenant conveys his share, the conveyee becomes a tenant in common with the remaining joint tenants of that part conveyed to him. 

If a joint tenant dies, his interest goes to the surviving joint tenants until one is left, and the latter takes all. This is not the case among tenants in common. There is no survivorship rule applicable to them.

A tenant in common has a right to possession of all or any part of the tenancy. If one of them works on the land, he may keep all he earns. He need not share with the others. The others have the same chance to work and keep what they earn themselves. Those tenants in common living on the land need not pay rent to the others. 

But the rent of all strangers living on the land is to be divided equally among all the tenants in common. 

One tenant in common, however, cannot bind other tenants in common by leasing property, unless he is authorized to act for them. 

Early v. Robinson, 128 Misc. 184; 218 N. Y. S. 55.

One claiming as a joint tenant or tenant in common, even though not in actual possession, may maintain a suit for partition in which all questions of title affecting the entire property may be tried. Ejectment, which used to be the remedy, is no longer the only one.

Should it be impossible to subdivide the land, e.g., because of structures on it, he can ask to have the land sold and the proceeds divided.

Civil Practice Act, § 1016; 

Fisher v. Lister, 130 Misc. 1; 223 N. Y. S. 321.

A co-tenant against whom a partition action has been brought is entitled to credit for improvements and repairs when he has acted in good faith and there has been a substantial benefit to the premises. 

The co-tenant is not allowed credit for any improvements and repairs made after the commencement of the partition action. Moreover, the tenant in common possessing the premises can be compelled to account for the use, rentals, occupation, crops and avails of the premises from and after the date of the commencement of the action for partition. Of course, the tenant in common who simply occupies the premises himself cannot be charged with rent for his own occupancy, unless he has agreed to pay rental or has ousted or denied the rights of his co-tenant as regards the use of the premises. 

Eldridge v. Wolf, 129 Misc. 617; 221 N. Y. S. 508.

The expenses of the land must be borne by those tenants in common that make use of the land.

Tenants by the Entirety 

If an estate is conveyed in one grant to a husband and wife without any words of exception, a tenancy by the entirety is created. 

So if Mr. Jones and Mr. Smith owned Greenacre as tenants in common, and Mr. Smith conveyed his rights to Mrs. Jones, Mr. and Mrs. 

Jones are tenants in common and not tenants by the entirety, because they were not grantees in one grant. Also, if conveyance was made to "Fred Beyer and Mary Beyer, his wife, share and share alike,” they would not be tenants by the entirety, as there were clear words of exception taking the grant out of the rule. 

Hill v. Gross, 226 A. D. 621; affd. 253 N. Y. 55; 

Miner v. Brown, 133 N. Y. 308.

If H deeds his realty to himself and his wife, a tenancy by the entirety is created, even though there was not apparently one grant. 

The husband's original estate is considered lost in the new estate, and unity of title, interest and time is thus spelled out. 

Boehringer v. Schmid, 133 Misc. 236; 232 N. Y. S. 360; 

Coon v. Campbell, 240 N. Y. S. 772.

A conveyance to two who are about to marry, and who later do not marry, creates a tenancy in common. 

A tenancy by the entirety can never be created by descent. 

In the construction of the deed of conveyance the real but unexpressed intention of the grantor does not count. The husband and wife take by the entirety, even though the grantor did not know that they were husband and wife, and even though the deed did not de-

REAL PROPERTY 

245 scribe them as husband and wife. And if the conveyance is made to "H. and D., husband and wife as tenants of the entirety," they do not take as such if they happen not to be husband and wife, because a tenancy of the entirety can only exist where there is a valid marital relation. They take as tenants in common, unless the conveyance shows an intention to create what amounts to a joint tenancy. Thus, where a deed ran to "Michael Cowley and Matilda Cowley, his wife, . . . survivor to take the fee," and they were not husband and wife, it was held that a joint tenancy was created. 

Dewey v. Brown, 133 Misc. 69; 231 N. Y. S. 165; 

Bambauer v. Schleider, 176 A. D. 562; 163 N. Y. S. 186; 

Armondi v. Dunham, 221 A. D. 679; affd. 248 N. Y. 603.

Where the purpose of the parties to take as tenants by the entirety has been defeated by the fact that the parties could not contract a valid marriage, then a court of equity, in a partition action, may take into account the amounts invested in the property by the respective tenants in common, in determining the shares thereof to which they are entitled. 

Perrin v. Harrington, 146 A. D. 292; 130 N. Y. S. 944.

The fee in the case of a tenancy by the entirety is an indivisible one and it is vested in both the husband and the wife. On the death of either, the fee vests entirely in the survivor, not because the survivor acquired any additional rights (as is the case in the survivorship rule in the joint tenancy), but because the survivor was the representative of the single ownership. 

Matter of Klatsl, 216 N. Y. 83; 

Armondi v. Dunham, 128 Misc. 881; affd. 248 N. Y. 603.

Neither tenant can convey a present interest in possession while the other lives, even though the parties are separated. 

One tenant by the entirety may not, in the absence of any legal consent of the other, subject the property to an easement in the nature of a right of way. 

Grassi v. Loweth, 130 Misc. 861; 225 N. Y. S. 91.

A tenant by the entirety in possession cannot acquire a co-tenant's interest by purchase at a tax sale, because it is the duty of a cotenant to pay taxes thereon.

Either the husband or the wife can convey a future interest or mortgage the land so that the conveyee, or mortgagee on the foreclosure sale, would get all, provided the grantor or mortgagor survives the other spouse. If the grantor or mortgagor is not the survivor, the conveyee or mortgagee cannot get any part, for on the death of the one tenant the other gets the fee, so that there is nothing left for strangers. 

Misiazek v. Roberts, 211 A. D. 417; 207 N. Y. S. 621.

A conveyance, however, of the share of the rents or profits of either is valid. The share of the rents or profits of each is one-half. 

If both convey the fee, the husband and wife are tenants in common of the proceeds, including the bond and mortgage which may be taken as part of the purchase price. There is no estate by entirety in personal property. Where, however, the husband has obtained the entire sales price, the wife may bring an equity action for an accounting of the proceeds. 

Villone v. Villone, 135 Misc. 512; 239 N. Y. S. 49; 

In re Blumenthal's Estate, 236 N. Y. 448.

Upon the annulment of the marriage, or divorce of the parties, the tenancy by the entirety becomes a tenancy in common with equal shares, and it does not matter that prior to the dissolution one had paid all the consideration for the premises. 

Carpenter v. Carpenter, 130 Misc. 701; 225 N. Y. S. 426.

Suppose A conveys to H, W and Z, the latter getting a one-half interest. H and W are husband and wife. Therefore, H and W must be tenants by the entirety; and they are such of one-half. Z is a tenant in common with H and W of the other half.¹ 

Conditions and Limitations 

The differences between a condition and a limitation are these. 

In order to defeat the estate in the case of a condition there must be an entry; whereas to defeat the estate in the case of a limitation there need be no act by anyone. The happening of the event is, in itself, the sign of the end of the estate. In addition, at the entry, in the case of a condition, the estate goes back to the grantor or his heirs, as part of the old estate; in the case of the termination of the limitation, a new estate is created in favor of a stranger. 

Lyon v. Hersey, 103 N. Y. 264.

We can now see how these differences affect various situations. 

A conveys to B and his heirs, with a condition that if there is a default in the performance of any covenants the land is to revert to the grantor. In this grant upon a condition subsequent, the grantee takes a determinable fee, and not a title in fee simple absolute. The grantor, however, retains nothing except the right to take advantage of the breach, which in effect is a mere possibility of reverter; it is not an estate or even an interest in real property. 

The right in the grantor is not assignable or devisable. Upon the death of the grantor, his heirs succeed to his rights of reentry for condition broken by force of representation, and not by descent. 

Trustees of Calvary Presby. Church v. Putnam, 221 A. D. 502; affd. 249 N. Y. 111; 

Upington v. Corrigan, 151 N. Y. 143. 

1Since dower rights and rights of curtesy have been abolished in New York, the discussion of these subjects is omitted.

REAL PROPERTY 

247 The condition is entirely abrogated for all time if once it is waived or the land devised or deeded to a third person. This was plainly seen in this case. In 1907 the state of New York conveyed to X a plot of land on condition that X should improve it. In 1909 the state granted to New York City "such right, title and interest as the state might have in the land." X later violated the condition and said that he did not care for the land. There was now a right to reenter. Both the state and city claimed the land. The state won, the court saying that in 1909 the state had a right of entry for condition broken which was merely a personal right of repossession on breach. It was no interest which could be conveyed or assigned, and so in 1909 the city got no right in case of breach. 

Nichols v. N. Y. & Erie R. R. Co., 12 N. Y. 121; 

People v. Wainwright, 237 N. Y. 407.

A deed conveying realty on the condition that the property is always to be used for the worship of God according to the doctrine of the Presbyterian Church, with right to reenter for breach of condition, creates, as we have seen, a fee with a possibility of reverter for condition broken.

Although the grantor cannot assign his right of reentry to a stranger, he or his heirs can put an end to his own right of reentry by a release or a deed to the grantee, before breach, or by an attempted conveyance to any other person. The waiver binds the unborn heirs also. 

Trustees of Calvary Presby. Church v. Putnam, 249 N. Y. 111.

If a grantee is unable to perform the condition because of an interfering act of God, the fulfillment of the condition is excused and the estate becomes absolute in the grantee or his heirs at law, unless there exists a covenant to the contrary. 

Conditions subsequent are not favored in the law, and are construed strictly because they tend to destroy estates. 

Trustees, etc. v. Putnam, 249 N. Y. 111.

The bringing of an action of ejectment is sufficient to constitute an entry. It need not be a forceful entry. 

It is now necessary to notice this case: A to B and his heirs so long as the monument stands. This is not, as in the former case, a condition subsequent. It is a state of being which is part and parcel of the description of the actual life of the estate. It is a "limitation." 

Therefore A has no right of entry for condition broken, but a possibility of reverter. So when the monument no longer stands, title automatically goes back, and no entry is necessary. The happening of the event limits the estate and it ends. So, A to B and his heirs until X returns from Rome, or until Danville is incorporated into a village, are also examples of estates on limitation.

A reversion does not arise by a conveyance, but arises by operation of law. It is something left in the grantor after the deed to property has been delivered by him, with only possession by him postponed until the happening of an event contemplated by the parties. 

Any separate agreement by the grantee to reconvey upon the happening of that event is mere surplusage. 

Second R. P. Church v. Trustees, 127 Misc. 498; affd. 221 N. Y. S. 396. Conveyances

A contract to sell any interest in realty or to lease for more than a year must be evidenced by a writing expressing the consideration, and subscribed by the lessor or grantor. The memo must state the entire contract with enough definiteness so that its substance will appear.

Real Property Law, § 259. 

Where a material element of what was to be done was left open for future negotiations, it was held that the agreement was not specifically enforceable as there was no compliance with the Statute of Frauds. In this case the memorandum of sale stated "Price $32,000, payable $12,000 cash and the balance on first mortgage for five years. Sum to be paid on signing of contract, March 26, 1915 to be agreed upon." 

Ansorge v. Kane, 244 N. Y. 395.

If interest is mentioned but not the rate, and the date of maturity of the mortgage is not mentioned at all, the law implies interest at the rate of 6 per cent, and that the mortgage is payable on demand. 

But if the writing says, "terms of interest and date of maturity of mortgage to be arranged on closing of title," there is no compliance with the Statute of Frauds, as a material element was left open for future negotiations. 

Drake v. Sop, 131 Misc. 573; 227 N. Y. S. 576.

If the contract is not in writing, it is voidable at the election of the parties sought to be charged. Mere payment of the price in a "sale" of realty is not enough.

Where there have been clear, definite and certain acts of part performance of an oral agreement to convey, which are in part performance of a complete agreement, the oral agreement to convey realty will be enforced. Where the plaintiff collected the rents, paid the taxes and the insurance, and had actual possession of the premises, it was held that there was sufficient part performance to take the case out of the Statute of Frauds. 

Whitaker v. Westberg, 124 Misc. 556; affd. 213 N. Y. 935; 

Zim v. Cohen, 221 A. D. 341; 233 N. Y. S. 424; 

Sleeth v. Sampson, 237 N. Y. 69.

REAL PROPERTY 

249 Where a vendee has made payments as part performance, and the contract to convey is not carried out because of default on the part of the vendor, the vendee has a lien upon the property to the amount of his payments. 

Giarratano v. McIlwain, 215 A. D. 644; 214 N. Y. S. 582.

The vendees under a contract for the sale of realty become the equitable owners of the property agreed to be purchased. The vendors hold title as trustees for the vendees and as security for the balance of the agreed purchase price. In equity the interest of the vendors is personalty and the interest of the vendees is realty. It should therefore be observed that, in the absence of any stipulation, the vendee of land upon which buildings insured by the vendor are destroyed by fire intermediate the contract of purchase and the vesting of title, is not entitled to the insurance moneys. The vendee bears the loss. 

Reife v. Osmers, 252 N. Y. 320.

A vendee in an ordinary contract for the purchase of realty at a foreclosure sale is not entitled to an apportionment of accrued interest not due and payable, under a mortgage subject to which he takes title, or to a tax allowance which has not yet become a lien, unless otherwise provided for in the contract. 

Al-Dit Realty v. Hefferan, 225 A. D. 567; 233 N. Y. S. 588.

If a contract involving the sale of realty provides for the passing of title upon a certain day, that does not result in making that date the essence of the contract. If upon the day set, performance cannot be effected by a party, that party has a reasonable time after that to perform. Where, however, a clear and unequivocal notice or statement setting a fixed time exists, that time is of the essence. 

Ballen v. Potter, 251 N. Y. 224.

A grant of a freehold must be in writing and takes effect on the delivery of the deed. The grantor (or his agent) must sign it, and it must be acknowledged before delivery or attested to by a witness. 

If neither of these last is done, the deed is ineffective as against a subsequent purchaser of the land, until acknowledged. So if A gave 

B an unacknowledged and unattested deed and A later gave C an acknowledged deed, C, not B, would have good title, for the unacknowledged deed is ineffective against a subsequent buyer. 

Real Property Law, § 242; 

Roggen v. Avery, 65 N. Y. 592.

Suppose A sells land to B. B fails to record his deed, but he takes possession. Then A sells the same land to C, who is acting in good faith. C records the deed. The property is owned by B nevertheless.

To defeat C's right, it was necessary that C should have only constructive notice. B's possession was that notice. 

Page v. Waring, 76 N. Y. 463. 

The principle of constructive notice does not apply to unimproved land or unfurnished dwelling houses. The possession must be actual, open and inconsistent with the title of the apparent record owner. 

Schenectady Sav. Bk. v. Wertheim, 261 N. Y. S. 193.

If an owner of land unequivocally delivers a deed to A, with directions to give it to the grantee T upon the owner's death, the grantee receives title upon the final delivery, and this title is deemed to have vested as of the time of the first delivery to A. The actual change of ownership occurs at the time of the owner's death. If, however, the owner retains control of the deed and does not unconditionally deliver it to A, but keeps the right to recall the same, the owner does not dispose of his property by such an instrument. 

Allen v. Leet, 127 Misc. 662; 217 N. Y. S. 274; 

Gridley v. Home Ins. Co., 226 A. D. 596; 236 N. Y. S. 205.

Suppose Y is in possession and claims to be the owner. R says that he is the owner. S believes R and buys the land from R, taking a deed. He tries to eject Y. He cannot do that under the deed, because the grant he got from R is void, for it was given by one not in possession against one who claimed adverse title. He can maintain an action to recover possession only in the name of the grantor. 

Real Property Law, § 260. 

The tenants of a grantor are protected as against the grantee as to all payments made by them to the grantor before they received notice of the grant.

Real Property Law, § 248. 

Conveyances of land made with intent to hinder, delay or defraud creditors (the intent being always a question of fact) are void as against them, their heirs, representatives or assigns, but if the grantee is a bona fide purchaser for value, he is protected as against the creditors.

Fixtures 

Real Property Law, §§ 263, 266. 

Certain chattels have (1) such a determinate character as movables that they remain personalty after their annexation to realty independently of any agreement between the owners of the chattels and realty, e.g., gas ranges attached by a coupling to the gas service pipe: electric fixtures annexed to the realty by the simple expedient of being screwed to electrical outlets. 

Lightolier Co. v. Del Mar Club, 259 N. Y. S. 97.

Therefore the rights of a conditional vendor of such articles are determined by Sections 65 and 66 of the Personal Property Law 

(see p.

259), and a mortgagee of real property would not upon

REAL PROPERTY 

251 foreclosure obtain such personalty though the conditional sales contract was not filed prior to the recording of the mortgage. If the mortgage contained a clause under which personal property as well as fixtures were covered, then even personal property in this first class would belong to a purchaser at a foreclosure sale where the conditional sales contract was not filed before the mortgage was recorded, pursuant to Sections 65 and 66 of the Personal Property Law. 

Cohen v. 1165 Fulton Ave. Corp., 251 N. Y. 24. (2) Other chattels, notwithstanding any agreement, become realty when attached, e.g., bricks, stone, plaster. Here the chattel is so annexed to the realty that it cannot be removed without practically being destroyed. The filing of a conditional sales contract, would not protect the conditional vendor against a subsequent mortgagee. (3) There are some chattels that are in a middle class. The intention and agreement of the parties determine whether such chattels are personalty or realty. Any agreement declaring articles of the third class personalty, and reserving title in the vendor until full payment, is not binding as against a bona fide purchaser for value of the realty, without notice of the contract of sale of the fixtures. If the goods are so attached, a reservation of title to said property by the seller is void as against a bona fide purchaser who had no notice of the conditional seller's title. The conditional seller should have filed the conditional contract in the office where a deed of the realty would be recorded to affect such realty. This is not pursuant to 

Sections 65 and 66, but pursuant to Section 67 of the Personal 

Property Law. One who purchases the property at a foreclosure sale, without knowledge of the conditional sale of the article so attached to the realty, is considered such bona fide purchaser as to take title to the article if the conditional sale contract has not been filed in the proper office. 

Kohler Co. v. Brasun, 249 N. Y. 224; 

Madfes v. Beverly, 251 N. Y. 12; 

East N. Y. Elec. Co. v. Petmaland, 243 N. Y. 477.

Suppose contractor X, in building a house for Banton, brings his engine onto the land. The engine is not considered realty, for X did not intend it to be that. Even if the engine were affixed to the land temporarily, it would still not be realty.

De Bevoise v. Maple, 228 N. Y. 496. 

Suppose that X tore down Banton's fences, intending to replace them after his work was completed. Before he does so, Banton sells the land. The buyer gets the fences, for the fences were intended to be kept as part of the realty when X tore them down. 

Where the agreement between a landlord and his tenant declares that title to fixtures after they are affixed to the realty is to remain

in the tenant, the fixtures can be removed by the tenant during the term. But that property in tenant's fixtures which are not removed at the expiration of the term of the lease or upon surrender of possession of the land to which they are attached vests in the landlord. 

Talbot v. Cruger, 151 N. Y. 117.

The tenant can remove from the premises whatever he erects for the purpose of carrying on trade, even though affixed to the land. 

Crater's Wharf v. Valvoline Oil Co., 196 N. Y. S. 815. 

Accretions and Avulsions

When one's property is increased by the gradual action of the elements, as the receding of a lake or river, the added land belongs to the owner of the old land. The increase must be gradual. The added land is subject to the same liens and mortgages as the old property, of which it has become a part. 

If part of the land becomes submerged by reason of avulsion the owner does not lose title to the land so submerged. He can, even after the submersion, put up a sea wall and thus make use of the land again. 

Schwartzstein v. B. B. Bathing Park, 203 A. D. 700; 197 N. Y. S. 490; 

City of N. Y. v. Realty Associates, 256 N. Y. 217.

Party Walls 

A party wall is for the common benefit of contiguous proprietors. 

Neither party may subject it to a use whereby it ceases to be continuously available for enjoyment by the other.

The owners of a party wall have title to the soil up to the division line which is the centre of the wall. This title is qualified by an easement which each owner has in the half standing on his neighbor's land of supporting his building by means of the common wall. This right exists as long as the wall continues to be sufficient for that purpose. When the wall must be repaired in order to be used for the purpose of support, either may call upon the other to contribute towards rebuilding the wall; and, in case of his default, to build it himself and call upon the other owner to reimburse him one-half the expense. 

Varriale v. B'klyn Edison Co., 252 N. Y. 222; 

Partridge v. Gilbert, 15 N. Ý. 601.

If the wall is repaired in a non-negligent manner, any damage resulting to the other owner because of an unforeseen accident, e.g., a storm, while the wall is in an unbuilt state, does not make the one who tore the wall down liable. 

Either party may, at his own risk, increase the height of the party wall when it can be done without injury to the adjoining building and the wall is able to bear the addition. The addition must be 1

REAL PROPERTY 

253 solid, without windows, though the court has hinted that circumstances may alter the requirement concerning windows. 

Brooks v. Curtis, 50 N. Y. 639; 

Varriale v. B'klyn Edison Co., 252 N. Y. 222.

Emblements 

If a life tenant plants crops and dies before the crops can be gathered, the representatives of the deceased life tenant get the crops when they are ready, because growing crops, which are the result of husbandry, are personalty. Any regular annual growth belongs to the remainderman. Annual growth is considered personalty. 

Matter of Chamberlain, 140 N. Y. 390.

But if Jensen were a lessee for a term and his lease expired July 1, he could take nothing from the land after July 1,—not even the results of husbandry. He knew when his term was over, and he should have acted accordingly. 

Reeder v. Sayre, 70 N. Y. 185.

Suppose A contracts with B in relation to the sale of growing timber. Is it a sale of realty or personalty? If A was to sell the timber "cut down and delivered as cord wood," it is a contract for the sale of personalty. If, however, title was to pass before severance, the sale would be one of realty. 

Killmore v. Howlett, 48 N. Y. 569.

Surface and Underground Waters 

Surface waters include water spread over the surface of land or gathering into natural depressions or into swamps, or percolating the soil beneath the surface if flowing in no definite channel. 

An adjacent owner of real estate is not required to take care of the surface water coming on to his land from the lands of another. 

He may not, it is true, gather this water and cast it upon adjoining premises by drains or ditches, but in good faith and for the purpose of improving his land, the lower proprietor may deal with his lands no matter what may be the consequences with reference to gathering or setting back surface waters upon the land of his neighbor from whose lands the surface waters flow. 

Bull v. State, 247 N. Y. S. 183; 

Barclay v. Wilcox, 86 N. Y. 140; 

Vanderville v. Taylor, 65 N. Y. 341; 

Bennett v. Cupina, 253 N. Y. 436.

It should be noted, however, that the appropriation of underground water must be for purposes connected with ordinary use or improvement of the plaintiff's land, and if the use of such underground water is excessive and interferes seriously with a natural surface stream on adjoining land, the plaintiff may be enjoined. 

Smith v. City of Bklyn., 160 N. Y. 357; 

Forbell v. City of New York, 104 N. Y. 522.

A riparian owner is one who owns the bank of a body of water. 

The ownership extends to the high water mark. Riparian rights are incidental to this ownership. They can be severed from the land, taken by grant or by condemnation, or they can be made to pass with the transfer of the land.

Onondaga v. Crown Mills, 132 Misc. 848; 230 N. Y. S. 691. 

Every riparian owner is entitled to the natural flow of the water of a running stream through or along his land in its accustomed channel, undiminished in quantity and unimpaired in quality, except as may be occasioned by the reasonable use of the stream by other like proprietors. Although the riparian owner owns no part of the stream, he may withdraw water from it and cause it to flow unnaturally through his land, provided he causes it, in its substantial volume, to return upon his land to the stream. The one who owns the land lower down bordering on the stream is entitled to the water in practically the same condition in which the upper owner found it. 

Fulton v. Rockwood, 238 N. Y. 109; 

Maylender v. Fulton, 131 Misc. 514; 227 N. Y. S. 209.

The riparian rights cannot be taken from the owner by the state without a just compensation. But the state may authorize the construction of bridges, piers, wharves, etc., in navigable rivers, for the purpose of navigation and commerce, even though they cut the access of the riparian owners to the channels; and, in such cases, no compensation need be given. 

In re West 205th Street, 240 N. Y. 68.

If, however, the state diverts the water of a river into a channel wholly distinct and apart from the river itself, in order to improve navigation in that channel and not in the river, the state must pay compensation to the riparian owners. 

West Shore R. R. Co. v. State, 216 A. D. 603; 215 N. Y. S. 684.

A riparian owner has no right to divert waters of a navigable river, and he cannot acquire title to the land under the water by filling it up.

The riparian owner has right of access to the channel or to the navigable part of the river for navigation, fishing and other uses commonly belonging to a riparian owner. He can make a pier for his own use or for that of the public. 

The purchaser of land bordering an artificial lake or pond, created by a dam, acquires no riparian rights.

The general character of a stream with reference to its navigability is not changed by the fact that at a particular place it is not, in fact, navigable for a short distance. 

People v. N. Y. & O. P. Co., 219 A. D. 114; 219 N. Y. S. 497.

SALES The Statute of Frauds¹ 

A contract to sell, or for the sale of personal property, of the value of fifty dollars or more, comes within the Statute of Frauds and is not enforceable by an action unless (1) The buyer shall accept part of goods contracted for and actually receive the same, or (2) The buyer shall give something to bind the contract or make part payment, or (3) Some note or memorandum in writing be signed by the party to be charged or his agent in that behalf. 

The existence of any one of these three situations takes the case out of the Statute of Frauds. A fourth is discussed on page 257. 

Personal Property Law, § 85. 

The rule is not affected by the fact that the goods are not yet in existence or are to be delivered at some future date.

Where many items are bought at one time, the price of each individual item being under $50 and the total exceeding that sum, the statute applies if the purchases are part of one contract. That is to be determined by the jury. (1) Acceptance and Receipt.-The acceptance which takes a case out of the Statute of Frauds must be one of affirmative expression by words or conduct, an assent to become the owner of the goods. 

Delivery to the buyer's place of business without the buyer's knowledge or for the purpose of examination does not take the case out of the Statute. A mere retention for even an unreasonable time for such purpose, although a sufficient acceptance to bar a rejection of the goods later, because of a breach of warranty (see infra), is not a sufficient acceptance to take it out of the statute. A very long detention might be such an affirmative acceptance, but how much is necessary is really a question of fact. 

Strachman v. Levy, 124 Misc. 160, 207 N. Y. S. 185.

A delivery to the carrier is not an acceptance by the buyer, even if the buyer had designated the carrier to receive the goods. The agency is at most for the receipt and carriage only. (2) Part Payment.-This payment may be in any amount and need not necessarily be by means of money. Goods or services will suffice. 

1As regards the pleading of the Statute of Frauds, see "The Adjective Law of New York." 255

An interesting situation occurred in a recent case. A buyer of electric bulbs of the value of over $50 paid for them by check. He subsequently stopped the check, before there was any receipt of goods by him. The seller sued and the defense was the Statute of Frauds. 

The buyer contended that there was no part payment, since a check is only conditional payment. The court disagreed, saying that the rule as to conditional payment operates solely for the protection of a creditor, and also that the purpose of the rule which requires payment in order to avoid the Statute of Frauds was to have some overt act in addition to the words of mouth. The delivery of the check, which is the commonly accepted method of payment, was such overt act and the Statute of Frauds was no defense. 

Ablett v. Sencer, 130 Misc. 416, 224 N. Y. S. 251. (3) The Memorandum.-The memorandum must specify the subject-matter and contain every essential term of the agreement, such as the consideration, the terms, and names of the parties, even though an agent sign. 

Stone v. Browning, 68 N. Y. 598; 

Byrne v. McDonough, 114 Misc. 529, 186 N. Y. S. 807.

In a recent case, the memorandum read, "Received from C. A. 

Haas, $25 as deposit on property (describing the property). Price $25,000, twenty five thousand dollars payable as follows: $2,500 on signing of contract, $9,500 on taking title, and balance first mortgage for five years standing. Contract to be for four months." This was signed by the defendant seller.

As a matter of fact, Haas was not the purchaser, but merely the broker for an undisclosed principal. The court held that the plaintiff could not obtain specific performance as the memorandum was insufficient. A memorandum does not fail of its effect if one described as principal is in truth only an agent, for the authority of the agent may be shown by parol, and the principal for whom he acts, except indeed in cases where there is a contract under seal, may sue and be sued as if his name had been disclosed. It is essential, however, that the agent shall bind himself as principal, if the writing does not identify the principal behind him. The memorandum may be adequate, though the agent is described as agent, provided the form of the contract purports to bind him personally. The memorandum is inadequate where by its terms the principal is not identified, and the agent, though his name is on the paper, is not there as a contracting party. Though this case dealt with realty it would not have been decided otherwise were personalty involved.

The memorandum need not be a formal document. It may consist of correspondence, telegrams or notations, provided that when they are considered together, all the necessary elements of a contract are found. 

Irvmor Corporation v. Rodewald, 253 N. Y. 472.

SALES 

257 If the parol agreement fixed no time for delivery, the memorandum is sufficient even if that item is omitted. The law supplies that missing term and requires delivery within a reasonable time. 

If, on the other hand, the time was fixed by the parties, the memorandum is insufficient if it does not mention the time for delivery. 

Berman v. Hirsh, 240 N. Y. 209.

The signature may be placed on any part of the memorandum. 

A name which is written or printed is not a signature unless inserted or adopted with an intent, actual or apparent, to authenticate the writing. A signature is adequate if the party to be charged writes "O.K." in the margin or on the top, and adds thereto his name in full or his initials. The delivery of a paper containing the order of goods written under the printed name of the seller, i.e., under his letterhead, has been held to be an acceptance of the contract, and the printed name in the letterhead held to be a signature. Where, however, in this latter case, there was a line for a signature next to the word "accepted" at the bottom of the page, the court held that this showed an intention on the part of the seller not to have the printed name in the letterhead constitute the signature, but that a later acceptance was necessary for the formation of the contract. 

Mesibov v. Cohen, 245 N. Y. 305. 

(4) Goods of a Special Nature.—If goods are to be manufactured by the seller especially for the buyer, and are not suitable for sale in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the provisions of the 

Statute of Frauds do not apply. 

Tilton v. Bachrach, 121 Misc. 708, 202 N. Y. S. 506.

If a contract was required to be in writing, an alleged oral extension of that contract will not be enforced, unless the party claiming the benefit of the oral extension could have performed within the original time set, but refrained from doing so in reliance upon the alleged extension. In the latter instance, the other party is estopped from setting up the defense of the Statute of Frauds. 

Davison v. Weston, 209 A. D. 514, affd. 240 N. Y. 705. 

Fraudulent Transfers

If a debtor should transfer personalty in trust for himself, it would be void as against existing creditors and subsequent ones. 

His good faith is immaterial. Of course, if there is an intention on the part of both parties to defraud the creditors of the transferor, any transfer is void.

Personal Property Law, § 34; 

Young v. Heermans, 66 N. Y. 374.

A person who has a tort claim against the transferor at the time the transfer is made is considered a creditor under this section, so that if the claimant obtains a judgment for the tort subsequent to

the transfer, he may maintain his action to set aside a fraudulent transfer. The rule applies to a fraudulent transfer of realty as well. 

In one case the defendant ran over the plaintiff. The defendant then transferred his property to his wife, without consideration. The plaintiff then obtained a judgment against the defendant, and he commenced an action to set aside the transfer as a fraudulent conveyance, made with the intent to hinder, delay or defraud the rights of creditors. It was held that he could maintain the action. 

Gatto v. Boyd, 241 N. Y. S. 626; 

Roberts & Co. v. Buckley, 145 Ń. Y. 215.

Where a conveyance is fraudulent as to a creditor whose claim has matured, he may, as against any person except an innocent purchaser for value or his assignee, (a) have the conveyance set aside to the extent necessary to satisfy his claim; (b) disregard the conveyance and attach or levy execution upon the property conveyed. 

If the creditor's claim has not yet matured, the court may (a) restrain the defendant from disposing of his property; (b) appoint a receiver of the property (c) set aside the conveyance; (d) make any order which the circumstances require. 

In neither of the above situations is a judgment essential before the creditor can act. 

Amer. Surety Co. v. Conner, 251 N. Y. 1.

In his action to set aside an alleged fraudulent transfer, the plaintiff must specify the property claimed to have been fraudulently conveyed and the time when the transfers were made. 

Herring v. Curtiss, 140 Misc. 857; 252 N. Y. S. 106.

If the transfer is made to a bona fide purchaser for value, the latter is protected as against creditors despite the intention of the transferor. 

Bulk Sales

Personal Property Law, § 40. 

We are now to discuss the cases where, otherwise than in the ordinary prosecution of the business, the whole or part of a stock in trade of a fixed place of business is sold or mortgaged in bulk, e.g., when separating, counting, weighing or measuring do not take place, but rather the mass is sold as one. Such a sale or mortgage is void as against present creditors of the seller, but the seller can prevent this by having all the creditors notified of the proposed sale or mortgage, at least five days before possession of this stock is taken. 

Feldstein v. Fusco, 238 N. Y. 58;

Personal Property Law, § 44. 

When an action is brought under § 44 of the Personal Property Law (Bulk Sales Act), the plaintiffs need not allege that they are judgment creditors. Any creditor of a seller, whether his claim has

SALES 

259 been put in judgment or not, may sue under the act. This does not apply to contingent claim creditors. The nature of their claim, however, must be set forth. 

Willi v. Lyon, 131 Misc. 73, 226 N. Y. S. 283.

The action may be maintained by the trustee in bankruptcy who represents the creditor of the seller. 

Mott v. Reeves, 125 Misc. 511, 211 N. Y. S. 375.

Only those who are judgment creditors of the seller may have a receiver appointed of the business bought. 

Hersch v. Goldberg, 126 Misc. 857, 214 N. Y. S. 389. 

Conditional Sales¹

Under a condition sales contract at common law no title to chattels not fixed and not to be fixed to realty passed to the buyer until the contract price had been paid. The contract was good against everybody without notice. By statute, the common law has been kept in force, provided the conditional vendor has acted in accordance with the statute.

If the conditional vendor of such unattached chattels wants to be protected, he must record the conditional sale in the county in which the vendee resides. Otherwise the sale will not be effective as against a later bona fide purchaser from the conditional vendee who bought for value without notice of the conditional sale, or as against one who has actually levied on the chattel, or as against a trustee in bankruptcy of the vendee. 

Baker v. Hull, 250 N. Y. 484;

Personal Property Law, §§ 64, 65, 66. 

Any waiver of a statutory requirement, whereby the vendee consents to any modification of the provisions of the statute, which is set forth in the conditional sales contract, is invalid. Where, however, a new contract arises for a new consideration passing between the parties, the modification agreed upon outside the instrument itself will be enforced. 

Fisher v. Stewart, 132 Misc. 225, 228 N. Y. S. 549.

Despite the fact that title may not yet have passed, the vendor in a conditional sales agreement may sue for the price if there is a default in the payment of an instalment.

In an action to obtain possession of a chattel under a conditional sales contract, the proper remedy is replevin. 

Marcus v. Sherr, 132 Misc. 734, 230 N. Y. S. 425.

Whenever a conditional seller has retaken possession of the goods, and the buyer at the time of retaking has paid at least half the pur- 

1For a further discussion, see supra "Fixtures" on p. 250. This discussion deals with group (1) of that section, i.e. chattels which are not so attached to realty that they obviously retain a classification of personalty.

chase price, a resale is compulsory. Such resale must take place within thirty days and at public auction.

Personal Property Law, § 79. 

In the event of the failure of the seller to resell the goods retaken in compliance with § 79, the buyer may recover from the seller his actual damages, if any, and in no event less than one-fourth of the sum of all payments which have been made under the contract, with interest.

Personal Property Law, § 80e; 

La Rocca v. Sanders, 230 A. D. 594; 245 N. Y. S. 262.

The general rule is that upon a sale of personal property at auction, the property must be at hand and within the view of those attending. This rule is not so absolute as to admit of no exceptions. 

The exceptions depend upon considerations of policy and fairness in view of circumstances. The thing sold may be too bulky to be brought within the view of the bidders. However, when for some special reason the chattel to be sold is not to be present in the auction room, and its nature is such that inspection by a bidder can have any possible utility, the notice of sale must state the place where the chattel is located, and give opportunity to view it.¹ 

Manhattan Taxi Corp. v. Checker C. M. Corp., 253 N. Y. 455. 

Chattel Mortgages

Every chattel mortgage, not accompanied by an immediate delivery, and a continued change of possession of the chattel mortgaged, is absolutely void as against creditors of the mortgagor, unless the mortgage is duly filed. If the mortgagee has unreasonably delayed the filing of his mortgage, he cannot, as against creditors at large, save the mortgage by filing it previously to the acquisition of a lien by such creditors. These creditors do not need to be judgmentcreditors. It should thus be noted that there is a decided difference in the law as respects creditors where a conditional sale is involved and where a chattel mortgage is involved. 

Baker v. Hull, 250 N. Y. 484.

Title to mortgaged chattels vests in the mortgagee immediately after default though they are not reduced to possession and the equity of redemption is not foreclosed. 

Sprague v. Olgren, 238 N. Y. S. 696.

Mortgages pledging subsequently acquired property will be enforced in equity as between mortgagor and mortgagee as agreements to give liens, and also as against purchasers with notice. They will not be enforced as against attaching or execution creditors. 

Diana Paper Co. v. Wheeler-Green, 240 N. Y. S. 108. 

1For a detailed discussion of the procedure upon default by the conditional vendee see Laufer v. Burghard, 261 Ñ. Y. S. 364.

SALES A chattel mortgage executed by a resident of another state and there recorded properly, protects the mortgagee and a sale by the mortgagor to a third party in New York does not pass good title unless the third party can show apparent authority to deal with the mortgaged property as his own. Possession alone is not such apparent authority. 

Farnham v. Eichin, 230 A. D. 639; 246 N. Y. S. 133.

Passing of Title 

The question "When did title pass" has been the cause of much litigation. Where goods are destroyed en route it becomes necessary to determine the owner of the title so that person usually must bear the loss. Where creditors attach goods in the possession of a debtor, the latter's lack of title to such goods vitally affects the creditor. 

As a general rule the title passes from the seller to buyer at the time the parties intend to have it pass. The following rules will be helpful in determining the time of passage of title where the intentions are unexpressed or ambiguous. 

(1) Future and Unascertained Goods.-At common law, title to goods not yet in existence or in possession of the seller passed at the time the contract was made. Under the Sales Act a buyer of future goods, e.g., crops to be grown, does not get title from the time of the bargain, but gets only a contract right.

In the case of future or unascertained goods, title passes (a) when the goods come into existence, and (b) they are in a deliverable state, and (c) appropriated to the contract by either party with the assent of the other. Such assent may be inferred from express words or circumstances.

Personal Property Law, § 86, Subd. 3; § 100, Subd. 4. 

Section 100, Rule 4, subdivision 2 of the Personal Property Law provides that where the seller delivers goods to the carrier for transmission to the buyer, the presumption exists that the seller has appropriated the goods to the contract. 

These rules cover C. O. D. transactions. The delivery of the goods to the carrier for transmission to the buyer effect the passing of title even though the purchaser is required to pay before he can obtain possession or inspect the goods.

Personal Property Law, § 100, Rule 4, Subd. 2. 

Where an article of personalty is to be built and paid for in instalments, title to as much as is completed does not pass either upon completion or upon payment of the instalment. The payment and its receipt are not considered the assent. 

Andrews v. Durant, 11 N. Y. 35.

In one case the buyer ordered goods which were to be packed and delivered to him. The goods were packed properly, and before shipment the buyer asked the seller to hold the goods for further instructions.
