It was held that title passed upon the notification, since the goods had been properly appropriated in the contract and the buyer had assented.

Taylor v. Kurzrok, 214 A. D. 308, 212 N. Y. S. 133. 

(2) Fungible Goods. Where a quantity of a specific mass is contracted for, e.g., 1,000 bushels of wheat from a heap, the buyer becomes a tenant in common with the owner of such proportionate share as the amount bought bears to the amount in the heap. If, before delivery, a large part of the heap is stolen or burnt, the buyer is responsible for his proportionate share of the loss, as he was a tenant in common with the owner of a proportionate share. This is called a contract to sell an undivided share of fungible goods. 

Personal Property Law, §§ 87, 98; 

Kimberly v. Patchin, 19 N. Y. 330. 

(3) Sale of Specific Goods.—Where goods are specific or ascer tained at the time a contract for the sale is entered into and the goods are in a deliverable state at that time, the presumption is that title was intended to pass at the making of the contract. Where something remains to be done for the purpose of putting the goods in a deliverable state, the title does not pass until that is done. 

Personal Property Law, § 99; § 100, Rules 1, 2. 

The expenses incurred in putting the goods in a deliverable state are borne by the seller in the absence of a contrary agreement. 

Personal Property Law, § 124, Subd. 3. 

It makes no difference that the buyer is to pay at a later date or is to get the goods later. So if a buyer enters a store and orders a coat which is ready for delivery, the buyer would stand the loss should the store and coat burn that night. There was (a) an unconditional contract (b) to sell specific goods and (c) the goods were in a deliverable state, i.e., in such a condition that the buyer would be bound to accept the goods under the contract. If the coat had lacked buttons, or trimmings, or needed any alteration, title would not have passed until those things had been taken care of. 

Procter & Gamble Co. v. Peters, 233 N. Y. 97;

Personal Property Law, § 100. 

(4) Sale or Return.-If goods are sent "on sale or return," title passes at once. If the buyer sends backs the goods within the time fixed in the contract, or, if no time has been fixed, within a reasonable time, the title reverts to the seller. 

Personal Property Law, § 100, Rule 3, Subd. 1.

SALES 

263 The buyer pays the freight where nothing in the contract covers the point. Therefore, a return to a railroad depot for shipment to the seller is not sufficient to revest title. 

Lyon v. Motley, 9 Misc. 500, 30 N. Y. S. 218. (5) Sale on Approval.—Where a party is permitted to take possession of specific goods with the right to determine within a period whether he is to become the owner, the arrangement is termed a sale on approval. Title passes only when the buyer shows that he accepts the goods, either by saying so or by retaining the goods beyond a reasonable time. What is "reasonable" is a question of fact. 

But a retention and use for sixteen months was held more than a reasonable time as a matter of law. 

Brown v. Potolski, 221 A. D. 299, 223 N. Y. S. 71;

Personal Property Law, § 100, Rule 3, Subd. 4. 

A sale by the possessor to a third party is deemed an approval and title will be considered as having passed. 

In the absence of any contrary arrangement, the seller must bear the expense of the return. The prospective buyer need only notify the seller of his dissatisfaction. (6) F. O. B. Contracts.-The letters F. O. B. mean "free on board." The seller is to deliver the goods free of expense to the buyer to the place stated after the F. O. B. Thus if goods are shipped "F. O. B. New York," the seller pays for packing, freight and other charges until the goods reach New York. 

Title passes when the goods arrive at the place stated after the 

F. O. B. If goods are sold "F. O. B. New York," by a seller in

New York to a buyer in Cleveland, title passes when the goods are delivered to the carrier in New York. 

Rosenberg Bros. v. Buffum, 234 N. Y. 338; 

Standard Casing v. California Casing, 233 N. Y. 413.

The fact that the consignee has the privilege of inspection does not transfer the risk. 

Porter v. Penn. R. Co., 217 A. D. 49, 215 N. Y. S. 727. (7) C. I. F. Contracts.-These letters stand for "cost, insurance and freight." A c. i. f. sale means that the agreed price includes the cost of goods at the point of shipment plus insurance and freight to the point stated after the c. i. f. 

In a c. i. f. contract the seller completes his contract and the risk is on the buyer after the seller delivers the merchandise called for to the shipper directed to the point of destination, pays the freight thereon to such point and sends the bill of lading with the insurance policy to the buyer. If goods are lost in transit, the buyer must look to the insurance for indemnity. 

Seaver v. Lindsay Light Co., 233 N. Y. 273.

In a recent case the contract declared itself to be a "c. i. f." contract. However, the insurance was "for the account of the sellers". 

Payment was declared to be "net cash against shipping documents upon arrival" manifesting an agreement that if the steamer did not arrive no payments were to be made and indicating that risk was on the seller. It was held that although the letters c. i. f. in the contract created an inference that the instrument was in fact a c. i. f. contract, the express terms of the contract were, however, utterly inconsistent with such inference. The express terms must prevail, and the letters c. i. f. must be limited and applied to the price and not to the passing of title. 

Cundill v. Millhauser, 257 N. Y. 416.

The measure of damages for the breach of such contract is the difference between the contract price and the market price at the time and place of delivery to the carrier, and not that difference at the point of destination.

Should the seller, instead of shipping the goods direct to the buyer as required by the contract, send them to a third party together with some goods for this third party, title does not pass to the buyer on delivery to the carrier. To pass title the goods must be consigned to the buyer. (8) Custom and Usage.-If there is any particular usage of trade in vogue, that, unless the parties clearly intend the contrary, governs the transaction, and the title rests as the usage dictates. 

Of course, the contract may declare where the risk is to be. The above rules apply when the contract does not so state. (9) Delivery to Carrier.-If the contract requires delivery to a particular place in order to pass title, title cannot pass until the goods are delivered there. But if there is no such stipulation, the place where the goods happen to be at the time of the sale is the place of delivery. If a third party happens to have possession of the goods, that party must acknowledge that he holds them for the buyer. 

Taylor v. Kursrok, 214 A. D. 308, 212 N. Y. S. 133.

If no delivery to a particular place is required for passage of title, and the seller sends the goods to the buyer, title passes to the buyer on the delivery to the carrier of the goods in a deliverable state. 

Risk of loss from that moment is on the buyer. The risk would remain on the seller only if the carrier happened to be the particular agent of the seller.

A sells goods to B and ships them by a common carrier to B. 

B is to be given the goods upon the payment of the purchase money. 

Through the negligence of the carrier, B obtains possessions of the goods without paying the money and he sells them to C, a bona fide purchaser, for value without notice. Neither A nor the carrier can

recover the goods from C. Upon the delivery of the goods to the carrier, B had obtained title, subject to A's rights. These rights A lost when a bona fide purchaser for value without notice obtained the goods. 

Penn. R. R. v. Bank of U. S., 214 A. D. 410, 212 N. Y. S. 437.

Risk of Loss 

The risk of accidental damage or loss is upon the party having title when the damage or loss occurs.

In these cases, the risk of loss is not where the title is: (1) When the seller takes the bill of lading to his own order. In such a case, if but for the fact that the seller so made out the bill of lading, title would have been in the buyer, the risk of loss is on the buyer. Title is in the seller for purposes of security only. The seller may still sue for the price. 

Personal Property Law, § 101. 

(2) Where delivery has been delayed through the fault of one of the parties and loss resulted therefrom, the risk of loss is on the party causing the delay, regardless of where the title might be. 

Personal Property Law, § 103. 

(3) If the goods are sent to the buyer under circumstances where it is usual to insure the goods, and the goods are not insured, nor the buyer notified of it in time to obtain his own insurance, risk of loss during transit is on the seller, even though the buyer has title. 

Miller v. N. Y. C. R. R., 205 A. D. 663; 200 N. Y. S. 287.

Negotiable Documents-Bills of Lading¹ 

The bill of lading serves three distinct functions: First, a receipt for the goods; second, a contract for their carriage; and, third, documentary evidence of title to the goods. As a receipt, it recites the place and date of shipment, describes the goods as to quantity, weight, dimensions, identification marks, and condition, quality and value. As a contract, it names the contracting parties which includes the consignee, fixes the route, destination, and freight rate or charges, and stipulates the rights of and obligations assumed by the parties. 

American Cotton Co. v. N. Y. C. R. R., 142 Misc. 821; 255 N. Y. S. 672; 

Blossom v. Dodd, 43 N. Y. 264.

A seller may, upon shipment of the goods by the carrier, have the latter issue a straight bill of lading to the buyer as consignee. 

This creates a presumption that the delivery to the carrier was an unconditional appropriation of the goods to the contract. If the seller names himself as consignee, the carrier is considered a bailee for the seller.

Personal Property Law, § 226, Rule 101, Subd. 2. 

1See discussion of "Bills of Exchange,” supra, pp. 30 et seq.

Suppose a document of title states that the goods are deliverable to the order of X. The document thus is apparently negotiable. 

Even if it is stamped "non-negotiable," the stamp does not change the negotiability; it remains negotiable. But if a common carrier had so stamped such a document of title, it would be protected if it delivered the goods without taking up the document and the document came into the hands of a holder in due course. 

If the document was not so stamped and the instrument was passed on to a holder in due course, the carrier would have to make good any loss such holder suffered because of its failure to take up the bill on the delivery of the goods. 

N. Y. C. R. Co. v. Fed. Sugar Ref. Co., 235 N. Y. 182.

Sometimes negotiable bills of lading are issued in sets by the carrier. Suppose that a bona fide purchaser buys one of these sets. 

The goods having already been delivered to the owner, the carrier is unable to deliver to the bona fide purchaser. The carrier is liable for any damages suffered by the bona fide purchaser unless the duplicate bill was plainly marked as a duplicate. 

If one other than the person holding a bill of lading claims ownership of the goods, the carrier has a reasonable time to ascertain the validity of the claim. 

Of course, if the bill of lading is non-negotiable, its endorsement gives the transferee only those rights which the transferor had. If the transferee wants full protection against any creditors of the transferor, he must notify the carrier that he is the transferee, for until such notice the creditors can attach the goods. 

If a draft is sent with a bill of lading and it is payable within three days after sight, the buyer must pay the draft before he can get the bill of lading. If it is payable more than three days after sight, acceptance is enough. 

Express Waranties

Warranties are express or implied. An express warranty is (1) an affirmation of fact or promise relating to the goods, (2) the natural tendency of which induced the purchase, and (3) the buyer must purchase in reliance upon the affirmation or promise.

Personal Property Law, § 93. 

If the seller says that the article is "very fair," "excellent," or "well-made," or "unbeatable," it is not a warranty, it is just selling talk.

If the seller expressly or impliedly, even though in good faith, warrants the goods (i.e., makes any kind of affirmation the tendency of which is to induce the buyer to buy) and title has not yet passed,

SALES 

267 this warranty may be considered a condition, and a breach of it permits a refusal on the part of the buyer to go on with the contract. 

Personal Property Law, § 92. 

But suppose the goods are sold and the satisfaction of the buyer is one of the conditions necessary. The rule here is that the buyer cannot unreasonably reject the goods where the personal taste of the buyer is not involved. Such cases include articles worked on by mechanics, builders and all unskilled labor. But if the personal taste of the buyer is involved (as in a case to paint a picture), the buyer must really be satisfied. If he is not genuinely satisfied, he may reject the article ordered. This is a rule of contracts. 

Duplex Safety Boiler Co. v. Garden, 101 N. Y. 387; 

Crawford v. Mail & Exp. Pub. Co., 163 N. Y. 404.

Where a seller's letter accompanying an invoice stated that the seller expected the goods to arrive in April, such a statement was a mere statement of expectancy and not a warranty. 

Eitingon -Schild Co. v. Friedman, 213 A. D. 663, 211 N. Y. S. 208.

Implied Warranties 

In addition to express warranties, the law will imply certain warranties unless the contrary is declared in the contract of purchase. 

An express refusal to make any warranties with respect to an article sold, which refusal is contained in a written instrument of sale, precludes the buyer from claiming under the warranties which the Personal Property Law implies, or from claiming an alleged breach of parol warranty, or a sale by sample. 

Sayeg v. Gloria Lighting Co., 236 A. D. 761; 259 N. Y. S. 492;

Personal Property Law, § 152; 

Lumbrazo v. Woodruff, 256 N. Y. 92.

It is the duty of the seller, however, to deliver the article contracted for in merchantable condition. 

Stroock v. Lichtenthal, 224 A. D. 19, 229 N. Y. S. 371.

Except in the case of a sheriff's sale, the seller impliedly warrants (1) That he has a right to sell the goods or that he will have such good title at the time when the property is to pass. 

Spotless Co. v. Comm. Trust Co., 215 A. D. 412, 214 N. Y. S. 10. (2) That the goods are free from incumbrances. (3) That the goods correspond with the description given or with the sample. (4) That the goods are fit for the purpose for which they are bought.

This warranty exists only when the buyer makes known to the seller, expressly or impliedly, the purpose for which the goods are

bought and the buyer has relied on the seller's judgment in buying. 

If the buyer has examined the goods and should have discovered the defect, there is no warranty. 

Hoisting Engines Sales Co. v. Hart, 237 N. Y. 30;

Personal Property Law, §§ 94, 95, 96. 

The purchase of food ordinarily used for human consumption in and of itself impliedly informs the seller of the purpose for which the purchase is made. The purchase without any other circumstance is the evidence of the buyer's reliance on the seller's judgment. 

Temple v. Keeler, 238 N. Y. 334.

The mere purchase by a customer from a retail dealer of an article, the use of which is ordinarily known, does by implication make known to the vendor the purpose for which the article is required. There exists a warranty of fitness. This warranty does not exist between the wholesaler and the retail purchaser. There is not any privity of contract between the wholesaler (a manufacturer) and the purchaser from the retailer.¹ 

Rinaldi v. Mohican Co., 225 N. Y. 70; 

Chysky v. Drake Bros. Co., Inc., 235 Ń. Y. 468.

In the case of a contract to sell or the sale of a specific article under a patent or trade name, e.g., a "No. 52 Therm-Oil Heating 

Equipment, manufactured by Kellogg Mfg. Co.," or "Velvet," a pipe tobacco, there is no implied warranty as to its fitness for any particular purpose although such goods must be of merchantable quality. 

Personal Property Law, § 96; 

Barcham v. Kane, 240 N. Y. S. 123; 

Foley v. Liggett, 136 Misc. 468; 241 N. Y. S. 233.

The fact, however, that an article sold happens to have a trade name does not exclude all warranties. Where a buyer in purchasing trade-mark oil relied upon the seller's representation that the oil was what the buyer's particular machinery required, and not upon the trade name, it was held that there was a warranty. 

Sachter v. Gulf Refining Co., 203 N. Y. S. 769. (5) Where the goods are bought by description from a seller who deals in goods of that description (whether he be the grower or manufacturer or not), there is an implied warranty that the goods shall be of merchantable quality.

Personal Property Law, § 96, Subd. 2. 

In a recent case the purchaser stated to the defendant that she wished to have a loaf of "Ward's Bread." The salesman gave her what she asked for, wrapped in a sealed package as it came from. the Ward Baking Company, the baker. The court held that recovery for damages resulting from the presence of a pin in that bread could 

1See infra for a discussion of Torts arising from Sales.

SALES 

269 not be based on the imputation of reliance upon the seller, under 

Section 96, subdivision 1, of the Personal Property Law, but since the buyer was entitled to goods of merchantable quality under subdivision 2 of that section, recovery was allowed on the latter section. 

Loaves baked with pins are not of merchantable quality. 

Ryan v. Progressive Stores, 255 N. Y. 388. (6) That the goods have no latent defect. But if the buyer examined or ought to have seen certain patent defects, there is no such warranty. (7) Where there is a contract to sell or a sale by sample, there implied warranty that the bulk shall correspond with the sample, and if the seller is a dealer in that kind of goods, that the goods shall not have any patent defects rendering them unmerchantable. 

Rights and Remedies of the Unpaid Seller 

(1) The Unpaid Seller's Lien.

-A seller may exercise a right of lien (a) If he has possession and the price is past due, or (b) If he has possession and the buyer has become insolvent. 

The insolvency of the buyer does not work a rescission. He may tender full payment before the rescission is declared and obtain the goods. By "insolvent" is not meant "bankrupt” but rather an inability to meet payment properly required of him. 

Personal Property Law, §§ 134, 135, 136, 137. 

The lien is lost when the unpaid seller delivers the goods to a carrier for transmission to the buyer without taking a bill of lading to his own order or without retaining the bill of lading to the buyer's order, or when the buyer lawfully obtains possession or when the seller does any other act waiving the lien. 

Tuthill v. Skidmore, 124 N. Y. 148. (2) Stoppage in Transit. A seller may stop the goods in transit and retake possession if (a) the buyer has become insolvent and (b) he has not yet obtained possession.

Whether the goods have been delivered to the carrier and title passed do not matter, so long as the buyer or his agent have not possession. Notification to hold the goods given to the carrier under circumstances which give it reasonable opportunity to retain the goods, is sufficient. Failure to hold the goods after proper notice attaches liability for their value. The carrier, however, may refuse to deliver to the seller until expenses are paid and outstanding bills of lading returned.

Personal Property Law, §§ 139, 140.

It is not always easy to determine whether transit has ended. The transfer of an order bill of lading to an innocent purchaser for value has been held to destroy the right to stop in transit. 

Personal Property Law, § 143. 

If a portion of the goods has been delivered to the buyer, the right to stop the balance remains provided the part delivery was not made under such circumstances as to show an intention to give possession. (3) Right of the Seller to Resell.—A seller who has asserted a lien or who has retaken possession during transit may resell the goods without liability in three circumstances. (a) Where the goods are perishable. (b) When the contract by its terms permits a resale. (c) Where the buyer has been in default for an unreasonable time.

Notice of the resale need not be given to the buyer. The failure to give notice is only evidence upon the question as to whether or not the resale took place within a reasonable time after the buyer's default under subdivision (c). 

D'Aprile v. Turner Looker Co., 239 N. Y. 427.

The resale may be by either public or private sale. The seller need exercise only due diligence and good faith in order to obtain the best price. (4) Seller's Right to Rescind.-The unpaid seller may rescind the contract of sale on the same grounds that any party may rescind a contract, e.g., duress, fraud in the inception, etc. In addition, the seller may rescind for failure on the part of the buyer to pay. When the latter ground is the basis for the rescission, one of the following must exist before the rescission is effective as such: (a) The default must exist for an unreasonable time, the seller must have possession, and the seller must have manifested his intention to rescind. The act evidencing the intention need not be communicated to the buyer, but the giving or failure to give notice is relevant in any issue involving the question of the buyer's default for an unreasonable time.

Personal Property Law, § 142. 

(b) The buyer has repudiated the contract or manifested his inability to perform, or has committed a material breach. In each such instance it is essential that the possession be in the seller. Furthermore, notice of rescission must be given to the buyer. 

Personal Property Law, § 146. 

It should be noted that the right to sue for damages exists as well as the right to rescind where a breach occurs. The seller may elect either.

SALES 

271 When the seller sells as lienor under Section 141 (supra, subheading 3) he acts as agent for the buyer and he is required to deduct the sum realized from the damages he may have sustained. 

Nevertheless, if the resale brings more than the contract price, the defaulting buyer is not entitled to the excess. When the resale takes place after a rescission under Section 142, the sale is for the seller's account since title is now revested in him. 

Henderson Tire Co. v. Wilson, 235 N. Y. 489. (5) Action for the Price.-Subject to two exceptions, a seller cannot maintain an action for the price unless title is in the buyer. 

These exceptions are: (a) Where the contract requires payment on a day certain. In such case an action for the price may be maintained though title has not passed. Even in this case if the seller at any time before judgment has manifested an inability to perform, it is a defense to the buyer. (b) Where the goods cannot be readily resold for a reasonable price, an action for the price may be maintained although title has not passed, provided a tender was made, the buyer refused to accept and the buyer notified that the seller is holding the goods as bailee for the buyer.

Personal Property Law, § 144; 

Murray v. Ridgewood, 132 Misc. 414, affd. 233 N. Y. S. 684; 

Glass Co. v. Misroch, 239 N. Y. 475. (6) Damages for Non-Acceptance.-This phase has previously been mentioned, but it is worth while to summarize the rules. Where the buyer refuses delivery, he is liable for any loss sustained and for reasonable charges for the care of the goods. Where there is an available market, the measure of damages in the absence of a showing of special proximate damages, is the difference between the contract price and market price at the time and place of delivery, or if no time has been fixed, at the time of the refusal to accept. 

If the article is to be manufactured and while in the process the seller is told by the buyer that no acceptance will be made, it is the duty of the seller to cease work. Where the breach occurs before any work has been done, the recovery is the difference between the contract price and the cost of manufacture.¹

Personal Property Law, §§ 132, 145; 

Tood v. Gamble, 148 N. Y. 382.

Rights and Remedies of the Buyer 

(1) Breach of Warranty.-In order to succeed on the theory of an implied warranty other than those implied by law from the very 

1For a further discussion of "Damages", see chapter under that heading in the "Adjective Law of New York."

fact of the sale, e.g.

, title in the seller, the party must establish two things: 

(a) That he made known to the seller the particular purpose for which he wanted the article, and (b) That he relied on the seller's skill and judgment that the article would serve that purpose. 

Bowser v. McCormack, 243 N. Y. S. 442. 

Where there has been a breach of warranty, the buyer may adopt one of the following remedies: (a) He may accept or keep the goods and set up against the seller the breach by way of recoupment in diminution or extinction of the price. (b) He may accept or keep the goods and sue for damages for breach of warranty. (c) He may refuse to accept the goods if title has not passed, and sue for damages for breach of warranty. The buyer has a reasonable time after receipt to examine and determine whether he will reject. If title has passed and there is this subsequent rejection, the buyer can recover only the purchase price paid. 

Apex Chemical Co. v. Compton, 171 N. Y. S. 60. (d) If title has passed and the buyer later discovers the breach, he may refuse to accept the goods. If they have been received, they may be returned. No damages for the breach may be recovered.

He is entitled only to the moneys already paid toward the purchase price. There cannot be any rescission and suit for damages; the remedies are exclusive.

A cause of action to recover the purchase price is based upon a rescission of the contract, and an action for damages is based upon affirmance thereof, these two actions cannot be united. 

Seggerman v. Rosenberg, 217 A. D. 7, 216 N. Y. S. 61; 

Merry Real Co. v. Shamokin, 230 N. Y. 316;

Personal Property Law, § 150; 

Richards v. Amer. Union Bank, 253 N. Y. 166.

If part of the goods were sold before the discovery of the breach, there can be no rescission since it is impossible to put the parties in status quo. An action for damages for breach of warranty is the remedy. Nor can there be a rescission if the buyer knew of the breach of warranty when he accepted the goods. 

Peterson v. Guarino, 221 A. D. 146, 223 N. Y. S. 107.

Acceptance does not waive any rights one may have because of the other party's breach of warranty.

Personal Property Law, § 130. 

Should the seller refuse to take back the goods, the buyer can hold the goods as a bailee with a lien for the purchase money. The sale

SALES by the bailee must take place within a reasonable time as the seller cannot be held liable for charges for a longer period. 

Jones v. Healy, 261 N. Y. S. 464.

The buyer has lost his right to take advantage of the breach of warranty if he failed to give notice to the seller within a reasonable time after he learned of the breach and continued to use the article for a long time thereafter. 

Ellis v. Allen, 125 Misc. 841, 211 N. Y. S. 488.

Where the goods were received August 9, the defects were discovered August 12, but no complaint was made until September 5, and on September 17 the buyers offered to return the goods, it was held that, as a matter of law, reasonable notice was not given, and the plaintiffs were not allowed to recover for damages, nor were they allowed to rescind. 

Silberman v. Engel, 125 Misc. 816, 211 N. Y. S. 584; 

Brunella v. Bracchi, 131 Misc. 301, 226 N. Y. S. 738.

The acceptance of an instalment of the merchandise does not preclude the defendant from asserting that the undelivered balance is defective. The retention of the goods received, without comment as to their defective condition, does not impose any liability upon the defendant to take the balance of the merchandise, if it did not satisfy the requirements of the contract. 

Foster v. Fox, 124 Misc. 740, 209 N. Y. S. 230.

The measure of damages for breach of warranty of condition or quality, in the absence of special circumstances showing proximate damages of a greater amount, is the loss directly resulting in the ordinary course of events from that breach. Generally it is the difference between the actual value of the goods at the time of delivery to the buyer and the value it would have had if it had been according to specifications. 

Gleason v. Lebolt, 223 A. D. 46, 227 N. Y. S. 336.

Damages arising from the use of the article after the discovery of the breach cannot be recovered. 

Standard Oil Co. v. Boyle, 261 N. Y. S. 512.

X, a wholesaler, sells goods to A, a retailer, and it is within the contemplation of the parties that the goods are being bought for the purposes of resale by A. The goods were warranted to be of a certain quality. A resells the goods to Y and Z, giving the same warranties to them as X gave to him. It is then found that the goods were of an inferior quality. Y and Z bring suit against A for breach of warranty. The court held that A can recover from X for breach of the similar warranty as well as the cost of defending suits brought against him by the third parties arising out of the resale. The expenses for defenses are to be pleaded as special damages. 

Lamborn v. Csarnikow-Rionda Co., 221 A. D. 737, 225 N. Y. S. 

228.

(2) Delivery of Wrong Quantity.-Where too little is delivered, the buyer may reject the delivery. Rejection includes notification of the fact to the seller, but not redelivery to him. But if the buyer accepts what has been sent, knowing it to be less than the amount ordered, he pays the proportionate contract price.

If the buyer has disposed of the goods before he knows that the seller does not intend to perform his contract in full, the buyer need pay only the reasonable value of the goods disposed of. 

Where too much is delivered, the buyer can reject the delivery or take out the correct amount, or accept all, in which last case he pays at the contract rate.

If the goods delivered contain both the kind of material ordered and a kind not ordered, he may reject all or accept what he ordered. 

Personal Property Law, § 125. 

Where there has been a defective delivery and the position of the buyer has not been affected to his detriment, the seller may correct the delivery within the contract period and meet the contract's terms. (3) Refusal or Failure to Deliver.-Suppose title has passed, but the seller wrongfully will not ship the goods. The buyer can sue in conversion, and get the market value of the goods at the time of the conversion. If stocks are the subject of sale, the highest market value within a reasonable time after the conversion took place is what he gets.

Personal Property Law, § 147. 

Where title has not passed, there is no conversion and the buyer can sue for damages. The measure of damages is the difference between the contract price and the market value of the goods at the time they ought to have been delivered. If no time for delivery was fixed, then at the time of the refusal to deliver. If there is no market value at the place of delivery, the proper value of the goods at the time fixed for delivery is to be shown by the best evidence possible. 

A neighboring market value would be competent evidence, after making due allowance for the cost of transportation between points. 

Schopflacher v. Zimmerman, 240 N. Y. 507. 

(4) Right to Inspect. It is the general rule that title to future goods does not pass until the buyer has had a chance to inspect them unless the seller has been authorized to appropriate the goods to the contract before delivery in which case the authorization is equivalent to a waiver of the right to inspect. When there has been no such authorization title does not pass if the buyer refuses to inspect the goods and accept them. 

Personal Property Law, § 128, Subd. 2; 

Glass Co. v. Misroch, 239 N. Y. 475.

SALES 

275 Should the goods be rejected because of the seller's refusal to permit examination, the title does not pass, although the goods do comply with the contract. 

Stern v. Wohl, 212 A. D. 216; 207 N. Y. S. 669; 

Beals v. Hirsch, 211 N. Y. S. 293; affd. 242 N. Y. 529.

Where the contract requires delivery to the buyer's place, the opportunity to inspect is a condition precedent to the passing of title. 

Where title has passed before delivery to the buyer, the latter's right to inspect does exist. If upon inspection he discovers that the goods do not conform to the contract, it is his duty to give prompt notice of the rejection and he may thus revest title in the seller. 

Unless previously agreed, there is no right to examine in a C.O.D. transaction. Where the buyer must pay before inspection, he is entitled to recover the payment made, if on inspection the goods do not comply with the contract. The action is one for money had and received. 

Liebovitz v. Rosenberg, 213 A. D. 143; 210 N. Y. S. 145.

Entire and Divisible Contracts of Sale 

A contract is entire when the parties intend that the promise by one party is conditional upon entire performance of his part by the other. It is severable when the part to be performed by one consists of several distinct and separate items, and the price to be paid by the other is apportioned to each item, or left to be implied by law. 

Portfolio v. Rubin, 233 N. Y. 439.

Where there is a contract to sell goods to be delivered in stated instalments, which are to be separately paid for, and the seller makes defective deliveries in respect of one or more instalments, or the buyer neglects or refuses to take delivery of or pay for one or more instalments, it depends in each case on the terms of the contract and the circumstances of the case whether the breach of contract is so material as to justify the injured party in refusing to proceed further and sue for damages for breach of the entire contract, or whether the breach is severable, giving rise to a claim for compensation, but not to a right to treat the whole contract as broken.

Personal Property Law, § 126.

SURETYSHIP 

Nature of the Guaranty

In the discussion to follow, "S" represents the surety or guarantor, “D” the debtor, and "C" the creditor.

A surety is one who undertakes to answer for the debt or default of another and who in addition also assumes the obligation of the principal debtor as an original promise. His liability arises as soon as D's debt is due.

A guarantor of payment is one who undertakes unconditionally that the debtor will pay. His liability arises as soon as D defaults. 

No notice of default need be given to the guarantor of payment. 

In re Simmons' Will, 213 A. D. 32; 209 N. Y. S. 724.

A guarantor of collection undertakes to pay if D does not and cannot be made to pay. His liability arises after C has obtained a judgment against D and execution has been returned unsatisfied. He must get notice of D's default within a reasonable time after the default. In addition, an unreasonable delay by C in enforcing his legal remedies will release the guarantor of collection. 

An agreement to act as surety for the benefit of a particular C is a "special guaranty" limited to this C. C cannot assign his rights so that S would be bound to the assignee. If, however, a cause of action has once arisen against S in favor of C, C may assign this cause of action.

Very often the nature of the guaranty contemplates a continual relationship between C and D for which S expresses his intention to stand good. A letter sent by S to C stating, "I will be responsible for what stock M. E. McKee (D) has had or may want hereafter to the amount of $500," was held to contemplate a continuous business and a standing credit up to $500. 

Gates v. McKee, 13 N. Y. 232.

With every sale by C to D, there arises a distinct and separate unilateral contract. The offer by S may be withdrawn before any specific sale has been made.

Guaranties have been divided into two classes. The first is where the entire consideration passes wholly at one time; the other where it passes at different times and is separable or divisible. The former are not revocable by the guarantor and are not affected by his death. 

The latter, on the contrary, may be revoked as to subsequent transactions by the guarantor upon notice to that effect and are determined by his death. It is wholly irrelevant whether or not the other parties acquire notice of the death. 

Amer. Chain Co. v. Arrow Grip Mfg. Co., 134 Misc. 321. 276

SURETYSHIP 

277 

The Formation of the Relationship 

The same qualifications necessary to allow one to be a party to an ordinary contract exist in the contract of suretyship. The privity, of course, exists between C and S, and not between D and S. A promise to guarantee the account of another, like every promise, requires the support of a consideration paid or promised, in order that an enforceable contract may have been formed. 

Witshafd v. Brody, 257 N. Y. 97.

Where a contract of guaranty is entered into concurrently with the principal obligation, a consideration which supports the principal contract supports the subsidiary one. 

Jonas v. Michlin, 220 A. D. 649; 222 N. Y. S. 442.

Where a guaranty of payment is made subsequent to the creation of the debt between D and C, the consideration of the original extension of credit to D will not support the guaranty, as it is a past consideration.

Thus, if D already owes C $1,000, and then S writes "I will personally guarantee the payment of the amount of $1,000 owing by D to C," S cannot be held to the written promise, in the absence of some consideration. 

Sun Oil Co. v. Heller, 248 N. Y. 28.

No notice of acceptance by C is needed to complete the contract. 

S's offer to be a surety is turned into a contract when C acts on it by advancing money to D or in any other way agreed upon. Of course, if S stipulates that he is to receive notice of acceptance before his liability is to attach, that notice must first be given. 

The Statute of Frauds 

Every undertaking of a surety or guarantor must be evidenced by a memorandum in writing. This defense of the Statute of Frauds, to avail the defendant, must be pleaded. It is an affirmative defense. 

The memorandum must contain all the substantial and material terms of the contract including the consideration. 

Standard Oil Co. v. Koch, 260 N. Y. 150.

The promise of the surety, in order to come within the statute, must be made to C. A promise by S to D that he will pay D's debt is not within the statute, because it is a contract of indemnity. Contracts of indemnity need not be in writing. 

Block v. Greenfield, 243 N. Y. S. 117. 

Note than in order for a contract of suretyship to exist, there must be an obligation which runs from D to C, and for which obligation S agrees to stand good. One who undertakes to pay for something which is given to D, is not a surety, because no credit was ever extended to D and D undertook no obligation. There was, therefore,

no debt owing by D for which one could be a surety. This promise to pay does not come within the Statute of Frauds. The same result would follow if S and D both promised jointly to pay for what C gave D. The obligations of S and D would both be original obligations. 

Maddock v. Root, 150 N. Y. 561; 

Kennedy v. Dzengielowski, 131 Misc. 635; 228 N. Y. S. 435.

Defenses of the Surety 

(1) Payment. If D's debt is paid or a tender is made, or D defeats C in C's suit for the debt alleged to be due, that absolutely frees S. It is a defense of payment. (2) Fraud and Duress.-Where C, by actual or passive fraud, induces D to enter into a valid contract, and S, in ignorance of this fraud, stands good for D, S is liable so long as D permits his contract with C to stand, even though he originally was entirely ignorant of the fraud, and D deliberately refused to use his defense.

If C gets S to be a surety and C knows something about D, e.g.: acts of embezzlement or of moral delinquency, which if known to S would deter S from becoming a surety, C's concealment of these facts discharges S of liability. 

Phillips v. U. S. F. & G. Co., 193 N. Y. S. 467 ; affd. 234 N. Y. 618.

If the obligation of D was obtained under duress, S can set up that defense against C, even though D does not. 

In this respect it differs from the rule in cases of fraud. 

Osborn v. Robbins, 36 N. Y. 365. 

(3) Illegality of Contract.-If the contract between C and D is illegal or contrary to public policy, the suretyship obligation is void. (4) Alteration of Original Contract.-Suppose S becomes a surety on a bond of a bank's bookkeeper, D. Later D is made a teller. S no longer is liable, because a different relationship between 

D and C (the bank) is now in force. A similar result would follow where S became surety for D on a contract involving 1,000 feet of lumber and the contract between D and C was changed to read 1,200 feet. Note that the fact that the change did not increase the risk is not important. If the change is of a material part of the contract, and not merely of an incidental part, it is then such a change as would discharge S. A good example is this case: A bank (S) guaranteed payment by D for "dried grapes." The bill of lading read "raisins." S was held justified in refusing to pay. Although both articles may be subject to the same influences, the subject-matter is apparently different, and this appears to be material enough to discharge S. The contract of a surety is strictissimi juris and is not to be extended beyond its express terms. This rule of strictissimi

SURETYSHIP 

279 juris applies in the application of the contract rather than to its interpretation. Where the question is as to the meaning of the language of the contract, there is no difference between the contract of a surety and that of anybody else. 

Herskovits Fur Co. v. Hollander, 138 Misc. 456; 246 N. Y. S. 588; 

Bank of Italy v. Merchants' Ntal. Bank, 236 N. Y. 106.

Where the surety has so acted as to consent, even orally, to a change in the original obligation, he will not be relieved. 

M. H. Metal Product Corp. v. April, 251 N. Y. 146; 

Merrill v. Equit. Surety Co., 131 Misc. 541; 227 N. Y. S. 266. (5) Extension of Time.--A binding agreement by C without the consent of S, to give D additional time releases S. Whether or not 

S is damaged is immaterial. But if C so agreed to extend time and reserved his rights against S, S is not discharged. If S is made to pay, he can then sue D and get back his money, for D would not have the defense against S that he would have against C. 

Natl. Park Bank v. Koehler, 204 N. Y. 174.

Payment by D of part of the payment then due to C for a promise to extend time, does not release S because there was no valid consideration for the extension agreement. 

Dussault v. Wellman, 130 Misc. 614; 224 N. Y. S. 321. (6) Refusal to Proceed to Collect Debt.-Failure by C to sue

D then solvent, does not discharge S even though D becomes insolvent unless during the solvency S expressly gave a clear and explicit request to C to sue D on the latter's default and C refused. 

Pain v. Packard, 13 Johns. 174. (7) Release of Security.-Suppose that C, holding D's property as security, releases some of it without getting other property substituted. S is discharged pro tanto. If Ċ releases half the security, S is discharged to that extent. The same result would follow if there is a release by C of one of a group of sureties; in such case remaining sureties are discharged pro tanto. 

(8) Personal Defenses.-Should D have a defense against C 

which is personal to D, S cannot avail himself of it. Examples of such defenses are: D's incapacity to contract; D's discharge in bankruptcy barring suit by C against him; completing of a composition with creditors. 

Klipstein v. Lipschits, 130 Misc. 291; 223 N. Y. S. 822. 

Failure of consideration from C to D is a defense to S.

Forsythe v. U. S. Fidelity, 224 N. Y. S. 330. 

If after C has executed his part of the contract with D, S dies, his estate is responsible as surety. (9) Statute of Limitations.—Suppose there are two sureties; the debt owing is barred by the Statute of Limitations; one of the sureties

pays the debt nevertheless. Is the other surety liable for half? Only the one who paid must stand the loss. One surety cannot revive the debt so as to affect the other. If the statute had run only in favor of one, and the other still bound had paid, he could get contribution in equity from the one in whose favor the statute had run, because the right to contribution does not arise until the payment is made. 

Hard v. Mingle, 206 N. Y. 179, 184. 

Rights of the Surety (1) Contribution.-Suppose S1, S2 and S3 are all co-sureties on 

D's debt for $3,000. On D's default the creditor can sue any one of the three sureties for the whole $3,000. The one who pays can then collect $1,000 from each of the other two, for each was responsible for one-third, they being three equal co-sureties. 

It is entirely immaterial whether they knew of each other when they became sureties; when they find out, they can get contribution. 

Suppose, however, that S1 was a surety for $1,000, S2 for $2,000 and S3 for $3,000. S1's share should therefore be one-sixth. All that S1 pays above one-sixth he can get from S2 and S3, they paying two-sixths and three-sixths respectively. But his right to contribution arises after he has paid more than his pro rata share. 

Therefore the Statute of Limitations as to his right of contribution begins to run when payment of more than his share is made. 

Suppose S2 became bankrupt after S1 paid the whole debt, each owing one-third. Does S3 have to pay one-half to S1 or only his original one-third share? In equity, S3 would have to pay one-half. 

Insolvent co-sureties are not counted in contribution. In law, S3 would have to pay only one-third. (2) Subrogation.-If S pays to C all of the debtor's debt, S is entitled to all the rights C had against D, and he can proceed against D, whose debt to C he has paid. This is the right of subrogation. 

If C held any securities, S can get them, or their value if C lost them. 

Citizens' Trust Co. v. Prescott, 131 Misc. 884; 227 N. Y. S. 514.

Actual payment, and not liability to pay, confers the right of subrogation. 

American Surety Co. v. Palmer, 240 N. Y. 63.

The doctrine of subrogation is of equitable and not contractual origin. Though it is invoked to obtain substantial justice in compelling the person primarily liable to pay his debt, it cannot be invoked against one whose equities are equal to or greater than those of the party seeking to be subrogated. Thus, a surety of a surety, though compelled to pay the creditor, is not entitled to be substituted in place of the creditor in order to enforce payment against the principal debtor, if the latter has paid his immediate surety. 

Bell v. Greenwood, 229 A. D. 550; 242 N. Y. S. 149.

SURETYSHIP 

281 (3) Indemnity.—In addition to his rights of subrogation, S has a right to indemnity after he pays D's debt in whole or in part. This is based on a promise from D, express or implied. The fact that 

C could not sue D because of any defense D had against C, or because of a release, does not mean that S has lost his indemnity rights. 

Since a surety's cause of action against the principal for reimbursement does not arise until the surety pays the principal's debt, a surety in a suit against him by the creditor is not entitled to join the principal debtor as a party defendant, for if the joinder were permitted, the surety might be permitted to recover indemnity from the debtor without paying the judgment against himself. 

Scripture v. Buckley, 241 N. Y. S. 635.

Suppose D defaults on a $5,000 debt. S settles for $3,000, and then sues for the whole $5,000. His rights being indemnity rights, he can recover only what he paid out-$3,000. (4) Marshalling Assets.-The rule with respect to marshalling of assets has been defined as one "which courts of equity sometimes invoke to compel a creditor who has the right to make his debt out of either of two funds to resort to that one of them which will not interfere with or defeat the rights of another creditor who has recourse to only one of these funds." 

In re Van Zandt's Estate, 142 Misc. 663; 255 N. Y. S. 359.

Suppose D and S both give securities as collateral. D defaults and C sells S's securities. C is liable in conversion, for knowing that the securities were not the debtor's, C should have gone against D's securities first.

This equitable doctrine of marshalling assets is seen in the following case. T owns two lots. He mortgages lots 1 and 2 to E and places an additional mortgage on lot 1, F being the mortgagee. There is a default in payment and E tries to foreclose on lot 1, rather than on lot 2. F can ask equity to compel E to exhaust the remedy against lot 2 on which he alone held the mortgage. F has a right to protection. 

Farmers' Loan & Trust Co. v. Kip, 192 N. Y. 285; 

Ingalls v. Morgan, 10 N. Y. 178; 

Diamond v. Tau Holding Corp., 131 Misc. 446; 226 N. Y. S. 129.

TORTS 

Who Are Liable for Their Torts-Exceptions

All persons including corporations are liable for their torts. There are, however, certain definite limitations to this rule which we shall first consider. (a) Insane Persons.-An insane person is liable for his torts, except that whenever malice is a necessary constituent of the tort for which an insane person is sued, the insane person is not liable. 

Williams v. Hays, 143 N. Y. 442. (b) Infants¹.—When an infant is of such tender years as to lack the experience which would enable him to realize the presence of a threatening danger, the infant is said to be non sui juris. 

There is a rebuttable presumption that a child of twelve or over is presumed to be sui juris and that a child less than twelve is non sui juris. The court has said that a child must be held responsible for such care as he may be reasonably expected to attain at this age and capacity. With the approach of maturity a greater degree of care may be expected. A child about five years old, who was run over while crossing a street, was not held as a matter of law, to be non sui juris and so not guilty of contributory negligence as a matter of law. 

Jacobs v. Koehler, 208 N. Y. 416.

An infant has no cause of action in tort for any prenatal harm due to the negligence of the defendant. Though the mother can recover for injuries to herself, she cannot recover for injuries to the child on the theory that they were injuries to her. 

Drobner v. Peters, 232 N. Y. 220; 

Lukes v. Lowenfels, 210 A. D. 869; 206 N. Y. S. 930. 

If an infant deceives an adult into making a contract with him, the adult cannot maintain an action in deceit if the gist of the damages in the action is a breach of the contract made with the infant. 

To hold an infant for a tort of this nature, the tort must be a positive wrong of itself. The test is, Can the infant be held liable without directly enforcing his contract? If so, he is liable for the tort.

The parents of an infant are not liable for the torts of the infant merely because they are the parents of that infant. 

An unemancipated infant has no cause of action in tort against his parents. A parent has no cause of action in tort against an unemancipated minor child, but he has if the child is emancipated. 

Sorrentino v. Sorrentino, 248 N. Y. 626; 

Ciani v. Ciani, 127 Misc. 304; 215 N. Y. S. 767;

Le Galbo v. Lo Galbo, 246 N. Y. S. 565. 

1For a discussion of Infants as trespassers, see infra, p. 295. 282

TORTS 

283 (c) Sovereign Powers.-Sovereign powers, such as the State and 

Federal Government, cannot be sued in tort without their consent. 

The State of New York by § 12a of the Court of Claims Act has waived immunity from liability for the torts caused by the malfeasance or negligence of its officers or employees. Notice of intent to sue must be served upon the attorney-general and the superintendent of public works within 60 days after the alleged injury occurred and the claim filed thereon within two years after the injury. 

Court of Claims Act, § 29. 

The state is liable for damages suffered from defects in state and county highways from May 1st and November 15th in any year.

Highway Law, § 176. 

The Court of Claims cannot entertain a suit by the state against an individual, but it may entertain the state's counterclaim against the plaintiff and grant affirmative judgment based upon it. 

Gross v. State, 243 N. Y. 629.

Appeals from the Court of Claims are taken to the Appellate 

Division. 

(d) Charitable Institutions.-A charitable institution (i. e., an institution not organized for profit) is not liable to any beneficiary for the negligence of some of its agents. Hospitals, universities and colleges are charitable institutions, even though fees are paid to them. 

If a patient is injured because of the negligence of a nurse, the hospital is not liable for the tort. If a student at college is hurt through the negligence of the teacher, the college is not liable. 

Doctors and nurses do not act as servants of the hospital, therefore the doctrine of respondeat superior does not apply. The hospital undertakes not to heal but to supply others who will attempt to heal on their own responsibility.

There is a duty upon the charitable institution to select its agents with due care. As soon as that is done, the members of the faculty, the physicians and surgeons are masters and not servants. 

Phillips v. Buffalo Gen. Hosp., 239 N. Y. 188; 

Hamburger v. Cornell University, 240 N. Y. 328.

The rule respecting charitable institutions should not be confused with the rule applicable to persons who commit torts in the execution of gratuitous acts. The rule stated in the preceding paragraphs is limited to charitable institutions. One who assumes to act, even though gratuitously, becomes subject to the duty of acting carefully if he acts at all. In a case where a landlord gratuitously undertook to repair a defective stairway, the court said that the plaintiff need not even prove that what was wrong has been made worse. The

plaintiff's case is made out when it appears that, by reason of the negligence, what was wrong is still wrong.¹ 

Marks v. Nambil Realty Co., 245 N. Y. 256.

Joint Tort Feasors 

If the plaintiff releases one of several joint tort-feasors, they all are released automatically, unless (1) the plaintiff, in releasing the one, expressly reserved his right to sue the others (the release in such case is really an agreement with him not to sue him); (2) or unless it can be shown that for some reason the release is void. 

Gilbert v. Finch, 173 N. Y. 455; 

McNamara v. Eastman Kodak Co., 232 N. Y. 18.

A jury cannot apportion the liability for damages caused by joint tort-feasors. If a jury should bring back a verdict against defendant 

X for $4,500.00 and against the joint defendant Y for $500.00, the court, even in the absence of the jury, can be asked to have the verdict read as a verdict for $4,500.00 the larger, not the combined amount against X and Y.

When a defendant has paid more than his pro rata share of such judgment, he is entitled to contribution from the other defendants in respect to the excess paid over his pro rata share. No defendant, however, can be made to pay to any other defendant more than his own pro rata share. The recovery against the joint defendant may be by an action, or if the defendant so sued has appeared in the original action, it may be made by motion on notice to the attorney of the other defendant. The statute which permits this right to contribution does not permit the defendant in a personal injury action to bring in a joint tort-feasor as a party defendant, as his right against another tort-feasor under this statute does not arise until he has paid an excess over his pro rata share. 

Rowe v. Denler, 135 Misc. 286; 238 N. Y. S. 9; 

Polsey v. Waldorf-Astoria, 216 A. D. 86; 214 N. Y. S. 600;

Civil Practice Act, § 211a; 

Davis v. Hank & Schmidt, 250 N. Y. S. 537; 

Ohlquist v. Nordstrong, 143 Misc. 502; 257 N. Y. S. 711.

An interesting point on this question was decided in a recent case. 

P was injured as the result of a collision between D1 and D2. P sued D1 and D2 in one action, and D1 sued D2 for property damage. 

D1 recovered a judgment thus determining that as between them D1 was not at fault. Thereafter P recovered judgment against both D1 and D2 which the latter paid. Thereupon D2 sued D1 for contribution. It was held that the judgment of D1 in his suit against D2 was not res adjudicata in this claim for contribution. The liability in 

1For a discussion of Privilege in Libel and Slander, see infra, p. 304.

TORTS 

285 contribution was based upon their negligence to P and not as to each other.

Neenan v. Woodside Astoria Transp. Co., 235 A. D. 9; 256 

N. Y. S. 38.

Where wrongdoers have not acted in concert, and the act or neglect of each results in damage, the liability of the wrongdoers is several. So where several parties polluted a running stream, each one separately from his own premises and independently of each other, each owner is liable separately for the injury done by his own acts. 

Chipman v. Palmer, 77 N. Y. 51.

Proximate Cause 

Before any recovery for a tort can be had, there must be (1) a violation by the defendent of a right belonging to the plaintiff, and (2) this violation must be a proximate cause of the injuries sustained. 

The fact that an act of the defendant caused injury to the plaintiff does not by itself mean that a legal right of the plaintiff has been violated. If no hazard to the plaintiff was apparent to the eye of ordinary vigilance, an act seemingly innocent and harmless, with reference to the plaintiff, does not become a tort though it happened to be a wrong with reference to one other than the plaintiff. Thus if A jostles B, in a large crowd, and as a result of this jostling a bomb carried by C, standing in the crowd, is dropped injuring X fifty feet away, X has no claim against A. The "eye of vigilance" could not perceive the damage. Where, however, A drives at a reckless speed through a crowded street, the eye of vigilance perceives the risk of damage, and though the force set in motion should pursue an unexpected path, A would be liable. Likewise, if 

A shoots in a place where people are likely to be present, he is liable for injuries sustained by one unexpectedly being hit. 

Palsgraf v. Long Island R. R. Co., 248 N. Y. 339.

Legal or proximate cause is always dependent upon the facts of a particular case, and it is for this reason that the words are beyond definition or conclusive explanation. An injury for which recovery may be had must directly result from an act that, according to common experience and the usual course of events, might under the particular circumstances have reasonably been expected. The act of the defendant may be one of commission, or it may be the omission of an obligation or duty which the defendant has left undischarged. 

Scott v. Del. Lack. W. R. Co., 222 A. D. 409; 226 N. Y. S. 287; 

O'Neill v. City of Port Jervis, 253 N. Y. 423.

The determination of proximate cause is not to be made after the occurrence. The real point is whether men of average prudence and ordinary care would have anticipated the accident under the circumstances. The determination is left to the jury. The law does not

impose a duty to guard against that which does not naturally suggest itself to a careful and prudent man as something which should be guarded against and which is very unlikely to occur. 

In one case, the plaintiff guest, sitting in the lobby of the defendant's hotel, was hurt when a 200-pound lamp fell upon him when another guest tilted his chair against the lamp. The court held that the defendant was not negligent for its failure to have the lamp securely fastened to the floor and to anticipate the misuse by a guest of an article of common use. It was not shown that the lamp would have fallen with very slight contact. On the other hand, where the defendant, while in a balloon, descended in a garden under circumstances which tended to invite people to go to his assistance or to stand around, and thus trampled on plaintiff's garden, the defendant was held liable for the damage caused. 

Guille v. Swan, 19 Johns. 381; 

McKinney v. N. Y. Consol. R. Co., 230 N. Y. 194; 

Polsey v. Waldorf-Astoria, 220 A. D. 613; 222 N. Y. S. 273.

Where a defendant left some film on a vacant lot, which were picked up by a small child and lighted, thus causing the child injuries, it was held that the defendant was not liable, as common experience would not indicate that such an accident would reasonably be expected from the defendant's act. 

Beickert v. G. M. Laboratories, 242 N. Y. 168.

In one case, the defendant's servants who had been using denatured alcohol marked "poison" in repairing a telephone wire, left a bottle of it on the highway. Two boys found the bottle, took it home and poured some of the fluid on the grass. One of them set fire to it and one boy was burnt. The defendant was not held liable, because the accident was not a result which might under the circumstances have reasonably been expected. 

Hall v. N. Y. Tel. Co., 214 N. Y. 49.

In another case, the defendant, in violation of a city ordinance, stored some boxes of nitro-glycerine caps upon public land. The containers were not marked. Two boys stole a box and then invited a third boy to come along. While carrying the box, it exploded and killed the third boy. It was held that the accident was not the proximate result of leaving the boxes on the highway, and that new and unexpected causes intervened before the explosion. 

Perry v. Rochester Lime Co., 219 N. Y. 60; 

Babcock v. Fitzpatrick, 248 N. Y. 608.

In a recent case, the plaintiff, who was eating pie in a restaurant, discovered that roaches were imbedded in the bottom crust of the pie. 

The plaintiff, seeing these, became very ill. The plaintiff recovered, as the jury found that the physical injuries were the natural and proximate result of the restaurateur's negligence. 

Carroll v. N. Y. Pie Baking Co., 215 A. D. 240; 213 N. Y. S. 553.

TORTS 

287 Under ordinary circumstances no one is chargeable with damages because he has not anticipated the commission of a crime by some third party. Even where certificates of stock, signed in blank by duly authorized officers, were stolen from the corporation by a servant who had access to them, and sold to innocent purchasers, no recovery has been permitted against the corporation for its failure to safeguard the public from the hazard of being defrauded in such manner. 

Benenson v. Natl. Surety Co., 260 N. Y. 299.

Words Negligently Spoken 

In some cases, words negligently spoken may justify recovery for proximate damage caused by faith in their accuracy. A bailee negligently misstating to his bailor where his goods are stored, knowing that the inquiry is made for insurance purposes, is liable for resulting loss. A public weigher hired by the seller to weigh goods, realizing that the buyer would rely on his statement in payment therefor, was held liable for erroneous statements concerning the weight. 

The liability in such cases arises only when there is a duty, if one speaks at all, to give correct information. There must be (1) knowledge, or its equivalent, that the information is desired for a specific purpose; (2) that the information will be acted upon; (3) that the injury will follow if the information is false; (4) the relationship between the parties must be such that, in morals and good conscience, the one has the right to rely upon the other for the information; and (5) the informant has a duty to give it due care. 

Intern. Prod. Co. v. Erie R. Co., 244 N. Y. 331; 

Glanzer v. Shepard, 233 N. Y. 236; 

Doyle v. Chatham & Phenix Bank, 253 N. Y. 369.

It thus follows that if public accountants prepare a balance sheet for a client which is the usual course of business will be exhibited to banks and creditors as the basis of financial dealings, they are not liable in negligence if the balance sheet was negligently drawn up causing a creditor to lose money because of advances made to the client in reliance upon the false balance sheet. The service of the accountants was primarily for the benefit of the client as a convenient instrumentality for use in the development of the business and only collaterally for the use of those to whom the client might later exhibit it.

If the accountants certify as a fact true to their knowledge, that balance sheet was in accordance with books of account whereas it was false, they are not exonerated. 

Ultramares Corp. v. Touche, 255 N. Y. 170.

Malicious Interference with Contracts 

One who having knowledge of an existing valid contract between others, intentionally, knowingly, and without reasonable justification

or excuse, induces one of the parties to the contract to breach it to the damage of the other party, is liable in an action to recover the damages suffered. The action is predicated on the intentional interference without justification with contractual rights, with knowledge thereof. Such interference constitutes a legal wrong, and, if damages result therefrom, a valid cause of action exists therefor. 

Hornstein v. Podwitz, 254 N. Y. 443.

This rule has not been extended to contracts to marry. There exists no cause of action against a third party for inducing A to break a contract to marry B. The remedy of the latter is to sue A for breach of the promise and to sue the third party for such slanderous or libelous words as may have been uttered. 

Ryther v. Lefferts, 232 A. D. 552; 250 N. Y. S. 699.

Limitations to Rule of Proximate Cause 

(1) Injuries from Fright.—A plaintiff cannot recover for fright and the injuries and mental anguish directly resulting therefrom caused by the defendant's negligence, unless there has been physical contact with the person of the plaintiff. If the fright is preceded, accompanied or followed by actual physical molestation, even in the slightest degree, the plaintiff can recover for the battery as well as for the mental anguish. There can be recovery for mental anguish and subsequent physical suffering without there having been a battery, when the damage and injuries are deliberate and not through mere negligence. 

Mitchell v. Rochester Ry. Co., 151 N. Y. 107; 

O'Brien v. Moss, 220 A. D. 464; 221 N. Y. S. 621; 

Beck v. Libraro, 220 A. D. 547; 221 N. Y. S. 737.

A defendant who has maliciously and wilfully libeled the plaintiff will be held liable for mental suffering caused thereby, as an element of special damage.

Garrison v. Sun Print. & Pub. Assn., 207 N. Y. 1. 

The tendency of the courts is to get away from the ruling that there can be no recovery for fright and the resulting injuries and mental anguish, unless there is physical contact. The courts have allowed the "physical contact" to be asserted through any of the five senses, as touch, feeling, taste, smell, or sight. Thus, where a plaintiff receiving his laundry package from the defendant saw a dead mouse therein and became ill as a result thereof, a motion by the defendant to dismiss the complaint on these facts was denied. 

Gregory v. Wallabout Laundry, N. Y. Law Jrl., May 5, 1927; 

Carroll v. N. Y. Pie Baking Co., 215 A. D. 240; 213 N. Y. S. 553; 

Sider v. Reid Ice Cream Co., 125 Misc. 835; 211 N. Y. S. 582.

The Appellate Division, in a recent case, further limited the rule and held for the plaintiff where he fractured his leg while trying to avoid the horses of the defendant which had run away. 

Maloney v. Knickerbocker Ice Co., 241 N. Y. S. 160.

TORTS 

289 (2) Fires.-The courts have arbitrarily stated that damage to property by fire further away than the adjoining properties, is not proximate damage. They have ruled where a fire is negligently started on defendant's lands, damage caused to property not adjoining the defendant's but which was caused by the fire spreading from the adjoining land, is not proximate damage. If a fire negligently started on a defendant's land "spreads" to a building on the abutting premises of a plaintiff and destroys it, there is liability; to constitute a legal cause, the defendant's fire must "immediately precede" the plaintiff's fire; if other lands intervene between the plaintiff and the defendant covered with inflammable material over which the defendant had no control, and without which the fire could not have extended upon the plaintiff's premises, there would be no liability. 

Hoffman v. King, 160 N. Y. 618.

If the fire which was negligently started on the defendant's land proceeds by sparks or flames across intervening land, nothing upon which is ignited, directly upon plaintiff's buildings or premises not contiguous to defendant's premises, and ignites a building, the defendant would be liable as the damage would be considered proximate without a fire on intervening land. 

Homac Corp. v. Sun Oil Co., 258 N. Y. 462. (3) Railroads.-At common law no man was obligated to fence his property against trespassing cattle. The owner of cattle was obliged to restrain his cattle in his own land. If the cattle strayed upon the plaintiff's land, he became liable for the damage caused by the trespass. By statute it is now the law in New York that the owner of adjoining lands must maintain a just and equitable portion of the division fence. If the animals of A are injured because of the fact that they went through B's fence not properly maintained, A may maintain an action against B, provided A can show active negligence or wilful or wanton injury by B.

In the same way, a railroad at common law was not obliged to erect fences along its right of way. Now every railroad corporation, by statute, is required to erect and maintain fences on the sides of its roads sufficient to prevent cattle, horses, sheep and hogs, from going upon its road from adjacent lands. If such fences are not kept in good repair, the railroad corporation is liable for all damages done by its agents, engines or cars to the animals that stray upon the property. This liability is absolute, the contributory negligence on the part of the plaintiff is immaterial if the fences are not maintained as the statute requires. 

Tator v. Rutland R. Co., 130 Misc. 904; 225 N. Y. S. 731;

Railroad Law, § 52.

The liability is limited only for damage to the animals who may be hurt by its agents, engines or cars. If a horse falls from an embankment upon the property of the railroad because the fence was defective, and is killed by the fall, the railroad company is not liable. 

Smack v. N. Y. Cent. R. Co., 223 A. D. 192; 227 N. Y. S. 739. 

Torts Arising from Sales

The law is settled on the point that if A manufactures and sells an article to B for retail sale, the purchasers from B have no cause of action against A for defects of the article, based on breach of warranty, because privity of contract does not exist between A and the purchasers. Such rights are limited to claims against B. Have these customers claims in tort against A for negligence? There has been a development of the doctrine that a manufacturer is held to the duty of proper inspection and a high degree of care in placing on the market an article having potential elements of danger to those using it or coming in contact with it, without full opportunity of discovering its hazards.

In one case a motor car company sent one of its cars to an independent distributor. The car was defective, and as a result the plaintiff, who had bought this car from the independent distributor, was hurt. The court held the defendant manufacturer liable in negligence for placing a dangerous instrumentality in a position where it was reasonably certain to endanger life and limb. 

MacPherson v. Buick Motor Co., 217 N. Y. 382. 

A company manufacturing pop bottles and selling them to bottlers was held liable for injuries resulting from an explosion, where a bottle was defective and inspection thereof inadequate. 

Smith v. Peerless Glass Co., 259 N. Y. 292.

The principle here enunciated has been applied where a manufacturer has placed upon the market prepared foodstuffs in sealed packages for human consumption or for external use, when the normal use would result with reasonable certainty in personal harm to the user of the article if not properly made. Thus, a manufacturer was held liable for negligently permitting a pin to be concealed in a package of sanitary napkins, causing injury to the purchaser from a retailer; for permitting a nail to remain in a piece of bread, injuring the mouth of the plaintiff; for permitting dead animal matter to be ground in pipe tobacco contained and sold in a sealed can. 

La Frumento v. Kotex Co., 131 Misc. 314; 226. N. Y. S. 750; 

Cohen v. Dugan Bros., 132 Misc. 896; mod. 236 N. Y. S. 769; 

Foley v. Liggett & Myers, 136 Misc. 468; 241 N. Y. S. 233.

In a recent case, the plaintiff sued a manufacturer of a mattress which had contained sharp points of metal and which points of metal injured the plaintiff when she lay upon the mattress. The mattress had been bought from X, a retailer. The court held the defendant

TORTS 

291 not liable, saying that a mattress was not a thing inherently or imminently dangerous, any more than would be a suit of clothes which might contain a needle which might prick the plaintiff. 

Jaronier v. Hasselbarth, 223 A. D. 182; 228 N. Y. S. 302.

A manufacturer was held not liable to a purchaser of shoes for injuries occurring when one heel broke, causing purchaser to lose balance and fall down stairs. 

Sherwood v. Lax, 145 Misc. 578; 259 N. Y. S. 948. 

The retailer, though liable for breach of warranty, is not liable in tort if he was under no duty to inspect the contents of each package sold, or if such examination was impossible because the package was sealed. 

Foley v. Liggett & Myers, 136 Misc. 468; 241 N. Y. S. 233.

Assault and Battery-Self-Defense 

To constitute an assault there must be (1) an attempt to do harm, however slight; and (2) an act tending toward such harm, together with (3) the real or apparent present ability to do the harm; and (4) a resulting fear on the part of the plaintiff. 

Thus, pointing an unloaded firearm at another in a threatening manner constitutes an assault, where the party at whom it is pointed does not know that it is not loaded or has no reason to believe that it is not loaded, and he is put in fear of bodily harm. 

People v. Tremaine, 129 Misc. 650; 222 N. Y. S. 432. 

The "battery" is the touching of the person or clothing of the plaintiff, or of some article held by him, as a result of an "assault." 

A person who is assaulted may use what is reasonable force, under the circumstances, to resist the attack. 

One who is attacked may resist with such force as is reasonable under the circumstances. One attacked with a dangerous weapon, or under circumstances in which he was reasonable in believing that his life was in danger, and he could not retreat, the use of a gun by him would be permissible.

When a man is assailed in his home or his home is invaded, he need not retreat, as he should in other cases of attack. He may stand his ground and he may protect his home by force and violence, even to the point of killing, if reasonably believed to be necessary. 

It does not matter that his home was used at that time for the illegal sale of liquors; as a home, it may be protected. 

People v. Wilcox, 245 N. Y. 404.

This rule of self-defense is also applicable to criminal law. 

The use of force upon the person of another is not unlawful in the following cases:

(1) When committed to a reasonable degree by a person in arresting one who has committed a felony. (2) When committed in self-defense, as outlined above. (3) When committed by a parent, guardian or teacher in the exercise of lawful authority to restrain or correct a child, and the force used is reasonable. (4) When committed by a carrier in rightfully ejecting a person, provided the train is not in motion and the force is reasonable. 

Protection and Recapture of Property 

A person may use any amount of force necessary to protect his property from immediate injury, or to retain possession of his property about to be taken away without liability for assault and battery. The value of the property which X seeks to defend is of no importance whatever when seeking to determine the amount of force which X may use in the defense of that property. 

A simple example will make the entire situation very clear. Suppose F has ten chickens on his farm. His neighbor B owns a very valuable dog. F sees the dog carry off one of his chickens. The next day F sees the dog standing near the fence, looking at the remaining chickens, and F, believing that the dog is planning to start for another chicken, shoots the dog. F is liable. His property was not in need of that kind of protection when he shot. He would not have been liable if he had shot the dog as it was about to put the chicken's neck between his jaws. In that case, even if the chicken had been worth only 50c and the dog $100, he would not be liable. 

Suppose X, another of F's neighbors, claimed that one of F's chickens were his. After an argument, X seized the chicken and was about to start away when F caught hold of him. They struggled, and during the struggle X's eye was discolored. X has no case in assault and battery, for F was within his rights when he attempted to retake the chicken. But if X had succeeded in getting away with the chicken and F had attempted to take it away from X the next day, F would be liable in battery. A later forceful recapture is not looked upon with favor by the law; it is taking the law into one's own hands. The same rule applies to realty as well. 

Bristor v. Burr, 120 N. Y. 427. 

False Imprisonment and Malicious Prosecution

There is a marked difference between an action for malicious prosecution and one for false imprisonment. The essential element of the former is the fact that a judicial proceeding has been commenced and prosecuted maliciously and without probable cause, while the gist of the latter is an unlawful detention. 

Graves v. Rudman, 257 N. Y. S. 212.

TORTS 

293 It is not necessary to show that physical force was employed against the body of the plaintiff to restrain him in order to maintain an action for false imprisonment. It is enough that when force sufficient to dominate the situation was displayed, the plaintiff yielded submission. If A is arrested and detained by one not an officer acting without a warrant, when no crime has been committed by him, an action for false imprisonment lies, regardless of the innocence of the intention of the defendant. 

McLoughlin v. N. Y. Edison Co., 252 N. Y. 202.
