The neceessary elements which must be alleged in a complaint reciting a cause of action for malicious prosecution are: (1) that the proceedings complained of were instituted by the defendant, (2) without probable cause, (3) with malice, and (4) that the proceedings complained of have terminated in the plaintiff's discharge or acquittal. 

Halsey v. N. Y. Soc. for Suppression of Vice, 234 N. Y. 1.

The malice may be inferred by the jury from the lack of a reasonable cause for the prosecution. A holding of the plaintiff by the magistrate for the grand jury, or an indictment by the grand jury, is prima facie evidence of the existence of probable cause. 

Where a defendant appears before a grand jury and the grand jury indicts after investigation, defendant has made out a prima facie defense and the plaintiff in his malicious prosecution case must therefore meet this (defendant's) prima facie evidence of probable cause by showing that the defendant did not make a full and complete statement of the facts either to the magistrate or to the district attorney, has misrepresented or falsified the evidence, or else has kept back information or facts which might have affected the result. 

Hopkinson v. Lehigh Valley R. Co., 249 N. Y. 296; 

Schults v. Greenwood Cemetery, 190 N. Y. 276.

A discharge by a magistrate does not per se condemn the complainant (defendant) for probable cause and is not necessarily inconsistent with a finding that there is not probable cause of guilt. The magistrate's discharge merely determines that the facts proven before him do not show probable guilt. 

Reiss v. Levy, 165 A. D. 1; 150 N. Y. S. 440. 

By termination of proceedings is meant a termination by judicial action of the proper court or official in a way involving the merits of the proceedings, or by a dismissal or discontinuance based on some act of the complainant, as his withdrawal or abandonment of the prosecution. If the proceedings have been terminated without regard to the merits, as by agreement or settlement or compromise, or as a result of some trick or act preventing consideration by the court of the merits, there is no such termination as may be availed of for the purpose of an action for malicious prosecution. 

Halberstadt v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 194 N. Y. 1.

In an ordinary civil action there can be no prosecution for malicious prosecution, where the property of the defendant (the plaintiff in the action for malicious prosecution) has not been interfered with, e.g., by employing a provisional remedy, or by forcing an unnecessary involuntary bankruptcy on him. 

Sigl v. Bresnahan, 216 A. D. 634; 215 N. Y. S. 735; 

Sacks v. Weinstein, 208 A. D. 360; 203 N. Y. S. 449.

The cause of action of malicious prosecution, for the purpose of the Statute of Limitations, accrues upon the termination of the action brought by the defendant against the plaintiff. 

Psaty v. 5th Ave. & 93rd St. Corp., 132 Misc. 278; 229 N. Y. S. 384. 

An action for false arrest will not lie if based upon an order of arrest issued out of the Supreme Court having jurisdiction, even though the order be erroneous and subsequently vacated. Likewise if a person discloses fairly and truthfully to an officer whose duty it is to prosecute crime, all matters within his knowledge, which as a man of reasonable intelligence he is bound to suppose would have a material bearing on the question of guilt, and leaves it to the prosecutor to act, he is not liable for malicious prosecution if indictment follows. The showing of want of probable cause does not aid the plaintiff. In order to succeed in the action for false arrest, the plaintiff must show that the order of arrest was procured as the result of fraud, unfairness, malice or oppression. 

Skinner v. Asano Bussan Co., 130 Misc. 578; 224 N. Y. S. 643.

All that one can recover in an action for false imprisonment is the actual loss in dollars suffered. But in an action for malicious prosecution, if there was any imprisonment, a recovery can be had for humiliation and all other damage that was suffered. 

Invitees, Business Visitors, Licensees and Trespassers 

The owner of premises is bound to use reasonable care and prudence, commensurate with the hazard to be apprehended, to keep his premises in such a condition that those who go thereon at his invitation, whether adults or infants, shall not unnecessarily or unreasonably be exposed to an unusual danger of which he knows or ought to know. He is not however an insurer of the safety of those whom he invites to visit his property. 

Di Biase v. Ewart, 228 A. D. 407; affd. 255 N. Y. 620.

Whenever a person enters upon the premises of another for a purpose connected with the business of the occupant of the premises, the person so entering is a business visitor and is to be regarded as an invitee in so far as liability for injury is concerned. 

Meiers v. Koch Brewery, 229 N. Y. 10; 

Heffron v. N. Y. C. & H. R. R. Co., 223 N. Y. 473; 

Haefeli v. Woodrich Eng. Co., 255 N. Y. 442.

TORTS 

295 If one has not been actually invited, but the owner did not forbid or interfere with his coming upon the premises, then he is at most a licensee, who must take the premises as he found them, and the owner's duty would be to abstain from acts which would amount to affirmative negligence. If an injury is sustained by reason of a mere defect in the premises, the owner is not liable for negligence. 

Mere darkness in a hallway at 9 a.m., for example, cannot be held an affirmative act of negligence so as to permit a bare licensee to recover for injury suffered because of the lack of light. 

Stacy v. Shapiro, 212 A. D. 723; 209 N. Y. S. 305.

There is no duty owing to the trespasser except to abstain from intentional, reckless and wanton injury unless he maintains some hidden engine of destruction, such as spring guns or kindred devices upon his property. 

Mendelowitz v. Neisner, 258 N. Y. 181.

In determining the liability of a defendant to the plaintiff, one must consider the relationship of one to the other. The plaintiff may be a trespasser toward X, yet not toward the defendant. In one case, the plaintiff was riding on the rear end of a truck without permission of the owner. The truck collided with a trolley, and the plaintiff sued the owner of the truck and the trolley company. It was held that the plaintiff was a trespasser as to the owner of the truck and the owner owed him the duty of refraining from doing wilful or wanton injury. As respects the trolley company, it made no difference whether the plaintiff was rightfully on the truck. He was legally on the thoroughfare, and the trolley company owed him the duty of exercising reasonable care. 

North v. David Donaldson Co., Inc., 131 Misc. 170; 226 N. Y. S. 382. 

The fact that the plaintiff operates an auto bus for the carriage of passengers, without lawful authority so to act as a carrier, does not make the auto bus a trespasser upon the public highway, and he can recover for damages to his bus, caused by negligence on the part of the defendant, unless such use caused the accident, for the plaintiff had a right to drive his bus on the street. 

Aspinall v. City of New York, 246 N. Y. 644.

The courts have added an element in the cases depending for their interpretation on the acts of infants who are trespassers. Even where it seemed clear that the infant plaintiff was a trespasser, he has been allowed to recover where he has been injured by "inherently dangerous articles" so stored as to be likely to be handled or touched by children, and which contact would be likely to produce an accident. In one case, a defendant had permitted the insulation of its electric wires to become worn and broken. The wires were situated above the sidewalk. Later, X, in repairing his own adjacent property,

built wooden props near the wires, upon which props children continually climbed. The plaintiff, eight years old, while climbing came in contact with the defective wire of the defendant and was killed. 

The defendant was held liable for resulting damage, even though he was not guilty of any wanton injury and even though the plaintiff was a trespasser. The reason is that the defendant was negligent in keeping inherently dangerous articles near a place which can be reached by children, and defendant was bound to anticipate the possibilities of such results. 

Travell v. Banneman, 174 N. Y. 47; 

Jaffy v. N. Y. C. & H. R. R., 118 Misc. 147; 192 N. Y. S. 852; 

O'Callaghan v. Commonwealth Eng. Corp., 247 N. Y. 527; 

Ferrari v. N. Y. Central, 224 A. D. 182; affd. 250 N. Y. 527.

The "attractive nuisance doctrine" plays no part in the determination of such situations as that doctrine is not accorded recognition in this state. 

Hockstein v. Cong. Talmud Torah, 258 N. Y. S. 479. 

If an infant plaintiff, playing on the defendant's lot, picks up some film left by the defendant, the defendant is not responsible for personal injuries to the plaintiff, which injuries result when this film is accidentally ignited by the plaintiff. These pieces of film were not inherently dangerous, but only became so when an entirely distinct act-the ignition-was effected.¹ 

Beickert v. G. M. Laboratories, 242 N. Y. 168. 

1The duty of landowners to trespassers and licensees is covered by the 

American Law Institute in its Tentative Draft No. 4 Law of Torts, chapter 2, sections 199 to 212, inclusive. Section 209 says: “A possessor of land is subject to liability for bodily harm to young children trespassing thereon caused by a structure or other artificial condition which he maintains upon the land, if "(a) the place where the condition is maintained is one upon which the possessor knows or should know that such children are likely to trespass, and "(b) the condition is one of which the possessor knows or should know and which he realizes or should realize as involving an unreasonable risk of death or serious bodily injury to, such children."

The comment is: "e. A possessor of land is, under the principles stated in 

Comment a, under a duty to keep so much of his land as he knows to be subject to the trespasses of young children, free from artificial conditions which involve an unreasonable risk of death or serious bodily injury to them."

This may be more liberal to the injured party than the long line of decisions in this state. 

Duties to the Public (a) Buildings.-If the lessee maintains complete control of leased premises the lessor would not be liable for injuries to the lessee or to others upon the premises in the right of the lessee, even though the injuries may have resulted from a defect existing when the lessee took possession. This rule does not apply if the lessor rents the premises for a public use to which he knows they are unsuited. Thus, a lessor was held liable for injuries resulting from a defect in a dock

TORTS 

297 leased by him when he knew its unsuitability therefor; from a defective grand stand in a leased athletic park under similar conditions; from a defective toboggan slide; from a defective boardwalk in an amusement park. 

Campbell v. Elsie S. Holding Co., 251 N. Y. 446. (b) Domestic Animals.-An owner of a domestic animal is not liable ipso facto for an injury caused to a plaintiff by a bite of that animal. To attach liability to the owner is must be shown that the owner knew of the vicious propensities of the animal, or that with the exercise of due care the owner would have had such knowledge. 

Muller v. McKesson, 73 N. Y. 195; 

Palmer v. Hampton, 129 Misc. 417; 220 N. Y. S. 768. (c) Violation of Ordinances.-A failure to comply with the requirements of a city ordinance or statute is evidence of negligence if there is a logical connection between the proven neglect of statutory duty and the alleged negligence. Therefore, if an ordinance states that a person may not swim in a certain specified channel, the fact that the person injured was swimming there would be evidence of negligence on his part. 

Walker v. Bradt, 225 A. D. 415; 233 N. Y. S. 388; 

Dodican v. Smith, 221 A. D. 383; 222 N. Y. S. 748.

In applying the principle to the case of domestic animals, the following case may be mentioned: An ordinance of the city states that a horse is not to be left unbridled or unattended in a street without being fastened or without having the wheels of the wagon to which it is harnessed tied together. It would be evidence of negligence if a horse were left unattended or untied. If such horse stepped upon the sidewalk and bit the plaintiff, the plaintiff can recover, in the absence of contributory negligence, upon the theory of the defendant's negligence. 

Turner v. Craighead, 155 N. Y. 631; 

Brown v. Shyne, 242 N. Y. 176; 

Koffler v. Amer. Ry. Express, 126 Misc. 838; 214 N. Y. S. 787.

A plaintiff operating a motor vehicle while under the age provided for drivers is not a trespasser so as to be barred from recovery for injuries, if his immature age in no way contributed to his injury. He is only prima facie guilty of negligence. 

Corbett v. Scott, 234 N. Y. 66. (d) Proximate Damage Without Liability.—The rule is that one is not responsible when the same damage would have resulted if all due care had been exercised, even though the one who caused the damage happened to have been negligent. Suppose X's car skids and hits the barrier along the edge of the road. The impact breaks the barrier and the car falls down an embankment. X cannot collect from the state though the barrier was defective, because a barrier

which was perfect in construction and quality would likewise have been broken. The state need not place a barrier along the road strong enough to support a car which crashes against it. 

Reuther v. State, 126 Misc. 773; 214 N. Y. S. 783. (e) Contributory Negligence. If the plaintiff has been guilty of contributory negligence, no matter how slight, he cannot recover from the defendant for the latter's negligence. But there is no such thing as contributory negligence in vacuo. The contributory negligence, in order to defeat the plaintiff's claim, must be negligence that actually contributes to the accident. 

Parillo v. Friss, 129 Misc. 340, 221 N.Y.S. 418.

There are some circumstances in which the fact that the plaintiff was guilty of contributory negligence will not aid the defendant to defeat recovery by the plaintiff. One such circumstance is the presence of the doctrine of the last clear chance. (f) Doctrine of the Last Clear Chance. This doctrine is brought out clearly in a recent case: The plaintiff was running diagonally across the street; the defendant chauffeur had a clear view of the plaintiff for at least 100 feet, but made no attempt to check or stop his car. Although the plaintiff was clearly negligent, he was allowed to recover, for the exercise of a reasonable amount of care by the defendant chauffeur under these circumstances would have avoided the accident. 

Kunze v. Pictorial Review Co., 246 N. Y. 600.

If the plaintiff has put himself in a dangerous position negligently, and the defendant is negligent in not seeing him, there is concurrent negligence, and the doctrine of the last clear chance does not apply. The doctrine of the last clear chance, however, is never wakened into action unless and until there is brought home to the defendant to be charged with liability, a knowledge that another is in a state of present peril, in which event there must be reasonable effort to counteract the peril and avert its consequences. 

Wolosznowski v. N. Y. Central, 254 N. Y. 206; 

Wright v. Union Ry. Co., 224 A. D. 55, affd. 250 N. Y. 526.

If a woman walks on the railroad tracks, and is therefore a trespasser, the fact that the train had weak brakes, or was going very fast, does not make the defendant railroad guilty of negligence. It is only after the engineer discovers the trespasser that any duty as to her falls upon him. 

Capitula v. N. Y. C. R. Co., 210 N. Y. S. 651, affd. 241 N. Y. 614.

Another occasion occurs when the defendant creates a nuisance which causes damage, but which damage could have been averted only if the plaintiff would deprive himself of natural and appropriate privileges. Thus, if an owner of land casts noisome smells into the

TORTS 

299 plaintiff's house, he cannot be heard to say that the plaintiff could have shut them out by closing doors and windows. This must not be taken to mean that in any action based on the nuisance of the defendant, the contributory negligence of the plaintiff is no defense. 

When a nuisance has its origin in negligence, one may not avert the consequences of his contributory negligence by affixing to the negligence of the wrongdoer the label of nuisance. Unless there be the deprivation of natural and appropriate rights, where the substance of the wrong is negligence, the plaintiff, though pleading nuisance, is under a duty to show care proportioned to the danger. 

If the danger is seen, there must be an effort to avoid it. If unseen, it becomes a question whether under the circumstances the average person would perceive the danger and escape it. Conversion 

McFarlane v. City of Niagara Falls, 247 N. Y. 340.

Conversion has been defined as an unauthorized assumption and exercise of the right of ownership over goods, or personal chattels, belonging to another, to the alteration of their condition, or the exclusion of the owner's rights. 

Melnick v. Kukla, 228 A. D. 321, 239 N. Y. S. 16.

Recovery in an action in conversion is dependent upon the following elements: (1) He must give proof of title in, or right of possession to, the goods in question. 

Riley v. Pierce Oil Corp., 245 N. Y. 152. (2) The plaintiff must show either a manual taking of the property or that the defendant applied it to his own use. It is sufficient if the defendant exercise dominion over it in defiance of the owner's rights. (3) The owner must make a demand if the defendant came into possession lawfully, and there must be a refusal before the plaintiff can maintain the action for conversion. The sole purpose of the demand is to turn an otherwise lawful possession into an unlawful one by non-compliance with it. The demand must include clear instructions as to the manner of return and must be made under proper circumstances so as to make a return reasonably possible. 

Thus, under ordinary circumstances, a demand made several hundred miles away from the chattels involved, is not sufficient. 

Shea v. Chinn, 223 A. D. 476, affd. 249 N. Y. 617.

No demand is necessary before suing a thief in conversion. Where the property has passed into the hands of an innocent purchaser from the thief, a demand must be made before such purchaser can be held liable as a wrongdoer for wrongful conversion. The innocent

purchaser may safely refuse to surrender until he has had a fair opportunity to clear up his doubts on the subject. 

Pease v. Smith, 61 N. Y. 477; 

Gillet v. Robinson, 57 N. Y. 28.

If the innocent purchaser learns that the articles he bought were stolen and also who the owner is, and despite this he continues to exercise dominion over the article, no demand need be made before commencing suit. 

Employers' Fire Ins. Co. v. Cotten, 245 N. Y. 102.

If an auctioneer sells stolen goods at an auction sale, he is liable in conversion to the true owners. His good faith is no excuse, nor that he sold the articles in the usual course of business. The auctioneer must look to his principal for his indemnity. 

Levy v. Harp, 124 Misc. 901, 209 N. Y. S. 720.

In case of an actual conversion by a bailee or other person rightfully in possession from the owner, no demand is necessary as a condition precedent to the owner's right of action. 

Mullen v. Quinlan & Co., 195 N. Y. 109.

Wrongful intent is not a necessary element of conversion. The fact that the defendant was acting as an innocent agent of another is no defense at all. 

Spraights v. Hawley, 39 N. Y. 441; 

Suzuki v. Small, 212 N. Y. S. 589, affd. 243 N. Y. 590.

Spitefulness or high-handed conduct plays no part. Only actual damage based on the value at the time of conversion is all that can be recovered. 

Zeppitella v. Cappellino, 199 N. Y. S. 273.

If one wilfully and deliberately damages another's property, the damages that can be assessed are not limited to the amount of the damages. The jury can add to this amount because of the wilfullness of the damage. He who causes the wilful damage cannot put up a defense that the plaintiff was guilty of contributory negligence. 

Magar v. Hammond, 183 N. Y. 387.

If the articles converted were wearing apparel or household goods, the amount of recovery is not restricted to the price which could be realized by a sale in the market. The owner should be allowed to recover the value to him based on actual money loss, all the circumstances and conditions considered resulting from his being deprived of the property, not including, however, any sentimental value. 

Lake v. Dye, 232 N. Y. 209.

Although there may be a conversion, the plaintiff may, at his option, waive the tort and sue upon an implied promise to pay. 

Schuyler v. Smith, 51 N. Y. 309, 314.

TORTS 

301 Blasting

If A, while blasting on his own land, throws some rocks on B's house, A is liable even though he was very careful. There was an actual trespass on B's land and A is therefore liable, his care notwithstanding. But if the damage had been done by the motion of sound waves, rather than by the contact between the rocks and the house, A's care would have been a good defense. Deceit¹ 

Booth v. Rome, 140 N. Y. 267.

There are four elements which must be present in order to establish a cause of action in deceit. (1) A material misrepresentation of something which appears to be a fact. If the one making the representation said, "I think the lot is worth $4,000," it is not a misrepresentation of something that sounds like a fact, even though it might have really been worth only $2,000. If he said, "I paid $4,000," whereas he had paid only $2,000, that would be a sufficient misrepresentation, for the statement sounded like a "fact"-the amount paid. 

Fairchild v. McMahon, 139 N. Y. 290; 

Clark v. Kirby, 133 Misc. 229, 231 N. Y. S. 544.

Though the speaker merely voice an opinion of value, such expression will suffice to support an action based on a misrepresentation where there is such overmastering influence on one side, or such weakness on the other, that unfair advantage is rendered probable, and if the speaker, known to the plaintiff, had means of knowing the value which the plaintiff had not, and the false opinion was deliberately given so as to cause reliance thereon. Likewise, a declaration of present intention, false when made, to perform an act in the future, constitutes a false representation of an existing fact. (2) The speaker must intend to deceive by making the misrepresentation knowing it to be a falsehood. (3) And he must actually succeed in deceiving, so that (4) The plaintiff is damaged as a proximate result of the deception. 

Thompson v. Thompson, 232 A. D. 488; 250 N. Y. S. 433, 438; 

Tindell v. Berkett, 171 N. Y. 520; 

Pease & Elliman v. Wegeman, 223 A. D. 682, 229 N. Y. S. 398; 

Weisheit v. Pabst Brewing Co., 181 A. D. 275, 168 N. Y. S. 340.

If the plaintiff knew that the statements made by the defendant were false, he cannot recover in deceit.2 

1The reader is referred to the section on Rescission of Contracts, p. 180. 

2The four elements of deceit also make up the elements of estoppel.

Where there is a fiduciary relationship, contributory negligence is not a defense to an action for wilful fraud or deceit.³ 

Ins. Co. of North America v. Whitlock, 216 A. D. 78. 214 N. Y. S. 697. 

Libel and Slander 

Before a recovery in libel or slander will be allowed, it must be shown that some third party heard or read the damaging words. 

The mere dictation of a letter by an employee of a corporation to the stenographer of the corporation has been held not to be a publication as the act of both was one joint act by the single corporate entity. 

Wells v. Belstrat, 212 A. D. 366, 208 N. Y. S. 625; 

Owen v. Ogilvie, 32 A. D. 465, 53 N. Y. S. 1033.

The dictation of a letter by a private employer to his stenographer has been held to be a publication. 

Ostrowe v. Lee, 256 N. Y. 36.

Any corporation, including a charitable or religious corporation not organized for profit, may sue or be sued for libel or slander the same as any individual. 

Edwards v. Ritter, 124 Misc. 898, 210 N. Y. S. 299; 

N. Y. Socy. for the Supp. of Vice v. Macfadden Pub., 260 N. Y. 167; 

O'Brien v. B. L. M. Bates Corp., 211 A. D. 743, 208 N. Y. S. 110.

There is no cause of action for damages against one who publishes false or defamatory matter about the dead. If the publication was maliciously libelous, it would be a basis for a criminal prosecution. 

Penal Law, § 1340; 

Patterson v. Colgate, 129 Misc. 417, 220 N. Y. S. 677.

In the following four instances it is not necessary to show any special damage, regardless of whether the damaging words were oral or written. The falsity of the words and the existence of damage are presumed. (1) If a woman is falsely called unchaste. (2) If one is falsely stated to be guilty of a crime, as "forger", "thief," "raper," "murderer," "pimp." 

Lander v. Wald, 218 A. D. 514, affd. 245 N. Y. 590, Case 1.

The Appellate Division in the First Department has held that the word "crook" was not slanderous per se. The Second Department has held that it was. The Court of Appeals has not passed on it. 

Hofstadter v. Bienstock (1st Dept.) 213, A. D. 807; 208 N. Y. S. 453; 

Weiner v. Liviton (2nd Dept.), 244 N. Y. S. 176. (3) If one is called a name which is so derogatory that it would tend to hurt him in his office, profession, trade or business. Such 

8For a discussion of damages recoverable in actions for fraud and deceit see 

Sainer "The Adjective Law of New York," Sixth Edition, pp. 261, 262.

TORTS 

303 would be calling a lawyer a "scheister," or a doctor a "quack," or a business man a "bankrupt," or publishing in the name of a wellknown author any literary work the authorship of which would tend to injure an author holding his position in the world of letters. 

Brown v. Tregoe, 236 N. Y. 497; 

Ben-Oliel v. Press Pub. Co., 251 Ñ. Y. 250. (4) Falsely stating that a man has a disease which is loathsomeas leprosy and syphilis. 

Sleight v. Woods, 260 N. Y. S. 825.

In addition to these four, there is one case in which special damage need not be proved, provided the slanderous words were in writing. That case is when the plaintiff is held up to ridicule and contempt. Setting in written form the statement that the plaintiff habitually fails to pay her hotel bills and has to be ejected for such non-payment, or that a married woman is the lady love of a man other than her husband, is libelous per se and special damage need not be proven in order to recover. 

Sydney v. Macfadden Pub., 242 N. Y. 208; 

Wells v. Belstrat Hotel Corp., 212 A. D. 366, 208 N. Y. S. 625.

In a recent case the defendant newspaper placed, adjacent to an article on evolution, the photograph of the plaintiff and a young gorilla. Under the plaintiff's picture were placed the words "not fundamentally different from the gorilla in physique." The publication was held libelous per se. 

Zbyszko v. N. Y. American, 228 A. D. 277, 239 N. Y. S. 411.

It should be noted that where newspapers hold up to ridicule and contempt men in public life, this rule is liberally construed in the newspapers' favor. But the newspaper articles must be only in the way of commentary and opinion. They cannot deliberately misstate facts, or attack a person's private life, or impute to him an unworthiness in his public trust. So it is considered libelous per se to write about a candidate for office that he is joining himself to a group of gamblers in order to break the prohibition laws. 

In all other cases special damage must be pleaded in order to win in libel or slander. If the words, written or oral, appear harmless, the plaintiff can still show that there was a hidden meaning (innuendo) which others who heard or read the words could understand, and which caused the damage. 

Sydney v. Macfadden, 242 N. Y. 208.

The innuendo cannot be used to enlarge the natural meaning of a word. If the words are not susceptible of the meaning ascribed to them by the plaintiff, and do not sustain the innuendo, the case should not be sent to the jury.

Werstein v. Postal Telegraph Cable Co., 131 Misc. 763.

If the words, as used in common usage, are susceptible of two meanings, it would be for the jury to say in which sense the words were used and understood. 

Rovira v. Boget, 240 N. Y. 314.

Evidence of the repetition of a libel or a slander by the same party will not be considered competent for the sole purpose of enhancing damages. The plaintiff should bring a separate action on the new publication. However, if it be necessary to prove actual malice, e.g., to rebut a claim of privilege, any evidence which legitimately bears on the question of malice ought to be received, even though it might be the subject of another action. 

Taylor v. Friedman, 214 A. D. 198, 212 N. Y. S. 26.

The fact that the defendant claims that he is simply repeating slanderous words another had said, or that when he stated the slander he prefixed it by saying that he had heard so and so say it, does not excuse him. One cannot under such a pretense escape the responsibility of having spread the unfair and damaging news. 

A defendant may, however, in an action for libel or slander, prove mitigating circumstances, including the sources of his information and the grounds for his belief, notwithstanding that he has pleaded or attempted to prove a justification.¹

Civil Practice Act, § 338. 

Privilege in Libel and Slander.—If a newspaper, without malice, makes a fair and true report of any judicial, legislative or other public or official proceeding, the proprietor of the paper or its agents and servants cannot be held liable, although the statements of those who testified at the proceeding were false, malicious and slanderous. 

Smith v. Buffalo Times, 124 Misc. 495, 209 N. Y. S. 225;

Civil Practice Act, § 337. 

A lawsuit from beginning to end is a judicial proceeding. In a recent case, a newspaper reported that the plaintiff was being sued on a swindle charge and set forth the allegations of the complaint in the swindle charge. The plaintiff claimed that the newspaper was not privileged because the complaint had not yet been brought before a court or judge. It was held that a privilege did exist, and that a newspaper could publish the contents of a complaint as soon as it was filed with a clerk. 

Campbell v. N. Y. Evening Post, 245 N. Y. 320. 

Privileges are of two kinds—absolute and qualified. ally destroys the latter, not the former.

Malice usu- 

A judge, sitting as a court, is absolutely privileged. He may say anything about anybody, whether relevant or not, and his motives are immaterial. 

Karelas v. Baldwin, 261 N. Y. S. 518. 

1See "Adjective Law of New York" 6th Ed., page 59, for pleading justification.

TORTS 

305 The privilege, as it applies to witnesses, parties or counsel in judicial proceedings, is qualified. If the alleged defamatory words used by counsel or a party in the course of a judicial proceeding may possibly be pertinent they are privileged. It is only when the language used goes beyond the bounds of reason and is so clearly impertinent and needlessly defamatory as not to admit of discussion that the privilege is lost. The underlying principle governing our courts is that the proper administration of justice depends upon freedom of conduct on the part of counsel and parties to litigation. The determination of the question of privilege is a question of law, and if it be determined that the language used was not impertinent, the privilege is absolute. 

People ex rel. Bensky v. Warden, 258 N. Y. 55.

A person has a qualified privilege in transmitting information of an alleged crime to the appropriate public officers. The privilege does not apply when the sender has no information or belief that the plaintiff has been guilty of any wrong. 

White v. Carroll, 42 N. Y. 161; 

Smith v. Lichterman, 134 Misc. 150, 234 N. Y. S. 676; 

Eisenberg v. Rosenberg, 252 N. Y. 490.

By a "judicial" proceeding is meant a proceeding before any tribunal having attributes similar to those of a court. An appeal to the governor for a pardon would be a judicial proceeding. 

Andrews v. Gardiner, 224 N. Y. 440. 

Members of one family, employers asked for references concerning a former employee, and partners in business may, in good faith and with honest intentions and in order to protect one another's interests, say slanderous things about another to each other. 

Stockholders of a corporation may discuss the character of the members of the board of directors, and their communications do not form the basis for an action in libel and slander, in the absence of actual malice. 

Ashcroft v. Hammond, 197 N. Y. 448. 

Legislators are not liable because of slanderous statements uttered while acting as such at legislative sessions. 

Comment on Matters of Public Importance 

A publication concerning a matter or person of public importance or interest, though false and slanderous, does not always give rise to a cause of action for damages. The publication is justified if it contains no imputations of corruption or dishonorable motive, except in so far as they are an inference which a fair-minded man might reasonably draw from the facts truly stated and represent the honest opinion of the writer. The defense of fair comment is available to every member of the community. 

Foley v. Press Pub. Co., 226 A. D. 535, 235 N. Y. S. 340.

Books, prints, pictures, and statuary publicly exhibited, and the architecture of public buildings, ability of coaches, actors and exhibitors are all the legitimate subjects of newspaper criticism, and such criticism fairly and honestly made is not libelous, however strong the terms of censure may be. 

Hoeffner v. Dunkirk Printing Co., 254 N. Y. 95.

This right of frank and impartial criticism is not a privileged communication. It is not a libel, as it is not legally a defamation. 

If it occasions damage, it is not compensable. 

Hoeffner v. Dunkirk Printing Co., 254 N. Y. 95.

The Effect of Death in Tort Suits¹ 

If the plaintiff or defendant dies during the pendency of an action for property damage, it does not abate but may be continued by or against the executors or administrators. If no action has been begun, that may be done by the executors or administrators. 

If the action is one for personal injuries, the cause of action terminates with the death of the defendant. Should the plaintiff and not the defendant die, the action also terminates with such death, unless the death of the plaintiff is a direct result of the injuries sustained. 

Hegerich v. Keddie, 99 N. Y. 258;

Decedent Estate Law, § 120. 

A father's action for loss of his infant child's services resulting from a tort by the defendant abates on the father's death even though the damages claimed include actual expenditures for medical attention. A cause of action for loss of services is regarded as a personal injury action. 

Bianco v. Sun Oil Co., 256 N. Y. S. 597; affd. 224 A. D. 817; 

Hunter v. Wittner, 142 Misc. 795; 256 N. Y. S. 598.

Should the executor refuse to bring the action, the husband, wife or next of kin may have an administrator appointed for the purpose of prosecuting such action for their benefit. The words "next of kin" refer to that class of persons mentioned in the Decedent Estate Law, who are distributees in case of intestacy. 

In re Gant's Estate; 254 N. Y. S. 715;

Decedent Estate Law, § 130. 

Any money recovered in an action for personal injuries resulting in death belongs to the husband, wife, or next of kin of the deceased, pursuant to § 133 of the Decedent Estate Law, and does not become part of the estate of the deceased. 

Stuber v. McEntree, 142 N. Y. 200. 

1See Sainer "Adjective Law of New York," 6th Edition, p. 260, for measure of damages for injuries causing death.

TORTS 

307 The fact that the defendant would participate in the distribution of the amount of any recovery in no wise affects the right to maintain the action.

Lo Galbo v. Lo Galbo, 246 N. Y. S. 565. 

The action is nominally brought by the executors or administrators of the decedent, but it is not a right of action belonging to the estate. The right goes directly to the benefit of the next of kin. 

The recovery allowed the next of kin is limited to the pecuniary loss suffered because of the death, but since the action itself is not connected with the estate of the decedent, evidence of the legacies left to the next of kin by the decedent's will is inadmissible. 

Closson v. Griffith, 219 A. D. 163, affd. 245 N. Y. 552.

A husband, as administrator of his wife's estate, may recover judgment for the wife's death against another person who was jointly negligent with himself in causing the death, even though the husband may be the sole beneficiary of the judgment as next of kin. 

Wallace v. D'Aprile, 221 A. D. 402, 222 N. Y. S. 740. 

A "personal injury," as used here, includes libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault, battery, and false imprisonment.

We have seen that a customer has a cause of action against a restaurateur for negligence and also for breach of warranty, when he sustains injuries to his mouth as a result of the presence of glass in his food eaten in the restaurant. Let us suppose that the complaint states two causes of action for such injuries, one in negligence and one for breach of warranty. During the pendency of the action, the defendant dies. Has the plaintiff lost both causes of action? Undoubtedly the negligence action abates under § 120 of the Decedent 

Estate Law. Section 116 of the Decedent Estate Law declares that actions upon contract may be maintained by and against executors where the case might have been maintained by and against their respective testators. But the courts have held that a cause of action for personal injuries abates with the death of the defendant regardless of the form of the complaint, whether it be in form ex contractu or ex delicto. So long as the damage alleged is to the person, and not to the estate of the plaintiff, the action abates on the death of the defendant. 

Bernstein v. Queens Co. Jockey Club, 222 A. D. 191, 225 N. Y. S. 449; 

Werra v. Cassedy, 243 N. Y. S. 545.

It is worth comparing with this situation a recent case in which the complaint set forth two causes of action against a doctor, one for malpractice and one for breach of contract based upon the acts constituting the malpractice during an operation. The action was commenced four years after the operation and the malpractice action was

clearly barred. Yet the court refused to hold that the second cause of action was barred by the Statute of Limitations. 

Conklin v. Draper, 241 N. Y. S. 529, affd. 254 N. Y. 620.

A representative of the decedent must commence an action for negligence causing the latter's death within two years after such death. Where, however, the decedent during his lifetime had allowed the Statute of Limitations to expire before instituting an action for the injuries, a suit cannot be maintained by the representatives even though it be commenced within two years after the death. 

Haas v. N. Y. P. G. Medical School, 131 Misc. 395, 226 N. Y.

S. 617.1 

1For a discussion of the Statute of Limitations in tort causes see Sainer "The Adjective Law of New York," 6th Edition, p. 53.

Powers A power is the right to create or make a charge on, or dispose of, real property or personalty. Any power for a legal purpose is valid and is not limited in number as trusts are. So E may devise his realty to his wife for life, with power to appoint the property on her death to the children as she thinks necessary. The provisions of the article relating to powers apply to personalty and realty. 

Real Property Law, § 131. 

A power must be in writing and may be created by deed or will. 

It may be implied from the language of another grant, as "A to B for life and whatever he does not dispose of, to C and his heirs." 

It is plain that B can dispose of the property, and so has a power. 

A gift to A, followed by a gift to B of such part as may remain at the death of A, can be enforced when the intention of the donor is clear and definite to limit the gift to A to a life estate with power to dispose of the principal during his lifetime, and give B such part as is not disposed of in the lifetime of A. 

Callahan v. Volke, 220 A. D. 379, 222 N. Y. S. 48.

A power is deemed irrevocable unless the instrument creating it contains a reservation that it is revocable. If such a reservation is there, the grantor is deemed the absolute owner of the fee as regards purchasers from him and his creditors. 

Real Property Law, §§ 144, 145, 148. 

Suppose R is the donee of a power to sell under a will, the power to be exercised at R's discretion. R dies without having exercised the power. B is then appointed in R's place as administrator with the will annexed. Can B exercise the power of sale? No. If, however, the power were peremptory and imperative, involving no discretion, it would pass to the successor. 

Bettinger v. Montgomery, 124 Misc. 906, 210 N. Y. S. 320.

If B, an owner of a life estate or an estate for years, is given an absolute power of disposition not accompanied by a trust, the estate of B is changed into a fee absolute, in respect to the rights of creditors or bona fide purchasers, subject to any future estate limited on B's original estate in case the power of absolute disposition is not executed and the property is not sold for the satisfaction of debts. 

Therefore creditors of B may force the exercise of this power for their benefit both during the lifetime of B or after his decease. 

B had an absolute fee as regards the creditors. 

In re Davies' Estate, 242 N. Y. 196;

Real Property Law, § 149. 

309

Should a power be created by will and no donee of the power (i.e., the one who is to exercise the power) be named, its execution devolves on the Supreme Court. 

If the beneficiary of a power may, under its terms, assign his interest, his creditors can compel its execution. The execution must be by an instrument in writing sufficient to pass the estate granted. 

Real Property Law, § 162. 

The intentions of the grantor of the power as to the essential conditions of its execution must be observed. Should the trustee of the power who had the right of selection die, its execution must be adjudged for the benefit equally of all persons designated as beneficiaries of the trust.

Real Property Law, § 160. 

Where X's will gave his daughter power to appoint property to her lineal descendants, the daughter by her will cannot give the property to her husband. The exercise was declared void. 

In re Trowbridge's Estate, 124 Misc. 317, 208 N. Y. S. 662. 

Uses and Trusts

A trust will be created if an owner declare that he himself holds the property in trust for the person designated. The trust may be in parol if relating to personal property. In the absence of an indication of a contrary design, a trust created by will has its inception on the day of the donor's death. 

In re Hagen's Will, 261 N. Y. S. 437, 441.

A trust affecting real property must be manifested or proved by some writing signed by the declarer of the trust. 

Real Property Law, § 242. 

The declaration need not be made to the beneficiary nor need the writing be given to him. His ignorance of the trust is immaterial. 

In re Brown's Will, 252 N. Y. 366.

In order to create an express trust in property, there must be: (1) a settlor, who may also be the trustee; (2) a trustee, who must not be the beneficiary; (3) a designated beneficiary; (4) a trust res sufficiently identified to enable title thereto to pass to the trustee; (5) actual delivery of the property or a legal assignment thereof to the trustee, with the intention of passing title to him as trustee. 

In re Leverich's Will, 135 Misc. 774, 238 N. Y. S. 533.

In cases where there is no trustee appointed, but the trust is otherwise valid, the vacancy will be filled by the court of equity, which will appoint a trustee or take the execution of the trust upon itself. This may not occur, however, where the requirements of

the trust are such as to necessitate personal discretion in the execiition of the trust on the part of the trustee. In such case the trust will fail. 

Benedict v. Dunning, 110 A. D. 303, 97 N. Y. S. 259.

Trusts in which the trustee is a mere depository of the legal estate, while possession and the beneficial use have been given to the cestui (dry or passive trusts), have been abolished, except where they arise by operation of law or statute. 

A person coming into possession and control of the property of another becomes a trustee thereof by implication of law. 

Little & Ives v. Acceptance Corp., 215 A. D. 427, 213 N. Y. S. 606.

Passive trusts are considered legal estates. So where T is the sole trustee and beneficiary, the trust fades into the legal estate. 

And where a testator devised Blackacre in trust for his children and authorized his executors to sell it so as to maintain the children, it was held that since there was no language to indicate the duration of the trust after the sale, the provision constituted a passive trust and the property passed to the children, the executors having merely a power of sale without title.

A mere trust as "X to the use of Y" gives Y everything. X has no duty to perform. He is merely a depository. Even if power to sell had been given to X, Y would still have everything, except that Y's rights would be subject to be divested when X sold. 

Real Property Law, §§ 92, 93. 

It might be advisable at the outset to discuss the legal position of the person who is given money by A to deliver to X-a situation which occurs frequently. It is apparent that the recipient may be intended by A to be his (A's) agent or to be a trustee for X. If the former, X will have no right of action to recover the moneys in the hands of the recipient; if the latter be A's intention, a cause of action for money had and received will arise against the receiver and in favor of X. The right arises, not as a result of the receiver's promise to pay to X, but because of property rights acquired by X, to enforce an implied promise to pay over. X may sue in his own name. 

If the recipient were a mere messenger to carry the property to X, no trust would be created and no cause of action in X would exist. 

Frequently money is given to a bank with instructions to pay to X. The bank is only an agent, and the agency is revocable at the will of the depositor. Of course, if an agreement existed that the bank was to pay to X, X would acquire an equitable interest permitting him to recover in an action for money had and received. 

The controlling factor is the intention of the owner plus an implied or expressed assumption of a trust duty, if a trust is intended. 

Sayer v. Wynkoop, 248 N. Y. 54.

The Trustee¹ 

A person may create a trust for the benefit of another and make himself the trustee. The delivery of the trust instrument to another and notice to the beneficiary is not necessary. 

The trustee and beneficiary must be distinct personalities or otherwise there could be no trust, and the merger of interests in the same person would effect a legal estate in him of the same duration as the beneficial interest designed. The legal and beneficial estate cannot exist and be maintained separately in the same person. 

Greene v. Greene, 125 N. Y. 506.

A trustee acting honestly with ordinary prudence is not liable for mere errors of judgment. If he so acts he would not be liable for retaining in a falling market securities he was authorized by the testator to hold. 

In re Clark's Will, 257 N. Y. 132.

Where a trustee mingles trust funds with his own funds and he spends part of the combined sum, it is presumed that he used his own money first.

If a trustee speculates with, or spends the trust moneys unlawfully, the gain in the speculation or the articles purchased are held for the cestui. If the trustee has nothing to show for the money dent and he loses his own money as well the following is the rule: 

In the amount left to the trustee was at any time less than the amount of the trust, the smallest sum so remaining is what the cestui has preference to above general creditors. If at no time did the trustee have an amount less than what the trust amounted to, the cestui can follow the full amount. So if at any time the trustee had drawn out all the money, the cestui can get nothing as a preferred creditor, even if the trustee had put in a very large sum the next day. 

Scandinavian Imp.-Exp. Co. v. Bachman, 195 A. D. 297, 186 

N. Y. S. 860.

A trustee is liable personally and not in his representative capacity for injuries sustained through the negligent management of premises constituting trust property. 

Keating v. Stevenson, 21 A. D. 604, 47 N. Y. S. 847.

If the tort were committed by the trustee's own negligent act, he is not entitled to indemnity from the estate. Where the trustee was without personal fault, he is entitled to reimbursement from the estate. Thus, though the personal and fiduciary nature of a trust relationship prevents a trustee from delegating to another the management and control of the trust estate or duties requiring the exercise of his own personal discretion, where the duties are of a purely 

1For the jurisdiction of the Surrogate's Court over Trustees, see Sainer's "The Adjective Law of New York," 6th Ed., p. 237, et seq.

ministerial nature, or of a type he could not reasonably be expected to perform personally, a trustee has the right to employ such assistance as may be necessary for the proper execution of the trust. As long as reasonable care has been exercised, in both the choice and supervision of an agent selected for the performance of a properly delegable duty, the trustee is entitled to reimbursement for any loss occasioned by the negligence or dishonesty of the former for which he had to make good for the estate. Thus a trustee is not liable for losses arising through the insolvency of an agent or attorney employed to collect debts, or of a banker with whom trust funds have been deposited, or through the theft or embezzlement of the funds by a collection agent, broker, attorney, depositary, or clerk employed in carrying on the trust business. 

In re Lather's Will, 137 Misc. 226; 243 N. Y. S. 366.

As a general rule, a trustee who has not had possession of the assets of the estate and suffers the co-trustees to obtain them, and is not otherwise negligent in allowing the co-trustees to assume exclusive possession and management of the affairs, is not liable for waste or misappropriation. On the other hand, the acceptance of a trust duty imposes certain positive responsibilities. If the trustee delivers over the whole management to others and shows indifference or gross negligence in regard to the interests of the cestui que trust, he will be held responsible. In a recent case, one trustee permitted a cotrustee to manage the estate for fifteen years, acquiescing in all his acts, though not affirmatively, and making no effort to see that the provisions of the trust were properly carried out. This passive trustee was held liable for the misappropriation of the active trustee who had absconded, and this despite the fact that the beneficiaries themselves had been lax in requiring proper accountings. 

Brown v. Phelan, 223 A. D. 393, 228 N. Y. S. 466.

Legal proceedings against a third party for the protection of trust property must be brought by, or on behalf of, the trustee. The beneficiary or cestui que trust cannot proceed directly against the third party, unless the trustee, after demand, refuses to bring the action, in which case the cestui que trust may joint the trustee and the third party as defendants. 

Drucker v. Man. R. R. Co., 213 N. Y. 543; 

Harvey v. McDownell, 113 N. Y. 526.

If a person claims that he has a lien upon a trust fund, or seeks to set aside a deed of trust on the ground that it is fraudulent and void, he is at liberty to proceed against the trustee without joining the cestui que trust. The trustee represents the beneficiary and defends in his behalf. 

Jackson v. Talmadge, 246 N. Y. 133.

Distinctions Between a Trust and a Debt 

In the case of a trust, the obligations of the trustee attaches to definite property. In the case of a debt, the debtor is obligated to pay out of his general property. If the trust property is lost or destroyed without fault on the part of the trustee, the trustee is not liable. A debtor, however, remains liable for the debt no matter how blameless he was in the case of loss or destruction. 

Money deposited in a bank by a depositor creates a debt with the bank as the debtor. This relationship is different in one respect from the ordinary case of debtor and creditor, because a formal demand for the payment of a deposit is a condition precedent to the maintenance of any action by the depositor, unless the bank has disclaimed liability or for some other reason a demand would be futile. In the ordinary debtor and creditor relationship there is no need of any demand by the creditor as a condition to the maintenance of a suit for the recovery of the debt. 

Cottle v. Marine Bank, 166 N. Y. 53.

Let us now suppose that a bank collects money on commercial paper on behalf of the holder. Is the money so collected a trust fund, or is the bank merely a debtor? If the arrangement or the course of dealing between the parties was such that the collecting bank was under a duty to preserve the proceeds intact and to keep them separate and apart, it is a trust fund. If it was the custom for the bank to send the money collected, immediately upon collection, to the client, the presumption is that a trust relationship was intended. If, however, the time for remittance was in the future and the bank in the meanwhile could rightfully mingle the proceeds with its own money, a debtor-creditor relationship is presumed. 

Natl. B. & D. Bank v. Hubbel, 117 N. Y. 384; 

Baldwin's Bank v. Smith, 215 N. Y. 76.

If a bank has suspended, it ought not receive any payments upon business paper previously deposited with it for collection. Any proceeds received after the bank becomes insolvent, will be held in trust and may be recovered in full. 

Friede v. Natl. City Bank, 222 A. D. 645, affd. 250 N. Y. 288.

Let us now assume that the bank to whom the paper for collection was given sent that commercial paper to another bank, which we shall call the sub-agent. Let us further suppose that the first bank had the right to use the proceeds collected until a like sum was to be transmitted to the client, thus creating a debtor-creditor relationship between the client and the first bank. The sub-agent collects the money and, before it has turned the money over to the first bank, the first bank fails. Can the client claim the entire money in the hands of the sub-agent, or is he merely a general creditor of the first bank? The client can claim the money in full, as

the relation of creditor and debtor does not commence until the first bank obtains possession of the money. Until the money is so paid by the sub-agent, the proceeds are considered a trust fund. 

Importers' & Traders' Natl. Bank v. Peters, 123 N. Y. 272.

A credit given by a bank for an item drawn on or payable at such bank shall be provisional, subject to revocation at or before the end of the day on which the item is deposited in the event the item is found not payable for any reason. Whenever a credit is given for an item deposited after banking hours, such right of revocation may be exercised during the following business day.

Negotiable Instruments Law, § 350b. 

Money received by a factor from the sale of his principal's goods constitutes trust money. 

Baker v. N. Y. Natl. Exchange Bank, 100 N. Y. 31.

Where a party holding a certain fund for another is under a obligation to pay interest for the period the fund is so held, the relationship between the holder of the fund and the beneficiary is that of debtor and creditor. The payment of the interest negatives the contention that the fund is a trust fund. 

Euclid Holding Co. v. Kermarse, 131 Misc. 466, 227 N. Y. S. 103.

Active Trusts 

Active trusts are limited by statute to five kinds in which the trustee has the legal title: 

(1) A trust to sell for the benefit of creditors. Note that it says "to sell" and not "to mortgage." A trust to mortgage would be a mere power and would give the trustee no title at all, as seen in the above rule in the previous paragraphs. The direction to sell must be absolute and imperative. A discretionary right is not sufficient. 

Steinhardt v. Cunningham, 130 N. Y. 292.

Coupled with the right to sell, there must also be the right to receive and apply the rents until a sale is made. (2) A trust to sell, mortgage or lease for a legatee's or annuitant's benefit, or to satisfy a charge on the realty. (3) A trust to apply or pay over the rents and profits to a person for life or for a term less than life. In this case the trustee may lease the property for five years or less without the approval of the Supreme Court, even though this term may extend beyond the duration of the trust estate. If the trustee exceeds his authority by leasing for a period beyond the duration of the trust, the lease is valid only for a period ending with the trust term. 

Raynolds v. Browning, 217 N. Y. S. 15, affd. 245 N. Y. 623.

Where no valid direction for the accumulation is given, the surplus of rents and profits beyond the sum necessary for the education and

support of the beneficiary is liable to the claims of his creditors. This applies to trusts of personal property and real property. 

Rowe v. Farmers' L. & T. Co., 132 Misc. 651, 230 N. Y. S. 382. (4) A trust to accumulate the rents and profits for an infant. 

Real Property Law, § 96. 

To constitute an accumulation the funds must have the following elements: (a) There must be a prohibition or withholding of the present enjoyment or present expending of the rent, income or interest, and (b) This rent, income or interest so gathered must be added to the fund for the period of time mentioned. 

Therefore, a testamentary trust to pay premiums on a life insurance policy taken out by the testator on the life of his son-in-law, payable to the son-in-law's wife as beneficiary, is not an accumulation, because there is no inhibition against the present spending or enjoyment of the income. The upkeep of an insurance policy is a present enjoyment.

An accumulation for the benefit of an individual ceases to be for his benefit when he becomes 21. It exists for the benefit of individuals while they are infants only. If the accumulation is directed to continue for a longer period than the minority of the beneficiary, the accumulation is void only as to the time beyond the minority. 

Cohen v. Wacht, 137 Misc. 679, 244 N. Y. S. 274.

The accumulation must not only be for infants, but it must be exclusively for infants. If an adult or person not in being is to share in the accumulation, the trust for the accumulation is void. 

In re Rogers, 22 A. D. 428, 48 N. Y. S. 175.

After the beneficiary reaches 21, the income accruing must be paid to the presumptive taker of the next succeeding estate. So if a will directs that the trustee accumulate rents and profits until John reaches 30, and then to pay the principal to Joseph, the accumulation ceases when John becomes 21. 

In re King's Estate, 121 Misc. 298, 200 N. Y. S. 829;

Real Property Law, § 63. 

By statute the following are permitted: (a) Income arising from personal property held in trust created by an employer as part of stock bonus, pension, or profit sharing plan, for the exclusive benefit of some or all of the employees, to which contributions are made by the employer, employees or both, for the purpose of distributing to such employees the earnings and principal of the fund accumulated, may be permitted to accumulate for a period not exceeding ten years from the date of the instrument creating the trust.

(b) If the instrument creating the accumulation of personal property directs the accumulation to commence from the date of the instrument, or from the death of the party executing the instrument, the accumulation is valid provided it is for the benefit of infants then in being, and to terminate before the majority of the infants. (c) If the accumulation is to commence after the execution of the instrument, or after the death of the creator, the accumulation in order to be valid, must commence within the time allowed for the suspension of alienation of personal property and during the minority of the beneficiary, and it must terminate before the majority. Any direction for an accumulation for a term longer than the minority of the beneficiary is void as respects the time beyond such minority. (d) If the accumulation is for the benefit of a college or literary institution, or for the purpose of providing for the maintenance of teachers in grammar school, the income may be permitted to accumulate until it shall amount to a sum sufficient in the opinion of the New York State Regents, to carry its purpose into effect. (e) One-fourth of a trust fund of which the beneficiary is a religious, educational, charitable, or benevolent organization, may be permitted to accumulate. This fourth must not exceed $50,000. and the total sum accumulated must not be over $100,000. 

Personal Property Law, § 16. 

(5) A trust for general charitable purposes. Indefiniteness of purpose, if the purpose is clearly charitable, or indefiniteness of beneficiaries, is no objection to the validity of a charitable trust. The trust, however, must be entirely for a charitable purpose, which includes, among other things, education and religion. The charitable trust is not subject to the rule against perpetuities. 

In re Cunningham, 206 N. Y. 601.

A trust to maintain a school for the Jones family is a private trust and not a general charitable one.

A trust, however, for the promotion of research in agricultural chemistry is a trust for a public purpose and for a general charitable purpose. A bequest, to be expended in the development of the Irish 

Republic, is not a charitable bequest. (In fact, it is void, as it tends to encourage a change in the fundamental law of a nation.) 

In re Frasch's Estate, 245 N. Y. 174; 

In re Killen's Estate, 124 Misc. 720, 209 N. Y. S. 206; 

In re Perkins' Estate, 245 N. Y. 469.

A trust for the purpose of having masses said, is a gift for a religious use. 

In re Beck's Estate, 130 Misc. 765, 225 N. Y. S. 187.

A trust to maintain a home for refined, educated, Protestant women whose means were small and whose home was made unhappy by living with relatives who thought her in their way, was held to be a valid charitable bequest, although the testatrix provided that preference was to be given to certain of her own relatives. 

In re MacDowell, 217 N. Y. 460.

If the trustees have several institutions from which to select and cannot agree upon the beneficiaries, the Attorney General is empowered to represent the beneficiaries in the enforcement of the trust. 

It is his duty to compel by judicial decree in the Supreme Court, the application of the fund to the public use contemplated by the testator. 

Personal Property Law, § 12; 

In re Philipoteaux, 245 N. Y. S. 303.

If no trustee is named for the charitable trust, the Supreme Court becomes the trustee. 

In re Beekman's Estate, 232 N. Y. 365.

Gifts to foreign trustees will be upheld if valid in the places where the trust is to be administered. The New York courts will not administer such foreign charity, but will direct money devoted to it to be paid over to the proper parties, leaving it to the state where the charity is to be administered to provide for its administration. 

In re Feehan's Estate, 241 N. Y. S. 669;

Real Property Law, § 113; 

In re Donchian's Estate, 120 Misc. 535, 199 N. Y. S. 107.

Lands may be conveyed in trust to (a) A college or unincorporated literary association to build an observatory, to found professorships, to maintain a cemetery, or for other purposes mentioned in its charter. (b) A municipal corporation for a public purpose. (c) School commissioners for the benefit of schools in their district. (d) For the perpetual care of cemetery lots. 

Real Property Law, § 114. 

Since the number and kind of trusts which can be treated are so limited, any "trust" which cannot take effect as such because it does not come under any of the five headings above mentioned (pages 

315-317) may be exercised as a power in trust. The legal title, not being then in the trustee, descends to the heirs or devisees of the cestui, subject always to the exercise of the power by the trustee who has the power.

Real Property Law, § 99. 

It should be noted that the trusts falling under subdivisions (3) and (4) listed on pages 315, 316 cannot be alienated in any way, un-

less one creates such a trust in his own favor. In such latter case he may assign all the interest he created in his own favor to his creditors. 

Real Property Law, §103; 

Schenck v. Barnes, 156 N. Y. 316.

Where land is conveyed to a trustee, and the instrument does not mention the fact that it is a trust all bona fide purchasers for value are protected. What the trustee does with the money after receipt of it from the bona fide purchaser in no way affects the latter. But if the word "trustee" is in the instrument, it is equivalent to notice, and the conveyee is no longer a bona fide purchaser. 

Real Property Law, § 104. 

If the trustee is allowed discretion as to the disposition of the property and he may apply it to purposes not charitable as well as charitable, the trust is not a charitable trust, even in part. 

Cy Pres Doctrine 

If the provisions are such that the trustees cannot carry them out, the court will administer the trust property so as to effectually accomplish the purpose of the trust as nearly as possible. This is known as the cy pres doctrine. The court, however, will act only on the application of the trustee, the attorney-general, or the creator of the trust if he be alive. 

In re MacDowell, 217 N. Y. 454.

If the particular objects of a bequest to charity existed when the testator died, but later ceased to function, the Supreme Court under the cy pres doctrine will carry out the trust. Thus a trust for the benefit of the Baptist Church of Lyons, N. Y., which ceased to function, was directed to be turned over to the Baptist Missionary Convention of New York, an organization having similar purposes. 

Graff v. Raymer, 136 Misc. 297, 240 N. Y. S. 658.

Where a testator created a trust for maintenance of indigent females and directed the use of his homestead, but conditions had changed so that the homestead was not suitable, the court applied the cy pres doctrine and permitted the trustee to administer the trust by caring for indigent females in private or institutional homes. 

In re Swan's Will, 261 N. Y. S. 428.

The Rule Against Perpetuities 

Every future estate is void in its creation which suspends the power of alienation for more than two lives in being at the creation of the estate. The suspension is not made valid even by agreement of all the parties. But a contingent remainder in fee may be created on a prior remainder in fee if it takes effect in the event that the one to whom the first remainder is limited die under 21. 

Real Property Law, § 42.

The only trust forms subject to the rule are the third and fourth, i.e., to collect the income and pay for it over, or to accumulate it. 

So whenever we have a suspension of the power of alienation (which means that there is no one capable of conveying the full fee), we must first determine whether these are involved. 

A devise in trust to receive the income of realty for A for life, B for life, and C for life, is bad altogether, for there is a suspension of the power of alienation for more than two lives in being at the creation of the estate. The following trust was also declared void. 

The testator set aside a part of his estate for the benefit of his wife and upon her death for the benefit of her three children. The principal was to be paid to each child when that child became 23 years old, and until that time the income was to be paid. It was further provided that in the event of the death of a child before the age of 23, without issue, that child's share was to be added in equal portions to the shares of the other surviving children. A part of the trust estate therefore became subject to a possible unlawful suspension, as the power of alienation of the property was suspended (a) during the life of the widow, (b) during the life of the child, and (c) during the lives of the children of the testator surviving the child. 

In re Trevor, 239 N. Y. 6.

Although one of the three during whose life the trust is to exist survives the creator of the trust by only a few days, the trust would nevertheless be held bad.

It is evident that a trust for a term of years would be bad, because any definite term, even one year, might be longer than two lives. Thus a trust "for three years" was held bad. But should the trust provide for a termination "at the end of a definite period or at the end of two lives who were in being at the creation of the estate," it is valid. From this it has been held that a trust for a person until he attains a certain age is valid, for it is to last till he attains that age, or sooner dies, and so is really for one life. And a trust "to collect rents during the joint lives of A, B and C" is bad. 

But if in addition to this it said, "and the trustees may distribute the principal, when they desire," it becomes valid, because of the alternative, for the trustees are persons in being who can at any time convey an absolute fee in possession. 

Matter of Hitchcock, 222 N. Y. 57; 

In re Lynch's Will, 102 Misc. 650, 169 N. Y. S. 321; 

Petition of Bahrenburg, 130 Misc. 196, 224 N. Y. S. 183.

Note that the power given to the trustee is absolute. If there be any limitation on the power of the trustee to terminate the trust and there still remains a suspension of the power of alienation, then the rule against perpetuities is applicable. The following case will bring out the point. A testator directed that the residue of his estate be divided into three equal parts, one part to be held in trust

for, and to pay the income to, his wife; one part for his son; and one for his daughter. On the death of his wife the fund held in trust for her was to be divided equally for the benefit of the children, and on the death of either, the principal was to be held in trust for the life of the survivor. Thus far, there is an invalid trust, as the trust is for three lives. The testator, however, provided that the trustees were permitted to convey the principal of the fund of any child to that child, if they considered the habits, capacity and ability of that child to warrant it. It was held that this latter clause did not validate the trust, because there existed a restriction of the trustees' right to alienate, for they might honestly find that the child was not fit to receive the fund before its natural termination. 

In re Perkins' Estate, 245 N. Y. 478.

The Court of Appeals, in a recent decision, opened up the door to future litigation. By the terms of a will, the whole estate of the testator, amounting to a considerable sum, was given to a trustee in trust, to pay from the income $50 per month to one Mabel Crans, "so long as she may live." The balance of the income was to be paid to the testator's wife, and if the said wife was survived by his son, then the balance of the income to that son. When the above payments ceased by reason of the deaths of the beneficiaries, the trustee was to divide the estate and distribute it in certain definite ways. Clearly the trust is for three lives, and violates the rule against perpetuities. The dissenting opinion so held. But the majority of the court held the trust valid, saying that the wife and the son were the chief objects of solicitude. The care of them was the primary purpose of the trust. The trifling provision for Mabel Crans was an incident and nothing more. The trust was held to be for two lives, and the trust for Mabel Crans was severed from the main trust, so that if it happened that Mabel Crans survived both the wife and son, the trustee was to hold within the trust an amount adequate to make provision for the monthly payment of $50. The court said that it was clearly not the intention of the testator to require that the entire estate should be held in trust to protect a small payment of $600 per year. If such were the intention of the testator then the entire estate would be void. The court admittedly struggled hard to find some way of circumventing the effect of the rule and stretched a point to be practical and give effect to the intention of the testator. 

In re Gallien, 247 N. Y. 195.

In another case the testator created a trust of his residuary estate "to receive all rents and income and to pay out to my wife during her life, $7,500; to my brother for life, $1,250 yearly; to my nephew for life, $1,250 yearly; to my sister for life, $1,250 yearly." The court held this trust good, stating that there can be no question that

the testator intended to provide: (a) A fund or funds to be held in trust which would be sufficient to produce an income of $7,500 yearly, to be paid to his wife during her life; (b) a fund or funds sufficient to provide for his brother the sum of $1,250 yearly; (c) a fund or funds sufficient to provide $1,250 for his nephew for his life; and (d) a fund or funds sufficient to provide for his sister for her life the sum of $1,250 yearly, and that although under the said paragraph these funds were to remain in solido, there was positively no tenable objection to an interpretation which holds that the testator intended separate and severable trusts for the beneficiaries named. The influence of the decision in the Gallien case was apparent throughout the opinion of the court. 

In re Shapiro, 124 Misc. 363, 208 N. Y. S. 35.

Successive life estates must be limited to persons in being at the time of the creation thereof. But you cannot have more than two successive life estates. To A for life, remainder to B for life, remainder to C for life, remainder to D for life, remainder to E in fee, means that E gets the fee after B dies. The first two life estates are valid. This paragraph has no application to trusts, but only to legal life estates.

Real Property Law, § 43. 

U. S. Trust Co. v. Webb, 110 Misc. 683, 181 N. Y. S. 850. 99 A will provided that the three children of the testator were to occupy certain property "for and during their respective lives or until they marry, and the property is to be distributed upon the expiration of all of said life uses.' The effect of this is that the three children are tenants in common. The devise lasts until two die or marry and will then be at an end and the third life use will be void. 

Berdel v. Berdel, 125 Misc. 496, 211 N. Y. S. 177.

In applying the rule against perpetuities to powers, the period of suspension is calculated from the time the power is created and not from the time it is to be executed. The doctrine of relation back is peculiar to the law of powers. 

Real Property Law, § 178. 

Thus an attempt of the donee of an appointment to make her children, who were not living at the date of the donor's death, the beneficiaries of the trust created by the donor, is void because it violates the rule against perpetuities. 

In re Trowbridge's Estate, 124 Misc. 317, 208 N. Y. S. 662.

A will bequeathed money in trust with a direction that the income was to be paid to A, and on his death the principal was to be paid among such members of the testator's family as A should designate in his will. This, of course, was a power in A. A's will created a trust of this fund and the income was directed to be paid to X for

life, then to Y for life, and on Y's death the principal was to be paid to Z. It is necessary to calculate from the time the power was created. Therefore, there was a suspension during the life of A, X and Y, and there being three lives, the suspension violated the rule against perpetuities. If X had predeceased A, one life would have been removed, and the trust would have been good. 

In re Dodge's Estate, 129 Misc. 390, 222 N. Y. S. 247. "I authorize and empower and direct the trustee of my estate to hereafter, within three to five years, to sell and dispose of my real estate

This was held a mandatory direction to sell and it was held not to be contra to the rule of perpetuities, despite the fact that a literal reading might justify a holding that the power of sale could not be exercised until three years had elapsed. 

The rule is that an executor or trustee being clothed with the power, and it being his imperative duty to sell the realty of the testator, the same will be deemed converted into personalty at once. limit of time is inoperative. 

In re Smith's Estate, 123 Misc. 69, 204 N. Y. S. 475. 

Trusts of Personalty The

The power of alienation of personalty cannot be suspended for more than two lives in being at the date of limitation or at the death of the testator if the limitation is set out in a will. 

A life in being or a minority in being includes a child begotten before the creation of the estate, but born thereafter. In other respects all limitations of future estates in personalty are subject to the same rules as limitations upon realty. But the purpose for which trusts can be created are not limited in personalty matters as they are in realty.

Personal Property Law, § 11. 

So a trust in personalty to continue during the lifetime of T's daughter and during the minority of the youngest two of his grandchildren who should survive him suspends the ownership too long. 

The limitation is void. (A suspension of the power of alienation during a minority is equivalent to a possible suspension during a life.) 

Suppose there is a trust of personalty for the lives of two sons and for the minority of the youngest grandchild, and at the end of the trust to be divided among X, Y and Z. The trust, of course, is void because of the perpetuity rule. But note that X, Y and Z's shares are not accelerated, because their legacies were not vested, but contingent, both as to time and person, and they were not separable from the rest of the invalid trust. It was one whole scheme and so if part is void, it must fall together. 

In re Douglass' Estate, 120 Misc. 193, 198 N. Y. S. 792; 

In re Moore's Will, 120 Misc. 825, 199 N. Y. S. 790.

In charitable trusts of personalty the cy pres doctrine applies. 

Accumulations of income of personalty are allowed, just as in realty. 

Personal Property Law, §§ 12, 13, 16; 

In re Donchian's Estate, 120 Misc. 535, 199 N. Y. S. 107.

A creator of a trust of personalty may revoke it in whole or in part upon the written consent of all the persons in being beneficially interested in the trust. Thereupon the estate of the trustee ceases to the extent of the revocation. 

In an action by the settlor to revoke the deed creating the trust, all the designated beneficiaries and the trustees are necessary parties.
