The exception to the rule which permits trustees to represent the beneficiaries as sole party applies where a stranger to the transaction seeks to set aside the trust agreement under a right anterior to the making of the trust deed. 

McKnight v. Bank of New York, 254 N. Y. 417.

If the creator happened to be the beneficiary of the trust and it is provided that after his death the corpus shall be distributed in the manner determined by the laws of New York, the creator by himself has the right to revoke, as there is no then existing person who has any interest in the trust fund outside of the creator of the trust.

Personal Property Law, § 23; 

Stella v. N. Y. Trust Co., 224 A. D. 50, 229 N. Y. S. 166.

A beneficiary of a trust cannot transfer his right to enforce the performance of the trust to receive the income of personal property and to apply it to the use of any person. If the trust, however, was created by the party receiving the income, the income is assignable. 

If created by a third party, the income is not assignable. 

Personal Property Law, § 15; 

Schenck v. Barnes, 156 N. Y. 316. 

Savings Bank Trusts

A deposit by one person of his own money, in his own name as trustee for another standing alone, does not establish an irrevocable trust during the lifetime of the depositor. It is a tentative trust merely, revocable at will, until the depositor dies or completes the gift in his lifetime by some unequivocal act or declaration, such as delivery of the pass book or notice to the beneficiary. In case the depositor dies before the beneficiary without revocation, or some decisive act or declaration of disaffirmance, a rebuttable presumption arises that an absolute trust was created as to the balance on hand at the death of the depositor. 

Matter of Totten, 179 N. Y. 112, 125. 

Constructive Trusts

These trusts are raised by the equity court, regardless of the intention of the parties, in order to effect justice. When property has

been acquired in such circumstances that the holder of the legal title may not in good conscience retain the beneficial interest, equity converts him into a trustee.

Beatty v. Guggenheim Exploration Co., 225 N. Y. 380. 

If A orally promises for a consideration to buy certain property in the name of the promisee or in the joint names of A and the promisee, or he promises to hold the property in trust for the promisee, and he violates this oral promise, equity will not raise a constructive trust, because to do so would violate the Statute of Frauds and because nothing has been done to induce equity to interfere. 

There are, however, some exceptions to this rule. Where the parties are in the position of confidential relationship, a constructive trust will be declared. This confidential relationship may be one of business or kinship. Where a son received realty from his mother under an oral promise to hold the property for her and her other children, a repudiation of that promise by the son gave rise to a constructive trust. Likewise a constructive trust of land arises where a confidential relationship would be abused by a repudiation by the person to whom the property was conveyed, without redress, of a trust orally declared. Thus if a husband paid for property, managed it, maintained it, paid for all upkeep and collected the rents, but took title in his wife's name, a trust would result in the husband's favor if the wife had promised, at the time of conveyance, to give him a deed upon demand or to dispose of the land in accordance with his wishes. This is so, even where the wife's promise was not in writing. 

Foreman v. Foreman, 251 N. Y. 237;

Goldsmith v. Goldsmith, 145 N. Y. 313. 

Resulting Trusts 

The resulting trust arises by operation of law and is implied by equity from the acts of the parties according to their apparent intentions.

Suppose land is conveyed to T and paid for by P. Under ordinary circumstances T takes full legal title. No trust results to P. But if P had creditors at that time, there arises a presumption of fraud and unless this presumption is rebutted, a trust results in favor of those persons who were creditors of P at the time of the transfer, up to the amount of their debts. A trust does not result in favor of such persons as subsequently became creditors. Proof that one paying the consideration was at the time not insolvent but had, aside from the consideration paid for the property granted, ample funds to pay his debts and that an inability to meet his obligations was not and could not reasonably be supposed to have been in his mind, is sufficient to rebut this presumption. 

Preston v. Conroy, 231 A. D. 442, 247 N. Y. S. 549.

If P had no creditors, but the land was conveyed to T and paid for with P's money without P's knowledge or permission, a constructive trust would result in P's favor. 

Real Property Law, § 94.

Who May Make a Will 

(1) A testator must be of the age of 18 years or more to validly bequeath personalty and 21 years or more to validly devise realty. (2) He must be sane enough to have the capacity to comprehend the nature of the transaction to recall his property, to know of what it consisted, to understand his relation to those who are the objects of his bounty as affected by his will.

His physical condition, hallucinations or religious beliefs are of no moment, unless of such a nature as to affect his sanity and therefore his knowledge of the condition of his property, his relationship to the object of his bounty, and the effects of his will. So if a man. is obsessed with the false idea that his daughter is immoral and as a result cuts her off, the will is void. 

In re Bossom's Will, 195 A. D. 339, 186 N. Y. S. 782; 

Lester v. Lester, 178 A. D. 438, 165 N. Y. S. 592.

Habitual and extreme intoxication does not of itself constitute an unsound mind or testamentary incapacity. There must be additional proof that at the time of the execution of the will the judgment of the testator was perverted or paralyzed because of intoxication. 

The disposition which a man makes of his property by will is not invalidated because its provisions are unequal or unjust, or the result of passion, or other unjustifiable sentiment. Neither is the presumption in favor of a will overcome by showing that the testator was of advanced age or enfeebled in mind and body. 

In re Radley's Will, 228 A. D. 119, 239 N. Y. S. 44. 

When a testator has become incompetent, and the committee of the testator for the purpose of procuring funds for the use and maintenance of a testator, has sold the subject of a specific bequest, the legatee is not entitled to the balance on hand from the sale. 

When the specific thing ceases to exist the legacy falls. 

In re Weland's Estate, 257 N. Y. 155.

The Execution of the Will 

There are three items to be remembered in determining whether the execution was proper or not. (1) A will to be valid must be in writing. There is one exception of this rule the nuncupative will. A nuncupative will can be made only by one who at the time he makes the will is a soldier in actual military service (not necessarily engaged in war), army nurse or commercial mariner engaged as such. A seaman on a vessel which 327

sails on a river within the United States is not a "mariner". The sea is considered the waters within the ebb and flow of the tide. 

No realty can be devised by a nuncupative will nor can any part of a valid existing will be revoked by an oral will. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 16. 

(2) The will must be subscribed by the testator at the physical end, otherwise probate will be denied. A mark of course will do if the testator cannot write. Likewise the fact that a witness aided the testator to sign does not invalidate the will. 

In re Caffrey's Will, 174 A. D. 398, 161 N. Y. S. 277; 

In re Barger's Will, 225 A. D. 674, 231 N. Y. S. 188.

If after the signature there is more writing of a dispository nature, the will is not considered signed at the end and the will is void in its entirety. But if the writing after the signature was not dispository, it does not void the will; the writing above the signature then constitutes the valid will. 

In re Ryan's Will, 252 N. Y. 620.

Where a holographic will contained in this order several bequests, the signature, three names of people declared to be executors, then the witnessed attestation clause, all done at one sitting and as part of one transaction, the will was held void. The appointment of executors is an important integral part of the testamentary disposition. 

In re Tyner, 130 Misc. 192, 245 N. Y. S. 206.

Thus, the rule can be formulated that the addition of a clause at a time after proper execution and not as part of the execution, does not affect the will. The addition alone is disregarded. 

A will consisting of two pages, signed at the end of the first, does not meet the statutory requirements and cannot be probated, despite the fact that both pages were intended by the testator to be considered as the will. The will was not signed at the end. 

In re Reilly's Will, 129 Misc. 77, 220 N. Y. S. 781.

Where a draftsman, after the testatrix had properly executed a will, wrote in the name of the decedent's son as executor after the signature, the added part was not admitted to probate, but the instrument actually subscribed by her, was her will. 

In re Mackey's Estate, 136 Misc. 413, 243 N. Y. S. 229; 

In re Serviera's Will, 205 A. D. 686, 200 N. Y. S. 464.

The will need not be written on separate and detached pages. 

One may paste pages on another sheet. And if on reading along, as one would from a pad, the signature is seen at the physical end, the signature is in the proper place. A seal is not necessary. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 21. 

In one case the will was written on a paper folded along the center, making four pages. The pages were marked as follows: The

outside page was marked "Page 1"; on turning over, the back of page one was marked "Page 3" and what was the page opposite, was marked "Page 2." The signature was found on the page marked "Page 3." The will was held invalid and void. Though this decision seems rather too technical, the fact that pages one and two each ended a paragraph may have had some bearing on the decision. 

Matter of Andrews, 162 N. Y. 1. (3) The signature of the testator must either be actually written or acknowledged by him before at least two witnesses. The testator, at the time of attestation, must tell these witnesses that the document is his will and his signature must be visible to them, though they need not actually read it. The request to the subscribing witresses may be made in any set form of language. It is sufficient to justify probate if it is apparent that the testator and the witnesses knew that the paper was the last will, that in substance he so declared it to be, and indicated his wish that the witnesses subscribe. 

In re Heller's Will, 222 A. D. 64, 225 N. Y. S. 244; 

In re Haber's Will, 118 Misc. 179, 192 N. Y. S. 616.

These witnesses must sign and write their home addresses. If they do not write their addresses, they may be liable $50 each to an interested person who sues for it, but the will itself is not affected. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 22. 

The witnesses need not sign any attestation clause¹, but it is advisable that they do so. It makes out a prima facie case that the witnesses knew it was a will. 

In re Conway's Will, 129 Misc. 361, 222 N. Y. S. 268.

The witnesses may sign together or separately. They need not see each other or the testator sign. If the testator signs after the attesting witnesses, the will is a nullity. But if the witnesses sign and immediately after, and as part of the same transaction, the testator signs, it is a valid execution, as the conduct of the witnesses under such circumstances amounts to an adoption of their signatures. 

In re Haber's Will, 118 Misc. 179, 192 N. Y. S. 616; 

Sisters of Charity v. Kelly, 67 N. Y. 409.

A will signed by one witness for another witness at the request of the latter, and in the presence of the latter, is properly executed. 

In re Hunniston's Estate, 128 Misc. 71, 218 N. Y. S. 234.

Suppose a soldier, before attacking the enemy, said to a comrade, "All I have I want to go to Miss K." Later he was captured and in prison he told another prisoner, “I want all I have to go to Miss K." This would be considered a good nuncupative will. It fulfills 

1For this clause see Form No. 3 in Sainer's "The Adjective Law of New 

York," 6th Ed., p. 276.

the requirements that two witnesses are needed even for a nuncupative will, even though the witnesses heard the will at different times. 

In re Mallery's Will, 217 N. Y. S. 489, affd. 247 N. Y. 580; 

In re Mason's Will, 121 Misc. 142, 200 N. Y. S. 901;

Surrogate's Court Act, § 141. 

The statute regulating the execution of a will must be strictly complied with even in the case of a holographic will, i.e., a will written out by the testator. 

In re Van Derzee's Will, 124 Misc. 539, 208 N. Y. S. 687.

Every will, in the absence of a clear direction to the contrary, speaks as of the time of the testator's death. So if Johnson gave a deed of a house to S, to hold till Johnson's death, and the deed ran to B, B would get the property on Johnson's death, even if Johnson's will left it to X. The title relates back to the instrument that spoke first, the deed.

Where a testator refers to an actually existing state of things, particularly itemizing his devises and bequests, his language refers to the date of the will and not of his death. Therefore such a devise will not carry after-acquired property. 

Stanfield v. Thorne, 217 A. D. 767, 216 N. Y. S. 410.

Specific and Demonstrative Legacies 

A legacy is specific if it gives, without any implied equivalent, a certain thing, particularly described and distinguished from all others of the same kind belonging to the testator, usually at the time of the making of the will or in some cases at the date of his death. 

The legacy is meant to be paid by the delivery only of the identical thing specified, and if it be not in existence at the date of the testator's death, the legatee does not get its equivalent out of the general estate. If the legacy be given with reference to a particular fund only as pointing out a convenient mode of payment, it is demonstrative, and the legatee will not be disappointed though the fund totally fail, but will receive the legacy out of the general assets. 

In re Strasenburgh's Will, 136 Misc. 91, 242 N. Y. S. 453.

If the legacies are specific, the legatees are entitled to the income from the date of the death of the decedent. If the legacies are demonstrative or general, the income from the date of death to such time as distribution should have been made, falls into the residue of the estate. 

In re Liell's Will, 139 Misc. 513, 247 N. Y. S. 386.

The Probate¹ 

The purpose of the probate of a will is to have it declared a valid will. It is rarely construed or interpreted in the probate pro- 

1For a discussion of the procedure in the probate of a will, see Sainer "The 

Adjective Law of New York," 6th Ed., p. 242, et seq.

ceedings. Before admitting the will to probate, the surrogate must be satisfied with its genuineness and validity of its execution, and, if it appears to the surrogate that it was duly executed and that the testator was competent to make a will and not under restraint, it must be admitted to probate. 

Surrogate's Court Act, § 144. 

It is no objection to the probate of a will that a testator has made a valid contract to dispose of his property in a different manner from that provided in his will, or that the will offered for probate revokes a will drawn in accordance with the terms of that contract. 

Schley v. Donlan, 131 Misc. 208, 225 N. Y. S. 453.

The burden of proving the will formally proper, the genuineness of the testator's signature, numbers and signatures of witnesses, circumstances of execution, testator's soundness of mind, and other statutory factors is on the proponent. 

In re Schellinger's Will, 258 N. Y. 186. 

A will coming from proper custody is presumed to be genuine. 

In re Oliver's Will, 126 Misc. 511, 214 N. Y. S. 154.

At least two subscribing witnesses must be produced and examined if they are in the state and would make competent witnesses. 

They must testify to at least two things: (1) to the publication of the will by the testator, and (2) that they signed as witnesses at his request. 

In re Huber, 181 A. D. 635, 168 N. Y. S. 890. 

Should the witnesses be dead or incompetent or should they testify against the existence of a valid will, the will can still be admitted to probate on proof by other people of the handwriting of the testator and of the witnesses and by additional testimony. The will cannot be admitted to probate without proof of the handwriting of the requisite two subscribing witnesses.

Surrogate's Court Act, §§ 141, 142; 

In re Cann's Estate, 240 N. Y. S. 840.

When the probate is contested, the proponent makes out a prima facie case by proving through the subscribing witnesses that the statutory requirements have been complied with and that the testator appeared to be of sound mind and free from restraint. The presumption of sanity of the testator has application, and no evidence is required to prove the testator's sanity if not impeached. When it is impeached the court must have evidence that affirmatively establishes the sanity of the testator in order to declare the will valid. 

In re Smith's Will, 180 A. D. 669, 168 N. Y. S. 135.

The contestants in a probate proceeding where objections have been filed are not bound by the testimony of the subscribing witnesses. Nor is the proponent so bound. 

In re Beeman's Will, 221 A. D. 129, 222 N. Y. S. 751.

Suppose a will has been lost or destroyed. In such a case it can be admitted to probate if there is evidence to justify a finding that the will was in existence after the testator's death, or that it was fraudulently destroyed after his death. The provisions must be proven by at least two credible witnesses, or else by one such witness and a correct copy of the will, which latter would equal one witness. 

Surrogate's Court Act, § 143. 

Special Circumstances Affecting the Will (1) Subsequent Marriage of Testator.-If T marries after the execution of his will and this will made no mention of any future wife or children, his wife and any children surviving are entitled to the same rights in the estate of the testator that they would have had if no will had been made. The same rule applies if the will had been made after marriage, or after another child is born, or one adopted. This child, even if born posthumously, gets his share as if the father had died intestate. The child so born or adopted recovers his portion from the devisees and legatees in proportion to and out of the parts devised and bequeathed to them by the will. 

Decedent Estate Law, §§ 26, 35; 

Bourne v. Dorney, 184 A. D. 476, 171 N. Y. S. 264.

If the prospective wife of a testator is referred to in the will by her maiden name, but the period of time between the making of the will and the marriage is short, and its provisions of such a character as to indicate that it was made with the prospective marriage in mind, the provisions may then be construed to have been made in contemplation of the new status and construed as an antenuptial bequest and the presumption of revocation overthrown. If the language can be fairly construed as indicating that the testator had the prospective status in mind, the presumption of revocation will be overthrown even though there be a considerable lapse of time between the will and the marriage. 

In re Scolpino's Will, 231 A. D. 690, 248 N. Y. S. 634. (2) The Witness as a Legatee. If a devisee or legatee was a necessary witness i.e., one of two, rather than one of three or more), he cannot take any legacy or devise, but the will itself is not thereby void, nor is he prevented from being a witness in any controversy. But note this: if this witness should have inherited something if the testator had died intestate, he takes that amount now, but provided only that what he would have taken had there been no will is less than what the testator did leave him in the will. If what the testator did leave him is less, then he takes that rather than what the intestacy laws would have given him. So if one is a necessary witness, he loses his bequest or devise unless he is so related that he would have taken under the intestacy laws. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 27.

It is to be noticed that those entitled to share because they were married or born to the testator after the making of the will, or a subscribing necessary witness who gets a share, can get that share by an action against the other beneficiaries. They cannot contest the probate of the will because of that fact. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 28. 

(3) Predecease of a Legatee.—Suppose (a) a descendant or (b) a brother or sister is mentioned by name and not as a member of a class (as "to my brother John" and not as "to all my children”), and that this beneficiary dies before the testator, and the will remains unchanged. If this devisee or legatee left a descendant, the devise or legacy, if the testator has not otherwise provided, vests in this descendant, as if this deceased devisee or legatee had left no will. 

This applies even if this deceased devisee or legatee had left a will. 

It applies only to descendants that are left to the deceased devisee or legatee.

Decedent Estate Law, § 29. 

If the devisee or legatee had not been a brother or sister or descendant, the devise or legacy would go into the residuary estate. 

But if this devisee or legatee who was not a descendant or brother or a sister was the residuary legatee, there would be an intestacy as regards the residue, and the residue would have to be divided in accordance with the rules applicable to intestacy. 

In re Aubke, 134 Misc. 310, 235 N. Y. S. 304. 

Suppose the testator left "my land to be divided among my brothers share and share alike." If one of the brothers had predeceased the testator and had left a child, this child would not take, as in such a case only those surviving in the class take. 

Suppose a devise to X absolutely, and in case of his death to Y. 

Y can take only if X predeceases the testator. 

In re Fort's Estate, 126 Misc. 28, 211 N. Y. S. 772. (4) Insufficiency of the Estate.-When the estate is insufficient to pay the general legacies in full, in the absence of an expressed indication that the testator intended otherwise, legacies abate pro rata. One exception to this is that where a legacy is given for the support, maintenance or education of a near relative otherwise unprovided for, it will be preferred. 

In re Neil's Estate, 238 N. Y. 138. (5) Renunciation of the Legacy. If a legatee renounces his legacy, it falls into the residurary estate. The legacy does not pass as intestate property. 

In re Southmayd's Estate, 124 Misc. 647, 207 N. Y. S. 558.

L is mentioned as a legatee. He murders the testator. L loses his legacy because the law will not permit a criminal act to create a claim. 

Riggs v. Palmer, 115 N. Y. 506. (6) Executor as Distributor.-Where the residue is given to an executor to be distributed among certain persons, including himself, in such amounts as he, the executor, deems right, he must either waive the right to take thereunder or refuse to act as executor. 

In re Lawler's Will, 215 A. D. 506, 213 N. Y. S. 723.

The Codicil 

A codicil has been defined as an addition to a will or an explanation or alteration of the former disposition of the testator and is to be taken as part of the will, all making but one testament. 

Brown v. Clark, 77 N. Y. 369, 375.

The codicil to be probated must have been executed with all the formality required by the statute for the execution of wills and when so executed operates as a republication of the will. The date of the execution of the codicil would then be the date of the execution of the will. 

In re Simeone's Estate, 253 N. Y. S. 683. 

Gifts to Charity

If a deceased leaves a spouse, child, descendant or parent surviving, he cannot, if any one of these objects, leave more than one-half of his estate (after his debts are paid) to any charitable, benevolent, literary, scientific, or religious purpose. 

The bequest or devise exceeding the one-half is considered as not having been bequeathed or devised, and is distributed in accordance with the intestacy laws. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 17. 

The provisions may be insisted upon by any one who would derive a benefit, though not a wife, a descendant or parent, provided, however, that a wife, descendant or parent is living. 

In re McQuirk's Estate, 130 Misc. 336, affd. 229 N. Y. S. 880. 

To ascertain whether a testator has given more than one-half of his estate, the whole estate must be treated as converted into money at his death, and if the money value of the portion given, is not more than one-half, then the statute has not been violated. No interest is allowed the "charitable" beneficiary because of a postponement in payment. Yet where the bequest to charity reads "Onehalf of my estate is hereby bequeathed to (naming the legatee)”, one-half of any increase between the date of death and the date of distribution belongs to the legatee. There cannot possibly be a vio-

lation of the statute as the will expressly limits the legatee to onehalf of the estate. 

In re Opdyke's Will, 255 N. Y. 255;

Decedent Estate Law, § 17. 

A gift in a will "to distribute where my executor shall consider it will be most effective in the advancement of Christ's Kingdom on Earth" was held to be a valid bequest, and not void for indefiniteness and uncertainty of purpose. The court held that the express purpose of the clause was to promote the cause of the Christian religion in a most general sense and was therefore for charitable, religious uses. 

In re Durbrow, 245 N. Y. 469.

Mutual Wills 

Parties may agree to execute mutual and reciprocal wills. Where one of the parties, in violation of the agreement, makes a later will making other disposition of his property, the first joint will should not be admitted to probate even though it was the result of a contract, for the reason that it is not the last will of the decedent. The devisees and legatees under the second will, which can be probated, can be compelled to perform the obligations of the contract. 

Hermann v. Ludwig, 186 A. D. 287, 174 N. Y. S. 467; 

Levenson v. Levenson, 242 N. Y. S. 165.

If one party dies before either will has been revoked, the wills become fixed obligations. Equity will enforce them if the survivor attempts to impair his will by subsequent testamentary provisions. 

The courts tend to respect contracts made by a testator during his lifetime, even though the effect be to distribute the property of a testator after his death. An oral contract between T (testator) and

B by the terms of which B agrees to do work for T in consideration of T's promise to leave B a definite legacy or devise, is good. If T violates the contract by omitting to make the promised provision by will, B becomes a creditor of the estate for whatever damage he may be able to prove. The Statute of Frauds is not a defense to B's action, because B has fully performed his contract and no interest or estate in property was created orally in contravention of the Real Property Law requiring a creation of such interest or estate to be in writing, because T had full title at all times. In a recent case, T said, "Now, John, I have had a lot of trouble and I want you to come back to work for me and I will pay you $15 per week and when I die I will give you this grocery business and this property." 

The promisee agreed to work as long as T was in business. The business was sold seven years later and during that time the services were rendered. T's will did not provide for the promisee, and the latter was allowed to recover the value of the business sold. 

In re Seifried's Estate, 130 Misc. 229, 224 N. Y. S. 275.

Advancements

If A, standing in loco parentis toward S, transfers property to S in anticipation of the share of the donor's estate which the donee would receive if A died intestate, that transfer is an advancement. 

The amount of realty given to S is deducted from the realty left him, and the personalty from the personalty left. If there is not enough realty to deduct from, then the personalty must be used and vice versa. Note that the advances must have been made with a view to settlement. Giving money for child's upkeep or education is not that. But money given for a business use now, instead of after death, is money given with a view to a settlement. 

Decedent Estate Law, §§ 96, 97. 

If A disposes of his whole estate by will, the doctrine of advancements has no application, unless the will specifically refers to advancements and defines which previous gifts shall be so considered. 

Even if the will expressly states that the testator's property is to be distributed in accordance with the intestacy laws, the doctrine of advancements does not apply, for there is a will. The effect of the will was to cancel any effect that the advancements might have on the distribution. Ademption

Ademption is the extinction or satisfaction of a legacy by some act of a testator. Thus, if after the execution of a will containing a bequest to the testator's daughter the latter in consideration of the payment to her of $1,000 agreed to take that sum in lieu of the legacy, she could not recover under the terms of the will. We are assuming that the will was left unchanged.

Ademption applies only to personalty. It is equivalent to a revocation of a bequest of the will, and the rule is applied where the testator stands in loco parentis towards the legatee. Mistakes 

Burnham v. Comfort, 108 N. Y. 535.

These are of two kinds, those that go to the essence, and those that do not.

Suppose T writes a will and is about to sign. Unobserved, his aunt changes it for another in which the only change is that she gets $100 more. The will is void, as it is not T's will. The mistake goes to the essence. The point is brought out even more strongly in a case where a husband and wife drew identical wills, making each other beneficiaries. By mistake he signed hers and she his. Both wills were void. No testimony is admissible to correct the situation. 

They did not sign their wills.

Suppose the will is written by T and through absent-mindedness he puts in a $10,000 legacy for S instead of the $1,000, and he signs.

The will stands as signed and executed. The mistake cannot be rectified. But if T's lawyer had written the will for him and T did not read the will before he signed, the court on probate would allow evidence to show the mistake and would throw out the mistaken part, as not being T's wish, for T was not in a position to be held accountable for the error. If the will had been read to T, and he signed, it would be the same as if T had written the will and signed. 

Where the testator at the time was very ill or illiterate there is no presumption that he knew what he was doing. The character of the will and the contents must be proven. The court cannot assume that the will was read to the testator.

Would the court, if it put out the erroneous bequest, put in the correct one? No, for after the bequest is denied there is a blank, and the court, though it will correct an evident error so as to make sense, will not, however, add anything for the testator and thus actually write a part of the will for him. 

Parol evidence may be admitted to explain a will where a latent ambiguity exists arising outside the instrument. It can never supply, contradict, enlarge or vary the written words. Where ordinary English words are used by the testator, parol evidence is inadmissible to show that the testator attached a peculiar meaning to them. It cannot be shown that the testator habitually, or at the execution of the will, used the plain English words in some peculiar manner. 

In re Quackenbush's Will, 127 Misc. 731, 217 N. Y. S. 493.

A will left $50 to the "Erieville Church". There was no church in Erieville by that specific name, but there was one church there, a Methodist church. The bequest was paid to the trustees of that institution. 

In re Card's Estate, 260 N. Y. S. 764.

The surrounding facts and circumstances can be used, as to what his attitude was toward his beneficiaries, and the situation of his property. But this extraneous evidence cannot supply, contradict or vary the written words. 

Conditional Will

Suppose A of Italy, here on business, executes a will "to be effective if he should die on the way home to Italy." He gets back to 

Italy safely, but he fails to destroy the will. That will cannot be probated, as it expressly states that it is a conditional will. If, however, A had written the condition into the will intending later to change it, but did not, it cannot be refused probate on the ground that it is a conditional will. 

In re Poonarman's Will, 234 N. Y. 329; 

In re Webb's Estate, 122 Misc. 129, 202 N. Y. S. 346.

Incorporation by Reference 

A testator may not import into his will an unattested memorandum of his mere desires and expectations, or his unexecuted plans. Thus if T bequeaths "$5,000 to each society, the list of their names to be found in my green trunk", the list is not part of the will. Even if T had stated "I hereby incorporate the list," the situation would not be altered.

Where the will incorporates by reference another accomplished fact, it is not necessary to incorporate in the will the details of the thing referred to. Thus in one case the testator conferred a power on his daughter to dispose of his estate and then added a provision in substance, that in case of her death before him and her consequent inability to execute the power, the legatees under her will should be deemed, none the less, to be the legatees under his own. The daughter died before her father. It was held that her will might be referred to for the purpose of determining the legatees entitled to take under his and and the extent of their respective shares. 

In re Pifford, 111 N. Y. 410. 

In a very recent case a bequest to a trust company in trust for the testator's daughter under the terms of a previous and existing trust agreement, "made part of this will as if fully set forth herein,' was held not invalid for failure to restate the purpose of the trust referred to, so a bequest to a son by cancelling whatever indebtedness appears upon the father's books, is a valid gift though to understand the extent of the legacy one must go beyond the will itself. 

In re Rausch's Will, 258 N. Y. 327.

A gift or devise of realty or personalty may be incorporated by reference in a validly executed will, provided all of the following elements are present: (1) The gift or devise must be for general charitable purposes. (2) The resolution, declaration or deed of trust referred to in the will must be identified as existing prior to the execution of such will and made to a corporation authorized by law to accept such devise or gift. (3) A copy of the resolution, declaration or deed of trust, certified by the secretary or assistant secretary or cashier or assistant cashier of the corporation, under the corporate seal, shall have been filed in the office of the Secretary of State of New York and in the office in which real property conveyances are to be filed for record in the county in which said corporation has its principal place of business.

Personal Property Law, § 12; 

Real Property Law, § 113.

Revocation

A man may revoke his entire will by burning, tearing, cancelling, obliterating or otherwise destroying the will or the signature, with intent to revoke. Where the testator crossed out his signature and wrote "Void, J. M. Cronin," underneath, it was held a good revocation, as the lines drawn across the signature amounted to an obliteration.

In re Cronin's Estate, 124 Misc. 848, 208 N. Y. S. 680; 

In re Kunta's Will, 251 N. Y. S. 403.

A testator may also tell another to destroy his will for him, but this must be done before, and proved by, at least two other witnesses. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 34. 

If a testator tears up or destroys a part of a will, intending to cancel that part alone, the will is not affected in any way. If the original language can be ascertained by competent evidence, the original will may be admitted to probate in the form and condition in which it was when originally executed. So it is clear that by tearing, etc., you cannot revoke a part of a will, if what the part contained can be ascertained. If what has been destroyed cannot be ascertained, that part of the will which remains can be probated as the testator's last will, unless it can be seen that the missing parts would affect or alter the remaining parts. 

In re Fox's Estate, 118 Misc. 352, 193 N. Y. S. 232; 

In re Bescher's Estate, 132 Misc. 625, 229 N. Y. S. 821. 

Can you then in any way at all revoke a part of a will? Yes, by a later writing executed with the statutory formalities, as if that revocatory part were a whole will in itself. This second writing, in so far as it is inconsistent with the first will, revokes the first. So if the first will left a house to T's son, and a later executed writing left it to his daughter, the latter would get the house. 

The latter writing may be in the form of interlineations. This would, of course, be valid only if properly executed, as a regular will would have to be.

Suppose Mr. D makes a will in 1890. Then he makes another in 

1900. In 1902 he tears up the 1900 will with intent to revoke it.

Is the 1890 will now in force? No, the making of a valid second will revokes the first. To have the first will in force it must be rewritten and reexecuted, or it must be republished, and the republication, if that form is preferred, must be made to the original attesting witnesses. If that were not done, Mr. D. would die intestate. Properly executing a statement in writing, as “I republish my 1890 will," is a good republication.

Decedent Estate Law, § 41. 

So it follows that if a testator revokes a codicil in which $1,000 was left to the Animal Institute, it does not put the $1,000 back into

the will. The codicil in its effect revoked a $1,000 part of the will. 

There is an intestacy as to that $1,000, unless there is the proper republication. 

In re Cable's Will, 123 Misc. 894, 206 N. Y. S. 501.

The law presumes that a will proved to have had existence and not found at the death of the testator was destroyed animo revocandi. 

It is likewise true that where a will is executed in duplicate or triplicate and one of the examples is not found at the death of the testator, the law presumes that the example not so found and produced was destroyed animo revocandi, and such destruction revokes all the examples. The burden of proof to overcome this presumption is upon the proponent. 

In re Vogelsang, 236 N. Y. S. 917. 

Fraud and Undue Influence 

Not every fraud invalidates a will. If the testator, through misrepresentation made by his neighbor as to the latter's heroic work in saving the testator's child, leaves the neighbor $1,000, the will would be probated, but the neighbor would get nothing. So if the misrepresentations of the neighbor also caused the testator to cut off his brother entirely because, as his neighbor said, the brother had proven himself a coward, the brother cannot go in as a legatee. The brother is limited to his action against the neighbor. The fraud to vitiate the will must go to the very essence. 

Undue influence is an improper or unlawful constraint, urging or persuasion, whereby the testator is induced against his will to so deal with his property as he would not do if left to act freely and of his own volition. To overthrow the will for undue influence, motive and opportunity to exert influence must appear, and the exertion of such influence to such extent that the will of the maker was overthrown and the will of another substituted. 

In re Connor's Will, 230 A. D. 163, 244 N. Y. S. 221. 

In re Dotterweich's Will, 205 N. Y. S. 580, 210 A. D. 131.

If there is undue influence used, the entire will is not good, because that goes to the essence, and the whole will is not that of the testator. 

A case where undue influence seems clear is where the one who was made the sole beneficiary became acquainted with the testatrix two weeks before the testatrix's death, superintended the execution, selected the witnesses and the draftsmen. 

In re Alfaya's Will, 122 Misc. 771, 204 N. Y. S. 90.

The burden of proof is upon the contestant upon an issue of undue influence. 

In re Kindberg's Will, 207 N. Y. 220, 228.

If a proponent be content to remain passive and refrain from explaining a legitimate influence flowing from his adversary's evi-

dence, his case must necessarily go against him; he cannot be allowed to ignore evidence advanced by one whose affirmative duty it is to bear the burden and produce the proof. 

In re Schellinger's Will, 258 N. Y. 186.

Rules for Construction of a Will 

The court in construing a will should construe it so as to carry out the testator's intent. Words he uses are to be given their ordinary meaning, and parol evidence is not admissible to show that the testator did not mean what he said in words, though these words may have been chosen by the attorney who drafted the will. But if giving the words their primary significance will render a will absurd, such meaning will not be followed. 

Dwight v. Fancher, 245 N. Y. 71. 

Where the intent of the testator cannot be ascertained with reasonable certainty from the will itself, extrinsic evidence may be received for the purpose of aiding the court in ascertaining the real intent of the testator.

A failure to use the proper technical language or legal terms will not defeat an intention clearly manifested and clearly disclosed by an examination of the whole will. 

In re White's Estate, 219 A. D. 502; affd. 245 N. Y. 587. 

There are these leading canons of interpretation: (1) The law favors the vesting of legacies. (2) The testator is presumed to have intended to dispose of all his property, and an interpretation which results in partial intestacy will not be favored. (3) If futurity is annexed to the substance of a gift, the vesting is suspended. (4) If the gift is absolute and only the time of payment is postponed, the gift is not suspended but vests at once. (5) Where the gift is only found in a direction to pay at a future time, time will be deemed to be of the essence of the gift; but if the amount of the gift is to be severed instanter from the general estate for the benefit of the legatee, and the interest thereon paid him until the period of distribution, a mere direction to pay at such a period will constitute a present gift. (6) Where a clause in a will is capable of two interpretations, the one should be adopted which prefers the persons of the testator's blood to strangers. (7) Where a testator has expressed two intentions, legally inconsistent, the court will stand in the place of the testator and give effect

to that intention which the testator would have preferred if compelled to choose between the two. (8) When an estate is given in one part of a will, in clear and decisive terms, it is an established interpretive rule that the estate will not be taken away or cut down by raising a doubt as to the meaning or application of a subsequent clause, but only by positive provision in words as clear and decisive as those giving the estate. 

In re Lindsay's Estate, 258 N. Y. S. 792; 

In re McCready's Will, 236 A. D. 390, 259 N. Y. S. 512, 517.

The rules have exceptions, and they give way to the intention of the testator as expressed in the will. 

Clark v. Cammaran, 106 N. Y. 315.

A devise speaks as to the time of the testator's death. All the estate of the testator which he then owns and could devise is included in the disposition. This rule differs from the common-law rule, which was that a devise passed title to real property owned at the time of the publication of the will: Under the common law, therefore, property acquired by the testator subsequent to the publication of the will was not carried along by the will. Under the statutory law, if a will shows an intention to dispose of all the testator's real estate, the will carries all after-acquired property. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 14. 

In one case the testator devised "all my right, title and interest in parcels A, B, C and D to my sister Julia, and the rest, residue and remainder to Sarah." At the time of the execution of the will the testator had a one-half interest in those four parcels and he owned a fifth parcel "E." Subsequent to the execution of the will the testator sold parcel "E" and obtained the full interest in parcels "A, B, C and D." It was held that Julia was entitled to all and 

Sarah to nothing. If the testator had stated that he devised "all my right, title and interest which I now possess, etc." Julia would have been limited to the interest of the testator at the time of the execution of the will.

U. S. Security Trust Co. v. Petrillo, 129 Misc. 15, 220 N. Y. S. 635. 

When a will speaks of the death of a devisee or legatee without issue, it means such death occurring during the life of the testator. 

So that where there is a devise to one person in fee, and in case of his death to another, the contingency referred to is the death of the first named devisee during the lifetime of the testator, and if such devisee survives the testator, he takes an absolute fee; the words of contingency do not create a remainder over to take effect upon the death, at any time, of the first taker. 

In re Martin's Will, 225 A. D. 502, 233 N. Y. S. 602.

A child en ventre sa mere, which is subsequently born alive and capable of living, is considered a child living from the date of in-

ception (which date is presumed to be 280 days before actual birth, unless it is shown that it was conceived later or earlier) in order to take a beneficial interest in a bequest or devise, where the description is "child living." 

Farmers L. & Tr. Co. v. Pierson, 130 Misc. 110, 222 N. Y. S. 532; 

In re Well's Will, 129 Misc. 447, 221 N. Y. S. 714. 

A bequest to "my children me surviving" includes only such children as are alive at the death of the testator. Without an expression in the will tending to contradict the use of the word "child" which denotes immediate offspring, living descendants of a predeceased child of the testator, are not included in the legacy. 

In re Harris' Will, 245 N. Y. S. 570.

It has been held that the terms "nephews" and "nieces" in their primary and ordinary sense do not include grandnephews and grandnieces, or more remote descendants, unless there is something in the will to show that the words were used in a broader sense. The word "children" does not mean grandchildren, unless there is something in the will other than the sue of that word to show that it was so intended. "Cousins" has been interpreted to mean first cousins. 

In re Salyer's Estate, 142 Misc. 300, 255 N. Y. S. 81. 

A will directed payment "to my nieces and nephews Edna, Rose.

Nettie, William and Floyd" after the death of A, who was given a life interest. Edna died before A. Was the estate of Edna entitled to the share of Edna or were the other four surviving A entitled? Since the beneficiaries were named, the individuals were meant. It was not a gift to a class. And since the payment was postponed only to let in an intermediate estate-A's life estate-Edna's estate vested only upon the death of the testator. A legacy is contingent when the legacy does not vest immediately, only when the enjoyment of it depends upon some particular event, e.g., the arrival of the legatee at a certain age. 

In re Rochester Trust Co., 129 Misc. 318, 222 N. Y. S. 256.

When an estate in fee simple is granted, a condition that the grantee shall not alien the land or be limited in his right to do so, is void. Therefore a condition that a grantee in fee simple shall alienate it only to those having the testator's family name, is void, for the reason that a fee-simple estate and such a restraint upon its alienation cannot in their nature coexist. A codicil which provided that the said property "should be kept in possession, and that neither 

Joseph J. Steffan nor Anthony J. Steffan, the grantees, can force a sale of said property at this time or at any other time in the future unless satisfactory to both Joseph J. Steffan and Anthony J. Steffan," was declared void. 

Steffan v. Steffan, 135 Misc. 508, 239 N. Y. S. 155.

If a will devises property to the wife of the testator, to be her absolute property, provided that on death or remarriage whatever property is undisposed of should go to A, B and C, etc., the wife has no power to dispose of the property by will. The courts have interpreted such clause to mean that the wife could use the property liberally for her own use, comfort, benefit and support, but such user had to be in good faith. 

Vincent v. Putnam, 248 N. Y. 76.

A general gift of income arising from personal property making no mention of the disposition of the principal, is equivalent to a general gift of the property itself. 

In re Bellman's Will, 255 N. Y. S. 550.

A gift to a class has been defined as the gift of a sum to a body of persons uncertain in number at the time of the gift, to be ascertained at a future time, and who are all to take in equal or in some other definite proportions, the share of each being dependent for its amount upon the ultimate number of persons. If there is a bequest to certain persons nominatum, or so described as to be fixed at the time of the gift, so that there can be no fluctuation, then if one of them dies in the lifetime of the testator his share lapses. 

In re Watson's Will, 144 Misc. 213, 258 N. Y. S. 755. 769.

Rights of Surviving Spouse of a Testator 

If a testator dies and leaves a will executed after August 31, 1930, the surviving spouse may, if he or she so desires, renounce the will and take his or her share as in intestacy, subject to these limitations: (1) The spouse cannot get more than one-half of the estate after deductions are made for debts, taxes and administration expenses. (2) If the intestate share exceeds $2500 and the testator has created a trust fund in amount equal to or greater than the intestate share, of which trust the spouse is life beneficiary, the spouse can take $2500 absolutely, and that amount is deducted from the principal of that trust fund. The remainder of the will is not affected. 

If the intestate share is less than $2500, the spouse can elect to take such intestate share and that share is in lieu of any provisions for his or her benefit, in the will.

If the will contains an absolute legacy or devise, general or specific, to the surviving spouse of at least $2500 and also a provision for trust fund for his or her benefit for life, the principal of which trust fund is at least equal to the difference between the amount bequeathed or devised and the intestacy share of the surviving spouse, no right of election exists. Where, however, the specific or general legacy or devise is less than $2500 and a trust fund is created for his or her benefit for life at least equal to the difference between the amount bequeathed or devised and the in-

testacy, the spouse can elect to take $2500 outright, and the difference between the legacy or devise and the sum of $2500 shall be deducted from the principal of the trust. The remainder of the will is not thereby affected. (3) Where the aggregate amount for the benefit of the surviving spouse, including the principal of a trust, is less than the intestate share, the surviving spouse can elect to take the difference between such aggregate amount and the intestate share, the rest of the will remaining unaffected.

It should be noted that the above provisions regarding trusts, with income payable for life to the surviving spouse, apply to legal life estates or to annuities. In computing the value of the provisions under the will, the capital value of the fund producing the income is the value of the life estate. 

The rights of election above mentioned are not given to a spouse who has been an unsuccessful defendant, or successful plaintiff, in a divorce action, against the deceased, the divorce decree being recognized as valid in this state. The same rule applies if the surviving spouse has been an unsuccessful defendant in a separation action brought by the deceased; or if the surviving spouse obtained a divorce from the deceased in another state, even though the divorce decree is not recognized as valid in New York. 

A husband who has neglected in his lifetime to provide for his wife or abandoned her has no right of election. So, also, if the wife had abandoned her husband.

The election may be made by the general guardian of an infant when permitted by the Surrogate having jurisdiction, or on behalf of an incompetent when permitted by the Supreme Court. 

An election made under this section is in lieu of any dower in lands acquired by the husband prior to September 1, 1930. The notice of election must be served personally upon the representatives of the estate personally or in such other manner as the surrogate may direct and by filing a copy with proof of service in the court where the will was probated. This service and filing must take place within six months from the issuance of letters, unless the time is extended by the surrogate before the expiration of the six months. 

In re Lottman's Estate, 261 N. Y. S. 400.

The right to elect is a purely personal right and may be waived by a spouse during the lifetime of the other by an instrument subscribed and acknowledged, or in an agreement of settlement so executed, before or after marriage. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 18. 

Though the will make no provision for the surviving spouse, it remains valid and otherwise unobjectionable except insofar as the

terms of the will are defeated by the exercise of the right of election. 

In re Mihlman's Will, 251 N. Y. S. 146.

Intestacy Laws Affecting Estates of Decedents 

If a person dies intestate, his property is distributed in accordance with a schedule entirely different from the above. The rules make uniform the devolution of both realty and personalty. 

The real property and surplus of personal property not devised, after payment of debts, shall be distributed as follows: (1) One-third share of the personalty and realty belongs to the surviving spouse if any children or grandchildren survive, and the residue in equal portions to the children and such persons as legally represents the children if any such have predeceased the intestate, per stirpes. (2) If a spouse and parents (but no descendants) survive, the former takes $5,000 and half the remainder and the parents divide the balance between them. If only one parent survives, that parent takes the balance. If the deceased in such case was illegitimate, the mother receives the share of both parents. (3) If there be no spouse or descendant surviving, the parents of the deceased take all in equal amounts. (4) If there be a spouse surviving and no descendant, parent, brother, sister, nephew or niece, the spouse takes all. (5) If there be a brother or sister or nephew or niece surviving, the spouse receives $10,000 and half the residue and the balance descends to the brothers and sisters and their representatives per stirpes. (6) If descendants survive and there is no surviving spouse, the children and such as legally represent them, take all, per stirpes. (7) If there be no surviving spouse, no children, no representative of a child, and no parent, the whole is distributed to the next of kin, in equal degree to the deceased. Brothers and sisters and the children of any deceased brother or sister take equally per stirpes. 

Relatives of half blood and their representatives take equally with those of the whole blood; posthumous children take equally with those born in the lifetime of the deceased and who survive him. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 83. 

It should be noted that beyond brothers and sisters and their descendants, only persons within the nearest degree of relationship to the decedent are entitled to inherent intestate real or personal propperty. In other words, where there is a group of first cousins, as the nearest relatives, they alone are entitled to inherit. In such case if there are children or grandchildren of deceased first cousins, they are

not entitled to inherit. The same rule applies to the ascertainment of the class of next of kin or heirs where there are second cousins or third cousins or more distant classes of relatives. When persons of the nearest degree of relationship establish that standing, those more remote are excluded. 

In re Wendel's Will, 257 N. Y. S. 87.

No distributive share is allowed a spouse whose marriage with the decedent has been dissolved under a decree recognized as valid in this state. If the decree is not so recognized, the spouse is not entitled to a share if he or she were the plaintiff in that action. 

Decedent Estate Law, § 87.
